All human endeavor requires the fiction of a starting line. Even science, that rigid accountant of reality, must invent a zero, a phantom point in the endless march of the stars where its clock declares time begins. Its less precise cousin, Poetry, is said to begin in the middle; yet upon closer inspection, she is merely following the same rule. Science counts backward as well as forward, slicing its unit into billions, and with its finger pointing at nothing, it truly launches *in medias res*. No memory can reach the true origin; whether our prologue unfolds in heaven or on earth, it is but a fragment of the vast, presupposed fact from which our story springs.

Was she beautiful, or was she not? What secret architecture of form or expression lent such dynamic force to her glance? Was it the good genius or the evil that dominated those beams? Likely the latter; otherwise, why did her effect stir unrest rather than undisturbed charm? Why did the desire to look again feel like coercion rather than a willing longing of the whole soul?

The woman posing these questions in Daniel Deronda's mind was currently gambling. Not in the open air beneath a southern sun, tossing copper coins on a crumbling wall with rags wrapped around her limbs, but within one of those magnificent resorts that the enlightenment of ages has crafted for this specific pleasure at a heavy price of gilded moldings, dark tones, and plump nudes. These elements formed a suitable condenser for the heavy human breath of the highest fashion, a mixture not easily inhaled elsewhere in such proportions by those of lesser station.

It was nearly four o'clock on a September afternoon, the atmosphere thick and well-brewed into a visible haze. Deep stillness reigned, broken only by a light clatter, a soft chink, a sweeping sound, and an occasional monotone in French, issuing from an ingeniously constructed automaton. Around two long tables gathered serried crowds of humanity, all save one having their faces and attention bent upon the games. That solitary exception was a melancholy little boy, his knees and calves clad simply in natural epidermis, but the rest of his person adorned in fancy dress. Alone, he faced the doorway, fixing upon it the blank gaze of a bedizened child stationed as a masquerading advertisement on the platform of an itinerant show. He stood close behind a lady deeply engrossed at the roulette table.

About this table fifty or sixty persons were assembled. In the outer rows, where new arrivals occasionally deposited themselves as mere spectators, one might observe a woman now and then placing down a five-franc note with a simpering air, merely to taste the passion of gambling. Those deriving pleasure at a higher intensity, absorbed in the play, displayed a wide variety of European types: Livonian and Spanish, Graeco-Italian and miscellaneous German, English aristocratic and English plebeian. Here certainly was a striking admission of human equality. The white, jeweled fingers of an English countess were nearly touching a bony, yellow, crab-like hand stretching a bare wrist to clutch a heap of coin—a hand easy to sort with the square, gaunt face, deep-set eyes, grizzled eyebrows, and ill-combed, scanty hair that seemed a slight metamorphosis of a vulture. Where else would her ladyship graciously consent to sit beside that dry-lipped feminine figure, prematurely old and withered after a short bloom like artificial flowers, holding a shabby velvet reticule before her and occasionally putting the point with which she pricked her card into her mouth?

Very near the fair countess sat a respectable London tradesman, blonde and soft-handed, his sleek hair scrupulously parted. Conscious of circulars addressed to the nobility and gentry, whose distinguished patronage allowed him to take his holidays fashionably and in their company, he possessed a well-fed leisure. Unlike the gambler's passion that nullifies appetite, his was a resource for winning money in business and spending it showily, then winning more in play to spend even more showily. He reflected that Providence had never manifested disapproval of his amusement, remaining dispassionate enough to cease if the sweetness of winning much and seeing others lose turned to the sourness of losing much and seeing others win. For the vice of gambling lay in losing money at it. In his bearing, there might be something of the tradesman, but in his pleasures, he was fit to rank with the owners of the oldest titles. Standing close to his chair was a handsome Italian, calm and statuesque, reaching across to place the first pile of napoleons from a fresh bag brought by an envoy with a scrolled mustache. The pile was pushed over in half a minute to an old, bewigged woman with eyeglasses pinching her nose. There was a slight gleam, a faint mumbling smile about the old woman's lips; but the statuesque Italian remained impassive, probably secure in an infallible system that placed his foot on the neck of chance, and immediately prepared a new pile. So did a man with the air of an emaciated beau or worn-out libertine, who looked at life through one eyeglass and held out his hand tremulously when asking for change. It could surely be no severity of system, but rather some dream of white crows, or the induction that the eighth of the month was lucky, which inspired the fierce yet tottering impulsiveness of his play.

Yet, while every single player differed markedly from every other, there existed a certain uniform negativeness of expression that had the effect of a mask—as if they had all eaten of some root that for the time compelled the brains of each to the same narrow monotony of action.

Deronda's first thought, when his eyes fell on this scene of dull, gas-poisoned absorption, was that the gambling of Spanish shepherd-boys had seemed to him more enviable: so far, Rousseau might be justified in maintaining that art and science had done a poor service to mankind. But suddenly, the moment became dramatic. His attention was arrested by a young lady standing at an angle not far from him, the last to whom his eyes traveled. She was bending and speaking English to a middle-aged lady seated at play beside her, but the next instant she returned to her game, revealing the full height of a graceful figure, with a face that might possibly be looked at without admiration, but could hardly be passed with indifference.

The inward debate she raised in Deronda gave his eyes a growing expression of scrutiny, drifting farther and farther away from the glow of mingled, undefined sensibilities forming admiration. At one moment they followed the movements of the figure, of the arms and hands, as this problematic sylph bent forward to deposit her stake with an air of firm choice; and the next they returned to the face which, at present unaffected by beholders, was directed steadily toward the game. The sylph was a winner. As her taper fingers, delicately gloved in pale gray, adjusted the coins pushed toward her to pass them back to the winning point, she looked around her with a survey too markedly cold and neutral not to contain a little of that nature we call art concealing an inward exultation.

But in the course of that survey, her eyes met Deronda's. Instead of averting them as she would have desired, she was unpleasantly conscious that they were arrested. How long? The darting sense that he was measuring her, looking down on her as an inferior, that he was of different quality from the human dross around her, that he felt himself in a region outside and above her, examining her as a specimen of a lower order, roused a tingling resentment that stretched the moment with conflict. It did not bring the blood to her cheeks, but it sent it away from her lips. She controlled herself by the help of an inward defiance, and without other sign of emotion than this lip-paleness, she turned to her play. But Deronda's gaze seemed to have acted as an evil eye. Her stake was gone. No matter; she had been winning ever since she took to roulette with a few napoleons at command and had a considerable reserve. She had begun to believe in her luck; others had begun to believe in it. She had visions of being followed by a *cortège* who would worship her as a goddess of luck and watch her play as a directing augury. Such things had been known of male gamblers; why should not a woman have a like supremacy? Her friend and chaperon, who had not wished her to play at first, was beginning to approve, administering the prudent advice to stop at the right moment and carry money back to England—advice to which Gwendolen had replied that she cared for the excitement of play, not the winnings. On that supposition, the present moment ought to have made the flood-tide in her eager experience of gambling. Yet, when her next stake was swept away, she felt the orbits of her eyes getting hot, and the certainty she had (without looking) of that man still watching her was something like a pressure that began to be torturing. The more reason she had not to flinch, but to go on playing as if she were indifferent to loss or gain.

Her friend touched her elbow and proposed that they should quit the table. For reply, Gwendolen put ten louis on the same spot. She was in that mood of defiance in which the mind loses sight of any end beyond the satisfaction of enraged resistance; and with the puerile stupidity of a dominant impulse, it includes luck among its objects of defiance. Since she was not winning strikingly, the next best thing was to lose strikingly. She controlled her muscles, showing no tremor of mouth or hands. Each time her stake was swept off, she doubled it. Many were now watching her, but the sole observation she was conscious of was Deronda's, who, though she never looked toward him, she was sure had not moved away. Such a drama takes no long while to play out; development and catastrophe can often be measured by nothing clumsier than the moment-hand. "Faites votre jeu, mesdames et messieurs," said the automatic voice of destiny from between the mustache and imperial of the croupier. And Gwendolen's arm was stretched to deposit her last poor heap of napoleons. "Le jeu ne va plus," said destiny. And in five seconds, Gwendolen turned from the table, but turned resolutely with her face toward Deronda and looked at him. There was a smile of irony in his eyes as their glances met; but it was at least better that he should have kept his attention fixed on her than that he disregarded her as one of an insect swarm who had no individual physiognomy. Besides, in spite of his superciliousness and irony, it was difficult to believe that he did not admire her spirit as well as her person: he was young, handsome, distinguished in appearance—not one of these ridiculous and dowdy Philistines who thought it incumbent on them to blight the gaming-table with a sour look of protest as they passed by it. The general conviction that we are admirable does not easily give way before a single negative; rather, when any of Vanity's large family, male or female, find their performance received coldly, they are apt to believe that a little more of it will win over the unaccountable dissident. In Gwendolen's habits of mind, it had been taken for granted that she knew what was admirable and that she herself was admired. This basis of her thinking had received a disagreeable concussion, and reeled a little, but was not easily to be overthrown.

In the evening, the same room was more stiflingly heated, brilliant with gas and with the costumes of ladies who floated their trains along it or were seated on the ottomans.

The Nereid in sea-green robes and silver ornaments, with a pale sea-green feather fastened in silver falling backward over her green hat and light brown hair, was Gwendolen Harleth. She was under the wing, or rather soared by the shoulder, of the lady who had sat by her at the roulette-table; and with them was a gentleman with a white mustache and clipped hair: solid-browed, stiff, and German. They walked about or stood to chat with acquaintances, and Gwendolen was much observed by the seated groups.

"A striking girl—that Miss Harleth—unlike others."

"Yes, she has got herself up as a sort of serpent now—all green and silver, and winds her neck about a little more than usual."

"Oh, she must always be doing something extraordinary. She is that kind of girl, I fancy. Do you think her pretty, Mr. Vandernoodt?"

"Very. A man might risk hanging for her—I mean a fool might."

"You like a *nez retroussé*, then, and long narrow eyes?"

"When they go with such an *ensemble*."

"The *ensemble du serpent*?"

"If you will. Woman was tempted by a serpent; why not man?"

"She is certainly very graceful; but she wants a tinge of color in her cheeks. It is a sort of Lamia beauty she has."

"On the contrary, I think her complexion one of her chief charms. It is a warm paleness; it looks thoroughly healthy. And that delicate nose with its gradual little upward curve is distracting. And then her mouth—there never was a prettier mouth, the lips curled backward so finely, eh, Mackworth?"

"Think so? I cannot endure that sort of mouth. It looks so self-complacent, as if it knew its own beauty—the curves are too immovable. I like a mouth that trembles more."

"For my part, I think her odious," said a dowager. "It is wonderful what unpleasant girls get into vogue. Who are these Langens? Does anybody know them?"

"They are quite *comme il faut*. I have dined with them several times at the *Russie*. The baroness is English. Miss Harleth calls her cousin. The girl herself is thoroughly well-bred, and as clever as possible."

"Dear me! and the baron?"

"A very good furniture picture."

"Your baroness is always at the roulette-table," said Mackworth. "I fancy she has taught the girl to gamble."

"Oh, the old woman plays a very sober game; drops a ten-franc piece here and there. The girl is more headlong. But it is only a freak."

"I hear she has lost all her winnings to-day. Are they rich? Who knows?"

This was the letter Gwendolen discovered upon her table:

DEAREST CHILD.—I have been expecting to hear from you for a week. In
your last correspondence, you mentioned that the Langens contemplated
departing Leubronn for Baden. How could you be so thoughtless as to
leave me in uncertainty regarding your address? I am in the greatest
anxiety lest this missive fail to reach you. In any event, you were
to return home at the end of September, and I must now implore you to
come back as quickly as possible. Should you exhaust your funds, I
shall be powerless to send you further assistance, and you must not
borrow from the Langens, for I could not repay them. This is the sad
truth, my child—I wish I could prepare you for it more gently—but a
dreadful calamity has befallen us all. You know nothing of business
and will not comprehend it; yet Grapnell & Co. have failed for a
million, and we are totally ruined—your aunt Gascoigne as well as I,
save that your uncle holds his benefice. Thus, by selling their
carriage and securing interest for the boys, the family can merely
subsist. All the property our poor father saved for us must now settle
these liabilities. There is nothing I can call my own. It is better
you know this at once, though it rends my heart to speak it. Naturally,
we cannot help wondering what a pity it is that you departed just
when you did. Yet I shall never reproach you, my dear child; I would
shield you from all trouble if I could. On your journey home, you will
have time to prepare yourself for the change you will find. We shall
perhaps leave Offendene immediately, for we hope Mr. Haynes, who
desired it previously, may be ready to purchase it from me. Of course,
we cannot move to the rectory—there is not a corner to spare. We must
secure some humble shelter and live on your uncle Gascoigne's charity
until I discover what else can be done. I shall be unable to pay the
debts to the tradesmen, nor even the servants' wages. Summon your
fortitude, my dear child; we must resign ourselves to God's will. But
it is hard to resign oneself to Mr. Lassman's wicked recklessness,
which they claim caused the failure. Your poor sisters can only weep
with me and offer no aid. If you were here, there might be a break in
the cloud—I always feel it impossible that you were meant for poverty.
If the Langens wish to remain abroad, perhaps you can place yourself
under someone else's care for the journey. But come as soon as you can
to your afflicted and loving mamma,

The initial impact of this letter upon Gwendolen was half-stupefying. The
implicit confidence that her destiny awaited her in luxurious ease, where
any trouble would be elegantly clad and fully provided for, had been
stronger in her own mind than in her mother's, nourished by her youthful
blood and that sense of superior claims which occupied a large portion
of her consciousness. It was nearly as difficult for her to believe
suddenly that her position had shifted to one of poverty and humiliating
dependence as it would have been to accept the chilling realization that
her death was imminent. She stood motionless for several minutes, then
removed her hat and mechanically glanced into the mirror. The coils of
her smooth, light-brown hair remained perfect enough for a ballroom; and
as on other nights, Gwendolen might have lingered before her reflection
for pleasure (surely an allowable indulgence); but now she took no
conscious note of her beauty, staring fixedly ahead as if jarred by a
hateful sound, waiting for its source to reveal itself. By and by, she
threw herself into the corner of the red velvet sofa, picked up the
letter again, and read it twice deliberately, letting it finally fall to
the floor while she rested her clasped hands on her lap, sitting perfectly
still, shedding no tears. Her impulse was to survey and resist the
situation rather than to wail over it. There was no inward exclamation
of "Poor mamma!" Her mother had never seemed to derive much enjoyment
from life, and if Gwendolen felt pity at this moment, she would bestow
it upon herself—for was she not naturally and rightfully the chief
object of her mother's anxiety? But it was anger, it was resistance that
possessed her; it was bitter vexation that she had lost her gains at
roulette, whereas if her luck had continued through that single day, she
would have carried a handsome sum home, or might have played on until
she won enough to support them all. Even now, was it not possible? She
had only four napoleons remaining in her purse, yet she possessed
ornaments she could sell: a practice so common in stylish society at
German baths that there was no need for shame. Even without her mother's
letter, she would likely have decided to raise funds for an Etruscan
necklace she had not worn since her arrival; indeed, she might have done
so with an agreeable sense of living with intensity and escaping the
humdrum. With ten louis at her disposal and a return of her former luck,
which seemed probable, what could she do better than continue playing for
a few days? If her friends at home disapproved of how she acquired the
money, as they certainly would, the money would still be hers. Gwendolen's
imagination dwelt on this course, creating agreeable consequences, but
not with the unbroken confidence and rising certainty characteristic of
the gambler's mania. She had approached the roulette table not out of
passion, but in search of it: her mind remained sanely capable of
picturing balanced probabilities, and while the chance of winning
allured her, the chance of losing thrust itself upon her with alternate
strength, causing a vision from which her pride sank sensitively. For she
was resolved not to inform the Langens of any misfortune befalling her
family, nor to make herself indebted to their compassion; and if she were
to part with her jewelry to any observable extent, they would interfere
with inquiries and remonstrances. The path holding the least risk of
intolerable annoyance was to raise money on her necklace early in the
morning, tell the Langens that her mother desired her immediate return
without giving a reason, and take the train for Brussels that evening.
She had no maid with her, and the Langens might make difficulties about
her returning home, but her will was peremptory.

Instead of retiring to bed, she made the light as brilliant as possible
and began to pack, working diligently, though constantly visited by
scenes of the coming day—now by tiresome explanations and farewells, and
the whirlwind journey toward a changed home, now by the alternative of
staying just another day and standing once more at the roulette table.
But in this latter scene, there was always the presence of Deronda,
watching her with exasperating irony, and—the two keen experiences were
inevitably revived together—beholding her again forsaken by luck. This
importunate image certainly helped to sway her resolve toward immediate
departure and urged her packing to the point where a change of mind would
become inconvenient. It had struck twelve when she entered her room, and
by the time she ensured she had left out only what was necessary, the
faint dawn was stealing through the white blinds, dulling her candles.
What use was sleep? Her cold bath was refreshment enough, and she saw
that a slight trace of fatigue around the eyes only made her look the
more interesting. Before six o'clock, she was completely equipped in her
gray traveling dress, including her felt hat, intending to walk out as
soon as she could count on seeing other ladies heading to the springs.
Happening to sit sideways before the long strip of mirror between her
two windows, she turned to gaze at herself, leaning her elbow on the
chair's back in an attitude that might have been chosen for her portrait.
It is possible to possess a strong self-love without self-satisfaction,
rather with a self-discontent that is more intense because one's own
little core of egoistic sensibility is a supreme care; but Gwendolen knew
nothing of such inward strife. She had a *naïve* delight in her fortunate
self, which any but the harshest saintliness would indulge in a girl who
daily saw a pleasant reflection of that self in her friends' flattery as
well as in the looking-glass. And even in this beginning of troubles,
while for lack of anything else to do she sat gazing at her image in the
growing light, her face gathered a complacency gradual as the
cheerfulness of the morning. Her beautiful lips curled into a more and
more decided smile, until finally she removed her hat, leaned forward,
and kissed the cold glass which had looked so warm. How could she believe
in sorrow? If it attacked her, she felt the force to crush it, defy it,
or run away from it, as she had already done. Anything seemed more
possible than that she could continue bearing miseries, great or small.

Madame von Langen never went out before breakfast, allowing Gwendolen to
safely conclude her early walk by returning home through the Obere
Strasse, where the needed shop was certain to be open after seven. At
that hour, any observers she minded would be either strolling in the
region of the springs or still in their bedrooms; but certainly there
was one grand hotel, the *Czarina*, from which eyes might follow her up
to Mr. Wiener's door. This was a chance to be risked: might she not be
going in to purchase something that had struck her fancy? This implicit
falsehood passed through her mind as she recalled that the *Czarina* was
Deronda's hotel; but she was then already far up the Obere Strasse,
walking on with her usual floating movement, every line in her figure
and drapery falling in gentle curves attractive to all eyes except those
which discerned in them too close a resemblance to the serpent and
objected to the revival of serpent-worship. She looked neither right nor
left, transacting her business in the shop with a coolness that gave
little Mr. Wiener nothing to remark except her proud grace of manner and
the superior size and quality of the three central turquoises in the
necklace she offered him. They had belonged to a chain once her father's:
but she had never known her father; and the necklace was in all respects
the ornament she could most conveniently part with. Who supposes that it
is an impossible contradiction to be superstitious and rationalizing at
the same time? Roulette encourages a romantic superstition regarding the
chances of the game, and the most prosaic rationalism concerning human
sentiments that stand in the way of raising needful money. Gwendolen's
dominant regret was that after all, she had only nine louis to add to the
four in her purse; these Jewish dealers were so unscrupulous in taking
advantage of Christians unfortunate at play! But she was the Langens'
guest in their hired apartment and had nothing to pay there; thirteen
louis would suffice to take her home. Even if she determined to risk
three, the remaining ten would more than suffice, since she intended to
travel right on, day and night. As she turned homeward, nay, entered and
seated herself in the *salon* to await her friends and breakfast, she
still wavered regarding her immediate departure, or rather she had
concluded to tell the Langens simply that she had received a letter from
her mother desiring her return, leaving undecided when she should start.
It was already the usual breakfast time, and hearing someone enter as she
leaned back, tired and hungry with her eyes shut, she rose expecting to
see one or other of the Langens—the words that might determine her
lingering at least another day, ready-formed to pass her lips. But it was
the servant bringing in a small packet for Miss Harleth, which had at
that moment been left at the door. Gwendolen took it in her hand and
immediately hurried into her own room. She looked paler and more agitated
than when she had first read her mother's letter. Something—she never
quite knew what—revealed to her before she opened the packet that it
contained the necklace she had just parted with. Wrapped in a cambric
handkerchief underneath the paper was a scrap of torn-off note-paper, on
which was written with a pencil, in clear but rapid handwriting—"_A
stranger who has found Miss Harleth's necklace returns it to her with
the hope that she will not again risk the loss of it._"

Gwendolen reddened with the vexation of wounded pride. A large corner of
the handkerchief seemed recklessly torn off to erase a mark; but she
immediately believed in the first image of "the stranger" that presented
itself to her mind. It was Deronda; he must have seen her enter the shop;
he must have gone in immediately after and repurchased the necklace. He
had taken an unpardonable liberty, daring to place her in a thoroughly
hateful position. What could she do? Not, assuredly, act on her
conviction that it was he who sent the necklace and straightway return it
to him: that would be to face the possibility that she had been mistaken;
nay, even if the "stranger" were he and no other, it would be something
too gross for her to let him know she had divined this

"Let no flower of the spring pass by us; let us crown ourselves with rosebuds before they be withered."

It was a pity that Offendene was not the soil of Miss Harleth's childhood, nor a place endeared to her by the roots of memory. A human life, surely, ought to be anchored in some corner of the native land, where it might drink deeply of tender kinship, learn to love the very face of the earth, and find nourishment in the labors of men and the haunting sounds of home. It is a spot where early memories are inwrought with affection, where kindness toward neighbors—even the dogs and donkeys—becomes a sweet habit of the blood rather than a sentimental effort. At five years old, mortals are not yet citizens of the world; they are not prepared to soar above preference into impartiality. That blind prejudice in favor of milk is merely the body and soul learning how to feed themselves for a time. The best introduction to astronomy is to view the nightly heavens as a little lot of stars belonging to one's own homestead.

But this blessed persistence, where affection can take root, had been wanting in Gwendolen's life. It was only a year prior to her recall from Leubronn that Offendene had been chosen as her mother's home, selected simply for its proximity to Pennicote Rectory. On a late October afternoon, when rooks cawed loudly overhead and yellow elm-leaves whirled in the wind, Mrs. Davilow, Gwendolen, and her four half-sisters had been driven along the avenue for the first time, the governess and maid following in another vehicle.

The season suited the aspect of the old oblong red-brick house, which was rather too anxiously ornamented with stone at every line, from the double row of narrow windows to the large square portico. The stone encouraged a greenish lichen, the brick a powdery gray, so that though the building was rigidly rectangular, there was no harshness in its physiognomy as it faced the three avenues cutting through the hundred yards of old plantation. One would have liked the house to stand on a knoll, looking beyond its own little domain to the long thatched roofs of distant villages, the church towers, the scattered homesteads, and the green breadths of undulating park that made up the beautiful face of the earth in that part of Wessex. But standing behind a screen amid flat pastures, it offered on one side a glimpse of the wider world in the lofty curves of the chalk downs, grand steadfast forms played over by the changing days.

The house was just large enough to be called a mansion, moderately rented, and lacking a manor. Its sombre furniture and faded upholstery made it difficult to let, yet inside and out, it was what no beholder could suppose to be inhabited by retired tradespeople—a certainty worth many conveniences to tenants who possessed the taste that shrinks from new finery and dwell in that border-territory of rank where annexation is a burning topic. To take up her abode in a house that had once sufficed for dowager countesses gave a perceptible tinge to Mrs. Davilow's satisfaction in having an establishment of her own. This possibility appeared suddenly, somewhat mysteriously to Gwendolen, on the death of her step-father, Captain Davilow, who had for the last nine years joined his family only in brief, fitful bursts. Yet Gwendolen cared much more for the fact than the explanation. All her prospects had become more agreeable in consequence. She had disliked their former way of life, roving from one foreign watering-place or Parisian apartment to another, always feeling new antipathies to new suites of hired furniture and meeting new people under conditions that made her appear of little importance. The variation of having passed two years at a showy school, where she was put foremost on all occasions of display, had only deepened her sense that so exceptional a person as herself could hardly remain in ordinary circumstances or in a social position less than advantageous. Any fear of this latter evil was banished now that her mother was to have an establishment. On the point of birth, Gwendolen was quite easy. She had no notion how her maternal grandfather acquired the fortune inherited by his two daughters, but he had been a West Indian—which seemed to exclude further question; and she knew that her father's family was so high as to take no notice of her mother, who nevertheless preserved with much pride the miniature of a Lady Molly in that connection. She would probably have known much more about her father but for a little incident when she was twelve. Mrs. Davilow had brought out, at wide intervals, various memorials of her first husband. While showing his miniature to Gwendolen, she recalled with a fervor that seemed to count on peculiar filial sympathy the fact that dear papa had died when his little daughter was in long clothes. Gwendolen, immediately thinking of the unlovable step-father whom she had known while her frocks were short, said, "Why did you marry again, mamma? It would have been nicer if you had not." Mrs. Davilow colored deeply, a slight convulsive movement passing over her face, and straightway shutting up the memorials, she said, with a violence quite unusual in her, "You have no feeling, child!" Gwendolen, who was fond of her mother, felt hurt and ashamed, and had never since dared to ask a question about her father.

This was not the only instance in which she had brought on herself the pain of filial compunction. It was always arranged, when possible, that she should have a small bed in her mother's room, for Mrs. Davilow's motherly tenderness clung chiefly to her eldest girl, who had been born in her happier time. One night, under an attack of pain, she found that the specific regularly placed by her bedside had been forgotten, and begged Gwendolen to get out of bed and reach it for her. That healthy young lady, snug and warm as a rosy infant in her little couch, objected to step out into the cold, lying perfectly still and grumbling a refusal. Mrs. Davilow went without the medicine and never reproached her daughter; but the next day Gwendolen was keenly conscious of what must be in her mother's mind and tried to make amends by caresses which cost her no effort. Having always been the pet and pride of the household, waited on by mother, sisters, governess, and maids as if she had been a princess in exile, she naturally found it difficult to think her own pleasure less important than others made it. When it was positively thwarted, she felt an astonished resentment apt, in her cruder days, to vent itself in one of those passionate acts which look like a contradiction of habitual tendencies. Though never even as a child thoughtlessly cruel—nay, delighting to rescue drowning insects and watch their recovery—there was a disagreeable silent remembrance of her having strangled her sister's canary-bird in a final fit of exasperation at its shrill singing, which had again and again jarringly interrupted her own. She had taken pains to buy a white mouse for her sister in retribution, and though inwardly excusing herself on the ground of a peculiar sensitiveness which was a mark of her general superiority, the thought of that infamous murder had always made her wince. Gwendolen's nature was not remorseless, but she liked to make her penances easy. Now that she was twenty and more, some of her native force had turned into a self-control by which she guarded herself from penitential humiliation. There was more show of fire and will in her than ever, but there was more calculation underneath it.

On this day of arrival at Offendene, which not even Mrs. Davilow had seen before—the place having been taken for her by her brother-in-law, Mr. Gascoigne—when all had got down from the carriage and were standing under the porch in front of the open door, so that they could have a general view of the place and a glimpse of the stone hall and staircase hung with sombre pictures but enlivened by a bright wood fire, no one spoke. Mamma, the four sisters, and the governess all looked at Gwendolen, as if their feelings depended entirely on her decision. Of the girls, from Alice in her sixteenth year to Isabel in her tenth, hardly anything could be said on a first view, but that they were girlish and that their black dresses were getting shabby. Miss Merry was elderly and altogether neutral in expression. Mrs. Davilow's worn beauty seemed the more pathetic for the look of entire appeal which she cast at Gwendolen, who was glancing round at the house, the landscape, and the entrance hall with an air of rapid judgment. Imagine a young race-horse in the paddock among untrimmed ponies and patient hacks.

"Well, dear, what do you think of the place?" said Mrs. Davilow at last, in a gentle, deprecatory tone.

"I think it is charming," said Gwendolen, quickly. "A romantic place; anything delightful may happen in it; it would be a good background for anything. No one need be ashamed of living here."

"There is certainly nothing common about it."

"Oh, it would do for fallen royalty or any sort of grand poverty. We ought properly to have been living in splendor, and have come down to this. It would have been as romantic as could be. But I thought my uncle and aunt Gascoigne would be here to meet us, and my cousin Anna," added Gwendolen, her tone changed to sharp surprise.

"We are early," said Mrs. Davilow, and entering the hall, she said to the housekeeper who came forward, "You expect Mr. and Mrs. Gascoigne?"

"Yes, madam; they were here yesterday to give particular orders about the fires and the dinner. But as to fires, I've had 'em in all the rooms for the last week, and everything is well aired. I could wish some of the furniture paid better for all the cleaning it's had, but I _think_ you'll see the brasses have been done justice to. I _think_ when Mr. and Mrs. Gascoigne come, they'll tell you nothing has been neglected. They'll be here at five, for certain."

This satisfied Gwendolen, who was not prepared to have their arrival treated with indifference; and after tripping a little way up the matted stone staircase to take a survey there, she tripped down again, and followed by all the girls looked into each of the rooms opening from the hall—the dining-room all dark oak and worn red satin damask, with a copy of snarling, worrying dogs from Snyders over the side-board, and a Christ breaking bread over the mantel-piece; the library with a general aspect and smell of old brown-leather; and lastly, the drawing-room, which was entered through a small antechamber crowded with venerable knick-knacks.

"Mamma, mamma, pray come here!" said Gwendolen, Mrs. Davilow having followed slowly in talk with the housekeeper. "Here is an organ. I will be Saint Cecilia: some one shall paint me as Saint Cecilia. Jocosa (this was her name for Miss Merry), let down my hair. See, mamma?"

She had thrown off her hat and gloves and seated herself before the organ in an admirable pose, looking upward; while the submissive and sad Jocosa took out the one comb which fastened the coil of hair, and then shook out the mass till it fell in a smooth light-brown stream far below its owner's slim waist.

Mrs. Davilow smiled and said, "A charming picture, my dear!" not indifferent to the display of her pet, even in the presence of a housekeeper. Gwendolen rose and laughed with delight. All this seemed quite to the purpose on entering a new house which was so excellent a background.

"What a queer, quaint, picturesque room!" she went on, looking about her. "I like these old embroidered chairs, and the garlands on the wainscot, and the pictures that may be anything. That one with the ribs—nothing but ribs and darkness—I should think that is Spanish, mamma."

"Oh, Gwendolen!" said the small Isabel, in a tone of astonishment, while she held open a hinged panel of the wainscot at the other end of the room.

Every one, Gwendolen first, went to look. The opened panel had disclosed the picture of an upturned dead face, from which an obscure figure seemed to be fleeing with outstretched arms. "How horrible!" said Mrs. Davilow, with a look of mere disgust; but Gwendolen shuddered silently, and Isabel, a plain and altogether inconvenient child with an alarming memory, said, "You will never stay in this room by yourself, Gwendolen."

"How dare you open things which were meant to be shut up, you perverse little creature?" said Gwendolen, in her angriest tone. Then snatching the panel out of the hand of the culprit, she closed it hastily, saying, "There is a lock—where is the key? Let the key be found, or else let one be made, and let nobody open it again; or rather, let the key be brought to me."

At this command to everybody in general, Gwendolen turned with a face which was flushed in reaction from her chill shudder, and said, "Let us go up to our own room, mamma."

The housekeeper, on searching, found the key in the drawer of the cabinet close by the panel, and presently handed it to Bugle, the lady's-maid, telling her significantly to give it to her Royal Highness.

"I don't know what you mean, Mrs. Startin," said Bugle, who had been busy up-stairs during the scene in the drawing-room, and was rather offended at this irony in a new servant.

"I mean the young lady that's to command us all—and well worthy for looks and figure," replied Mrs. Startin in propitiation. "She'll know what key it is."

"If you have laid out what we want, go and see to the others, Bugle," Gwendolen had said, when she and Mrs. Davilow entered their black and yellow bedroom, where a pretty little white couch was prepared by the side of the black

"Marriage is a sacred thing," declared Gorgibus, his voice rising with the fervor of a man defending a holy relic. "And it is the mark of a gentleman to begin with it."

"Oh, my dear sir!" Madelon laughed, the sound light and dismissive, like a bird shaking off rain. "If everyone were like you, a novel would end before the first page was turned! Imagine if Cyrus married Mandane immediately, and Aronce wed Clélie right then and there! Let us weave our story at our own leisure, and do not rush us toward the conclusion."

The scene shifted, the French wit dissolving into the quiet, dust-moted air of a country rectory. It would be easy to fault the rector of Pennicote for viewing his niece, Gwendolen, through the lens of a brilliant match. Was he not merely a man of his time, expecting a young woman of such charm to secure a prosperous union? Yet, his intentions were pure, devoid of the cynical calculus that might have suggested she dress in rags to tempt a marquis or hide away to be saved by a stranger. His plans were rational, grounded in the sensible desire to present her advantageously within the neighborhood's best circles.

These plans aligned perfectly with Gwendolen's own heart. But one must not mistake her ambition. She did not dream of marriage as the ultimate prize, nor did she view it as the direct reward for her graces on horseback or her social poise. While she accepted that matrimony was inevitable, and that it would certainly be above the mundane mediocrity of most women, she did not see it as the fulfillment of her soul's yearning. To be the object of desperate sighs and legal suits was a delightful proof of her power; but to become a wife, bound by the heavy chains of domesticity, seemed a tedious necessity.

To Gwendolen, marriage appeared a dreary state where a woman lost her freedom, bore too many children, and sank into a humdrum existence. Of course, it offered social elevation, a promotion she could not refuse, yet promotions often come with bitter herbs. A title does not replace the need for leadership. This delicate, twenty-year-old sylph intended to lead. Though the passions that drive such ambition usually dwell in the hearts of men, they resided here too, nestled among her feminine trappings. Her knowledge, however, was not of the world-shaking variety; she possessed no leverage to move the machinery of state or science. Instead, she sought to do what pleased her in a striking manner, or rather, whatever she could do to dazzle others and feel, through their admiration, a more intense pulse of living.

"Gwendolen will not rest until she has the world at her feet," whispered Miss Merry, the meek governess. These were hyperbolic words, now softened to mean simply that she commanded the attention of a few polite acquaintances in a genteel suburb. Yet, for Gwendolen, standing high in her young self-exaltation, the phrase carried a vast, hazy promise. Others allowed themselves to be swept away like empty ships, tossed by winds they could not control. Not so she. She would not be sacrificed to lesser creatures; she would master the chances life offered, conquering circumstances with her exceptional cleverness.

Certainly, her position at Offendene, marked by the notice of Lady Brackenshaw, the archery club, and dinner invitations from the Arrowpoints, did not scream of extraordinary opportunity. But Gwendolen's confidence rested entirely on herself. She felt fully equipped to rule her destiny. Regarding her past, she believed she had been treated harshly, yet her education had left her unscathed. In the schoolroom, her sharp mind had absorbed the rigid starch of unexplained rules and disconnected facts, shielding her from the pain of ignorance. Through novels, plays, and poetry, she knew enough of the rest of the world. Her French and music were flawless, the standard justifications for a young lady's station. Add to this the innate sense of capability that some are born with—the feeling that any subject they touch reveals their own power to judge it—and who could wonder why Gwendolen felt ready to steer her own ship?

There were countless subjects in the world, perhaps the majority, that bored her, appearing as dull as dim light to the elderly. Yet, if they arose in conversation, she would not falter. No one had ever disputed her power or her general superiority. From her arrival at Offendene onward, the first thought of everyone around her was always, *What will Gwendolen think?* If a footman's boots creaked, if the laundry was subpar, if the wood smoke filled the bedroom, apologies were offered directly to her. If she arrived late to breakfast, the concern was solely that her coffee and toast remain hot and crisp. When she entered, her light-brown hair brushed fresh, her large brown eyes gleaming like a wave-washed onyx beneath long lashes, she was the one who had to exercise tolerance. She had to gently chide Alice for her stiff posture and tell Isabel to retreat, leaving the room to Miss Merry.

She was always the princess in exile, entitled to a breakfast roll made of the finest flour from seven golden ears of wheat, and to have her silver fork preserved during a general evacuation. How was this to be explained? The answer seemed obvious: her beauty, her unusualness, her decisive will that animated her graceful movements and clear tones. On a rainy day when everyone else felt flaccid and the purpose of things faded, her presence alone seemed sufficient reason to maintain the forms of life. Even hotel waiters moved with greater alacrity to clear crumbs and creases when she was near. This potent charm, combined with her status as the eldest daughter—toward whom her mother had always felt a penitent sorrow for the hardships inflicted by a stepfather—seemed a complete explanation for her domestic empire. To seek another cause would be to ask for the reason of daylight when the sun shines.

But beware of drawing conclusions without comparison. I have witnessed the same assiduous, apologetic attention paid to those who were neither beautiful nor unusual, whose firmness lacked grace, and who were not eldest daughters with tender, compunctious mothers. Some were merely common men. The only thread connecting them was a strong determination to have what they desired, paired with a total fearlessness in becoming disagreeable or dangerous when denied. Who is cajoled and served with trembling by the weak females of a household more than the unscrupulous male, capable of doing worse elsewhere if he lacks freedom at home? Thus, I am forced to doubt whether, even without her charm and her special filial position, Gwendolen might not still have played the queen in exile, had she merely kept her inborn energy of egoistic desire and her power to inspire fear regarding what she might say or do.

However, she possessed the charm. Those who feared her were also fond of her; the fear and the affection were perhaps heightened by the iridescence of her character—the play of various, nay, contrary tendencies. Macbeth's rhetoric about the impossibility of being many opposite things at once referred to the clumsy necessities of action, not the subtler possibilities of feeling. We cannot speak a loyal word and remain meanly silent in the same breath; we cannot kill and refrain from killing simultaneously. But a moment is wide enough to hold the loyal and the mean desire, the outlash of a murderous thought, and the sharp, sudden stroke of repentance.

"Her wit
Values itself so highly, that to her
All matter else seems weak."
—_Much Ado About Nothing._

Gwendolen's arrival in the neighborhood was a triumph that perfectly matched her uncle's expectations. From the imposing Brackenshaw Castle down to the cozy Firs at Wanchester, where Mr. Quallon the banker hosted with generous warmth, she was greeted with open admiration. Even those who harbored reservations about her found comfort in her presence; for hostesses, much like ministers assembling their cabinets, must balance their social rosters on grounds far broader than mere personal affection. To secure Gwendolen as a guest, one need not fear the disagreeable, for Mrs. Davilow always cut a quiet, picturesque figure as her chaperone, and Mr. Gascoigne was universally sought after for his own charming sake.

Yet, among the households where Gwendolen was tolerated rather than loved, Quetcham Hall stood out. One of her earliest invitations was to a grand dinner there, a gathering designed to serve as a general introduction for her to the local elite. It was a select assembly of thirty, carefully balanced across ages, leaving few respectable families unrepresented. No youthful figure in the room could rival Gwendolen's as she drifted through the long suites of rooms, illuminated by light and flowers. At first, she appeared as a slim silhouette floating in white drapery, moving from one wide doorway into another until the full brilliance of her presence revealed itself. She had never experienced such a promenade before, and she felt a surge of exultation that it suited her perfectly; to any observer, it seemed as though long galleries and lackeys had always been the backdrop of her life. Her cousin Anna, however, who was genuinely more accustomed to such settings, felt nearly as awkward as a rabbit suddenly dropped into a brightly lit cage.

"Who is that with Gascoigne?" asked the Archdeacon, abandoning his discussion of military maneuvers, to which he had been naturally summoned as a clergyman. Simultaneously, his son, a hopeful young scholar who had recently proposed "not less elegant than ingenious" corrections to Greek texts, murmured, "By George! Who is that girl with the awfully well-set head and jolly figure?"

But to a mind of general benevolence, hoping everyone looked their best, it was maddening to witness how Gwendolen eclipsed all others. Even the handsome Miss Lawe, daughter of Lady Lawe, suddenly appeared broad, heavy, and lifeless in comparison. And Miss Arrowpoint, unfortunately also dressed in white, instantly resembled a *carte-de-visite* where one might suspect the skirt alone had been charged for. Since Miss Arrowpoint was generally cherished for her amiable, unpretending manner of wearing her fortune—and served as a softening screen for her mother's oddities—there seemed a distinct incongruity in Gwendolen appearing so much more like a person of inherent social importance.

"She is not really so handsome if you come to examine her features," Mrs. Arrowpoint confided later that evening to Mrs. Vulcany. "It is a certain style she possesses, which produces a great effect at first, but afterward she becomes less agreeable."

Indeed, Gwendolen, without intending it, had offended her hostess. Though Mrs. Arrowpoint was neither splenetic nor vindictive, she possessed sharp susceptibilities. Several conditions converged in the Lady of Quetcham to create an impression that the neighborhood's reasoners found essential: she was occasionally recalled to be the heiress of a fortune gained through some obscure city business, an origin seemingly linked to her squat figure, harsh parrot-like voice, and systematically high head-dress. Since these external traits rendered her somewhat ridiculous, it appeared natural to many that she should harbor what were termed literary tendencies. A closer comparison, however, would have shown these points were unrelated; daughters of aldermen were often well-grown and well-featured, pretty women sometimes possessed harsh voices, and the production of feeble literature was compatible with every conceivable physique, masculine or feminine.

Gwendolen, possessing a keen sense of absurdity in others but a kindly disposition toward anyone who could make life agreeable, intended to win Mrs. Arrowpoint's favor with special interest and attention. Yet, self-confidence often addresses an imaginary dullness in others; the wealthy cajole the poor, and the vigorous talk artificially to the elderly, hastily assuming them deaf and imbecile. Gwendolen, despite her cleverness and purpose to be agreeable, could not escape this form of stupidity. Unreflectingly, she concluded that because Mrs. Arrowpoint was ridiculous, she must also lack penetration, proceeding through her little scenes without suspicion that her varied behaviors were being closely noted.

"You are fond of books as well as of music, riding, and archery, I hear," Mrs. Arrowpoint said, approaching her for a *tête-à-tête* in the drawing-room after dinner. "Catherine will be very glad to have so sympathetic a neighbor." This remark, which might have seemed the most graceful politeness delivered in a low, melodious tone, carried a twang, fatally loud, that gave Gwendolen a sense of exercising patronage. She answered gracefully:

"It is I who am fortunate. Miss Arrowpoint will teach me what good music is. I shall be entirely a learner. I hear that she is a thorough musician."

"Catherine has certainly had every advantage. We have a first-rate musician in the house now—Herr Klesmer; perhaps you know all his compositions. You must allow me to introduce him to you. You sing, I believe. Catherine plays three instruments, but she does not sing. I hope you will let us hear you. I understand you are an accomplished singer."

"Oh, no!—'die Kraft ist schwach, allein die Lust ist gross,' as Mephistopheles says."

"Ah, you are a student of Goethe. Young ladies are so advanced now. I suppose you have read everything."

"No, really. I shall be so glad if you will tell me what to read. I have been looking into all the books in the library at Offendene, but there is nothing readable. The leaves all stick together and smell musty. I wish I could write books to amuse myself, as you can! How delightful it must be to write books after one's own taste instead of reading other people's! Home-made books must be so nice."

For an instant, Mrs. Arrowpoint's glance sharpened, but the perilous resemblance to satire in the last sentence took the hue of girlish simplicity when Gwendolen added,

"I would give anything to write a book!"

"And why should you not?" said Mrs. Arrowpoint, encouragingly. "You have but to begin as I did. Pen, ink, and paper are at everybody's command. But I will send you all I have written with pleasure."

"Thanks. I shall be so glad to read your writings. Being acquainted with authors must give a peculiar understanding of their books: one would be able to tell then which parts were funny and which serious. I am sure I often laugh in the wrong place." Here Gwendolen herself sensed danger and quickly added, "In Shakespeare, you know, and other great writers that we can never see. But I always want to know more than there is in the books."

"If you are interested in any of my subjects I can lend you many extra sheets in manuscript," said Mrs. Arrowpoint—while Gwendolen felt herself painfully in the position of the young lady who professes to like potted sprats.

"These are things I dare say I shall publish eventually: several friends have urged me to do so, and one doesn't like to be obstinate. My Tasso, for example—I could have made it twice the size."

"I dote on Tasso," said Gwendolen.

"Well, you shall have all my papers, if you like. So many, you know, have written about Tasso; but they are all wrong. As to the particular nature of his madness, and his feelings for Leonora, and the real cause of his imprisonment, and the character of Leonora, who, in my opinion, was a cold-hearted woman, else she would have married him in spite of her brother—they are all wrong. I differ from everybody."

"How very interesting!" said Gwendolen. "I like to differ from everybody. I think it is so stupid to agree. That is the worst of writing your opinions; you make people agree with you." This speech renewed a slight suspicion in Mrs. Arrowpoint, and again her glance became examining. But Gwendolen looked very innocent, continuing with a docile air:

"I know nothing of Tasso except the _Gerusalemme Liberata_, which we read and learned by heart at school."

"Ah, his life is more interesting than his poetry; I have constructed the early part of his life as a sort of romance. When one thinks of his father Bernardo, and so on, there is much that must be true."

"Imagination is often truer than fact," said Gwendolen, decisively, though she could no more have explained these glib words than if they had been Coptic or Etruscan. "I shall be so glad to learn all about Tasso—and his madness especially. I suppose poets are always a little mad."

"To be sure—'the poet's eye in a fine frenzy rolling'; and somebody says of Marlowe,

"For that fine madness still he did maintain,
Which always should possess the poet's brain.'"

"But it was not always found out, was it?" said Gwendolen innocently. "I suppose some of them rolled their eyes in private. Mad people are often very cunning."

Again a shade flitted over Mrs. Arrowpoint's face, but the entrance of the gentlemen prevented any immediate mischief between her and this too-quick young lady, who had over-acted her *naïveté*.

"Ah, here comes Herr Klesmer," said Mrs. Arrowpoint, rising; and presently bringing him to Gwendolen, she left them to a dialogue which was agreeable on both sides. Herr Klesmer was a felicitous combination of the German, the Slav, and the Semite, with grand features, brown hair floating in artistic fashion, and brown eyes behind spectacles. His English had little foreignness except its fluency; and his alarming cleverness was made less formidable just then by a certain softening air of silliness which sometimes befalls even Genius in the desire to be agreeable to Beauty.

Music was soon begun. Miss Arrowpoint and Herr Klesmer played a four-handed piece on two pianos, convincing the company generally that it was long, and Gwendolen in particular that the neutral, placid-faced Miss Arrowpoint possessed a mastery of the instrument that put her own execution out of question—though she was not discouraged regarding her often-praised touch and style. After this, everyone became anxious to hear Gwendolen sing; especially Mr. Arrowpoint, as was natural in a host and a perfect gentleman, of whom no one had anything to say but that he married Miss Cuttler and imported the best cigars. He led her to the piano with easy politeness. Herr Klesmer closed the instrument in readiness for her, smiling with pleasure at her approach, then placed himself a few feet away so he could watch her.

Gwendolen was not nervous; what she undertook to do she did without trembling, and singing was an enjoyment to her. Her voice was a moderately powerful soprano (someone had told her it was like Jenny Lind's), her ear good, and she could keep in tune, so that her singing gave pleasure to ordinary hearers, and she was used to unmingled applause. She had the rare advantage of looking almost prettier when singing than at other times, and the fact that Herr Klesmer was in front of her seemed not disagreeable. Her song, decided upon beforehand, was a favorite aria of Bellini's, in which she felt quite sure of herself.

"Charming!" said Mr. Arrowpoint, who had remained near, and the word was echoed around without more insincerity than we recognize in a brotherly way as human. But Herr Klesmer stood like a statue—if a statue can be imagined wearing spectacles; at least, he was as mute as a statue. Gwendolen was pressed to keep her seat and double the general pleasure, and she did not wish to refuse; but before resolving to do so, she moved a little toward Herr Klesmer, saying with a look of smiling appeal, "It would be too cruel to a great musician. You cannot like to hear poor amateur singing."

"No, truly; but that makes nothing," said Herr Klesmer, suddenly speaking in an odious German fashion with staccato endings, quite unobservable in him before, and apparently depending on a change of mood, as Irishmen resume their strongest brogue when they are fervid or quarrelsome. "That makes nothing. It is always acceptable to see you sing."

Was there ever so unexpected an assertion of superiority—at least before the late Teutonic conquest? Gwendolen colored deeply, but, with her usual presence of mind, did not show an ungraceful resentment by moving away immediately. Miss Arrowpoint, who had been near enough to overhear (and also to observe that Herr Klesmer's mode of looking at Gwendolen was more conspicuously admiring than was quite consistent with good taste), now with the utmost tact and kindness came close to her and said,

"Imagine what I have to go through with this professor! He can hardly tolerate anything we English do in music. We can only put up with his severity, and make use of it to find out the worst that can be said of us. It is a little comfort to know that; and one can bear it when everyone else is admiring."

"I should be very much obliged to him for telling me the worst," said Gwendolen, recovering herself. "I dare say I have been extremely ill taught, in addition to having no talent—only liking for music." This was very well expressed considering that it had never entered her mind before.

"Yes, it is true: you have not been well taught," said Herr Klesmer, quietly. Woman was dear to him, but music was dearer. "Still, you are not quite without gifts. You sing in tune, and you have a pretty fair organ. But you produce your notes badly; and that music which you

"Croyez-vous m'avoir humiliée pour m'avoir appris que la terre tourne autour du soleil? Je vous jure que je ne m'en estime pas moins."
—FONTENELLE: *Pluralité des Mondes*.

That lofty rebuke had struck a fresh, stinging blow to Gwendolen's vanity. She would never have admitted to herself, let alone confessed to anyone, that she felt a profound regret for lacking Miss Arrowpoint's musical training. Without that foundation, she could not hope to challenge Herr Klesmer's taste with the authority of true knowledge. Even worse, she could not deny that every encounter with Miss Arrowpoint ignited a sharp, unwonted spark of jealousy within her. It was not merely because the other girl was an heiress; it was because it was infuriating that a woman of slight build, average height, small features, and a sallow complexion possessed a mental superiority that defied explanation. There was an exasperating thoroughness in Miss Arrowpoint's musical command, a fastidious discernment in her general tastes that commanded awe and made it impossible to force her admiration. This seemingly insignificant young lady of twenty-four might secretly believe Miss Harleth's accomplishments were merely common, yet she masked such thoughts beneath a veneer of perfect kindness.

Gwendolen refused to dwell on facts that cast such an unfavorable light on her own standing. The Musical Magus who had so suddenly expanded her horizons was not always present; his constant travel between London and Quetcham soon offered opportunities for her to convert him into a more admiring state of mind. Meanwhile, the evident pleasure her singing brought at Brackenshaw Castle, the Firs, and elsewhere restored her equanimity. She was inclined to trust approval far more than objection, and she was certainly not one of those rare souls who thirst for a perfection their neighbors do not demand. Perhaps it would have been rash to claim she was exceptional inwardly, or that the unusual in her extended beyond her rare grace of movement and a certain daring that added spice to a very common egoistic ambition. Such ambition often hides under clumsy exteriors, unnoticed by the world. After all, the tilt of the head does not truly determine the hunger of the inner self for supremacy; it only alters the method of attainment and the degree of success, especially for a girl whose passion for being remarkable finds its ideal limit in consistency with high breeding and a complete freedom from the sordid need for income. Gwendolen was inwardly rebellious against the restraints of family conditions, ready to look through obligations to her own fundamental lack of feeling for them, as if sustained by the boldest speculations. Yet, she possessed no such speculations. She would have instantly distinguished herself from any theoretical or practical reformer by satirizing them. She rejoiced in feeling exceptional, but her horizon remained that of the genteel romance: a heroine whose soul pours out in journals full of vague power, originality, and rebellion, while her life moves strictly within the sphere of fashion. If she wanders into a swamp, the pathos lies partly in her wearing satin shoes. This restraint, provided by nature and society, holds captive a soul burning with a sense of what the universe is not, ready to consume all existence as fuel, keeping it prisoner to the ordinary wirework of social forms and preventing it from doing anything particular.

This commonplace result was what Gwendolen found herself threatened with, even amidst the novelty of the first winter at Offendene. She was clear that she did not wish to lead the same life as ordinary young ladies, but she was unclear on how to begin leading another, or what specific acts she would perform to assert her freedom. Offendene remained a good background if anything exciting were to happen there, but on the whole, the neighborhood was at fault.

Beyond the initial impact of her beauty, there was little excitement to be gleaned from her earliest invitations. She returned home after little sallies of satire and knowingness, such as had offended Mrs. Arrowpoint, to fill the intervening days with girlish devices. Her strongest assertion of individual claim was to skip Alice's lessons (on the principle that Alice would excel in ignorance) and employ her with Miss Merry and the maid who waited on all the ladies. Together, they helped arrange various dramatic costumes Gwendolen had prepared for future charades or theatrical pieces, occasions she intended to create by sheer force of will. She had never acted professionally, only posed in *tableaux vivans* at school, but she felt assured of her ability. Having visited the Théâtre Français and heard her mother speak of Rachel, her waking dreams often turned to whether she should become an actress like that thin Jewess, since she was more beautiful. The wet days before Christmas were spent pleasantly preparing Greek, Oriental, and composite costumes. Gwendolen attitudinized and speechified before a domestic audience, even pressing the housekeeper to swell the notes of applause. However, after the housekeeper pointed out that Miss Harleth looked far more like a queen in her own dress than in a baggy outfit with bare arms, she was not invited again.

"Do I look as well as Rachel, mamma?" Gwendolen asked one day, displaying herself in a Greek dress to Anna and enacting scraps of scenes with much tragic intention.

"You have better arms than Rachel," Mrs. Davilow replied. "Your arms would do for anything, Gwen. But your voice is not so tragic as hers; it is not so deep."

"I can make it deeper, if I like," Gwendolen said provisionally, then added with decision, "I think a higher voice is more tragic: it is more feminine. And the more feminine a woman is, the more tragic it seems when she does desperate actions."

"There may be something in that," Mrs. Davilow said languidly. "But I don't know what good there is in making one's blood creep. And if there is anything horrible to be done, I should like it to be left to the men."

"Oh, mamma, you are so dreadfully prosaic! As if all the great poetic criminals were not women! I think the men are poor cautious creatures."

"Well, dear, and you—who are afraid to be alone in the night—I don't think you would be very bold in crime, thank God."

"I am not talking about reality, mamma," Gwendolen said impatiently. Then, as her mother was called out, she turned quickly to her cousin. "Anna, do ask my uncle to let us get up some charades at the rectory. Mr. Middleton and Warham could act with us—just for practice. Mamma says it will not do to have Mr. Middleton consulting and rehearsing here. He is a stick, but we could give him suitable parts. Do ask, or else I will."

"Oh, not till Rex comes. He is so clever, and such a dear old thing, and he will act Napoleon looking over the sea. He looks just like Napoleon. Rex can do anything."

"I don't in the least believe in your Rex, Anna," Gwendolen laughed. "He will turn out to be like those wretched blue and yellow water-colors of his which you hang up in your bedroom and worship."

"Very well, you will see," Anna said. "It is not that I know what is clever, but he has got a scholarship already, and papa says he will get a fellowship, and nobody is better at games. He is cleverer than Mr. Middleton, and everybody but you calls Mr. Middleton clever."

"So he may be in a dark-lantern sort of way. But he *is* a stick. If he had to say, 'Perdition catch my soul, but I do love her,' he would say it in just the same tone as, 'Here endeth the second lesson.'"

"Oh, Gwendolen!" Anna exclaimed, shocked at these promiscuous allusions. "And it is very unkind of you to speak so of him, for he admires you very much. I heard Warham say one day to mamma, 'Middleton is regularly spooney upon Gwendolen.' She was very angry with him; but I know what it means. It is what they say at college for being in love."

"How can I help it?" Gwendolen said, rather contemptuously. "Perdition catch my soul if I love *him*."

"No, of course; papa, I think, would not wish it. And he is to go away soon. But it makes me sorry when you ridicule him."

"What shall you do to me when I ridicule Rex?" Gwendolen asked wickedly.

"Now, Gwendolen, dear, you *will not*?" Anna said, her eyes filling with tears. "I could not bear it. But there really is nothing in him to ridicule. Only you may find out things. For no one ever thought of laughing at Mr. Middleton before you. Every one said he was nice-looking, and his manners perfect. I am sure I have always been frightened at him because of his learning and his square-cut coat, and his being a nephew of the bishop's, and all that. But you will not ridicule Rex—promise me." Anna ended with a beseeching look that touched Gwendolen.

"You are a dear little coz," she said, touching the tip of Anna's chin. "I don't ever want to do anything that will vex you. Especially if Rex is to make everything come off—charades and everything."

When Rex finally arrived, the animation he brought to Offendene and the rectory, along with his ready partnership in Gwendolen's plans, left her no inclination for any ridicule that was not open and flattering. He was a fine, open-hearted youth with a handsome face resembling his father's and Anna's, yet softer in expression and larger in scale. He possessed a bright, healthy, loving nature that enjoyed ordinary innocent things so much that vice held no temptation for him. His knowledge of vice lay too entirely in the outer courts of his mind for him to think of it with great repulsion. Vicious habits were simply "what some fellows did"—"stupid stuff" he liked to keep aloof from. He returned Anna's affection as fully as could be expected of a brother whose pleasures apart from her exceeded the sum total of hers; he had never known a stronger love.

The cousins were continually together, chiefly at Offendene where there was more freedom, or rather where Gwendolen held a more complete sway. Whatever she wished became a ruling purpose for Rex. The charades came off according to her plans, along with other impromptu scenes where her acting shone. It was at Offendene that the rehearsals and presentations took place. Mrs. Davilow saw no objection to Mr. Middleton joining them now that Rex was there, especially since his services were indispensable. Warham, studying for India with a Manchester "coach," had no time to spare and was generally dismal under a cram of everything except the answers needed for his examination, which might disclose the welfare of the Indian Empire to be connected with a quotable knowledge of Browne's Pastorals.

Mr. Middleton was persuaded to play various grave parts, Gwendolen flattering him on his enviable immobility of countenance. At first, a little pained and jealous of her comradeship with Rex, he presently drew encouragement from the thought that this cousinly familiarity excluded any serious passion. Indeed, he occasionally felt that her more formal treatment of him was such a sign of favor as to warrant his making advances before he left Pennicote, though he had intended to keep his feelings in reserve until his position was more assured. Miss Gwendolen, quite aware that she was adored by this unexceptionable young clergyman with pale whiskers and a square-cut collar, felt nothing more on the subject than that she had no objection to being adored. She turned her eyes on him with calm mercilessness, causing him many mildly agitating hopes by seeming always to avoid dramatic contact with him—for all meanings, we know, depend on the key of interpretation.

Some might have thought beforehand that a young man of Anglican leanings, with a sense of sacredness exercised on small as well as great things, rarely laughing save from politeness, and regarding the mention of spades by their naked names as coarse, would not see a fitting bride in a girl who was daring in ridicule and showed none of the special grace required in a clergyman's wife. Or that a young man informed by theological reading would reflect he was unlikely to meet the taste of a lively, restless young lady like Miss Harleth. But are we always obliged to explain why the facts are not what some persons thought beforehand? The apology lies on their side, who had that erroneous way of thinking.

As for Rex, who would possibly have been sorry for poor Middleton if he had been aware of the excellent curate's inward conflict, he was too completely absorbed in a first passion to have observation for any person or thing. He did not observe Gwendolen; he only felt what she said or did, and the back of his head seemed a good organ of information as to whether she was in the room or out. Before the end of the first fortnight, he was so deeply in love that it was impossible for him to think of his life except as bound up with Gwendolen's. He could see no obstacles; his own love seemed a guarantee of hers, since it was one with the unperturbed delight in her image, so that he could no more dream of her giving him pain than an Egyptian could dream of snow. She sang and played to him whenever he liked, was always glad of his companionship in riding, though his borrowed steeds were often comic, and was ready to join in any fun of his, showing a right appreciation of Anna. No mark of sympathy seemed absent. That Gwendolen was the most perfect creature in the world and thus destined for a grand match had not occurred to him. He had no conceit—at least not more than goes to make up the necessary gum and consistence of a substantial personality. It was only that in the young bliss of loving, he took Gwendolen's perfection as part of that good which had seemed one with life to him, being the outcome of a happy, well-embodied nature.

One incident during their dramatic attempts impressed Rex as a sign of her unusual sensibility. It showed an aspect of her nature that could not have been preconceived by anyone who, like him, had only seen her habitual fearlessness in active exercises and her high spirits in society.

After much rehearsal, it was resolved that a select party should be invited

"The kindliest symptom, yet the most alarming crisis in the ticklish
state of youth; the nourisher and destroyer of hopeful wits; * * * the
servitude above freedom; the gentle mind's religion; the liberal
superstition."—CHARLES LAMB.

The first sign of the unimagined snow-storm was like the transparent
white cloud that seems to set off the blue. Anna was in the secret of
Rex's feeling; though for the first time in their lives he had said
nothing to her about what he most thought of, and he only took it for
granted that she knew it. For the first time, too, Anna could not say
to Rex what was continually in her mind. Perhaps it might have been a
pain which she would have had to conceal, that he should so soon care
for some one else more than for herself, if such a feeling had not been
thoroughly neutralized by doubt and anxiety on his behalf. Anna admired
her cousin—would have said with simple sincerity, "Gwendolen is always
very good to me," and held it in the order of things for herself to be
entirely subject to this cousin; but she looked at her with mingled
fear and distrust, with a puzzled contemplation as of some wondrous and
beautiful animal whose nature was a mystery, and who, for anything Anna
knew, might have an appetite for devouring all the small creatures that
were her own particular pets. And now Anna's heart was sinking under
the heavy conviction which she dared not utter, that Gwendolen would
never care for Rex. What she herself held in tenderness and reverence
had constantly seemed indifferent to Gwendolen, and it was easier to
imagine her scorning Rex than returning any tenderness of his. Besides,
she was always thinking of being something extraordinary. And poor Rex!
Papa would be angry with him if he knew. And of course he was too young
to be in love in that way; and she, Anna had thought that it would be
years and years before any thing of that sort came, and that she would
be Rex's housekeeper ever so long. But what a heart must that be which
did not return his love! Anna, in the prospect of his suffering, was
beginning to dislike her too fascinating cousin.

It seemed to her, as it did to Rex, that the weeks had been filled with
a tumultuous life evident to all observers: if he had been questioned
on the subject he would have said that he had no wish to conceal what
he hoped would be an engagement which he should immediately tell his
father of: and yet for the first time in his life he was reserved not
only about his feelings but—which was more remarkable to Anna—about
certain actions. She, on her side, was nervous each time her father or
mother began to speak to her in private lest they should say anything
about Rex and Gwendolen. But the elders were not in the least alive to
this agitating drama, which went forward chiefly in a sort of pantomime
extremely lucid in the minds thus expressing themselves, but easily
missed by spectators who were running their eyes over the *Guardian* or
the *Clerical Gazette*, and regarded the trivialities of the young ones
with scarcely more interpretation than they gave to the action of
lively ants.

"Where are you going, Rex?" said Anna one gray morning when her father
had set off in his carriage to the sessions, Mrs. Gascoigne with him,
and she had observed that her brother had on his antigropelos, the
utmost approach he possessed to a hunting equipment.

"Going to see the hounds throw off at the Three Barns."

"Are you going to take Gwendolen?" said Anna, timidly.

"She told you, did she?"
