"No—indeed—you are very kind—but it would alarm her too much. I dare take any leap when she is not by; but I do it and don't tell her about it."

"We can let the carriage pass and then set off."

"No, no, pray don't think of it any more: I spoke quite randomly," said Gwendolen. She began to feel a new objection to carrying out her own proposition.

"But Mrs. Davilow knows I shall take care of you."

"Yes, but she would think of you as having to take care of my broken neck."

There was a considerable pause before Grandcourt said, looking toward her, "I should like to have the right always to take care of you."

Gwendolen did not turn her eyes on him. It seemed to her a long while that she was first blushing, and then turning pale, but to Grandcourt's rate of judgment she answered soon enough, with the lightest flute-tone and a careless movement of the head, "Oh, I am not sure that I want to be taken care of: if I chose to risk breaking my neck, I should like to be at liberty to do it."

She checked her horse as she spoke and turned in her saddle, looking toward the advancing carriage. Her eyes swept across Grandcourt as she made this movement, but there was no language in them to correct the carelessness of her reply. At that very moment, she was aware that she was risking something—not her neck, but the possibility of finally checking Grandcourt's advances—and she did not feel contented with that possibility.

"Damn her!" thought Grandcourt as he too checked his horse. He was not a wordy thinker, and this explosive phrase stood for mixed impressions which eloquent interpreters might have expanded into sentences full of an irritated sense that he was being mystified, and a determination that this girl should not make a fool of him. Did she want him to throw himself at her feet and declare that he was dying for her? It was not by that gate that she could enter on the privileges he could give her. Or did she expect him to write his proposals? Equally a delusion. He would not make his offer in any way that could place him definitely in the position of being rejected. But as to her accepting him, she had done it already in accepting his marked attentions; and anything which happened to break them off would be understood to her disadvantage. She was merely coquetting, then?

However, the carriage came up, and no further tête-à-tête could well occur before their arrival at the house, where there was abundant company. To whom Gwendolen, clad in riding-dress with her hat laid aside, and clad also in the repute of being chosen by Mr. Grandcourt, was naturally a centre of observation. Since the objectionable Mr. Lush was not there to look at her, this stimulus of admiring attention heightened her spirits and dispersed, for the time, the uneasy consciousness of divided impulses which threatened her with repentance of her own acts. Whether Grandcourt had been offended or not there was no judging: his manners were unchanged, but Gwendolen's acuteness had not gone deeper than to discern that his manners were no clue for her, and because these were unchanged she was not the less afraid of him.

She had not been at Diplow before except to dine. Since certain points of view from the windows and the garden were worth showing, Lady Flora Hollis proposed after luncheon, when some of the guests had dispersed and the sun was sloping toward four o'clock, that the remaining party should make a little exploration. Here came frequent opportunities when Grandcourt might have retained Gwendolen apart and spoken to her unheard. But no! He indeed spoke to no one else, but what he said was nothing more eager or intimate than it had been in their first interview. He looked at her not less than usual; and some of her defiant spirit having come back, she looked full at him in return, not caring—rather preferring—that his eyes had no expression in them.

But at last it seemed as if he entertained some contrivance. After they had nearly made the tour of the grounds, the whole party stopped by the pool to be amused with Fetch's accomplishment of bringing a water lily to the bank like Cowper's spaniel Beau, and having been disappointed in his first attempt insisted on his trying again.

Here Grandcourt, who stood with Gwendolen outside the group, turned deliberately, fixing his eyes on a knoll planted with American shrubs and having a winding path up it. He said languidly, "This is a bore. Shall we go up there?"

"Oh, certainly—since we are exploring," said Gwendolen. She was rather pleased, and yet afraid.

The path was too narrow for him to offer his arm, and they walked up in silence. When they were on the bit of platform at the summit, Grandcourt said, "There is nothing to be seen here: the thing was not worth climbing."

How was it that Gwendolen did not laugh? She was perfectly silent, holding up the folds of her robe like a statue, and giving a harder grasp to the handle of her whip, which she had snatched up automatically with her hat when they had first set off.

"What sort of a place do you prefer?" said Grandcourt.

"Different places are agreeable in their way. On the whole, I think, I prefer places that are open and cheerful. I am not fond of anything sombre."

"Your place of Offendene is too sombre...."

"It is, rather."

"You will not remain there long, I hope."

"Oh, yes, I think so. Mamma likes to be near her sister."

Silence for a short space.

"It is not to be supposed that *you* will always live there, though Mrs. Davilow may."

"I don't know. We women can't go in search of adventures—to find out the North-West Passage or the source of the Nile, or to hunt tigers in the East. We must stay where we grow, or where the gardeners like to transplant us. We are brought up like the flowers, to look as pretty as we can, and be dull without complaining. That is my notion about the plants; they are often bored, and that is the reason why some of them have got poisonous. What do you think?" Gwendolen had run on rather nervously, lightly whipping the rhododendron bush in front of her.

"I quite agree. Most things are bores," said Grandcourt, his mind having been pushed into an easy current, away from its intended track. But, after a moment's pause, he continued in his broken, refined drawl, "But a woman can be married."

"Some women can."

"You, certainly, unless you are obstinately cruel."

"I am not sure that I am not both cruel and obstinate." Here Gwendolen suddenly turned her head and looked full at Grandcourt, whose eyes she had felt to be upon her throughout their conversation. She was wondering what the effect of looking at him would be on herself rather than on him.

He stood perfectly still, half a yard or more away from her; and it flashed through her mind what a sort of lotus-eater's stupor had begun in him and was taking possession of her. Then he said, "Are you as uncertain about yourself as you make others about you?"

"I am quite uncertain about myself; I don't know how uncertain others may be."

"And you wish them to understand that you don't care?" said Grandcourt, with a touch of new hardness in his tone.

"I did not say that," Gwendolen replied, hesitatingly, and turning her eyes away whipped the rhododendron bush again. She wished she were on horseback that she might set off on a canter. It was impossible to set off running down the knoll.

"You do care, then," said Grandcourt, not more quickly, but with a softened drawl.

"Ha! my whip!" said Gwendolen, in a little scream of distress. She had let it go—what could be more natural in a slight agitation?—and—but this seemed less natural in a gold-handled whip which had been left altogether to itself

The morning sun broke upon Gwendolen Harleth with a brilliance that seemed to scrub clean the doubts of the previous night. She stood tall and vibrant, a newly opened lily in a garden of high society, her energy a sharp reaction against yesterday's hesitation. The prospect of the archery match at Cardell Chase filled her with a delightful anticipation; she imagined herself moving through the beech trees like a wood-nymph, graceful and unburdened. While she knew Grandcourt would not be the passionate, lyrical suitor of romance novels, she calculated his advances with a cool, rational satisfaction. She foresaw him making slow, deliberate approaches toward a declaration, and she prepared herself to receive it with the encouragement she had promised her uncle.

Breakfast was a scene of polite domesticity, though the air hummed with unspoken tension regarding the letters waiting on Gwendolen's plate. When she read one with a gathering smile and passed it to her mother, Mrs. Davilow's face lit up with renewed cheerfulness. The mother, sensing her daughter's restored spirits, gently suggested that Gwendolen write to Dover immediately, as the family was departing that very morning. Gwendolen, however, dismissed the urgency with a brusque refusal, insisting she would write tomorrow. Only then, feeling a fleeting pang of guilt, did she soften her tone, cooing affectionately at her "old, beautiful mamma." The exchange was a dance of affectionate deflection; Gwendolen insisted her mother was hardly twenty-five years older, while Mrs. Davilow, buoyed by the certainty of her daughter's engagement, found happiness in the stability of the arrangement. "The sooner I get my palaces and coaches the better," Gwendolen declared merrily, sealing the moment with a saucy pout.

Yet, a shadow fell over the day's festivities when the rector was detained by magistrate's business. Gwendolen cared little that Mrs. Gascoigne and Anna stayed behind, but she felt a distinct loss of momentum without her uncle's presence to validate the decision she was inching toward. The realization of her own desires was becoming a heavy, defining weight. As she wished for more, the potential for what she might get began to shrink into mere limitation. Still, she clung to the comforting illusion that marriage was the gateway to a vast, new freedom.

The meeting place, Green Arbor, offered a sheltering amphitheatre of hanging wood where servants prepared the picnic. The plan was to stroll briefly before luncheon, saving the main expedition for the golden light of the afternoon. The muster was swift, and as the groups scattered into the dappled shadows of the beeches and oaks, the scene was picturesque. The roving archery, unlike the stationary target games, required a different skill, and Gwendolen did not distinguish herself in the early shots. She dressed in white and green, the same attire that had marked her introduction to Grandcourt. Though he remained constantly by her side, their relationship appeared unchanged to the casual observer, yet the air between them crackled with the unspoken understanding that they were on the eve of engagement.

As the groups drifted back toward Green Arbor, chatting aimlessly, the words that sealed her fate were exchanged. Grandcourt asked, "Do you know how long it is since I first saw you in this dress?" Gwendolen laughed, noting the date discrepancy—"nearly three weeks"—and added, "I am not good at calculating." Grandcourt paused, then replied, "That is a great loss of time." When she protested that his knowing her caused the loss, he corrected her: "It is because of the gain that I feel the loss." He pressed further, asking if she liked uncertainty. Gwendolen, suddenly beaming with a playful smile, admitted she did, sensing there was "more in it." Grandcourt's steady gaze into her eyes seemed to strip away her usual self-consciousness. When he asked if she meant more torment, she blushed and turned away, shaking her head. "No, that would make me sorry."

This moment of vulnerability was decisive, yet Grandcourt, aware of the public eye, hesitated to press further as they descended the steep path to Green Arbor. He offered his hand to help her, and they walked in silence, observed by the crowd below, including Mrs. Arrowpoint. The gossip mill churned; Mrs. Arrowpoint openly compared Grandcourt unfavorably to his uncle, Sir Hugo Mallinger, deeming him too languid for a woman of Catherine's high standards. Mrs. Davilow, however, listened with quiet cheerfulness, her melancholy suspended by the certainty of her daughter's future.

The picnic lunch was a blur of excellent food and champagne, a performance of high-society ease where advantages were taken with a bored, high-bred depreciation. While the gentlemen smoked cigars, Grandcourt was notably absent. Instead, Mr. Lush, usually a blot on the scene to Gwendolen, was particularly serviceable, fetching bows and managing the ladies' needs. When the group prepared to resume the roving, Gwendolen noticed her bow was missing. Fearful that Mr. Lush might take it, she hurried to retrieve it herself. A valet intercepted her, handing over the bow and a letter addressed to her. Written in a delicate, feminine hand, the note commanded her to slip away from the party after passing the Whispering Stones to hear a secret that would decide her fate. It warned of the consequences of disobedience and demanded absolute secrecy.

Gwendolen felt an inward shock, but her youthful absorption in the idea of revelation blinded her to any suspicion of contrivance. She resolved to go unobserved to the stones, thrusting the letter into her pocket. The sense of a secret to guard gave her a bracing quality, helping her maintain composure as she rejoined the company.

To everyone's surprise, Grandcourt did not appear with the group. Lord Brackenshaw joked that they would find him later, attributing his absence to the distraction of a lover absorbed in thought. Gwendolen accepted the hint with quietude, though her mind raced with the terrifying possibility: *Can he too be starting away from a decision?* It was a thought that unsettled her, yet it aligned with a strange, hazy obstinacy she sensed in him. Grandcourt, seemingly suffering from a fit of diseased numbness when an end seemed near, had likely chosen to desist simply because he could, independent of any motive. He sat smoking a second cigar, radiating a vague, hazy defiance.

The rovers set off without depression, leaving behind the less vigorous ladies, including Mrs. Davilow. The afternoon grew exquisite, the forest shifting from roofed groves to open glades as the shadows lengthened. They played an extemporized version of *As You Like It*, and when a compliment was paid to Gwendolen regarding her role as Rosalind, she felt compelled to surpass all expectations in loveliness. An excitement, born of the letter and the uncertainty, fueled her need for adventure and control over her retreat.

Nearly an hour into the roving, they arrived at the Whispering Stones, two tall, conical blocks leaning toward each other like gray-mantled giants. Under the soft sunlight, Gwendolen felt daring. She fell back among the group, then, as they hurried obliquely forward under Mr. Lush's guidance, she spotted her opportunity. She slipped away, moving silently until she was out of sight. Without running, she seemed to fly along the ground, counting the moments until she returned to the stones.

On the other side, she found a stranger. A lady with large dark eyes met hers at a foot's distance. Startled, Gwendolen shrank back but managed to take in the stranger's figure—exceedingly handsome—and noticed two children seated on the grass nearby.

"Miss Harleth?" the lady asked.

"Yes." Wonder filled Gwendolen's consciousness.

"Have you accepted Mr. Grandcourt?"

"No."

"I have promised to tell you something. And you will promise to keep my secret. However you may decide, you will not tell Mr. Grandcourt, or anyone else, that you have seen me?"

"I promise."

"My name is Lydia Glasher. Mr. Grandcourt ought not to marry anyone but me. I left my husband and child for him nine years ago. Those two children are his, and we have two others—girls—who are older. My husband is dead now, and Mr. Grandcourt ought to marry me. He ought to make that boy his heir."

She gestured to the boy, a cherub with brown curls and a hat hanging backward, puffing his cheeks in a failed attempt to blow a tiny trumpet. Gwendolen's eyes followed the gesture, then returned to Lydia Glasher.

"You are very attractive, Miss Harleth. But when he first knew me, I too was young. Since then my life has been broken up and embittered. It is not fair that he should be happy and I miserable, and my boy thrust out of sight for another."

The words were delivered with a biting accent but a determined restraint. Watching the woman's face, Gwendolen felt a sort of terror, as if a ghastly vision had whispered, "I am a woman's life."

"Have you anything more to say to me?" Gwendolen asked in a low, proud tone. The revulsion within her did not soften; everyone seemed hateful.

"Nothing. You know what I wished you to know. You can inquire about me if you like. My husband was Colonel Glasher."

"Then I will go," Gwendolen said, moving away with a ceremonious inclination returned with equal grace.

Minutes later, Gwendolen was back in the beech grove, but her party had vanished, and no search was evident. Solitude enveloped her until she reached the avenue pointed out by the warden. Determined to return quickly, she used rapid movements to suspend the thoughts that might prevent her from behaving with due calm. She had already made up her mind.

Upon her return, Mrs. Davilow was astonished. Gwendolen explained that she had lingered to look at the stones and lost sight of the party, choosing the shorter route back. She feigned relief, claiming she had had enough walking. When Mrs. Arrowpoint hinted that Grandcourt was missing, Gwendolen flashed a look of defiance and a light laugh. "No," she said. "And we didn't see any carvings on the trees, either. Where can he be? I should think he has fallen into the pool or had an apoplectic fit."

Despite her resolve not to betray her agitation, her tone was unusually high and hard. Mrs. Davilow felt sure something unpropitious had occurred. Gwendolen ordered the carriage, citing fatigue. As the horses were fetched, the roving party reappeared, bringing Grandcourt with them.

"Ah, there you are!" Lord Brackenshaw exclaimed to Gwendolen, who was arranging her mother's shawl. "We thought at first you had alighted on Grandcourt and he had taken you home... However, we didn't suppose you could be in any danger."

Grandcourt approached with his usual air, as if he did not conceive that there had been any omission on his part. Lord Brackenshaw moved aside.

"Yes, we are going," Gwendolen said, looking busily at her scarf arranged across her shoulders in a Scotch fashion.

"May I call at Offendene tomorrow?"

"Oh yes, if you like," Gwendolen replied, sweeping him from a distance with her eyelashes. Her voice was light and sharp as the first touch of frost.

Mrs. Davilow accepted Grandcourt's arm to lead her to the carriage. But in that moment, with incredible swiftness, Gwendolen got in advance of them and sprang into the carriage. "I got in, mamma, because I wished to be on this side," she said apologetically. She had successfully avoided Grandcourt's touch; he merely lifted his hat and walked away, carrying the impression that she meant to show herself offended by his neglect.

The mother and daughter drove for five minutes in silence. Then Gwendolen declared, "I intend to join the Langens at Dover, mamma. I shall pack up immediately on getting home, and set off by the early train. I shall be at Dover almost as soon as they are; we can let them know by telegraph."

"Good heavens, child! what can be your reason for saying so?"

"My reason for saying it, mamma, is that I mean to do it."

"But why do you mean to do it?"

"I wish to go away."

"Is it because you are offended with Mr. Grandcourt's odd behavior in walking off today?"

"It is useless to enter into such questions. I am not going in any case to marry Mr. Grandcourt. Don't interest yourself further about it."

"What can I say to your uncle, Gwendolen? Consider the position you place me in. You led him to believe only last night that you had made up your mind in favor of Mr. Grandcourt."

"I am very sorry to cause you annoyance, mamma, dear, but I can't help it," Gwendolen said, her tone hardening with resistance. "Whatever you or my uncle may think or do, I shall not alter my resolve, and I shall not tell my reason. I don't care what comes of it. I don't care if I never marry anyone. There is nothing worth caring for. I believe all men are bad, and I hate them."

"But need you set off in this way, Gwendolen?" Mrs. Davilow asked, miserable and helpless.

"Now mamma, don't interfere with me. If you have ever had any trouble in your own life, remember it and don't interfere with me. If I am to be miserable, let it be by my own choice."

The mother was reduced to trembling silence. She realized the difficulty would be lessened if Gwendolen went away. And she did go. The packing was done carefully that evening, and not long after dawn the next day, Mrs. Davilow accompanied her daughter to the railway station. The sweet dews of morning, the cows and horses looking over the hedges, the early travelers with their bundles—all seemed melancholy and purposeless to them both. The dingy torpor of the railway station was worse still.

Gwend

"Festina lente"—speed tempered with delay.

Gwendolen had spent her time abroad in the electric haze of gambling, imagining herself an empress of fortune. She carried from these experiences a vague, modern conviction that in this chaotic world, actions held no inherent meaning; they were merely distractions for amusement. Alongside her, a mysterious entity known as Grapnell & Co., equally obsessed with ruling the realm of luck and seeking diversion, had orchestrated a painful shift in her family's circumstances. Consequently, she had returned home, dragging with her, against her will, a necklace she had pawned and which someone else had redeemed.

As she traveled back to England, Grandcourt was moving to intercept her. Yet, he approached this pursuit in his own peculiar fashion. He did not rush via express train from Diplow to Leubronn, where she was believed to be. Instead, he lingered at Baden-Baden, indulging in appointments with Russian acquaintances, only to break them later when his desire to reach Leubronn became urgent. Grandcourt's passions were intermittent and flickering; they rarely burned with intense flame. Yet, a vast amount of life proceeds without such fervor: countless cravats are tied with precision, dinners attended, and toasts offered to august figures, all devoid of the zest born of deep desire. A man can maintain a formidable appearance in high society—feigned erudition, scientific reserves, political opinions, and the sentiments of the English gentleman—all at a minimal cost of vital energy. He can remain obstinate or persistent at a low rate, displaying sudden impulses that falsely mimic demonic strength because they seem inexplicable. Often, however, their secret lies merely in a lack of regulated channels for the soul; without the ducts of habit, human nature turns to ooze and mud, yielding nothing but a spurt or a puddle under pressure.

Grandcourt was not entirely displeased by Gwendolen's flight from the splendid chance he had held out to her. The act possessed a certain piquancy. He liked to believe it stemmed from resentment regarding his careless behavior at Cardell Chase, which, upon reflection, did seem rather cool. To bring her so close to a tender admission, only to watch her walk headlong away from further opportunities of securing her consent, was enough to provoke a girl of spirit. And to be worth mastering, she ought to possess some spirit. Doubtless she expected him to follow, and he expected the same. Yet, for a full week, he took no measures to start, nor did he inquire where Miss Harleth had gone. Mr. Lush felt a triumph mixed with deep distrust; Grandcourt had said nothing to him about her, appearing as neutral as an alligator. One could never predict what might surface in the slowly churning depths of his mind. Still, delaying a decision allowed for the wasteful dissipation of Grandcourt's energy.

The guests at Diplow felt more curiosity than their host. How was it that nothing more was heard of Miss Harleth? Was it credible that she had refused Mr. Grandcourt? Lady Flora Hollis, a lively middle-aged woman well-endowed with curiosity, felt a sudden urge to make a round of calls with Mrs. Torrington, including the rectory, Offendene, and Quetcham. Through this tour, she not only doubled her efforts but also gleaned from the Arrowpoints that Miss Harleth had gone to Leubronn with old friends, the Baron and Baroness von Langen. The immediate agitation and disappointment of Mrs. Davilow and the Gascoignes had resolved itself into a collective wish that Gwendolen's disappearance should not be interpreted as eccentric or something requiring secrecy. The rector's mind, indeed, entertained the possibility that the marriage was merely deferred, for Mrs. Davilow had not dared to tell him of the bitter determination with which Gwendolen had spoken. Despite his practical ability, some of his experience had hardened into maxims and quotations. Amaryllis fleeing desired her hiding place to be known; and that love will find the way "over the mountain and over the wave" may be said without hyperbole in this age of steam. Gwendolen, he conceived, was an Amaryllis of excellent sense but coquettish daring; the question remained whether she had dared too much.

Lady Flora, returning charged with news about Miss Harleth, saw no reason why she should not attempt to electrify Mr. Grandcourt by mentioning it at the table. In doing so, she dropped a few hints of a rumor that he was a disappointed adorer. Grandcourt heard with quietude but with keen attention. The next day, he ordered Lush to arrange a decent pretext for breaking up the party at Diplow within another week, claiming he intended to go yachting to the Baltic or elsewhere. It was impossible to stay at Diplow as if he were a prisoner on parole, surrounded by a set of people he had never wanted. Lush needed no clearer announcement that Grandcourt was heading to Leubronn, though he might proceed with the slowness of a creeping billiard ball, sticking to the path. What Mr. Lush intended was to render himself indispensable so that he might accompany his patron; he succeeded. Gwendolen's repulsion for him was a fact that only amused his patron, making him none the less willing to keep Lush always at hand.

Thus, it came to pass that Grandcourt arrived at the *Czarina* on the fifth day after Gwendolen had left Leubronn, finding there his uncle, Sir Hugo Mallinger, with his family, including Deronda. It is not necessarily a pleasure, either to the reigning power or the heir presumptive, when their separate affairs—a touch of gout in the one, a touch of willfulness in the other—bring them to the same spot. Sir Hugo was an easy-tempered man, tolerant of differences and defects. However, a point of view differing from his own concerning the settlement of the family estates fretted him more than issues of Church discipline or the ballot. Faults were less venial when they belonged to a person whose existence was inconvenient to him. In no case could Grandcourt have been a nephew after his own heart; yet, as the presumptive heir to the Mallinger estates, he was the sign and embodiment of a chief grievance in the baronet's life: the want of a son to inherit the lands, in no portion of which had Sir Hugo more than a life-interest. For in the ill-advised settlement chosen by his father, Sir Francis, even Diplow with its modicum of land had been left under the same conditions as the ancient and wide inheritance of the two Toppings—Diplow, where Sir Hugo had lived and hunted through many seasons in his youth, and where his wife and daughters ought to have been able to retire after his death.

This grievance had naturally gathered emphasis as the years advanced. Lady Mallinger, after bearing three daughters in quick succession, had remained childless for eight years until now, when she was over forty, without producing another girl. Sir Hugo, almost twenty years older, was at a time of life when, notwithstanding the fashionable retardation of everything from dinners to marriages, a man's hopefulness shows signs of wear, until restored by second childhood.

In fact, he had begun to despair of a son. This confirmation of Grandcourt's interest in the estates certainly made his image and presence more unwelcome; yet, on the other hand, it carried circumstances that disposed Sir Hugo to ensure their relationship remained as friendly as possible. It led him to dwell on a plan that had grown alongside his disappointment: to try and secure Diplow as a future residence for Lady Mallinger and her daughters, keeping this pretty bit of family inheritance for his own offspring despite the lack of an heir. His knowledge of his nephew's disposition and affairs encouraged the belief that Grandcourt might consent to a transaction granting him a good sum of ready money as an equivalent for his prospective interest in the domain of Diplow and the moderate land attached to it. If, after all, the unhoped-for son should be born, the money would have been wasted, and Grandcourt would have been paid for giving up interests that turned out to be worthless. But Sir Hugo set this risk as *nil*, and of late years he had husbanded his fortune so well through mining and lease sales that he was prepared for the outlay.

Here was an object that made him careful to avoid any quarrel with Grandcourt. Some years prior, when making improvements at the Abbey and needing Grandcourt's concurrence to fell an obstructive mass of timber on the demesne, he had congratulated himself on finding no active spite in his nephew's peculiar mind. Nothing had since occurred to make them hate each other more than was compatible with perfect politeness or any accommodation that could be strictly mutual.

Grandcourt, on his side, thought his uncle a superfluity and a bore, feeling that the list of things in general would improve whenever Sir Hugo was expunged. But he had been made aware, through Lush, always a useful medium, of the baronet's inclinations concerning Diplow. He was gratified to have the alternative of the money in his mind: even if he did not think it likely he would accept it, his sense of power would be flattered by his ability to refuse what Sir Hugo desired. The hinted transaction had influenced his motives for asking for a year's tenancy of Diplow, which had rather annoyed Sir Hugo to grant, since the excellent hunting in the neighborhood might decide Grandcourt not to part with his chance of future possession—a man who has two places, in one of which the hunting is less good, naturally desiring a third where it is better. Also, Lush had thrown out to Sir Hugo the probability that Grandcourt would woo and win Miss Arrowpoint; in that case, ready money might be less of a temptation. Hence, on this unexpected meeting at Leubronn, the baronet felt much curiosity to know how things had been going on at Diplow, was bent on being as civil as possible to his nephew, and looked forward to some private chat with Lush.

Between Deronda and Grandcourt there existed a more faintly marked but peculiar relation, depending on circumstances yet to be revealed. But on no side was there any sign of suppressed chagrin at their first meeting at the *table d'hôte*, an hour after Grandcourt's arrival. When the quartette of gentlemen afterward met on the terrace, without Lady Mallinger, they moved off together to saunter through the rooms. As they entered the large *saal*, Sir Hugo said to them,

"Did you play much at Baden, Grandcourt?"

"No; I looked on and betted a little with some Russians there."

"Had you luck?"

"What did I win, Lush?"

"You brought away about two hundred," said Lush.

"You are not here for the sake of the play, then?" said Sir Hugo.

"No; I don't care about play now. It's a confounded strain," said Grandcourt, whose diamond ring and demeanor, as he moved along playing slightly with his whisker, were being stared at by rouged foreigners interested in a new milord.

"The fact is, somebody should invent a mill to do amusements for you, my dear fellow," said Sir Hugo, "as the Tartars get their praying done. But I agree with you; I never cared for play. It's monotonous—it knits the brain up into meshes. And it knocks me up to watch it now. I suppose one gets poisoned with the bad air. I never stay here more than ten minutes. But where's your gambling beauty, Deronda? Have you seen her lately?"

"She's gone," said Deronda, curtly.

"An uncommonly fine girl, a perfect Diana," said Sir Hugo, turning to Grandcourt again. "Really worth a little straining to look at her. I saw her winning, and she took it as coolly as if she had known it all beforehand. The same day Deronda happened to see her losing like wildfire, and she bore it with immense pluck. I suppose she was cleaned out, or was wise enough to stop in time. How do you know she's gone?"

"Oh, by the Visitor-list..." said Deronda, with a scarcely perceptible shrug. "Vandernoodt told me her name was Harleth, and she was with the Baron and Baroness von Langen. I saw by the list that Miss Harleth was no longer there."

This held no further information for Lush than that Gwendolen had been gambling. He had already looked at the list and ascertained that Gwendolen had gone, but he had no intention of thrusting this knowledge on Grandcourt before he asked for it; he had not asked, finding it enough to believe that the object of search would turn up somewhere or other.

But now Grandcourt had heard what was rather piquant, and not a word about Miss Harleth had been missed by him. After a moment's pause, he said to Deronda,

"Do you know those people—the Langens?"

"I have talked with them a little since Miss Harleth went away. I knew nothing of them before."

"Where is she gone—do you know?"

"She is gone home," said Deronda, coldly, as if he wished to say no more. But then, from a fresh impulse, he turned to look markedly at Grandcourt and added, "But it is possible you know her. Her home is not far from Diplow: Offendene, near Winchester."

Deronda, turning to look straight at Grandcourt, who was on his left hand, might have been a subject for those old painters who liked contrasts of temperament. There was a calm intensity of life and richness of tint in his face that, on a sudden gaze, was rather startling, often making him seem to have spoken, so that servants and officials asked him automatically, "What did you say, sir?" when he had been quite silent. Grandcourt himself felt an irritation, which he did not show except by a slight movement of the eyelids, at Deronda's turning round on him when he was not asked to do more than speak. But he answered, with his usual drawl, "Yes, I know her," and paused with his shoulder toward Deronda to look at the gambling.

"What of her, eh?" asked Sir Hugo of Lush as the three moved on a little way. "She must be a new-comer at Offendene. Old Blenny lived there after

Men are like planets, possessing both a visible history traced by astronomers and an invisible one woven from the hidden pathways of feeling and thought. To truly narrate human action, one must thread these dark currents leading to every moment of intense suffering, where anguish becomes a force greater than the sea or sky itself.

For Daniel Deronda, life had been marked by a single, burning epoch: a July afternoon in a Gothic cloister, where pink roses shed their petals on the grass. Thirteen years old, he lay prone in the shade, his curly head resting on his arms over Sismondi's *History of the Italian Republics*. Beside him sat his tutor, Mr. Fraser, a capable young Scot, reading political economy. Suddenly, Deronda lowered his arm and asked in pure boyish tones, "Mr. Fraser, how was it that the popes and cardinals always had so many nephews?"

Fraser, roused reluctantly from his studies, answered with the emphatic cadence of his native tongue: "Their own children were called nephews."

"Why?" Deronda pressed.

"It was for propriety," Fraser replied, his patience thinning as he turned back to his book. "Priests don't marry; the children were illegitimate."

The word struck Deronda like a physical blow. He sat up abruptly, his back to his tutor. He had always known Sir Hugo Mallinger as his uncle. When he had once asked about his parents, the baronet had simply said, "You lost your father and mother when you were quite a little one; that is why I take care of you." Daniel remembered a vague, dreamlike twilight of kisses and scented drapery, ending when his fingers brushed something hard and sharp, causing him to cry. But those memories were distant. His world was the one he knew: a life of delightful indulgence under an uncle he considered perfect. Diplow, the Mallinger estate, was a romantic architectural outgrowth from an abbey, filled with portraits of ancestors ranging from armored knights to powdered politicians. Yet, as he walked the cloisters, he saw no reflection of himself in those gallery faces. He was handsome, possessing a childlike nobility that seemed to consecrate the world, but he felt disconnected from the lineage that defined his home.

That day, however, a new grief entered his heart. The tutor's casual remark about illegitimacy sparked a terrifying possibility: that the man he loved was actually his father, and that his birth carried a secret shame. The ardor he had poured into his imaginary worlds suddenly rushed to explain his own history. The uncle he adored took on the aspect of a father who had done him wrong, hiding truths behind a veil of silence. The thought of his mother, whom he must have been taken from, felt like a fire falling in flakes around him. He could not speak of it; to utter such words felt like a betrayal. Tears streamed down his face unchecked until Mr. Fraser's voice broke the spell, asking if he was sitting on his book. Daniel moved the book without turning, rose, and walked into the open grounds to dry his tears alone.

The shock settled into a premature reserve. He began to suspect that others knew things they chose not to mention. A month later, this suspicion hardened into a specific wound. After singing "Sweet Echo" for a gathering of guests, Sir Hugo playfully suggested Daniel become a great singer, adored by the world like Mario or Tamberlik. Daniel reddened and answered with angry decision, "No; I should hate it!" He fled the room, retreating to his window-sill to watch the rain. The idea that his uncle viewed him as a spectacle, or worse, that his birth disqualified him from the life of a gentleman, stung him deeply. He recalled a vague memory of a farm bailiff winking at his mother while he drank whey, a silly face that now seemed laden with cruel knowledge. Was he like his mother? Was his father a Mallinger who had changed his name? The questions gnawed at him, but he checked himself, refusing to pry into the locked cabinet of family maps where the truth might lie.

Despite his turmoil, Sir Hugo's behavior eventually clarified. The baronet, a man of easy conscience who had been a bachelor until fifty, assumed Daniel was his son and was pleased by the public suspicion of it. He never considered how the boy might feel about the mystery of his origins. When Daniel finally asked what he intended for him, Sir Hugo offered a generous settlement of seven hundred a year and suggested a career in law or politics, assuming Daniel would remain at Eton and then Cambridge. Daniel accepted the plan, finding comfort in the uncertainty of his birth; if his conjectures were wrong, he could simply be a gentleman's son.

At Cambridge, Daniel's nature remained distinct. He was not a competitive scholar in the traditional sense. Instead of striving for prizes, he possessed a meditative yearning for wide knowledge and a fierce sympathy for others. He was willing to yield victory to avoid hurting a rival, a trait that marked him as eccentric but morally superior. His path changed dramatically through his friendship with Hans Meyrick, a talented but impoverished student from Christ's Hospital. Hans, burdened by the responsibility of supporting his mother and sisters, struggled with erratic impulses. When Hans contracted a severe eye infection from traveling in third-class carriages to buy an engraving, Daniel devoted himself entirely to his friend's studies, sacrificing his own mathematical scholarship to ensure Hans could win the classical prize.

When Daniel subsequently lost his own scholarship, he realized the futility of a university system that demanded rote retention over true understanding. He resolved to leave Cambridge and study abroad, seeking a broader perspective. He confronted Sir Hugo with this decision, arguing that he needed to understand other points of view and shed a merely English attitude. Surprisingly, the baronet agreed, offering only mild advice to keep an "English cut" and not become indifferent to bad tobacco. Before departing, Daniel visited Hans in Chelsea, where the shy sisters accepted him as an ideal, painting him in their minds as the legendary Prince Camaralzaman. Thus, Daniel Deronda, carrying the silent weight of his unknown past and a heart full of tender compassion, stepped out into the wider world, ready to define his own destiny.

"This is true the poet sings,
That a sorrow's crown of sorrow
Is remembering happier things."
—TENNYSON: *In Memoriam*.

On a fine evening near the end of July, Daniel Deronda rowed alone on the Thames. It had been nearly a year since he returned to England, ostensibly to finish his education and secure a place in society. Though he had begun reading law to satisfy Sir Hugo and ward off idleness, the study had only deepened his internal indecision. Now, with the Mallingers in town, his old love for boating had revived with renewed force. The river offered a stillness he could find nowhere else. He owned a boat at Putney, and whenever Sir Hugo did not require him, it became his chief holiday to row until the stars appeared. He was not in a sentimental phase, but rather in a contemplative mood common to many young men of his generation: a questioning of whether it was worthwhile to engage in the world's battle, specifically the battle fought by those who possess a small fraction of capital accumulated by others. Sir Hugo found it puzzling that a man who stood in such splendid contrast to the sickly and puling could be hampered by ideas that, to an accomplished Whig, seemed merely spectral illusions. Especially since Deronda rejected authorship, the vocation understood to turn foolish thinking into funds.

Rowing in a dark-blue shirt and skull-cap, with curls closely clipped and a mouth beset by abundant soft waves of beard, Deronda bore only disguised traces of the seraphic boy "trailing clouds of glory." Yet, even one who had not seen him since childhood might look at him with slow recognition, perhaps due to the peculiarity of the gaze Gwendolen called "dreadful," though it possessed a very mild sort of scrutiny. His voice, sometimes audible in subdued snatches of song, was merely a high baritone; indeed, only his lithe, powerful frame and the firm gravity of his face would suggest he lacked the rare, ravishing tenor nature reluctantly produces. Look at his hands: they were not small and dimpled with tapering fingers that seemed to have only a deprecating touch. They were long, flexible, firmly-grasping hands, such as Titian painted to show the combination of refinement with force. There was a likeness between the faces belonging to such hands—in both the uniform pale-brown skin, the perpendicular brow, and the calmly penetrating eyes. No longer seraphic, he was thoroughly terrestrial and manly; yet of a kind to raise belief in a human dignity that can afford to recognize poor relations.

Such types appear occasionally among average conditions; in a workman, for example, whistling over a bit of measurement and lifting his eyes to answer a question about the road. Often, the grand meanings of faces, as well as written words, lie chiefly in the impressions that happen just now to be of importance in relation to Deronda. He rowed on the Thames in very ordinary equipment for a young Englishman at leisure, passing under Kew Bridge with no thought of an adventure in which his appearance was likely to play any part. In fact, he objected strongly to the notion, which others had not allowed him to escape, that his appearance was of a kind to draw attention. Hints of this, intended to be complimentary, found an angry resonance in him, stemming from mingled experiences to which a clue had already been given. His own face in the glass had, for many years, associated for him with thoughts of someone he must resemble—one about whose character and lot he continually wondered and never dared to ask.

In the neighborhood of Kew Bridge, between six and seven o'clock, the river was no solitude. Several persons sauntered on the towing-path, and here and there a boat plied. Deronda had been rowing fast to get over this spot when, becoming aware of a great barge advancing toward him, he guided his boat aside and rested on his oar within a couple of yards of the river-brink. All the while, he unconsciously continued the low-toned chant that had haunted his throat all the way up the river—the gondolier's song in *Otello*, where Rossini set to music the immortal words of Dante:

"Nessun maggior dolore
Che ricordarsi del tempo felice
Nella miseria."

As he rested on his oar, the pianissimo fall of the melodic wail "nella miseria" was distinctly audible on the brink of the water. Three or four persons had paused to watch the barge pass the bridge, and doubtless included the young gentleman in the boat; but probably it was only to one ear that the low vocal sounds came with more significance than if they had been an insect-murmur amidst the sum of current noises. Deronda, awaiting the barge, turned his head to the river-side and saw, a few yards distant, a figure that might have been an impersonation of the misery he was unconsciously giving voice to: a girl hardly more than eighteen, of low slim figure, with a most delicate little face. Her dark curls were pushed behind her ears under a large black hat, and a long woolen cloak draped her shoulders. Her hands hung down clasped before her, and her eyes were fixed on the river with a look of immovable, statue-like despair.

This strong arrest of his attention made him cease singing. Apparently, his voice had entered her inner world without her noting whence it came, for when it suddenly ceased, she changed her attitude slightly. Looking round with a frightened glance, she met Deronda's face. It was but a couple of moments, but that seemed a long while for two people to look straight at each other. Her look was something like that of a fawn or other gentle animal before it turns to run away: no blush, no special alarm, but only some timidity which yet could not hinder her from a long look before she turned. In fact, it seemed to Deronda that she was only half conscious of her surroundings. Was she hungry, or was there some other cause of bewilderment? He felt an outleap of interest and compassion toward her; but the next instant she had turned and walked away to a neighboring bench under a tree. He had no right to linger and watch her; poorly-dressed, melancholy women were common sights. It was only the delicate beauty, the picturesque lines and color of the image that were exceptional, and these conditions made it more markedly impossible that he should obtrude his interest upon her. He began to row away and was soon far up the river, but no other thoughts were busy enough quite to expel that pale image of unhappy girlhood. He fell again and again to speculating on the probable romance that lay behind that loneliness and look of desolation, then to smile at his own share in the prejudice that interesting faces must have interesting adventures, then to justify himself for feeling that sorrow was the more tragic when it befell delicate, childlike beauty.

"I should not have forgotten the look of misery if she had been ugly and vulgar," he said to himself. But there was no denying that the attractiveness of the image made it likelier to last. It was clear to him as an onyx cameo: the brown-black drapery, the white face with small, small features and dark, long-lashed eyes. His mind glanced over the girl-tragedies going on in the world, hidden and unheeded, as if they were but tragedies of the copse or hedgerow, where the helpless drag wounded wings forsakenly and streak the shadowed moss with the red moment-hand of their own death. Deronda, in his solitary excursions, had been occupied chiefly with uncertainties about his own course. Those uncertainties, being much at their leisure, were wont to have such wide-sweeping connections with all life and history that the new image of helpless sorrow easily blended itself with what seemed to him the strong array of reasons why he should shrink from getting into that routine of the world which makes men apologize for all its wrong-doing and take opinions as mere professional equipment. Why he should not draw strongly at any thread in the hopelessly-entangled scheme of things.

He used his oars little, satisfied to go with the tide and be taken back by it. It was his habit to indulge himself in that solemn passivity which easily comes with the lengthening shadows and mellow light, when thinking and desiring melt together imperceptibly, and what in other hours may have seemed argument takes the quality of passionate vision. By the time he had come back again with the tide past Richmond Bridge, the sun was near setting. The approach of his favorite hour—with its deepening stillness and darkening masses of tree and building between the double glow of the sky and the river—disposed him to linger as if they had been an unfinished strain of music. He looked out for a perfectly solitary spot where he could lodge his boat against the bank, and, throwing himself on his back with his head propped on the cushions, could watch out the light of sunset and the opening of that bead-roll which some oriental poet describes as God's call to the little stars, who each answer, "Here am I." He chose a spot in the bend of the river just opposite Kew Gardens, where he had a great breadth of water before him reflecting the glory of the sky, while he himself was in shadow. He lay with his hands behind his head, propped on a level with the boat's edge, so that he could see all round him but could not be seen by anyone at a few yards' distance. For a long while he never turned his eyes from the view right in front of him. He was forgetting everything else in a half-speculative, half-involuntary identification of himself with the objects he was looking at, thinking how far it might be possible habitually to shift his center till his own personality would be no less outside him than the landscape—when the sense of something moving on the bank opposite him, where it was bordered by a line of willow bushes, made him turn his glance thitherward.

In the first moment, he had a darting presentiment about the moving figure; and now he could see the small face with the strange dying sunlight upon it. He feared to frighten her by a sudden movement and watched her with motionless attention. She looked round, but seemed only to gather security from the apparent solitude, hid her hat among the willows, and immediately took off her woolen cloak. Presently she seated herself and deliberately dipped the cloak in the water, holding it there a little while, then taking it out with effort, rising from her seat as she did so. By this time Deronda felt sure that she meant to wrap the wet cloak round her as a drowning shroud; there was no longer time to hesitate about frightening her. He rose and seized his oar to ply across; happily her position lay a little below him. The poor thing, overcome with terror at this sign of discovery from the opposite bank, sank down on the brink again, holding her cloak half out of the water. She crouched and covered her face as if she kept a faint hope that she had not been seen, and that the boatman was accidentally coming toward her. But soon he was within brief space of her, steadying his boat against the bank, and speaking, but very gently,

"Don't be afraid. You are unhappy. Pray, trust me. Tell me what I can do to help you."

She raised her head and looked up at him. His face was now toward the light, and she knew it again. But she did not speak for a few moments which were a renewal of their former gaze at each other. At last she said in a low sweet voice, with an accent so distinct that it suggested foreignness and yet was not foreign, "I saw you before," and then added dreamily, after a like pause, "nella miseria."

Deronda, not understanding the connection of her thoughts, supposed that her mind was weakened by distress and hunger.

"It was you, singing?" she went on, hesitatingly—"Nessun maggior dolore." The mere words themselves uttered in her sweet undertones seemed to give the melody to Deronda's ear.

"Ah, yes," he said, understanding now, "I am often singing them. But I fear you will injure yourself staying here. Pray let me take you in my boat to some place of safety. And that wet cloak—let me take it."

He would not attempt to take it without her leave, dreading lest he should scare her. Even at his words, he fancied that she shrank and clutched the cloak more tenaciously. But her eyes were fixed on him with a question in them as she said, "You look good. Perhaps it is God's command."

"Do trust me. Let me help you. I will die before I will let any harm come to you."

She rose from her sitting posture, first dragging the saturated cloak and then letting it fall on the ground—it was too heavy for her tired arms. Her little woman's figure, as she laid her delicate chilled hands together one over the other against her waist, and went a step backward while she leaned her head forward as if not to lose sight of his face, was unspeakably touching.

"Great God!" the words escaped Deronda in a tone so low and solemn that they seemed like a prayer become unconsciously vocal. The agitating impression this forsaken girl was making on him stirred a fiber that lay close to his deepest interest in the fates of women—"perhaps my mother was like this one." The old thought had come now with a new impetus of mingled feeling, and urged that exclamation in which both East and West have for ages concentrated their awe in the presence of inexorable calamity.

The low-toned words seemed to have some reassurance in them for the hearer: she stepped forward close to the boat's side, and Deronda put out his hand, hoping now that she would let him help her in. She had already put her tiny hand into his which closed around it, when some new thought struck her, and drawing back she said,

"I have nowhere to go—nobody belonging to me in all this land."

"I will take you to a lady who has daughters," said Deronda immediately. He felt a sort of relief in gathering that the wretched home and cruel friends he imagined her to be fleeing from were not in the near background. Still she hesitated, and said more timidly than ever,

"Do you belong to the theatre?"

"No; I have nothing to do with the theatre," said Deronda in a decided tone. Then

Life is a fickle mother: sometimes she adorns herself in jewels, strides up marble staircases with chin high, ignoring the servants trailing behind; at other times, she wears rags, wanders dark alleys, swallows hot gin, and screams amidst the chaos of the poor. Yet tonight, she arrived as a frugal matron, neat and efficient, brimming with cheerful morning thoughts and a clever knack for finding abundance in scarcity.

Mrs. Meyrick's home was quiet. The front parlor overlooked the river, the rear faced the gardens, allowing the window to remain open even as she read aloud to her daughters, refreshing the air of the small double room where a lamp and two candles burned. The candles sat on a separate table for Kate, who was sketching illustrations for a publisher; the lamp served not only the reader but also Amy and Mab, who were embroidering satin cushions destined for "the great world."

From the outside, the house appeared narrow and shabby, the bright light filtering through the holland blind revealing a heavy, old-fashioned window frame. Yet there is a profound comfort in knowing that many such grim-walled slices of space in our foggy London have been, and still are, the homes of a culture spotlessly free from vulgarity. Poverty renders display an impersonal question, turning the grand shows of the world into mere spectacles that stir only petty rivalry or vain efforts at possession.

The Meyricks belonged to this kind of home. They clung to this specific row-house because its interior was filled with objects always in their proper places. For the mother, these items held memories of her marriage; for the young ones, they were as necessary and unquestioned a part of their world as the stars of the Great Bear seen from the back windows. Mrs. Meyrick had endured much financial stinginess to preserve certain engravings specially cherished by her husband. The narrow walls held a world history in scenes and heads the children had memorized since childhood. The chairs and tables were old friends preferred to anything new. In these two little parlors, devoid of furniture a broker would have cared to cheapen except for the prints and piano, there existed space and apparatus for a wide-glancing, nicely-selected life, opened to the highest things in music, painting, and poetry. Even in times of greatest scarcity, before Kate could earn paid work, they likely never lacked a servant to light fires and sweep rooms. Yet they remained fastidious, unable to believe that the manners of ladies in the fashionable world were as coarse, selfish, and laced with slang as depicted in so-called literary photographs. The Meyricks possessed their little oddities, streaks of eccentricity flowing from both mother and father; their minds were like medieval houses with unexpected recesses and openings, flights of steps, and sudden outlooks.

Mother and daughters were bound by a triple knot: family love, admiration for the finest work and best actions, and habitual industry. Hans's desire to spend his money on making their lives more luxurious had been resisted by all. Both they and he were thus saved from regretting the threatened triumph of his artistic yearning over the attractions of secured income—a triumph that would eventually force him to give up his fellowship. They could all laugh at his Gavarni caricatures and hold him blameless for following a natural bent, their unselfishness and independence leaving no obstacle in his path. It was enough for them to continue as before, enjoying only a grand treat of opera-going in the gallery when Hans visited.

Seeing the group they made that evening, one could hardly wish them to change their way of life. They were all alike small, perfectly proportioned to their miniature rooms. Mrs. Meyrick was reading aloud from a French book; she was a lively little woman, half French, half Scotch, with a pretty articulateness of speech that seemed to bring daylight into her hearer's understanding. Though not yet fifty, her rippling hair, covered by a Quakerish net cap, was chiefly gray, while her eyebrows were brown as the bright eyes below them. Her black dress, almost like a priest's cassock with its rows of buttons, suited a neat figure hardly five feet high. The daughters matched the mother, except that Mab possessed Hans's light hair and complexion, with a bossy, irregular brow and other quaintnesses reminding one of him. Everything about them was compact: from the firm coils of their hair, fastened back *à la Chinoise*, to their gray skirts in Puritan nonconformity with the fashion, which at that time would have demanded that four feminine circumferences fill all the free space in the front parlor. All four, if they had been wax figures, might have been packed easily into a fashionable lady's traveling trunk. Their faces seemed full of speech, as if their minds had been shelled, after the manner of horse-chestnuts, becoming brightly visible. The only large thing of its kind in the room was Hafiz, the Persian cat, comfortably poised on the brown leather back of a chair, opening his large eyes now and then to ensure the lower animals were not causing mischief.

The book Mrs. Meyrick held was Erckmann-Chatrian's *Historie d'un Conscrit*. She had just finished reading it aloud when Mab, who had let her work fall on the floor while stretching her head forward to fix her eyes on the reader, exclaimed, "I think that is the finest story in the world."

"Of course, Mab!" said Amy. "It is the last you have heard. Everything that pleases you is the best in its turn."

"It is hardly to be called a story," said Kate. "It is a bit of history brought near us with a strong telescope. We can see the soldiers' faces: no, it is more than that—we can hear everything—we can almost hear their hearts beat."

"I don't care what you call it," said Mab, flicking away her thimble. "Call it a chapter in Revelations. It makes me want to do something good, something grand. It makes me so sorry for everybody. It makes me like Schiller—I want to take the world in my arms and kiss it. I must kiss you instead, little mother!" She threw her arms around her mother's neck.

"Whenever you are in that mood, Mab, down goes your work," said Amy. "It would be doing something good to finish your cushion without soiling it."

"Oh—oh—oh!" groaned Mab as she stooped to pick up her work and thimble. "I wish I had three wounded conscripts to take care of."

"You would spill their beef tea while you were talking," said Amy.

"Poor Mab! Don't be hard on her," said the mother. "Give me the embroidery now, child. You go on with your enthusiasm, and I will go on with the pink and white poppy."

"Well, ma, I think you are more caustic than Amy," said Kate, drawing her head back to look at her drawing.

"Oh—oh—oh!" cried Mab again, rising and stretching her arms. "I wish something wonderful would happen. I feel like the deluge. The waters of the great deep are broken up, and the windows of heaven are opened. I must sit down and play the scales."

Mab began opening the piano while the others laughed at this climax, when a cab stopped before the house, followed immediately by a quick rap of the knocker.

"Dear me!" said Mrs. Meyrick, starting up. "It is after ten, and Phoebe is gone to bed." She hastened out, leaving the parlor door open.

"Mr. Deronda!" The girls heard this exclamation from their mother. Mab clasped her hands, whispering loudly, "There now! Something *is* going to happen." Kate and Amy abandoned their work in amazement. But Deronda's tone in reply was so low they could not hear his words, and Mrs. Meyrick immediately closed the parlor door.

"I know I am trusting to your goodness in a most extraordinary way," Deronda continued after giving his brief narrative. "But you can imagine how helpless I feel with a young creature like this on my hands. I could not go with her among strangers, and in her nervous state I should dread taking her into a house full of servants. I have trusted to your mercy. I hope you will not think my act unwarrantable."

"On the contrary. You have honored me by trusting me. I see your difficulty. Pray bring her in. I will go and prepare the girls."

While Deronda returned to the cab, Mrs. Meyrick turned back into the parlor and said, "Here is somebody to take care of instead of your wounded conscripts, Mab: a poor girl who was going to drown herself in despair. Mr. Deronda found her only just in time to save her. He brought her along in his boat and did not know what else it would be safe to do with her, so he has trusted us and brought her here. It seems she is a Jewess, but quite refined, he says—knowing Italian and music."

The three girls, wondering and expectant, came forward and stood near each other in mute confidence that they were all feeling alike under this appeal to their compassion. Mab looked rather awe-stricken, as if this answer to her wish were something preternatural.

Meanwhile, Deronda approached the door of the cab where the pale face was now gazing out with roused observation. "I have brought you to some of the kindest people in the world," he said. "There are daughters like you. It is a happy home. Will you let me take you to them?"

She stepped out obediently, putting her hand in his and forgetting her hat. When Deronda led her into the full light of the parlor where the four little women awaited her, she made a picture that would have stirred much duller sensibilities than theirs. At first, she was a little dazed by the sudden light, and before she could concentrate her glance, he had put her hand into the mother's. He was inwardly rejoicing that the Meyricks were so small; the dark-curled head was the highest among them. The poor wanderer could not be afraid of these gentle faces so near hers. Now she looked at each of them in turn while the mother said, "You must be weary, poor child."

"We will take care of you—we will comfort you—we will love you," cried Mab, no longer able to restrain herself, and taking the small right hand caressingly between both her own. This gentle welcoming warmth penetrated the bewildered one. She hung back just enough to see better the four faces in front of her, whose goodwill was being reflected in hers, not in any smile, but in that undefinable change which tells us that anxiety is passing into contentment. For an instant she looked up at Deronda, as if referring all this mercy to him, and then, turning to Mrs. Meyrick with more collectedness in her sweet tones than he had heard before, she said, "I am a stranger. I am a Jewess. You might have thought I was wicked."

"No, we are sure you are good," burst out Mab.

"We think no evil of you, poor child. You shall be safe with us," said Mrs. Meyrick. "Come now and sit down. You must have some food, and then you must go to rest."

The stranger looked up again at Deronda, who asked, "You will have no more fears with these friends? You will rest tonight?"

"Oh, I should not fear. I should rest. I think these are the ministering angels."

Mrs. Meyrick wanted to lead her to a seat, but again hanging back gently, the poor weary thing spoke as if with a scruple at being received without a further account of herself. "My name is Mirah Lapidoth. I am come a long way, all the way from Prague by myself. I made my escape. I ran away from dreadful things. I came to find my mother and brother in London. I had been taken from my mother when I was little, but I thought I could find her again. I had trouble—the houses were all gone—I could not find her. It has been a long while, and I had not much money. That is why I am in distress."

"Our mother will be good to you," cried Mab. "See what a nice little mother she is!"

"Do sit down now," said Kate, moving a chair forward, while Amy ran to get some tea.

Mirah resisted no longer but seated herself with perfect grace, crossing her little feet, laying her hands one over the other on her lap, and looking at her friends with placid reverence. Whereupon Hafiz, who had been watching the scene restlessly, came forward with tail erect and rubbed himself against her ankles. Deronda felt it time to go.

"Will you allow me to come again and inquire—perhaps at five tomorrow?" he said to Mrs. Meyrick.

"Yes, pray; we shall have had time to make acquaintance then."

"Good-bye," said Deronda, looking down at Mirah and putting out his hand. She rose as she took it, and the moment brought back to them both strongly the other moment when she had first taken that outstretched hand. She lifted her eyes to his and said with reverential fervor, "The God of our fathers bless you and deliver you from all evil as you have delivered me. I did not believe there was any man so good. None before have thought me worthy of the best. You found me poor and miserable, yet you have given me the best."

Deronda could not speak, but with silent adieux to the Meyricks, he hurried away.

"I pity the man who travels from Dan to Beersheba and declares, 'It is all barren.' Yet so it is; and so is the entire world to anyone unwilling to cultivate its hidden fruits." —Sterne, *Sentimental Journey*

To label Daniel Deronda merely "romantic" would be a profound misunderstanding. Beneath his composed, slightly restrained exterior burned a fervor that allowed him to discover poetry and drama in the most mundane threads of daily life. Perhaps such magic is as abundant as ever, waiting only to be seen by those whose hearts are not numbed by a phlegmatic indifference. It exists in the same room as the microscope and rattles within railway carriages, vanishing only from the minds of those who have lost the capacity for awe or the sense of fellowship that connects the near to the distant. For a mind devoid of these tremors, how could the vast machinery of heaven and earth—from the furthest star to the tender bosom of a nursing mother—fail to compose a verse?

For Deronda, the moment of finding Mirah was as electrifying as any mythic encounter for Orestes or Rinaldo. He sat awake half the night, reliving the instant he first glimpsed her on the riverbank with a vividness that felt newly minted. When he attempted to read a book to dull this urgent inner vision, the printed words dissolved into a mere lattice through which he still saw and heard everything with crystal clarity. He perceived not only the actual events of the past two hours but also the swirling possibilities of what had been and what might be, fed by the warm blood of passionate hope and fear. A deep resonance within his own soul caused Mirah's desperate search for her mother to grip his imagination with peculiar force. His initial impulse was pure sympathy: to aid her, utilizing the subtle, almost scientific methods available in London to locate missing persons. Yet, the complex emotions born of his own family history quickly transfused themselves into his anxiety for her.

The desire to know his own mother was perpetually haunted by dread. In imagining Mirah's fate, it struck him with sudden clarity that finding the mother and brother from whom she had been torn as a child could prove to be a calamity. In the boat, she had spoken of them as good; yet that goodness might have been a projection of her own innocent ignorance and yearning. Ten or twelve years were ample time for change, for corruption. Despite his natural tendency to side with the oppressed, Deronda had never felt a practical draw toward existing Jews. The facts he knew of them—whether they walked conspicuously in fine clothes or lurked in shadowed by-streets—were largely repugnant. He assumed that learned and accomplished Jews had abandoned their faith to merge with their host nations. While scorn cast at a Jew as a member of a race would have stirred his deepest sympathies, the indiscriminate hatred of a people often strikes the individual who has well-earned it, marking them as a rascal son of Adam. Even the Caribs, with their limited theology, seem to view thieving as uniquely Christian; perhaps they could cite experimental grounds for such a bias. Deronda could not escape knowing the ugly stories of Jewish characteristics and occupations. Though he protested against severing past from present, he shared the common failure of never caring to look deeper than the general virtues and vices of a long-oppressed race. Now, however, Mirah's longing forced a closer survey of details, and very disagreeable images arose: what it might mean to find this middle-aged woman and her son.

Surely, there was the exquisite refinement and charm of Mirah herself to suggest her kin were equally noble, but he must wait. Perhaps through Mrs. Meyrick he could gather guiding hints from Mirah's own lips. Her voice, her accent, her looks—all the sweet purity that clothed her like a consecrating garment—made him shrink even further from associating her, either in thought or deed, with anything hateful or contaminating. Yet these fine words are often used to fumigate and obscure unpleasant realities; they are not the language of true thought. Deronda's mind raced with rapid images of possibility: he saw himself guided by an official scout into a dingy street, entering a dim doorway where a hawk-eyed, rough-headed woman bargained away a hungry girl's last finery. Or, in a quarter hideous precisely because of its pretension, he found himself breathing the air of a young Jew, talkative and familiar, eager to display his acquaintance with gentlemanly tastes and unfastidious in any transaction they might favor. Such scenes played out in the brief chapter of his experience. Excuse him: his mind was not prone to spontaneous insults or the wit that equates Moses with an advertisement sheet. But he was currently governed by dread. If Mirah's parents had been Christian, the only difference would be that his forebodings would have been fed by wider knowledge. It was the habit of his mind to connect dread with unknown parentage, and in this case, as in his own, there was enough reason to make the connection logical.

But what was to be done with Mirah? She needed shelter and protection in the fullest sense. All his chivalrous sentiment rose to insist that the sooner and more fully he could engage the interest of others besides himself, the better he would fulfill her claims upon him. He had no right to provide for her entirely, though he might be able to; the very depth of the impression she had made drove him to desire that she understand herself as entirely independent of him. Vague visions of the future, which he tried to dispel as fantastic, left their influence behind as an anxiety stronger than any rational motive: that those who observed his actions closely should be acquainted from the start with the history of his relation to Mirah. He had learned to hate secrecy regarding the grand ties and obligations of his life, hating it the more because a powerful spell of interwoven sensibilities hindered him from breaking such silence. Deronda had vowed to himself that since the truths which disgrace mortals are not always of their own making, he would never make the truth a disgrace to another through his own act. He was not without terror lest he break this vow and fall into the apologetic philosophy that explains the world away as containing nothing better than one's own conduct.

At one moment, he resolved to tell the whole of his adventure to Sir Hugo and Lady Mallinger the next morning at breakfast. But the possibility that something entirely new might reveal itself on his next visit to Mrs. Meyrick checked this impulse. Finally, he fell asleep on the conclusion that he would wait until that visit had been made.

"It will hardly be denied that even in this frail and corrupted world, we sometimes meet persons who, in their very mien and aspect, as well as in the whole habit of life, manifest such a signature and stamp of virtue, as to make our judgment of them a matter of intuition rather than the result of continued examination."—ALEXANDER KNOX: quoted in Southey's Life of Wesley.

Mirah claimed she had slept well that night. When she descended in Mab's black dress, her dark hair curling in fresh fibrils as it gradually dried from its plenteous bath, she appeared as one beginning to take comfort after the long sorrow and watching which had paled her cheek and drawn blue semicircles beneath her eyes. It was Mab who carried her breakfast and ushered her down, displaying a certain pride in the effect of a pair of tiny felt slippers she had rushed out to buy, for there were no shoes in the house small enough for Mirah. Her borrowed dress ceased about her ankles, revealing the cheap clothing that moulded itself upon her feet, seeming an adornment as choice as the sheaths of buds. The farthing buckles were bijoux.

"Oh, if you please, mamma?" cried Mab, clasping her hands and stooping toward Mirah's feet as they entered the parlor. "Look at the slippers, how beautiful they fit! I declare she is like the Queen Budoor—'two delicate feet, the work of the protecting and all-recompensing Creator, support her; and I wonder how they can sustain what is above them.'"

Mirah looked down at her own feet in a childlike manner and then smiled at Mrs. Meyrick, who was saying inwardly, "One could hardly imagine this creature having an evil thought. But wise people would tell me to be cautious." She returned Mirah's smile and said, "I fear the feet have had to sustain their burden a little too often lately. But to-day she will rest and be my companion."

"And she will tell you so many things and I shall not hear them," grumbled Mab, who felt herself in the first volume of a delightful romance and obliged to miss some chapters because she had to go to pupils.

Kate was already gone to make sketches along the river, and Amy was away on business errands. It was what the mother wished, to be alone with this stranger, whose story must be a sorrowful one, yet was needful to be told.

The small front parlor was as good as a temple that morning. Sunlight lay on the river and soft air came in through the open window; the walls showed a glorious silent cloud of witnesses—the Virgin soaring amid her cherubic escort; grand Melancholia with her solemn universe; the Prophets and Sibyls; the School of Athens; the Last Supper; mystic groups where far-off ages made one moment; grave Holbein and Rembrandt heads; the Tragic Muse; last-century children at their musings or their play; Italian poets—all were there through the medium of a little black and white. The neat mother, who had weathered her troubles and come out of them with a face still cheerful, was sorting colored wools for her embroidery. Hafiz purred on the window-ledge, the clock on the mantle-piece ticked without hurry, and the occasional sound of wheels seemed to lie outside the more massive central quiet. Mrs. Meyrick thought that this quiet might be the best invitation to speech on the part of her companion, and chose not to disturb it by remark. Mirah sat opposite in her former attitude, her hands clasped on her lap, her ankles crossed, her eyes at first traveling slowly over the objects around her, but finally resting with a sort of placid reverence on Mrs. Meyrick. At length she began to speak softly.

"I remember my mother's face better than anything; yet I was not seven when I was taken away, and I am nineteen now."

"I can understand that," said Mrs. Meyrick. "There are some earliest things that last the longest."

"Oh, yes, it was the earliest. I think my life began with waking up and loving my mother's face: it was so near to me, and her arms were round me, and she sang to me. One hymn she sang so often, so often: and then she taught me to sing it with her: it was the first I ever sang. They were always Hebrew hymns she sang; and because I never knew the meaning of the words they seemed full of nothing but our love and happiness. When I lay in my little bed and it was all white above me, she used to bend over me, between me and the white, and sing in a sweet, low voice. I can dream myself back into that time when I am awake, and it often comes back to me in my sleep—my hand is very little, I put it up to her face and she kisses it.
