What passion seems more absurd, when we have got outside it and looked at calamity as a collective risk, than this amazed anguish that I and not Thou, He or She, should be just the smitten one? Yet perhaps some who have afterward made themselves a willing fence before the breast of another, and have carried their own heart-wound in heroic silence—some who have made their deeds great—nevertheless began with this angry amazement at their own smart, and on the mere denial of their fantastic desires raged as if under the sting of wasps which reduced the universe for them to an unjust infliction of pain. This was nearly poor Gwendolen's condition. What though such a reverse as hers had often happened to other girls? The one point she had been all her life learning to care for was that it had happened to *her*: it was what *she* felt under Klesmer's demonstration that she was not remarkable enough to command fortune by force of will and merit; it was what *she* would feel under the rigors of Mrs. Mompert's constant expectation, under the dull demand that she should be cheerful with three Miss Momperts, under the necessity of showing herself entirely submissive, and keeping her thoughts to herself. To be a queen disthroned is not so hard as some other down-stepping: imagine one who had been made to believe in his own divinity finding all homage withdrawn, and himself unable to perform a miracle that would recall the homage and restore his own confidence. Something akin to this illusion and this helplessness had befallen the poor spoiled child, with the lovely lips and eyes and the majestic figure—which seemed now to have no magic in them.

She rose from the low ottoman where she had been sitting purposeless, and walked up and down the drawing-room, resting her elbow on one palm while she leaned down her cheek on the other, and a slow tear fell. She thought, "I have always, ever since I was little, felt that mamma was not a happy woman; and now I dare say I shall be more unhappy than she has been."

Her mind dwelt for a few moments on the picture of herself losing her youth and ceasing to enjoy—not minding whether she did this or that: but such picturing inevitably brought back the image of her mother.

"Poor mamma! it will be still worse for her now. I can get a little money for her—that is all I shall care about now." And then, with an entirely new movement of her imagination, she saw her mother getting quite old and white, and herself no longer young but faded, and their two faces meeting still with memory and love, and she knowing what was in her mother's mind—"Poor Gwen too is sad and faded now"—and then, for the first time, she sobbed, not in anger, but with a sort of tender misery.

Her face was toward the door, and she saw her mother enter. She barely saw that; for her eyes were large with tears, and she pressed her handkerchief against them hurriedly. Before she took it away she felt her mother's arms round her, and this sensation, which seemed a prolongation of her inward vision, overcame her will to be reticent; she sobbed anew in spite of herself, as they pressed their cheeks together.

Mrs. Davilow had brought something in her hand which had already caused her an agitating anxiety, and she dared not speak until her darling had become calmer. But Gwendolen, with whom weeping had always been a painful manifestation to be resisted, if possible, again pressed her handkerchief against her eyes, and, with a deep breath, drew her head backward and looked at her mother, who was pale and tremulous.

"It was nothing, mamma," said Gwendolen, thinking that her mother had been moved in this way simply by finding her in distress. "It is all over now."

But Mrs. Davilow had withdrawn her arms, and Gwendolen perceived a letter in her hand.

"What is that letter?—worse news still?" she asked, with a touch of bitterness.

"I don't know what you will think it, dear," said Mrs. Davilow, keeping the letter in her hand. "You will hardly guess where it comes from."

"Don't ask me to guess anything," said Gwendolen, rather impatiently, as if a bruise were being pressed.

"It is addressed to you, dear."

Gwendolen gave the slightest perceptible toss of the head.

"It comes from Diplow," said Mrs. Davilow, giving her the letter.

She knew Grandcourt's indistinct handwriting, and her mother was not surprised to see her blush deeply; but watching her as she read, and wondering much what was the purport of the letter, she saw the color die out. Gwendolen's lips even were pale as she turned the open note toward her mother. The words were few and formal:

*Mr. Grandcourt presents his compliments to Miss Harleth, and begs to know whether he may be permitted to call at Offendene to-morrow after two and to see her alone. Mr. Grandcourt has just returned from Leubronn, where he had hoped to find Miss Harleth.*

Mrs. Davilow read, and then looked at her daughter inquiringly, leaving the note in her hand. Gwendolen let it fall to the floor, and turned away.

"It must be answered, darling," said Mrs. Davilow, timidly. "The man waits."

Gwendolen sank on the settee, clasped her hands, and looked straight before her, not at her mother. She had the expression of one who had been startled by a sound and was listening to know what would come of it. The sudden change of the situation was bewildering. A few minutes before she was looking along an inescapable path of repulsive monotony, with hopeless inward rebellion against the imperious lot which left her no choice: and lo, now, a moment of choice was come. Yet—was it triumph she felt most or terror? Impossible for Gwendolen not to feel some triumph in a tribute to her power at a time when she was first tasting the bitterness of insignificance: again she seemed to be getting a sort of empire over her own life. But how to use it? Here came the terror. Quick, quick, like pictures in a book beaten open with a sense of hurry, came back vividly, yet in fragments, all that she had gone through in relation to Grandcourt—the allurements, the vacillations, the resolve to accede, the final repulsion; the incisive face of that dark-eyed lady with the lovely boy: her own pledge (was it a pledge not to marry him?)—the new disbelief in the worth of men and things for which that scene of disclosure had become a symbol. That unalterable experience made a vision at which in the first agitated moment, before tempering reflections could suggest themselves, her native terror shrank.

Where was the good of choice coming again? What did she wish? Anything different? No! And yet in the dark seed-growths of consciousness a new wish was forming itself—"I wish I had never known it!" Something, anything she wished for that would have saved her from the dread to let Grandcourt come.

It was no long while—yet it seemed long to Mrs. Davilow, before she thought it well to say, gently,

"It will be necessary for you to write, dear. Or shall I write an answer for you—which you will dictate?"

"No, mamma," said Gwendolen, drawing a deep breath. "But please lay me out the pen and paper."

That was gaining time. Was she to decline Grandcourt's visit—close the shutters—not even look out on what would happen?—though with the assurance that she should remain just where she was? The young activity within her made a warm current through her terror and stirred toward something that would be an event—toward an opportunity in which she could look and speak with the former effectiveness. The interest of the morrow was no longer at a deadlock.

"There is really no reason on earth why you should be so alarmed at the man's waiting a few minutes, mamma," said Gwendolen, remonstrantly, as Mrs. Davilow, having prepared the writing materials, looked toward her expectantly. "Servants expect nothing else than to wait. It is not to be supposed that I must write on the instant."

"No, dear," said Mrs. Davilow, in the tone of one corrected, turning to sit down and take up a bit of work that lay at hand; "he can wait another quarter of an hour, if you like."

It was very simple speech and action on her part, but it was what might have been subtly calculated. Gwendolen felt a contradictory desire to be hastened: hurry would save her from deliberate choice.

"I did not mean him to wait long enough for that needlework to be finished," she said, lifting her hands to stroke the backward curves of her hair, while she rose from her seat and stood still.

"But if you don't feel able to decide?" said Mrs. Davilow, sympathizingly.

"I *must* decide," said Gwendolen, walking to the writing-table and seating herself. All the while there was a busy under-current in her, like the thought of a man who keeps up a dialogue while he is considering how he can slip away. Why should she not let him come? It bound her to nothing. He had been to Leubronn after her: of course he meant a direct unmistakable renewal of the suit which before had been only implied. What then? She could reject him. Why was she to deny herself the freedom of doing this—which she would like to do?

"If Mr. Grandcourt has only just returned from Leubronn," said Mrs. Davilow, observing that Gwendolen leaned back in her chair after taking the pen in her hand—"I wonder whether he has heard of our misfortunes?"

"That could make no difference to a man in his position," said Gwendolen, rather contemptuously.

"It would to some men," said Mrs. Davilow. "They would not like to take a wife from a family in a state of beggary almost, as we are. Here we are at Offendene with a great shell over us, as usual. But just imagine his finding us at Sawyer's Cottage. Most men are afraid of being bored or taxed by a wife's family. If Mr. Grandcourt did know, I think it a strong proof of his attachment to

Desire trims the sails, but it is circumstance that provides the wind.

Outside, Grandcourt rode his magnificent black stallion, Yarico, with his groom trailing behind on Criterion, enjoying the leisurely journey from Diplow to Offendene. Inside, however, the air was thick with anticipation. Gwendolen sat before the mirror while her mother, Mrs. Davilow, gathered the cascading mass of her daughter's light-brown hair into a neat coil.

"Only gather it up easily and make a coil, mamma," Gwendolen instructed softly.

"Shall I bring you some earrings, Gwen?" Mrs. Davilow asked, her voice brightening as she studied their reflection. The mother couldn't help but notice the eyes seemed clearer, a shadow lifted from the face, restoring the placid youthfulness to the features. "You really want your earrings?"

"No, mamma. I shall wear none. I'll put on my black silk. Black is the only attire for refusing an offer," Gwendolen replied, offering one of her practiced, enigmatic smiles before rising to shed her dressing gown.

"Suppose the offer isn't made after all?" Mrs. Davilow ventured, a sly note of hope in her tone.

"Then that will simply be because I refused it beforehand," Gwendolen countered, her head tossing with a proud little jerk. "It comes to the same thing."

As she descended the stairs in her long black robes, she moved with a firm poise and elastic grace that had been absent recently, like a parched plant suddenly watered. Her mother watched, thinking, *She is quite herself again. It must be the joy of his coming. Can her mind truly be made up against him?*

Gwendolen would have been furious if that thought had been spoken aloud. In truth, for the last twenty hours, she had been trapped in a whirlwind of alternating images and arguments regarding her marriage to Grandcourt. The resolve she had formed had dissolved; the counterbalancing thoughts born of counterbalancing desires had left her in a state where no conclusion felt fixed. She did not intend to accept him. From the moment she received his letter, she had intended to refuse. Yet, this very refusal forced her to confront the unwelcome reasons head-on, stripping away her awe of them. Her imagination, fueled by uncertainty, reshaped her knowledge in a dozen different ways. By staring at a dubious object with a constructive mind, one can conjure twenty different shapes. The vague hesitations she had felt at the Whispering Stones now counted for nothing, submerged beneath a final, overwhelming repulsion.

*If it hadn't been for that day at Cardell Chase,* she told herself, *there would have been no obstacle.* On that day, she had not reasoned; she had acted with an impulsive force against which all questioning was merely a voice against a torrent. That impulse stemmed not only from maidenly pride and jealousy, but from a vague, terrifying dread of wrongdoing. She had no scruples about anything consistent with being a lady, but she shrank from the dim region of disgrace, guilt, and wrong-doing with mingled pride and terror. Even apart from shame, the idea of deliberately injuring another placed her in the realm of guilt.

But did she know the exact state of affairs regarding Mrs. Glasher and her children? She had promised not to interfere. Would another woman marrying Grandcourt actually obstruct those wishes, or cause real injury? Or might it be better for Grandcourt to marry? After all, what could not a woman achieve when married, if she knew how to assert herself? Here, too, was constructive imagination. Gwendolen possessed as accurate a conception of marriage—with its mutual influences, demands, and duties—as she did of magnetic currents or storm laws.

"Mamma managed badly," she summarized her mother's experience. *I shall manage quite differently.*

The trials of matrimony were the last topic Mrs. Davilow wished to explore with her daughter. *I wonder what mamma and my uncle would say if they knew about Mrs. Glasher!* Gwendolen debated inwardly. Not that she could imagine telling them, even if she weren't bound by silence. *I wonder what anybody would say; or what they would say to Mr. Grandcourt marrying someone else and having other children!* Considering what "anybody" would say released her from the difficulty of judging where everything was obscure. Her memories proved that "anybody" regarded illegitimate children as more shameful and socially deprived than illegitimate fathers. The verdict of the public seemed to absolve her of concern for Mrs. Glasher.

Yet, there was another source of distress. Public opinion could not erase the indignation and loathing she felt at being expected to unite herself with an outworn life, full of backward secrets that must have been more keenly felt than any association with her own. True, the question of love on her part had played little role in her view of Grandcourt. The desirability of marriage had always seemed rooted in feelings other than love; being enamored was the man's part, upon whom the advances depended. Gwendolen had found no objection to Grandcourt's manner of being enamored until she glimpsed his past, which she resented as a deliberate offense. His advances to *her* had been deliberate, and she felt a retrospective disgust for them. Perhaps other men's lives were similarly fraught with secrets, rendering the ignorant suppositions of the women they courted a farce.

These feelings of disgust and indignation had sunk deep. Though recent troubles had dulled them from passion to memory, it was their reverberating activity that kept her firm in her understanding: she would not accept Grandcourt. She had never meant to form a new determination; she had only been considering what might be thought or said. If anything could have changed her mind, it would have been the prospect of making things easy for "poor mamma"—a temptation she admitted. But no! She was going to refuse him. Meanwhile, the thought that he was coming to be refused was invigorating. She held the white reins in her hands again; a new current flowed through her, reviving her from the beaten-down consciousness left by her interview with Klesmer. She was no longer going to crave an opinion on her capabilities; she was going to exercise her power.

Was this what caused her heart to palpitate annoyingly when she heard the horse's footsteps on the gravel? When Miss Merry opened the door to announce that Grandcourt was in the drawing-room, the hours of preparation and the triumph of the situation seemed useless. She might as well have seen him suddenly appear in the midst of her despondency.

Walking into the drawing-room required her to concentrate all her energy into self-control, appearing gravely gracious. She gave her hand to him and answered his hope that she was well in a voice as low and languid as his own. A moment later, seated on two wreath-painted chairs—Gwendolen upright with downcast eyelids, Grandcourt two yards away, leaning an arm over the back of his chair and gazing at her while holding his hat in his left hand—anyone viewing them as a picture would have concluded they were in a stage of love-making suspense. And certainly, the courtship had begun. She already felt herself being wooed by this silent man seated at an agreeable distance, surrounded by a subtle atmosphere of attar of roses and an attention bent wholly on her. He, too, considered himself to be wooing; he was not a man to suppose his presence carried no consequences, and he was exactly the type to feel the utmost piquancy in a girl he had not found entirely calculable.

"I was disappointed not to find you at Leubronn," he began, his usual broken drawl tinged with amorous languor. "The place was intolerable without you. A mere kennel of a place. Don't you think so?"

"I can't judge what it would be without myself," Gwendolen replied, turning her eyes on him with a recovered sense of mischief. "*With* myself, I like it well enough to have stayed longer, if I could. But I was obliged to come home on account of family troubles."

"It was very cruel of you to go to Leubronn," Grandcourt said, ignoring the troubles, though Gwendolen—she hardly knew why—wished for a clear understanding at once. "You must have known that it would spoil everything: you knew you were the heart and soul of everything that went on. Are you quite reckless about me?"

It would be impossible to say "yes" seriously, nor "no." What else could she say? In her difficulty, she lowered her eyelids again and blushed over face and neck. Grandcourt saw her in a new phase and believed she was showing inclination, yet he was determined she should show it more decidedly.

"Perhaps there is some deeper interest? Some attraction—some engagement—which it would have been only fair to make me aware of? Is there any man who stands between us?"

Inwardly, the answer framed itself: *No; but there is a woman.* Yet how could she utter this? Even if she hadn't promised that woman silence, it would be impossible to enter the subject with Grandcourt. How could she arrest his wooing by beginning a formal speech—"I perceive your intention—it is most flattering, etc."? A fish honestly invited to be eaten has a clear path to decline, but how does one swim against a net? Apart from the net, would she dare say anything decisive? Gwendolen had no time to clarify. Compelled to silence, she waited. After a pause, Grandcourt asked, "Am I to understand that some one else is preferred?"

Impatient with her own embarrassment, Gwendolen decided to rush at the difficulty and free herself. Raising her eyes, she said with something of her former clearness and defiance, "No"—wishing him to understand, *What then? I may not be ready to take you.* There was nothing Grandcourt could not understand that would affect his *amour propre*.

"The last thing I would do is to importune you. I should not hope to win you by making myself a bore. If there were no hope for me, I would ask you to tell me so at once, that I might just ride away to—no matter where."

Almost to her own astonishment, Gwendolen felt a sudden alarm at the image of Grandcourt finally riding away. What would remain? Nothing but the former dreariness. She liked him to be there. She snatched at the subject that would defer any decisive answer.

"I fear you are not aware of what has happened to us. I have lately had to think so much of my mamma's troubles that other subjects have been thrown into the background. She has lost all her fortune, and we are going to leave this place. I must ask you to excuse my seeming preoccupation."

In eluding a direct appeal, Gwendolen recovered some self-possession. She spoke with dignity and looked straight at Grandcourt, whose long, narrow, impenetrable eyes met hers, mysteriously arresting them. The subtle drama between man and woman is often such that it cannot be rendered in words strung together like dominoes. The single word *Love* expresses no more of the myriad modes of mutual attraction than *Thought* informs you of your neighbor's mind. It was hard to tell on which side—theirs or his—the influence was more mixed. At that moment, his strongest wish was to be completely master of this creature, this piquant combination of maidenliness and mischief; that she knew things that made her start away from him spurred him to triumph over that repugnance, and he believed he would triumph. And she—ah, piteous equality in the need to dominate!—was overcome like the thirsty one drawn toward the seeming water in the desert, overwhelmed by the suffused sense that here, in this man's homage to her, lay the rescue from helpless subjection to an oppressive lot.

All the while they looked at each other. Grandcourt said slowly and languidly, as if of no importance, "You will tell me now, I hope, that Mrs. Davilow's loss of fortune will not trouble you further. You will trust me to prevent it from weighing upon her. You will give me the claim to provide against that."

The little pauses and refined drawls gave Gwendolen time to dream a life. As the words penetrated her, they had the effect of a draught of wine, suddenly making all things easier, desirable things not so wrong, and people generally less disagreeable. She had a momentary phantasmal love for this man who chose his words so well, a mere incarnation of delicate homage. Repugnance, dread, scruples—these were dim as remembered pains, while she tasted relief under the immediate pain of hopelessness. She imagined herself springing to her mother, playful again. Yet when Grandcourt ceased speaking, there was an instant where she was conscious of standing at the turning of the ways.

"You are very generous," she said, not moving her eyes, with a gentle intonation.

"You accept what will make such things a matter of course?" Grandcourt asked, without new eagerness. "You consent to become my wife?"

This time, Gwendolen remained quite pale. Something made her rise from her seat in spite of herself and walk to a little distance. Then she turned, hands folded before her, standing in silence.

Grandcourt immediately rose too, resting his hat on the chair but still holding it. The evident hesitation of this destitute girl to take his splendid offer stung him into a keenness of interest he hadn't known for years. None the less, he attributed her hesitation entirely to her knowledge of Mrs. Glasher. In that posture of preparation, he asked, "Do you command me to go?" No familiar spirit could have suggested more effective words.

"No," said Gwendolen. She could not let him go; that negative was a clutch. She seemed to herself to be, after all, only drifted toward the tremendous decision—but drifting depends on something besides the currents when the sails have been set beforehand.

"You accept my devotion?" Grandcourt asked, holding his hat by his side and looking straight into her eyes, unmoving. Their eyes meeting allowed any length of pause: but wait as long as she would, how could she contradict herself? What had she detained him for? He had shut out any explanation.

"Yes," came gravely from Gwendolen's lips, as if answering to her name in a court

"Knowing a man in general is easier than knowing a particular man." — La Rochefoucauld.

An hour after Grandcourt departed, the news of Gwendolen's engagement rippled through the rectory, transforming the evening at Offendene into a celebration of social ascent. Mr. and Mrs. Gascoigne, accompanied by Anna, gathered to witness the triumph. The rector, beaming with paternal approval, congratulated Mrs. Davilow on securing such a formidable attachment. "You look serious," he observed, patting Gwendolen's shoulder with a smile that treated her gravity as innocent naughtiness. "But consider the good arising from our adversity; it has revealed your future husband's delicate liberality." He alluded to Grandcourt's calculated implication that he would provide for Mrs. Davilow, a detail Gwendolen had memorized with precise accuracy.

Mrs. Gascoigne, feeling the weight of her duty to validate the match, assured Gwendolen that Grandcourt would have behaved equally handsomely had she remained in England and been engaged months prior. "Now, there is no room for caprice," she declared, urging her daughter to feel the proper debt of gratitude for a man who persevered. Gwendolen offered a saucy, grave reply, admitting she was unsure what was "proper" to feel, a sentiment that drew a knowing nod from the rector and a suppressed sigh from Anna.

Anna, usually the quiet observer, found herself struggling to maintain her composure. In recent days, she had nurtured a fragile romance in her mind, imagining a future where Rex's longing might soften Gwendolen's heart, allowing them to marry once Rex secured wealth. Now, with Gwendolen bound to Grandcourt, Anna's hope had curdled into a prayer for grace, wishing only to serve as a useful sister while Rex remained poor. The evening was dominated by the rector's decisive remarks on settlements, which he deemed unnecessary, leaving everything to Grandcourt's discretion. Mrs. Davilow inquired about the properties, Ryelands and Gadsmere. Mr. Gascoigne described Ryelands as a magnificent seat built by Inigo Jones, worth twelve thousand a year, complete with livings in the Grandcourt gift. Mrs. Gascoigne counted off the Grandcourt assets on her fingers—the estates, the baronetcy, and the peerage—lamenting only that the peerage carried no additional land. The rector dismissed the peerage as a remote chance, cautioning against the concentration of such immense power in one man, yet acknowledging the value of Sir Mallinger Grandcourt Mallinger.

As the evening wound down, Mrs. Gascoigne urged Henry to write to Mrs. Mompert, citing the engagement as a shield against offense. Gwendolen, internally dismissing the name Mompert as mere mumbo-jumbo, remained silent throughout the festivities. That night, however, she could not sleep. In her rare wakefulness, she lay staring into the darkness, appalled by the realization that she was committing to a path she had once fled with repugnance. For the first time, the question of right and wrong terrified her; the old comforts of atonement through gifts and caresses held no sway. The lawlessness she had once embraced—the belief that she need only amuse herself—now frightened her with the shadow of possible calamity. Religious teachings and a deeper, inexorable sense of awe converged in her mind, framing her choice as sacrilege. The brilliant position she craved tasted of poison. Images of the unhappy woman and Grandcourt's past relations with her haunted her, clinging like a disgrace until she finally cried out for her mother.

Mrs. Davilow responded instantly, and Gwendolen fell asleep on her mother's shoulder, only to dream of a lit ballroom before waking to find her mother holding a small packet. "The groom has brought Criterion," Mrs. Davilow explained, handing over a delicate enameled casket containing a splendid diamond ring and a letter.

*Pray wear this ring when I come at twelve in sign of our betrothal. I enclose a check drawn in the name of Mr. Gascoigne, for immediate expenses. Of course Mrs. Davilow will remain at Offendene, at least for some time. I hope, when I come, you will have granted me an early day, when you may begin to command me at a shorter distance.—Yours devotedly, H. M. GRANDCOURT.*

The check was for five hundred pounds. Gwendolen showed it to her mother with feigned delight. "How very kind and delicate!" Mrs. Davilow exclaimed, though she expressed a desire not to be dependent on a son-in-law. "Mamma, if you say that again, I will not marry him," Gwendolen snapped, her anger flaring at the attempt to diminish her agency. Mrs. Davilow pleaded that she did not wish Gwendolen to marry solely for her sake, but Gwendolen tossed her head away, irritated by the motive. Deep down, she knew she was drawn to the marriage by forces stronger than her mother's renunciation could counteract. She resolved to meet the night's alarms with daylight logic, convincing herself that the doubts would prove weak.

"I thought the lover always put on the betrothal ring himself," Gwendolen said laughingly, slipping the ring onto her finger with a charming tilt of her head. "He would rather make me put it on than ask me to let him do it. Aha! He is very proud. But so am I. We shall match each other." She dismissed the idea of a suitor kneeling, declaring she would hate a man who fawned. "He really is not disgusting," she added, a moderate praise that satisfied her mother.

Dressed in her riding-habit to avoid delay, Gwendolen descended the stairs, eager to stir her blood with the intoxication of youth and reclaim her daring. "Go back and make yourself look like a duchess, mamma," she commanded, turning sharply. "Put your point-lace over your head. You must not take things humbly."

When Grandcourt raised her hand to inspect the ring, he spoke gravely of his foresight. "You will tell me if there is anything I forget?" he asked, keeping her hand softly within his own. "I will do anything you wish." Gwendolen smiled, admitting she was unreasonable in her wishes. "Yes, I expect that. Women always are."

"Then I will not be unreasonable," Gwendolen retorted, withdrawing her hand and tossing her head saucily. "I will not be told that I am what women always are."

"I did not say that," Grandcourt replied with his usual gravity. "You are what no other woman is."

"And what is that, pray?" Gwendolen teased, moving away with a hint of menace.

Grandcourt paused before answering. "You are the woman I love."

"Oh, what nice speeches!" Gwendolen laughed, the familiarity of his love for another woman under strange circumstances settling over her. She demanded a return speech regarding their wedding date. "Not yet," Grandcourt said, his eyes alight with anticipation. "Not till we have had a gallop over the downs. I am so thirsty for that... It will begin in ten days!"

"Let us be married in ten days, then," Grandcourt agreed. "And we shall not be bored about the stables."

"What do women always say in answer to that?" Gwendolen asked mischievously.

"They agree to it," the lover replied, caught slightly off guard.

"Then I will not!" Gwendolen declared, putting on her gauntlets while her eyes gathered fun.

The scene was a delightful drama, played out with mutual understanding. Grandcourt preferred the theater of courtship, and Gwendolen, left at ease, found her spirits rising as she played at reigning. After a glorious gallop, she was in a state of exhilaration that made her eager to hasten the marriage, consenting to a date three weeks hence despite the difficulties of the trousseau.

Meanwhile, Lush, aware of the engagement through abundant signs, grew impatient under Grandcourt's silence. He sensed the change would affect his prospects and, lacking a motive for opposition, sought to secure his own future. He wrote to Sir Hugo Mallinger, suggesting that Daniel Deronda be invited to Diplow to discuss a potential financial arrangement. Lush argued that Deronda, possessing Sir Hugo's confidence, could approach Grandcourt with a definite offer without arousing suspicion, potentially trading the chance of the Diplow estate for a substantial sum.

Sir Hugo, reading the letter with Deronda, found the suggestion plausible. Though Deronda expressed a reluctance to visit on such transparent terms, he eventually agreed, driven by a complex mix of obligation and a latent curiosity about Gwendolen's story. He recalled hearing of her flight from a previous suit and her subsequent poverty, seeing in her a nature prone to struggle and difficulty—a quality that attracted his sympathy. Deronda felt a strange pull toward her, a fascination with her womanhood that mingled with his Utopian visions, yet he remained reticent, guarding his feelings as one might protect a delicate bird.

Grandcourt, meanwhile, spent the evening in solitary meditation, his thoughts circling like ripples in a dark pool. He derived no satisfaction from the belief that Gwendolen loved him; rather, he found pleasure in the fact that she had accepted him despite her initial resistance. He viewed her as a spirited woman destined to command everyone but himself, a master to be ruled by a woman who would have liked to master another. He had no taste for tenderness or petitioning solicitude; he desired a partner who would submit to his mastery. As he sat amidst new publications, he felt a strange, premature energy, a chemical reaction within him that hinted at biting or explosive potential, preparing him for the uncertain future that awaited him with Gwendolen.

"Surely whoever speaks to me in the right voice,
him or her I shall follow.
As the water follows the moon, silently,
with fluid steps anywhere around the globe."

"Now my cousins are at Diplow," Grandcourt announced, his voice smooth and deliberate. "Will you go there? Tomorrow? The carriage shall come for Mrs. Davilow. You can tell me what you would like done in the rooms. Things must be put in decent order while we are away at Ryelands. And tomorrow is the only day."

He sat sideways on a sofa in the drawing-room at Offendene, one hand and elbow resting on the back, the other thrust between his crossed knees. It was the posture of a man deeply invested in observing the person beside him. Gwendolen, who had once despised needlework, now held a piece of white embroidery with apparent zeal, though a closer inspection would reveal many false stitches. For the past eight or nine days, their hours had been chiefly spent on horseback, yet some margin remained for this more static companionship, which Gwendolen had surprisingly not found disagreeable. She was very well satisfied with Grandcourt. His answers to her lively inquiries about his travels and experiences bore his drawling cadence remarkably well. From the start, she had noticed that he knew exactly what to say; she constantly felt that he possessed nothing of the fool in his composition, and by some subtle alchemy, he communicated to her the impression that all folly lay with others, who did what he did not care to do. A man who seems capable of commanding the best possesses a sovereign power of depreciation. Grandcourt's behavior as a lover had barely exceeded the limits of an amorous homage that was as inobtrusive as a wafted scent of roses, spending all its effects on a gratified vanity. One day, indeed, he had kissed not her cheek but her neck a little below her ear. Gwendolen, taken by surprise, had started up with marked agitation, causing him to rise and ask, "I beg your pardon—did I annoy you?" "Oh, it was nothing," Gwendolen had replied, rather afraid of herself, "only I cannot bear—to be kissed under my ear." She had sat down again with a little playful laugh, but all the while her heart beat with a vague fear; she was no longer at liberty to flout him as she had flouted poor Rex. Her agitation seemed not uncomplimentary, and he had been content not to transgress again.

Today, a slight rain hindered riding, but to compensate, a package had arrived from London. Mrs. Davilow had just left the room after bringing in for admiration the beautiful things (arranged by Grandcourt's ordering) scattered across the tables. Gwendolen was then enjoying the scenery of her life. She let her hands fall to her lap and said with a pretty air of perversity, "Why is tomorrow the only day?"

"Because the next day is the first with the hounds," Grandcourt replied.

"And after that?"

"After that I must go away for a couple of days—it's a bore—but I shall go one day and come back the next." Grandcourt noticed a change in her face. Releasing his hand from under his knees, he laid it on hers and said, "You object to my going away?"

"It's no use objecting," Gwendolen said coldly. She was resisting to the utmost her temptation to tell him that she suspected where he was going—the temptation to make a clean breast, speaking without restraint.

"Yes it is," Grandcourt said, enfolding her hand. "I will put off going. And I will travel at night, so as only to be away one day." He thought that he knew the reason for what he inwardly called this bit of temper, and she was particularly fascinating to him at this moment.

"Then don't put off going, but travel at night," Gwendolen said, feeling that she could command him, and finding in this peremptoriness a small outlet for her irritation.

"Then you will go to Diplow tomorrow?"

"Oh, yes, if you wish it," Gwendolen said in a high tone of careless assent. Her concentration on other feelings had really hindered her from noticing that her hand was being held.

"How you treat us poor devils of men!" Grandcourt said, lowering his tone. "We are always getting the worst of it."

"Are you?" Gwendolen asked in a tone of inquiry, looking at him more naively than usual. She longed to believe this commonplace badinage as the serious truth about her lover; in that case, she too was justified. If she knew everything, Mrs. Glasher would appear more blamable than Grandcourt. "Are you always getting the worst?"

"Yes. Are you as kind to me as I am to you?" Grandcourt asked, looking into her eyes with his narrow gaze.

Gwendolen felt herself stricken. She was conscious of having received so much that her sense of command was checked, sinking away in the perception that, look around her as she might, she could not turn back. It was as if she had consented to mount a chariot where another held the reins; and it was not in her nature to leap out in the eyes of the world. She had not consented in ignorance, and all she could say now would be a confession that she had not been ignorant. Her right to explanation was gone. All she had to do now was to adjust herself so that the spikes of that unwilling penance which conscience imposed should not gall her. With a sort of mental shiver, she resolutely changed her mental attitude. There had been a little pause during which she had not turned away her eyes; and with a sudden break into a smile, she said, "If I were as kind to you as you are to me, that would spoil your generosity: it would no longer be as great as it could be—and it is that now."

"Then I am not to ask for one kiss," Grandcourt said, contented to pay a large price for this new kind of love-making, which introduced marriage by the finest contrast.

"Not one?" Gwendolen said, getting saucy, and nodding at him defiantly.

He lifted her little left hand to his lips and then released it respectfully. Clearly, it was faint praise to say of him that he was not disgusting; he was almost charming. She felt at this moment that it was not likely she could ever have loved another man better than this one. His reticence gave her some inexplicable, delightful consciousness.

"Apropos," she said, taking up her work again, "is there anyone besides Captain and Mrs. Torrington at Diplow? Or do you leave them tête-à-tête? I suppose he converses in cigars, and she answers with her chignon."

"She has a sister with her," Grandcourt said with his shadow of a smile, "and there are two men besides—one of them you know, I believe."

"Ah, then, I have a poor opinion of him," Gwendolen said, shaking her head.

"You saw him at Leubronn—young Deronda—a young fellow with the Mallingers."

Gwendolen felt as if her heart were making a sudden gambol, and her fingers, which tried to keep a firm hold on her work, grew cold.

"I never spoke to him," she said, dreading any discernible change in herself. "Is he not disagreeable?"

"No, not particularly," Grandcourt said in his most languid way. "He thinks a little too much of himself. I thought he had been introduced to you."

"No. Someone told me his name the evening before I came away. That was all. What is he?"

"A sort of ward of Sir Hugo Mallinger's. Nothing of any consequence."

"Oh, poor creature! How very unpleasant for him!" Gwendolen said, speaking from the lip and not meaning any sarcasm. "I wonder if it has left off raining!" she added, rising and going to look out of the window.

Happily, it did not rain the next day, and Gwendolen rode to Diplow on Criterion as she had done on that former day when she returned with her mother in the carriage. She always felt the more daring for being in her riding-dress, besides having the agreeable belief that she looked as well as possible in it—a sustaining consciousness in any meeting which seemed formidable. Her anger toward Deronda had changed into a superstitious dread, due perhaps to the coercion he had exercised over her thought, lest the first interference of his in her life might foreshadow some future influence. It is of such stuff that superstitions are commonly made: an intense feeling about ourselves which makes the evening star shine at us with a threat, and the blessing of a beggar encourage us. And superstitions carry consequences which often verify their hope or their foreboding.

The time before luncheon was taken up for Gwendolen by going over the rooms with Mrs. Torrington and Mrs. Davilow, and she thought it likely that if she saw Deronda, there would hardly be need for more than a bow between them. She meant to notice him as little as possible.

And after all, she found herself under an inward compulsion too strong for her pride. From the first moment of their being in the room together, she seemed to herself to be doing nothing but notice him; everything else was the automatic performance of an habitual part.

When he took his place at lunch, Grandcourt had said, "Deronda, Miss Harleth tells me you were not introduced to her at Leubronn?"

"Miss Harleth hardly remembers me, I imagine," Deronda said, looking at her quite simply as they bowed. "She was intensely occupied when I saw her."

Now, did he suppose that she had not suspected him of being the person who redeemed her necklace?

"On the contrary. I remember you very well," Gwendolen said, feeling rather nervous but governing herself and looking at him in return with new examination. "You did not approve of my playing at roulette."

"How did you come to that conclusion?" Deronda asked gravely.

"Oh, you cast an evil eye on my play," Gwendolen said with a turn of her head and a smile. "I began to lose as soon as you came to look on. I had always been winning till then."

"Roulette in such a kennel as Leubronn is a horrid bore," Grandcourt remarked.

"I found it a bore when I began to lose," Gwendolen said. Her face was turned toward Grandcourt as she smiled and spoke, but she gave a sidelong glance at Deronda and saw his eyes fixed on her with a look so gravely penetrating that it had a keener edge for her than his ironical smile at her losses—a keener edge than Klesmer's judgment. She wheeled her neck round as if she wanted to listen to what was being said by the rest, while she was only thinking of Deronda. His face had that disturbing kind of form and expression which threatens to affect opinion—as if one's standard was somehow wrong. (Who has not seen men with faces of this corrective power till they frustrated it by speech or action?) His voice, heard now for the first time, was to Grandcourt's toneless drawl, which had been in her ears every day, as the deep notes of a violoncello to the broken discourse of poultry and other lazy gentry in the afternoon sunshine. Grandcourt, she inwardly conjectured, was perhaps right in saying that Deronda thought too much of himself—a favorite way of explaining a superiority that humiliates. However, the talk turned on the rinderpest and Jamaica, and no more was said about roulette. Grandcourt held that the Jamaica negro was a beastly sort of Baptist Caliban; Deronda said he had always felt a little with Caliban, who naturally had his own point of view and could sing a good song; Mrs. Davilow observed that her father had an estate in Barbadoes but that she herself had never been in the West Indies; Mrs. Torrington was sure she should never sleep in her bed if she lived among blacks; her husband corrected her by saying that the blacks would be manageable enough if it were not for the half-breeds; and Deronda remarked that the whites had to thank themselves for the half-breeds.

While this polite pea-shooting was going on, Gwendolen trifled with her jelly and looked at every speaker in turn that she might feel at ease in looking at Deronda.

"I wonder what he thinks of me, really? He must have felt interested in me, else he would not have sent me my necklace. I wonder what he thinks of my marriage? What notions has he to make him so grave about things? Why is he come to Diplow?"

These questions ran in her mind as the voice of an uneasy longing to be judged by Deronda with unmixed admiration—a longing which had had its seed in her first resentment at his critical glance. Why did she care so much about the opinion of this man who was "nothing of any consequence"? She had no time to find the reason; she was too much engaged in caring. In the drawing-room, when something had called Grandcourt away, she went quite unpremeditatedly up to Deronda, who was standing at a table apart turning over some prints, and said to him, "Shall you hunt tomorrow, Mr. Deronda?"

"Yes, I believe so."

"You don't object to hunting, then?"

"I find excuses for it. It is a sin I am inclined to—when I can't get boating or cricketing."

"Do you object to my hunting?" Gwendolen said with a saucy movement of the chin.

"I have no right to object to anything you choose to do."

"You thought you had a right to object to my gambling," persisted Gwendolen.

"I was sorry for it. I am not aware that I told you of my objection," Deronda said with his usual directness of gaze—a large-eyed gravity, innocent of any intention. His eyes had a peculiarity which had drawn many men into trouble; they were of a dark yet mild intensity which seemed to express a special interest in everyone on whom he fixed them, and might easily help to bring on him those claims which ardently sympathetic people are often creating in the minds of those who

No penitence and no confessional,
No priest ordains it, yet they're forced to sit
Amid deep ashes of their vanished years.

Imagine a rambling, patchy house, the finest parts built of gray stone and red tile, with a round tower jutting from one corner. Its conical roof, dark and mellow, is topped by a weather-vane that makes a pleasing silhouette against the summer greens or the winter snows. The grounds are shaded by spreading trees; on one side, a great tree flourishes near broken banks where Scotch firs stand with naked roots, and beyond lies a rookery. On the other, a pool hangs beneath overhanging bushes, where waterfowl flutter and scream. Surrounding it all is a vast meadow, effectively a park, bordered by an old plantation and guarded by stone lodges that resemble tiny prisons. Beyond the gate, the countryside, once purely rural and lovely, is now blackened by coal mines, chiefly inhabited by men and brothers wearing candles in their hats—their diabolish complexions making them peculiarly suspect in the eyes of the children at Gadsmere. These are Mrs. Glasher's four beautiful children, who have lived there for about three years.

Now, in November, with flower beds empty, trees leafless, and the pool shivering blackly, the place seems somberly in keeping with the black roads and black mounds that seem to mourn the district—except when the children play on the gravel with the dogs for company. Yet Mrs. Glasher, under her present circumstances, likes Gadsmere as well as any other abode. The complete seclusion, secured by the land's unattractiveness, suits her perfectly. When she drives her two ponies with a waggonet full of children, she meets no gentry in carriages, only men of business in gigs. At church, there are no eyes she needs to avoid; the curate and his wife are either ignorant of anything to her disadvantage or simply ignore it. To them, she is merely a widow lady, the tenant of Gadsmere; and the name of Grandcourt holds little interest in that district compared to the names of Fletcher and Gawcome, the lessees of the collieries.

It has been ten years since the elopement of an Irish officer's beautiful wife with young Grandcourt, followed by a duel where bullets wounded only the air. Those who remember the affair now wonder what became of Mrs. Glasher, whose beauty and brilliance made her conspicuous in foreign lands where she was known to live with young Grandcourt. That he should have disentangled himself seemed natural and desirable. As for her, it was thought a woman who had forsaken her child along with her husband had probably sunk lower. Grandcourt, of course, grew weary of her. Given to the pursuit of women, a man in his position would now desire a suitable marriage with the fair daughter of a noble house. No one spoke of Mrs. Glasher anymore, any more than they spoke of the victim in a manslaughter trial a decade prior; she was a lost vessel after whom no expedition would be sent, while Grandcourt was seen in harbor with his colors flying, registered as seaworthy as ever.

Yet, in truth, Grandcourt had never truly disentangled himself from Mrs. Glasher. His passion for her had been the strongest and most lasting he had ever known. Though it was now as dead as the music of a cracked flute, it left a certain dull disposition. Upon the death of her husband three years prior, this prompted in him a vacillating notion of marrying her, in accordance with understandings often expressed during his first ardor. At that early time, Grandcourt would have willingly paid for the freedom won by a divorce, but the husband refused, unwilling to be remarried himself or to have his domestic habits printed in evidence.

The altered poise of the years had changed Mrs. Glasher completely. Initially, she was comparatively careless about marriage. Escaping a disagreeable husband to find bliss with a lover who completely fascinated her—young, handsome, amorous, and living in the best style—was enough. She was an impassioned, vivacious woman, fond of adoration and exasperated by five years of marital rudeness. The sense of release was so strong it stilled anxiety for more than she actually enjoyed. An equivocal position mattered little to her then; she envied no dull, disregarded wife. The only spot spoiling her vision of her new pleasant world was the knowledge that she had left her three-year-old boy, who died two years later. His first tones saying "mamma" retained a difference from those of the children who came after. But now, years had brought many changes beyond the contour of her cheek and throat. That Grandcourt should marry her had become her dominant desire. The equivocal position she had not minded for herself now told upon her through her children, whom she loved with a devotion charged with the added passion of atonement. She had no repentance except in this direction. If Grandcourt married her, the children would be none the worse off; they would see their mother in a dignified position, face no disadvantage, and her son could be made his father's heir. It was this yearning that gave supreme importance to Grandcourt's feeling for her. Her love for him had long resolved itself into anxiety that he should give her the unique, permanent claim of a wife. She expected no other happiness in marriage than the satisfaction of her maternal love and pride—including her pride for herself in the presence of her children. For this result, she was prepared to endure anything quietly in marriage with tragic firmness. She possessed the acuteness to cherish Grandcourt's flickering purpose negatively, avoiding passionate appeals and scene-making. In her, as in everyone else who wanted something from him, his incalculable turns and tendency to harden under beseeching had created a reasonable dread—a slow discovery born of no presentiment in the bearing of a youthful lover with a fine line of face and the softest manners. But reticence had necessarily cost something to this impassioned woman, and she was the bitterer for it. There is no quailing, even that forced on the helpless and injured, which lacks an ugly obverse: the withheld sting was gathering venom.

She was absolutely dependent on Grandcourt. Though he had always been liberal with expenses, he kept everything voluntary on his part. With the goal of marriage before her, she would ask for nothing less. He had said he would never settle anything except by will. When thinking of alternatives, it often occurred to her that even if she did not become Grandcourt's wife, he might never have a son with a legitimate claim, and the end might be that her son was made heir to the best part of his estates. No son at that early age could promise to have more of his father's physique. Yet her becoming Grandcourt's wife was so far from an extravagant notion that even Lush had entertained it, saying he would as soon bet on it as any other likelihood regarding his familiar companion. Lush, indeed, upon inferring that Grandcourt had a preconception of using his residence at Diplow to win Miss Arrowpoint, thought it well to fan that project, taking it as a tacit renunciation of the marriage with Mrs. Glasher, which had long been a mark for the hovering and wheeling of Grandcourt's caprice. But both prospects were negatived by Gwendolen's appearance on the scene. It was natural enough for Mrs. Glasher to enter with eagerness into Lush's plan of hindering that new danger by setting up a barrier in the mind of the girl being sought as a bride. She entered into it with an eagerness containing passion as well as purpose, some of the stored-up venom delivering itself in that way.

Afterward, she heard from Lush of Gwendolen's departure and the probability that all danger from her was gone, but there had been no letter to tell her the danger had returned and become a certainty. Since then, she had written to Grandcourt as she did habitually, and he had been longer than usual in answering. She inferred he might intend coming to Gadsmere at the time he was actually on his way, and she was not without hope—what construction of another's mind is not strong wishing equal to?—that a certain sickening from that frustrated courtship might dispose him to slip more easily into the old track of intention.

Grandcourt had two grave purposes in coming to Gadsmere: to convey the news of his approaching marriage in person, to make this first difficulty final; and to obtain from Lydia his mother's diamonds, which he had long ago confided to her and wished her to wear. Her person suited diamonds, making them appear worth the money given for them. These particular diamonds were not mountains of light—mere peas and haricots for the ears, neck, and hair—but they were worth thousands, and Grandcourt necessarily wished to have them for his wife. Formerly, when he asked Lydia to put them into his keeping again, simply on the ground that they would be safer and ought to be deposited at the bank, she had quietly but absolutely refused, declaring they were quite safe. Finally, she had said, "If you ever marry another woman I will give them up to her: are you going to marry another woman?" At that time, Grandcourt had no motive urging him to persist, and he possessed a grace in him: the disposition to exercise power either by cowing or disappointing others, or exciting in them a rage they dared not express—a disposition active in him as other propensities became languid—had always been in abeyance before Lydia. A severe interpreter might say that the mere facts of their relation, the melancholy position of this woman who depended on his will, made a standing banquet for his delight in dominating. But there was something else in his forbearance toward her: the surviving though metamorphosed effect of the power she had had over him. It was this effect, the fitful dull lapse toward solicitations that once had the zest now missing from life, which had again and again inclined him to espouse a familiar past rather than rouse himself to the expectation of novelty. But now novelty had taken hold of him and urged him to make the most of it.

Mrs. Glasher was seated in the pleasant room where she habitually passed her mornings with her children round her. It had a square projecting window looking out onto broad gravel and grass sloping toward a little brook entering the pool. The top of a low black cabinet, the old oak table, and the chairs in tawny leather were littered with the children's toys, books, and garden garments, at which a maternal lady in pastel looked down from the walls with smiling indulgence. The children were all there. The three girls, seated round their mother near the window, were miniature portraits of her—dark-eyed, delicate-featured brunettes with a rich bloom on their cheeks, their little nostrils and eyebrows singularly finished as if they were tiny women, the eldest being barely nine. The boy sat on the carpet at some distance, bending his blonde head over the animals from a Noah's ark, admonishing them separately in a voice of threatening command, and occasionally licking the spotted ones to see if the colors would hold. Josephine, the eldest, was having her French lesson; the others, with dolls on their laps, sat demurely enough for images of the Madonna. Mrs. Glasher's toilet had been made very carefully—each day now she told herself that Grandcourt might come. Her head, which, despite emaciation, possessed an ineffaceable beauty in the fine profile, crisp curves of hair, and clearly marked eyebrows, rose impressively above her bronze-colored silk and velvet, and the gold necklace which Grandcourt had first clasped round her neck years ago. Not that she derived pleasure from her toilet; her chief thought of herself seen in the glass was, "How changed!"—but such good in life as remained to her she would keep. If her chief wish were fulfilled, she could imagine herself attaining the comeliness of a matron fit for the highest rank. The little faces beside her, almost exact reductions of her own, seemed to tell of the blooming curves which had once been where now was sunken pallor. But the children kissed the pale cheeks and never found them deficient. That love was now the one end of her life.

Suddenly, Mrs. Glasher turned her head from Josephine's book and listened. "Hush, dear! I think someone is coming."

Henleigh, the boy, jumped up. "Mamma, is it the miller with my donkey?"

He received no answer and, going up to his mother's knee, repeated his question in an insistent tone. But the door opened, and the servant announced Mr. Grandcourt. Mrs. Glasher rose in some agitation. Henleigh frowned at him in disgust at his not being the miller, and the three little girls lifted their dark eyes to him timidly. None of them had any particular liking for this friend of mamma's—in fact, when he had taken Mrs. Glasher's hand and then turned to put his other hand on Henleigh's head, that energetic scion began beating the friend's arm away with his fists. The little girls submitted bashfully to be patted under the chin and kissed, but on the whole, it seemed better to send them into the garden, where they were presently dancing and chatting with the dogs on the gravel.

"How far are you come?" said Mrs. Glasher as Grandcourt put away his hat and overcoat.

"From Diplow," he answered slowly, seating himself opposite her and looking at her with an unnoting gaze which she noted.

"You are tired, then."

"No, I rested at the Junction—a hideous hole. These railway journeys are always a confounded bore. But I had coffee and smoked."

Grandcourt drew out his handkerchief, rubbed his face, and in returning it to his pocket looked at his crossed knee and blameless boot, as if any stranger were opposite him, instead of a woman quivering with a suspense which every word and look of his was to incline toward hope or dread. But he was really occupied with their interview and what it was likely to include. Imagine the difference in the rate of emotion between this woman whom the years had worn to a more conscious dependence and sharper eagerness, and this man whom they were dulling into a more neutral obstinacy.

"I expected to see you—it was so long since I had heard from you. I suppose the weeks seem longer

"A wild dedication of yourselves
To unpath'd waters, undreamed shores."

The morning of Gwendolen Harleth's wedding to Mr. Grandcourt was crisp and clear, the low sun casting a frost that curled the edges of the leaves. The procession moving toward the church was a spectacle in itself, drawing the entire village of Pennicote to line the path. An old friend of the rector officiated, while the rector himself stepped forward to act as the bride's father—a role that added a certain gravity to the proceedings. Yet, amidst the general rejoicing, two faces stood out in their sorrow: Mrs. Davilow's and Anna's. The mother's eyes were rimmed with pink, betraying tears shed throughout the night, a natural grief for the daughter who was the pride of her life. Anna's distress, however, puzzled the onlookers; she was dressed as a bridesmaid in splendor, yet her spirit seemed heavy.

The villagers, mostly, reflected the brilliance of the occasion, especially the bride. Gwendolen possessed the figure and carriage of a true "lady o' title." Her face, perhaps, lacked the rosy bloom one might expect of a titled woman, but the groom, noted for his pale complexion—much like the miller's wife's husband—made the match seem perfectly balanced in the public eye. They whispered that he must love her dearly, hoping he would never hold against her her humble past as a governess or her mother's modest cottage life. Even the fourteen-year-old miller's daughter, raised on tales of gentry cruelty, couldn't believe such high-born men treated their wives poorly, until her mother corrected her with a grim anecdote about a squire who whipped his wife in her own room.

"That's unlucky talk for a wedding, Mrs. Girdle," the tailor interjected. "A quarrel may end with the whip, but it starts with the tongue, and it's the women who carry the weight of that."

"The Lord gave it to them to use," Mrs. Girdle replied. "He never intended for you to have it all your own way."

The conversation drifted to Mr. Grandcourt's reputation. The groomer from Offendene had mentioned that the gentleman spoke with "wonderful little tongue," expecting everything to be done silently and obediently.

"Then he's the more whip, I suspect," Mrs. Girdle murmured. "She's got tongue enough, I warrant. Look, there they come!"

Gwendolen, radiant in her trousseau, displayed an elasticity of bearing and a lustre in her brown eyes that suggested the thrill of strong excitement. She was not feeling pain, but a feverish exhilaration akin to standing at a gambling table with a full house watching. She was beginning to lose, yet she enjoyed the risk. Any nagging unease from her conscience was dismissed as a minor ailment amidst the gratification of her ambition and desire for luxury. She did not truly repent accepting Grandcourt; fears of the future were drowned out by the glowing reality of the moment, where she was the central object of admiration.

Beneath the surface, however, a storm raged. She knew she was doing wrong. She knew a punishment might be hanging over her. She knew the woman she had betrayed was suffering in bitterness, and that Daniel Deronda likely despised her now as he had despised her for gambling. She felt the cord that once bound her to her lover being flung around her neck. This mixture of dim facts and vivid, terrifying images had plagued her during the engagement. But this morning, that agitation was surmounted by a defiant exultation. She felt herself entering a game of life with many eyes upon her, daring everything to win, or if she lost, to lose with éclat. She believed she was gaining the official strength of marriage to manage her circumstances, intoxicated by a renewed egoism. She walked among illusions with an erect head and elastic step, unaware that she was already slightly drunk on the dream.

"Thank God you bear it so well, my darling!" Mrs. Davilow cried, helping her remove the bridal white for her traveling dress. The mother trembled, her agitation urging Gwendolen to view the morning as a triumph.

"Why, you might have said that if I were going to Mrs. Mompert's, you dear, sad, incorrigible mamma!" Gwendolen laughed, pressing her hands to her mother's cheeks before spreading her arms to display herself. "Here am I—Mrs. Grandcourt! What else would you have me?"

"Hush, my child!" Mrs. Davilow whispered. "How can I help feeling it? But I can bear anything gladly if you are happy."

"Not gladly, mamma, no!" Gwendolen shook her head, smiling brightly. "Willingly you would bear it, but always sorrowfully. Sorrowing is your sauce; you can take nothing without it." She kissed her mother's cheeks profusely. "And you shall sorrow over my having everything at my beck—splendid houses, horses, diamonds! I shall have diamonds! Going to court, being Lady Certainly, and grand everywhere, yet always loving you better than anyone else."

"My sweet child! But I shall not be jealous if you love your husband better; he will expect to be first."

Gwendolen made a pretty grimace. "Rather a ridiculous expectation. However, I don't mean to treat him ill, unless he deserves it."

They embraced, and Gwendolen could not stop a rising sob as she wished her mother were coming with her. The dew on her long eyelashes only enhanced her charm as she handed her hand to Grandcourt to lead her to the carriage.

The rector stopped to offer a final blessing, telling Mrs. Davilow that Gwendolen was in a position well suited to her, chosen entirely for her own sake, and that she should feel proud.

***

The journey to Ryelands took an hour by rail. By the time they reached the station, the sky had darkened to twilight. As they drove through the park, Gwendolen gazed out the window at the grand outlines of the estate—the winding drives, the vast grassy spaces, and finally, the white house framed by a hanging wood.

She had been lively during the train ride, chatting incessantly, ignoring the shift in their dynamic. Grandcourt sat quietly, almost ecstatically so, while she seized his hand with both of hers, her vivacity that of a kitten refusing to be petted. She was becoming febrile with excitement. The changing light and scenery made her heart palpitate. Was it the novelty, or the fulfillment of her girlish dreams of being "somebody"? Walking through corridors of her own design, under ceilings of impossible height, where her painted Spring shed flowers and her Zephyrs blew trumpets? Where her servants were mere lackeys in her presence? Was it the closeness of this dream realized, or a dim, insistent dread mixing with her triumph? Hers was a nature where exultation inevitably carried an infusion of dread ready to curdle.

As they approached the gates, she fell silent. When Grandcourt said, "Here we are at home!" and kissed her on the lips, she barely registered it. It was merely the passive acceptance of a greeting amidst an absorbing show. After the half-willful excitement of the day, a numbness had settled over her.

The hall was brilliant with light, warmth, matting, carpets, and statues. Servants moved assiduously, though there were few of them. Gwendolen was led by Grandcourt down a subtly scented corridor into an ante-room, where an open doorway sent out a rich glow.

"These are our dens," Grandcourt said. "You will like to be quiet here till dinner. We shall dine early."

He pressed her hand to his lips and moved away, more in love than he had ever expected to be.

Gwendolen yielded her hat and mantle, threw herself into a chair by the glowing hearth, and saw her reflection in the glass panels, surrounded by her faint-green satin. The housekeeper lingered, seemingly eager to admire the new mistress, but Gwendolen, impatient for solitude, dismissed her maid.

"Will you tell Hudson when she has put out my dress to leave everything? I shall not want her again, unless I ring."

The housekeeper stepped forward. "Here is a packet, madam, which I was ordered to give into nobody's hands but yours, when you were alone. The person who brought it said it was a present particularly ordered by Mr. Grandcourt; but he was not to know of its arrival till he saw you wear it. Excuse me, madam; I felt it right to obey orders."

Gwendolen took the packet, letting it rest on her lap until the doors closed behind the housekeeper. It occurred to her that the packet might contain the diamonds Grandcourt had promised. In this moment of confused feeling and creeping luxurious languor, she was glad of the diversion—glad to try on her own diamonds.

Inside the sealed paper was a box, and inside the box, a jewel-case. Now she felt no doubt she held the diamonds. But the instant she opened the case and saw them gleam, she saw a letter lying atop them. She recognized the handwriting immediately. It was as if an adder had coiled upon the jewels. Her heart gave a leap that seemed to drain her strength; her hands trembled as she unfolded the thin paper.

*These diamonds, which were once given with ardent love to Lydia Glasher, she passes on to you. You have broken your word to her, that you might possess what was hers. Perhaps you think of being happy, as she once was, and of having beautiful children such as hers, who will thrust hers aside. God is too just for that. The man you have married has a withered heart. His best young love was mine: you could not take that from me when you took the rest. It is dead: but I am the grave in which your chance of happiness is buried as well as mine. You had your warning. You have chosen to injure me and my children. He had meant to marry me. He would have married me at last, if you had not broken your word. You will have your punishment. I desire it with all my soul.*

*Will you give him this letter to set him against me and ruin us more—me and my children? Shall you like to stand before your husband with these diamonds on you, and these words of mine in his thoughts and yours? Will he think you have any right to complain when he has made you miserable? You took him with your eyes open. The willing wrong you have done me will be your curse.*

At first, Gwendolen's eyes seemed spellbound, reading the horrible words over and over as a doom of penance. Suddenly, a new spasm of terror made her lean forward, stretching the paper toward the fire, lest accusation and proof meet all eyes. The letter flew like a feather from her trembling fingers, caught instantly in a great draft of flame. In her movement, the casket fell to the floor, and the diamonds scattered. She paid no attention, collapsing back into her chair, helpless. She could not see her reflection anymore; the glass showed only women petrified white. Coming near, one might have seen the tremor in her lips and hands. She sat thus for a long while, knowing little more than that she felt ill, and that those written words kept repeating themselves in her mind.

Truly, here were poisoned gems, and the poison had entered this poor young creature.

Finally, there was a tap at the door. Grandcourt entered, dressed for dinner. The sight of him brought a fresh, violent shock. Gwendolen screamed again and again with hysterical violence. He had expected to find her dressed and smiling, ready to be led down. Instead, he found her pallid, shrieking as if with terror, the jewels scattered around her on the floor. Was it a fit of madness?

In some form or other, the furies had crossed his threshold.

In every age, it has been a favored notion that a powerful love possesses the nature of an isolated fate, to which the mind's opinions and accustomed resolutions are entirely alien. Consider Daphnis's frenzy, where conviction of Heraclitus's doctrines would have been useless; or Tristan's potion-born passion, where even the depth of Duns Scotus could not have saved his reasoning from the cup's corruption; or Romeo's sudden taking of Juliet, where objections drawn from Ptolemy would have made little difference beneath the balcony. Yet not all love is thus severed from reason. Nay, this passion has as vast a scope as any for allying itself with every operation of the soul; it acknowledges effects from the imagined light of unproven firmaments and sets its scale to the grander orbits of what has been and shall be.

Upon his return to town, Deronda could assure Sir Hugo that he had lodged in Grandcourt's mind a distinct understanding: that fifty thousand pounds could be obtained by surrendering a prospect that was probably distant and not absolutely certain. Yet, regarding Grandcourt's disposition, Deronda saw no further sign than an evident inclination to maintain friendly communications.

"And what did you think of the future bride on a nearer survey?" asked Sir Hugo.

"I thought better of her than I did in Leubronn. Roulette was not a good setting for her; it brought out something of the demon. At Diplow she seemed much more womanly and attractive—less hard and self-possessed. I thought her mouth and eyes had quite a different expression."

"Don't flirt with her too much, Dan," said Sir Hugo, meaning to be agreeably playful. "If you make Grandcourt savage when they come to the Abbey at Christmas, it will interfere with my affairs."

"I can stay in town, sir."

"No, no. Lady Mallinger and the children can't do without you at Christmas. Only don't make mischief—unless you can get up a duel, and manage to shoot Grandcourt, which might be worth a little inconvenience."

"I don't think you ever saw me flirt," said Deronda, not amused.

"Oh, haven't I, though?" said Sir Hugo, provokingly. "You are always looking tenderly at the women, and talking to them in a Jesuitical way. You are a dangerous young fellow—a kind of Lovelace who will make the Clarissas run after you instead of you running after them."

What was the use of being exasperated at a tasteless joke? Only the exasperation comes before the reflection on utility. Few friendly remarks are more annoying than the information that we are always seeming to do what we never mean to do. Sir Hugo's notion of flirting, it was to be hoped, was rather peculiar; for his own part, Deronda was sure that he had never flirted. But he was glad that the baronet had no knowledge about the repurchase of Gwendolen's necklace to feed his taste for this kind of rallying.

He would be on his guard in future; for example, in his behavior at Mrs. Meyrick's, where he was about to pay his first visit since his arrival from Leubronn. For Mirah was certainly a creature in whom it was difficult not to show a tender kind of interest both by looks and speech.

Mrs. Meyrick had not failed to send Deronda a report of Mirah's well-being in her family. "We are getting fonder of her every day," she had written. "At breakfast-time we all look toward the door with expectation to see her come in; and we watch her and listen to her as if she were a native from a new country. I have not heard a word from her lips that gives me a doubt about her. She is quite contented and full of gratitude. My daughters are learning from her, and they hope to get her other pupils; for she is anxious not to eat the bread of idleness, but to work, like my girls. Mab says our life has become like a fairy tale, and all she is afraid of is that Mirah will turn into a nightingale again and fly away from us. Her voice is just perfect: not loud and strong, but searching and melting, like the thoughts of what has been. That is the way old people like me feel a beautiful voice."

But Mrs. Meyrick did not enter into particulars which would have required her to say that Amy and Mab, who had accompanied Mirah to the synagogue, found the Jewish faith less reconcilable with their wishes in her case than in that of Scott's Rebecca. They kept silence out of delicacy to Mirah, with whom her religion was too tender a subject to be touched lightly; but after a while Amy, who was much of a practical reformer, could not restrain a question.

"Excuse me, Mirah, but *does* it seem quite right to you that the women should sit behind rails in a gallery apart?"

"Yes, I never thought of anything else," said Mirah, with mild surprise.

"And you like better to see the men with their hats on?" said Mab, cautiously proposing the smallest item of difference.

"Oh, yes. I like what I have always seen there, because it brings back to me the same feelings—the feelings I would not part with for anything else in the world."

After this, any criticism, whether of doctrine or practice, would have seemed to these generous little people an inhospitable cruelty. Mirah's religion was of one fibre with her affections, and had never presented itself to her as a set of propositions.

"She says herself she is a very bad Jewess, and does not half know her people's religion," said Amy, when Mirah was gone to bed. "Perhaps it would gradually melt away from her, and she would pass into Christianity like the rest of the world, if she got to love us very much, and never found her mother. It is so strange to be of the Jews' religion now."

"Oh, oh, oh!" cried Mab. "I wish I were not such a hideous Christian. How can an ugly Christian, who is always dropping her work, convert a beautiful Jewess, who has not a fault?"

"It may be wicked of me," said shrewd Kate, "but I cannot help wishing that her mother may not be found. There might be something unpleasant."

"I don't think it, my dear," said Mrs. Meyrick. "I believe Mirah is cut out after the pattern of her mother. And what a joy it would be to her to have such a daughter brought back again! But a mother's feelings are not worth reckoning, I suppose" (she shot a mischievous glance at her own daughters), "and a dead mother is worth more than a living one?"

"Well, and so she may be, little mother," said Kate; "but we would rather hold you cheaper, and have you alive."

Not only the Meyricks, whose various knowledge had been acquired by the irregular foraging to which clever girls have usually been reduced, but Deronda himself, with all his masculine instruction, had been roused by this apparition of Mirah to the consciousness of knowing hardly anything about modern Judaism or the inner Jewish history. The Chosen People have been commonly treated as a people chosen for the sake of somebody else; and their thinking as something (no matter exactly what) that ought to have been entirely otherwise; and Deronda, like his neighbors, had regarded Judaism as a sort of eccentric fossilized form which an accomplished man might dispense with studying, and leave to specialists.
