Experience had rendered him morbidly alive to how poverty and physical disadvantage cheapen ideas, unless one is a Peter the Hermit ringing a tocsin for the rabble. Yet, he was too sane and generous to attribute his spiritual banishment solely to the excusable prejudices of others; certain incapacities of his own had sealed the sentence of exclusion. Thus, his imagination constructed another man, something more ample than the "second soul" bestowed by Cabalistic notions to help an insufficient first. This would be a blooming human life, ready to incorporate all that was worthiest in an existence whose visible part was burning itself fast away. His inward need for this expanded, prolonged self was reflected as an outward necessity. The thoughts of his heart, that ancient phrase best shadows the truth, seemed too precious, too closely interwoven with the growth of things to lack a further destiny. As the more beautiful, stronger, more executive self took shape in his mind, he loved it beforehand with an affection half identifying, half contemplative and grateful.

Mordecai's mind wrought so constantly in images that his coherent trains of thought often resembled the significant dreams attributed to sleepers by inventors in their waking moments. Nay, they often resembled genuine dreams in their way of breaking off the passage from the known to the unknown. For a long while, he habitually thought of the Being answering to his need as one distantly approaching or turning his back, darkly painted against a golden sky. The reason for the golden sky lay in one of Mordecai's habits. He was keenly alive to the poetic aspects of London; a favorite resort was the bridges, especially at sunrise or sunset. Even when bending over watch-wheels or seated in a small upper room facing dingy bricks and cracked windows, his imagination spontaneously planted him on a spot with a far-stretching scene. His thoughts moved in wide spaces, and whenever possible, he sought the influences of a large sky. Leaning on the parapet of Blackfriars Bridge, gazing meditatively, the breadth and calm of the river, its long vista half hazy, half luminous, the grand dim masses of tall buildings signaling world-commerce, and the oncoming boats from the distance entered his mood. They blended indistinguishably with his thinking, as a fine symphony makes a medium that bears up our spiritual wings. Thus, the figure representative of Mordecai's longing was mentally seen darkened by the excess of light in the aerial background.

But in the inevitable progress of his imagination toward fuller detail, he ceased to see the figure with its back turned. It began to advance, and a face became discernible. The words youth, beauty, refinement, Jewish birth, and noble gravity turned into hardly individual but typical form and color, gathered from his memory of faces seen among the Jews of Holland and Bohemia, and from paintings that revived that memory. Reverently, it must be said of this mature spiritual need that it was akin to the boy's and girl's picturing of the future beloved. But the stirrings of such young desire are feeble compared with the passionate current of an ideal life straining to embody itself, made intense by resistance to imminent dissolution. The visionary form became a companion and auditor, keeping a place not only in the waking imagination but in those dreams of lighter slumber where it is truest to say, "I sleep, but my heart waketh"—when the disturbing trivial story of yesterday is charged with the impassioned purpose of years.

Of late, the urgency of irremediable time, measured by the gradual choking of life, had turned Mordecai's trust into an agitated watch for the fulfillment that must be at hand. Was the bell on the verge of tolling, the sentence about to be executed? The deliverer's footstep must be near—the one who would rescue Mordecai's spiritual travail from oblivion and give it an abiding-place in the best heritage of his people. An insane exaggeration of his own value, even if his ideas were as true and precious as those of Columbus or Newton, many would count this yearning as the sublimer part for a man to say, "If not I, then another," and hold cheap the meaning of his own life. But the fuller nature desires to be an agent, to create, and not merely to look on. Strong love hungers to bless, not merely to behold blessing. And while there is warmth enough in the sun to feed an energetic life, there will still be men to feel, "I am lord of this moment's change, and will charge it with my soul."

Yet, with that mingling of inconsequence that belongs to us all, saving us from many effects of mistake, Mordecai's confidence in the friend to come did not suffice to make him passive. He tried expedients, pathetically humble, within his reach, to communicate something of himself. It had been two years since he took up his abode under Ezra Cohen's roof, where he was regarded with much goodwill as a compound of workman, dominie, vessel of charity, inspired idiot, man of piety, and (if inquired into) dangerous heretic. During that time, little Jacob had advanced into knickerbockers and into that quickness of apprehension already manifest regarding hardware and exchange. Jacob had also advanced in attachment to Mordecai, regarding him as an inferior but liking him none the worse, taking his helpful cleverness as one might take the services of an enslaved Djinn.

As for Mordecai, he had given Jacob his first lessons, and his habitual tenderness easily turned into the teacher's fatherhood. Though fully conscious of the spiritual distance between himself and the parents, and never attempting communication from his peculiar world, the boy moved him with an idealizing affection that merged the qualities of the individual child in the glory of childhood and the possibilities of a long future. This feeling drew him on, first without premeditation and afterward with conscious purpose, to a sort of outpouring in the ear of the boy that might have seemed wild to any excellent man of business who overheard it. But none overheard when Jacob went up to Mordecai's room one day, for example, when there was little work to be done or when the work was ended. After a brief lesson in English reading or numeration, Jacob was induced to remain standing at his teacher's knees, or chose to jump astride them, often to the patient fatigue of the wasted limbs. The inducement was perhaps the mending of a toy or some mechanical device in which Mordecai's well-practiced fingertips had exceptional skill. With the boy thus tethered, Mordecai would begin to repeat a Hebrew poem of his own, into which years before he had poured his first youthful ardors for that conception of a blended past and future which was the mistress of his soul, telling Jacob to say the words after him.

"The boy will get them engraved within him," thought Mordecai; "it is a way of printing."

None were readier than Jacob at this fascinating game of imitating unintelligible words. If no opposing diversion occurred, he would sometimes carry on his share as long as the teacher's breath would last. For Mordecai threw into each repetition the fervor befitting a sacred occasion. In such instances, Jacob showed no other distraction than reaching out to survey the contents of his pockets, or drawing down the skin of his cheeks to make his eyes look awful, rolling his head to complete the effect, or alternately handling his own nose and Mordecai's as if to test the relation of their masses. Under all this, the fervid reciter would not pause, satisfied if the young organs of speech would submit themselves. But most commonly, a sudden impulse sent Jacob leaping away into some antic or active amusement. Instead of following the recitation, he would return upon the foregoing words most ready to his tongue, mouthing or gabbling, with a see-saw suited to the action of his limbs, a verse on which Mordecai had spent some of his too scanty heart's blood. Yet he waited with such patience as a prophet needs, and began his strange printing again undiscouraged on the morrow, saying inwardly, "My words may rule him some day. Their meaning may flash out on him. It is so with a nation—after many days."

Meanwhile, Jacob's sense of power was increased and his time enlivened by a store of magical articulation with which he made the baby crow, drove the large cat into a dark corner, or promised himself to frighten any incidental Christian of his own years. One week he had unfortunately seen a street mountebank, and this carried off his muscular imitativeness in sad divergence from New Hebrew poetry, after the model of Jehuda ha-Levi. Mordecai had arrived at a fresh passage in his poem; for as soon as Jacob had got well used to one portion, he was led on to another, and a fresh combination of sounds generally answered better in keeping him fast for a few minutes. The consumptive voice, generally a strong high baritone with variously mingling hoarseness, like a haze amidst illuminations, and its occasional incipient gasp, had more than the usual excitement while giving forth Hebrew verses with a meaning something like this:

"Away from me the garment of forgetfulness.
Withering the heart;
The oil and wine from presses of the Goyim,
Poisoned with scorn.
Solitude is on the sides of Mount Nebo,
In its heart a tomb:
There the buried ark and golden cherubim
Make hidden light:
There the solemn gaze unchanged,
The wings are spread unbroken:
Shut beneath in silent awful speech
The Law lies graven.
Solitude and darkness are my covering,
And my heart a tomb;
Smite and shatter it, O Gabriel!
Shatter it as the clay of the founder
Around the golden image."

In the absorbing enthusiasm with which Mordecai had intoned rather than spoken this last invocation, he was unconscious that Jacob had ceased to follow him and had started away from his knees. But pausing, he saw, as by a sudden flash, that the lad had thrown himself on his hands with his feet in the air, mountebank fashion, and was picking up with his lips

"Before the wise ones stand,
Safe is it in all cases!
If you long torment yourself,
He knows exactly where you lack.
Also, you may hope for applause;
For he knows where you have hit the mark."

—GOETHE: *West-östlicher Divan*.

Momentous things befell Deronda that very evening following his visit to the modest Chelsea house, concerning the discussion of Mirah's public name. Yet, for the family gathered there, the most significant sequence of events unfolded two days later. Around four o'clock, the wheels of a carriage paused before the door, followed by a knock accompanied by a ring—a sound that serves to magnify the sense of social existence in a neighborhood where the most lively signals usually belong to the muffin-man. All the girls were present, and the two rooms had been merged to accommodate Kate's drawing and a vast length of embroidery that had replaced the satin cushions—a sort of *pièce de résistance* in their needlework, undertaken by any clever fingers available. It stretched across the front room with picturesque effect: Mrs. Meyrick bent over it at one corner, Mab in the middle, and Amy at the far end. Mirah, whose sewing skills hovered at the makeshift level of the tailor-bird, her education in that art having been largely neglected, acted as reader to the party, seated on a camp-stool; in this position, she also served as Kate's model for a title-page vignette, symbolizing a fair public absorbed in the successive volumes of the family tea-table. She was reading aloud with charming distinctness the delightful essay of Elia, "The Praise of Chimney-Sweeps," and all were smiling over the "innocent blackness" until the imposing knock and ring called their thoughts to loftier spheres, and they looked up in wonderment.

"My goodness!" exclaimed Mrs. Meyrick. "Could it be Lady Mallinger? Is there a grand carriage, Amy?"

"No—only a hansom cab. It must be a gentleman."

"The Prime Minister, I should think," said Kate dryly. "Hans says the greatest man in London may get into a hansom cab."

"Oh, oh, oh!" cried Mab. "Suppose it should be Lord Russell!"

The five bright faces were all looking amused when the old maid-servant, bringing in a card, distractedly left the parlor-door open, revealing a figure quite unlike that of the respected Premier—tall and physically impressive even in his kid and kerseymere, with a massive face, flamboyant hair, and gold spectacles: in fact, as Mrs. Meyrick saw from the card, Julius Klesmer.

Even embarrassment could hardly have made the "little mother" awkward, but quick in her perceptions, she was at once aware of the situation and felt well satisfied that the great personage had come to Mirah instead of requiring her to come to him, taking it as a sign of active interest. But when he entered, the rooms seemed to shrink into closets; the cottage piano, Mab thought, seemed a ridiculous toy, and the entire family existence appeared petty and private, like an establishment of mice in the Tuileries. Klesmer's personality, especially his way of glancing around, immediately suggested vast areas and a multitudinous audience, and probably they made the usual scenery of his consciousness, for we all carry on our thinking in some habitual locus where there is a presence of other souls, and those who take in a larger sweep than their neighbors are apt to seem mightily vain and affected. Klesmer was vain, but not more so than many contemporaries of heavy aspect, whose vanity leaps out and startles one like a spear out of a walking-stick; as to his carriage and gestures, these were as natural to him as the length of his fingers; and the rankest affectation he could have shown would have been to look diffident and demure. While his grandiose air made Mab feel herself a ridiculous toy to match the cottage piano, he was taking in the details around him with a keen and thoroughly kind sensibility. He remembered a home no larger than this on the outskirts of Bohemia; and in the figurative Bohemia too, he had had large acquaintance with the variety and romance which belong to small incomes. He addressed Mrs. Meyrick with the utmost deference.

"I hope I have not taken too great a freedom. Being in the neighborhood, I ventured to save time by calling. Our friend, Mr. Deronda, mentioned to me an understanding that I was to have the honor of becoming acquainted with a young lady here—Miss Lapidoth."

Klesmer had really discerned Mirah in the first moment of entering, but, with subtle politeness, he looked round bowingly at the three sisters as if he were uncertain which was the young lady in question.

"Those are my daughters: this is Miss Lapidoth," said Mrs. Meyrick, waving her hand toward Mirah.

"Ah," said Klesmer, in a tone of gratified expectation, turning a radiant smile and a deep bow to Mirah, who, instead of being in the least taken by surprise, had a calm pleasure in her face. She liked the look of Klesmer, feeling sure that he would scold her, like a great musician and a kind man.

"You will not object to beginning our acquaintance by singing to me," he added, aware that they would all be relieved by getting rid of preliminaries.

"I shall be very glad. It is good of you to be willing to listen to me," said Mirah, moving to the piano. "Shall I accompany myself?"

"By all means," said Klesmer, seating himself, at Mrs. Meyrick's invitation, where he could have a good view of the singer. The acute little mother would not have acknowledged the weakness, but she really said to herself, "He will like her singing better if he sees her."

All the feminine hearts except Mirah's were beating fast with anxiety, thinking Klesmer terrific as he sat with his listening frown on, and only daring to look at him furtively. If he did say anything severe, it would be so hard for them all. They could only comfort themselves with thinking that Prince Camaralzaman, who had heard the finest things, preferred Mirah's singing to any other; also, she appeared to be doing her very best, as if she were more instead of less at ease than usual.

The song she had chosen was a fine setting of some words selected from Leopardi's grand Ode to Italy:

"_O patria mia, vedo le mura e gli archi
E le colonne e i simulacri e l'erme
Torride degli avi nostri_,"

This was recitative; then followed,

"_Ma la gloria—non vedo_,"

a mournful melody, a rhythmic plaint. After this came a climax of devout triumph—passing from the subdued adoration of a happy Andante in the words,

"_Beatissimi voi.
Che offriste il petto alle nemiche lance
Per amor di costei che al sol vi diede_,"

to the joyous outburst of an exultant Allegro in,

"Oh viva, oh viva:
Beatissimi voi
Mentre nel monde si favelli o scriva."

When she had ended, Klesmer said after a moment,

"That is old Leo's music."

"Yes, he was my last master—at Vienna: so fierce and so good," said Mirah, with a melancholy smile. "He prophesied that my voice would not do for the stage. And he was right."

"Continue, if you please," said Klesmer, putting out his lips and shaking his long fingers, while he went on with a smothered articulation quite unintelligible to the audience.

The three girls detested him unanimously for not saying one word of praise. Mrs. Meyrick was a little alarmed.

Mirah, simply bent on doing what Klesmer desired and imagining that he would now like to hear her sing some German, went through Prince Radzivill's music to Gretchen's songs in the *Faust*, one after the other without any interrogatory pause. When she had finished, he rose and walked to the extremity of the small space at command, then walked back to the piano, where Mirah had risen from her seat and stood looking toward him with her little hands crossed before her, meekly awaiting judgment; then, with a sudden unknitting of his brow and with beaming eyes, he stretched out his hand and said abruptly, "Let us shake hands: you are a musician."

Mab felt herself beginning to cry, and all the three girls held Klesmer adorable. Mrs. Meyrick took a long breath.

But straightway the frown came again; the long hand, back uppermost, was stretched out in quite a different sense to touch with fingertip the back of Mirah's, and with protruded lip he said,

"Not for great tasks. No high roofs. We are no skylarks. We must be modest." Klesmer paused here. And Mab ceased to think him adorable: "as if Mirah had shown the least sign of conceit!"

Mirah was silent, knowing that there was a specific opinion to be waited for, and Klesmer presently went on—"I would not advise—I would not further your singing in any larger space than a private drawing-room. But you will do there. And here in London that is one of the best careers open. Lessons will follow. Will you come and sing at a private concert at my house on Wednesday?"

"Oh, I shall be grateful," said Mirah, putting her hands together devoutly. "I would rather get my bread in that way than by anything more public. I will try to improve. What should I work at most?"

Klesmer made a preliminary answer in noises which sounded like words bitten in two and swallowed before they were half out, shaking his fingers the while, before he said, quite distinctly, "I shall introduce you to Astorga: he is the foster-father of good singing and will give you advice." Then addressing Mrs. Meyrick, he added, "Mrs. Klesmer will call before Wednesday, with your permission."

"We shall feel that to be a great kindness," said Mrs. Meyrick.

"You will sing to her," said Klesmer, turning again to Mirah. "She is a thorough musician, and has a soul with more ears to it than you will often get in a musician. Your singing will satisfy her:

'Vor den Wissenden sich stellen;'

you know the rest?"

"'Sicher ist's in allen Fällen,'" said Mirah, promptly. And Klesmer saying "Schön!" put out his hand again as a good-by.

He had certainly chosen the most delicate way of praising Mirah, and the Meyrick girls had now given him all their esteem. But imagine Mab's feeling when suddenly fixing his eyes on her, he said decisively, "That young lady is musical, I see!" She was a mere blush and sense of scorching.

"Yes," said Mirah, on her behalf. "And she has a touch."

"Oh, please, Mirah—a scramble, not a touch," said Mab, in anguish, with a horrible fear of what the next thing might be: this dreadful divining personage—evidently Satan in gray trousers—might order her to sit down to the piano, and her heart was like molten wax in the midst of her. But this was cheap payment for her amazed joy when Klesmer said benignantly, turning to Mrs. Meyrick, "Will she like to accompany Miss Lapidoth and hear the music on Wednesday?"

"There could hardly be a greater pleasure for her," said Mrs. Meyrick. "She will be most glad and grateful."

Thereupon Klesmer bowed round to the three sisters more grandly than they had ever been bowed to before. Altogether it was an amusing picture—the little room with so much of its diagonal taken up in Klesmer's magnificent bend to the small feminine figures like images a little less than life-size, the grave Holbein faces on the walls, as many as were not otherwise occupied, looking hard at this stranger who, by his face, seemed a dignified contemporary of their own, but whose garments seemed a deplorable mockery of the human form.

Mrs. Meyrick could not help going out of the room with Klesmer and closing the door behind her. He understood her, and said with a frowning nod,

"She will do: if she doesn't attempt too much and her voice holds out, she can make an income. I know that is the great point: Deronda told me. You are taking care of her. She looks like a good girl."

"She is an angel," said the warm-hearted woman.

"No," said Klesmer, with a playful nod; "she is a pretty Jewess: the angels must not get the credit of her. But I think she has found a guardian angel," he ended, bowing himself out in this amiable way.

The four young creatures had looked at each other mutely till the door banged and Mrs. Meyrick re-entered. Then there was an explosion. Mab clapped her hands and danced everywhere inconveniently; Mrs. Meyrick kissed Mirah and blessed her; Amy said emphatically, "We can never get her a new dress before Wednesday!" and Kate exclaimed, "Thank heaven my table is not knocked over!"

Mirah had reseated herself on the music-stool without speaking, and the tears were rolling down her cheeks as she looked at her friends.

"Now, now, Mab!" said Mrs. Meyrick; "come and sit down reasonably and let us talk?"

"Yes, let us talk," said Mab, cordially, coming back to her low seat and caressing her knees. "I am beginning to feel large again. Hans said he was coming this afternoon. I wish he had been here—only there would have been no room for him. Mirah, what are you looking sad for?"

"I am too happy," said Mirah. "I feel so full of gratitude to you all; and he was so very kind."

"Yes, at last," said Mab, sharply. "But he might have said something encouraging sooner. I thought him dreadfully ugly when he sat frowning, and only said, 'Continue.' I hated him all the long way from the top of his hair

"Within the soul a faculty abides,
That with interpositions, which would hide
And darken, so can deal, that they become
Contingencies of pomp; and serve to exalt
Her native brightness, as the ample moon,
In the deep stillness of a summer even,
Rising behind a thick and lofty grove,
Burns, like an unconsuming fire of light,
In the green trees; and, kindling on all sides
Their leafy umbrage, turns the dusky veil
Into a substance glorious as her own,
Yea, with her own incorporated, by power
Capacious and serene."
—WORDSWORTH: *Excursion*, B. IV.

Daniel Deronda stepped out of the narrow Chelsea house with a restless spirit, craving physical exertion to burn off the heat rising in his chest. He intended to walk toward the city, but the sight of the Chelsea Stairs and the waiting boats instantly resolved his plan. He would avoid the passive confinement of a carriage; instead, he hailed a wherry and took an oar himself.

His destination was Ram's bookshop. He had arrived there the previous day too late for Mordecai's midday vigil and had been informed that the man invariably returned between five and six. Deronda felt a pressing need to deepen his acquaintance with this enigmatic inhabitant of the Cohen household. It was a prerequisite for redeeming his ring; he feared that their conversations would once again end abruptly, leaving him stranded like a recruit dismissed before the battle began. As he warmed to the rhythm of the oar, fixing his mind on the errand and his desire to aid Mirah, a shift occurred within him. He mentally stepped out of his own shoes and viewed the situation through Mordecai's eyes. He chided himself for resembling a recruiting sergeant who cares only for the numbers he gathers, ignoring the human drama that brings them.

"I suppose if I secure the information I seek," Deronda mused, "I shall be content if he offers nothing more of himself, or explains why he seems to expect something from me that has gone unfulfilled. My curiosity might fade, yet perhaps we have passed each other like ships in the night, each carrying an exile who would have recognized the other had they looked face to face. There is little chance of a special bond between us, for his voyage, I suspect, is nearly over. Still, I wonder if there is a profound mutual absence between strangers who exchange blank glances, or even those who long for one another's departure in a crowded room. Yet, by adopting the recruiting sergeant's strategy, one creates opportunities for such meetings."

As the wherry approached Blackfriars Bridge, where Deronda planned to land, the clock struck half-past four. The gray afternoon was dying gloriously; the western clouds had shattered into narrowing purple strata before a vast, saffron-clear sky that held a monumental calm. On the river, however, this light danced as a luminous movement—the flashing ripples, the sudden glow of a brown sail, the transition of laden barges from shadow to color—creating an active response to the brooding glory above.

Feeling sufficiently heated, Deronda set down the oar and pulled his Inverness cape around him. As he fastened the top button, his gaze lifted and caught a familiar face framed against the parapet of the bridge. The western light rendered it startlingly distinct, an illuminated emblem of bodily emaciation and spiritual urgency. It was Mordecai. The invalid had also spotted the approaching boat, holding his gaze fixed upon it. First, he watched simply because it was moving; then, a shiver of presentiment ran through him until the nearing figure raised its face toward him—the face of his visions. Immediately, Mordecai raised a white, trembling hand and beckoned repeatedly.

Deronda, eager for recognition, had signaled without delay, and the answer came instantly. Mordecai lifted his cap and waved it, feeling in that instant that his inner prophecy was fulfilled. Obstacles, contradictions, all dissolved into a sense of completion flooding his soul. His exultation mirrored that of an experimenter witnessing the first stirrings of a change he had foreseen in concentrated vision. The prefigured friend emerged from the golden background and signaled; this was reality; the rest was merely to be lived.

In three minutes, Deronda had landed, paid the boatman, and joined Mordecai, who stood perfectly still, waiting.

"I was very glad to see you standing here," Deronda said. "I intended to go to the bookshop and look for you again. I was there yesterday—perhaps they mentioned it?"

"Yes," Mordecai replied with simple gravity. "That was the reason I came to the bridge."

This answer struck Deronda as mysteriously cryptic. Were the peculiarities of this man truly linked to some form of mental alienation, as Cohen had hinted?

"You knew nothing of my being at Chelsea?" Deronda asked after a pause.

"No; but I expected you to come down the river. I have been waiting for you these five years." Mordecai's deep-sunk eyes were fixed on Deronda's with a look of affectionate dependence, both pathetic and solemn. Deronda's sensitivity responded fully, even though he could not help believing this strangely disclosed relationship was founded on an illusion.

"It will be a satisfaction to me if I can be of any real use to you," Deronda answered earnestly. "Shall we take a cab and drive wherever you wish? You have probably had enough walking with your short breath."

"Let us go to the bookshop. It will soon be time for me to be there. But now look up the river," Mordecai said, turning back toward the water. He spoke in undertones of an excited calm, so absorbed by a sense of fulfillment that he felt no barrier to a complete understanding with Deronda. "See the sky, how it is slowly fading. I have always loved this bridge; I stood on it when I was a little boy. It is a meeting-place for spiritual messengers. It is true what the Masters said—that each order of things has its angel: that means the full message of each from afar. Here I have listened to the messages of earth and sky. When I was stronger, I used to stay and watch for the stars in the deep heavens. But this time, just about sunset, was what I loved best. It sank into me and dwelt with me—fading, slowly fading: it was my own decline. It paused, it waited, till at last it brought me my new life—my new self, who will live when this breath is all breathed out."

Deronda remained silent, strangely affected. The first suspicion that Mordecai might be hallucinating, a monomaniac strained by a diseased organism, gave way to a more submissive expectancy. His nature was too broad, too ready to conceive regions beyond his own experience, to settle immediately on the easy explanation of "madness" whenever a consciousness showed fullness and conviction where his own was blank. It suited his disposition to meet rather than resist claims arising from another's need. This claim brought a sense of solemnity that seemed to radiate from Mordecai, nullifying his outward poverty and lifting him into authority, as if he were that preternatural guide from universal legend who suddenly drops his mean disguise to stand as a manifest Power. This impression was sanctioned by a resolved quietude in Mordecai's manner. After a moment of silence, he said, "Let us go now," and when they were in the cab, added, "We will get down at the end of the street and walk to the shop. You can look at the books, and Mr. Ram will be going away directly and leave us alone."

It seemed this enthusiast was just as cautious, just as alive to the judgments of others, as if he were the antipode of all enthusiasm, a "man of the world."

While they rattled along in the cab, Mirah was still present in Deronda's mind amidst this strange experience, but he foresaw that the conversation would be dictated by Mordecai, not himself. He was no longer confident of the questions he could ask. Reacting to his own mood, he thought inwardly, "I suppose I am in a state of complete superstition, awaiting a destiny that could interpret an oracle. Yet, some strong relation must exist between me and this man, since he feels it so strongly. Great heaven! What relation has proved more potent in the world than faith even when mistaken—than expectation even when perpetually disappointed? Is my side of the relation to be disappointing or fulfilling? Well, if it is ever possible for me to fulfill, I will not disappoint."

In ten minutes, the two men, with an intensity as if they were undeclared lovers, found themselves alone in the small, gas-lit bookshop. They turned face to face, each bare-headed from an instinctive desire to see the other fully. Mordecai leaned his back against the little counter, while Deronda stood against the opposite wall, hardly four feet away. One wishes one could perpetuate those two faces as Titian's "Tribute Money" has perpetuated two contrasting types. Imagine—the pathetic stamp of consumption, with its brilliance of glance, set upon a sharply defined structure of features that reminds one of a forsaken temple, giving a far-off look of one unwillingly withdrawing. Imagine it on a Jewish face, naturally accentuated for the expression of an eager mind—the face of a man little above thirty, yet bearing the age of time lengthened by suffering. His black hair and beard throw out the yellow pallor of his skin; his difficult breathing marks the mobile nostril; his wasted yellow hands are conspicuous on his folded arms. Give to this yearning consumptive glance the look of a slowly dying mother when her one loved son visits her bedside, and the flickering power of gladness leaps out as she says, "My boy!"—for the sense of spiritual perpetuation in another resembles that maternal transference of self. Seeing such a portrait, you would see Mordecai.

Opposite to him stood a face not more distinctly Oriental than many types seen among the Latin races. It was rich in youthful health, possessing a forcible masculine gravity in its repose that gave value to the judgment with which he met the gaze of this mysterious son of poverty who claimed him as a long-expected friend. The exquisite quality of Deronda's nature—that keenly perceptive sympathetic emotiveness running alongside his speculative tendency—was never more thoroughly tested. He felt nothing that could be called belief in the validity of Mordecai's impressions concerning him or in the probability of any greatly effective issue. What he felt was a profound sensibility to a cry from the depths of another, accompanied by a summons to be receptive rather than superciliously prejudging. Receptiveness is a rare and massive power, like fortitude; and this state of mind gave Deronda's face its utmost expression of calm, benignant force—an expression that nourished Mordecai's confidence and made an open way before him.

"You cannot know what has guided me to you and brought us together at this moment," Mordecai began. "You are wondering."

"I am not impatient," Deronda said. "I am ready to listen to whatever you may wish to disclose."

"You see some of the reasons why I needed you," Mordecai said quietly, as if reserving his strength. "You see that I am dying. You see that I am as one shut up behind bars by the wayside, who if he spoke to any would be met only by head-shaking and pity. The day is closing—the light is fading—soon we should not have been able to discern each other. But you have come in time."

"I rejoice that I am come in time," Deronda said feelingly. He would not say, "I hope you are not mistaken in me"; the very word "mistaken," he thought, would be a cruelty at that moment.

"But the hidden reasons why I need you began afar off," Mordecai continued. "They began in my early years when I was studying in another land. Then ideas, beloved ideas, came to me, because I was a Jew. They were a trust to fulfill, because I was a Jew. They were an inspiration, because I was a Jew, and felt the heart of my race beating within me. They were my life; I was not fully born till then. I counted this heart, and this breath, and this right hand"—Mordecai pathetically pressed his hand upon his breast, then stretched its wasted fingers out before him—"I counted my sleep and my waking, and the work I fed my body with, and the sights that fed my eyes—I counted them but as fuel to the divine flame. But I had done as one who wanders and engraves his thought in rocky solitudes, and before I could change my course came care and labor and disease, and blocked the way before me, and bound me with the iron that eats itself into the soul. Then I said, 'How shall I save the life within me from being stifled with this stifled breath?'"

Mordecai paused to rest that poor breath, taxed by the rising excitement of his speech. He also wished to check that excitement. Deronda dared not speak; the silence in the narrow space seemed alive with mingled awe and compassion before this struggling fervor. Presently, Mordecai went on:

"But you may misunderstand me. I speak not as an ignorant dreamer—as one bred up in the inland valleys, thinking ancient thoughts anew, and not knowing them ancient, never having stood by the great waters where the world's knowledge passes to and fro. English is my mother-tongue; England is the native land of this body, which is but as a breaking pot of earth around the fruit-bearing tree, whose seed might make the desert rejoice. But my true life was nourished in Holland at the feet of my mother's brother, a Rabbi skilled in special learning: and when he died I went to Hamburg to study, and afterwards to Göttingen, that I might take a larger outlook on my people, and on the Gentile world, and drank knowledge at all sources. I was a youth; I felt free; I saw our chief seats in Germany; I was not then in utter poverty. And I had possessed myself of a handicraft. For I said, I care not if my lot be as that of Joshua ben Chananja: after the last destruction he earned his bread by making needles

"It is a part of probability that many improbable things will happen."
—ARISTOTLE: *Poetics*.

The evening following his interview with Mordecai brought a storm of internal conflict to Daniel Deronda's mind. For a youth of ordinary sensibility, the encounter might have been merely a strange diversion, a curious episode to occupy his thoughts. But for Deronda, whose nature was built to feel intensely and to question actively, the event had struck a chord so deep that his intellect immediately recoiled, beginning a rigorous examination of his own emotions. He feared the danger of being swept away by Mordecai's energetic certainty and fervent trust. His characteristic instinct was to recoil from the moral blindness of undervaluing what had touched him so closely, of ignoring the sacred crisis unfolding in his own life because it did not fit the comfortable patterns of his daily existence.

He asked himself why he should feel ashamed of his agitation simply because he dressed for dinner in a white tie and moved in circles where seriousness was often mocked as foolish self-importance. Such cowardice before the obtuseness of fashion repelled him. Yet, he also feared letting his course be dictated by mere emotional contagion, allowing a reverential pity for spiritual struggle to rush him down a dimly seen path without the consent of reason.

What, truly, had occurred? He knew the answer Sir Hugo would give: a dying man, consumed by fanaticism, had latched onto Deronda as the living embodiment of a visionary hope—a desperate longing for the propagation of his beliefs. Such instances were not rare; fanaticism was abundant. While Mordecai waited on the bridge for his visions to fulfill, others believed they held the mathematical key to the universe or the metaphysical solution to all problems, convinced that the world conspired to silence them. These were scattered everywhere, men with glittering eyes or dull stares, searching for a listener. Mordecai possessed a more pathetic, poetic intensity than the typical monomaniac, yet he belonged to the same class. A man of the world would know how to handle such a figure: offer a little comfort, but remain skeptical. Mordecai's conviction that he had found a new executive self might only lead to the worst possible disappointment.

Deronda heard these negative whispers echoing in his mind, repeating them distinctly to himself. This was not the first time he had faced the question of the "family likeness" among the heirs of enthusiasm, whether prophets or dreamers. The kinship of human passion inevitably filled fact with burlesque and parody. Error and folly had their martyrs. If one reduced the greatest figures of history to an abstract statement of their qualities, they appeared in dangerous company alongside the inventors of perpetual motion. One could not fairly judge spirits by such tests. To avoid condemning the innocent, one needed a capacity to understand the subject-matter of the conviction and a fellowship with human toil that prevented any deep experience from being scanned lightly.

This train of thought was familiar to Deronda and saved him from contemptuous prejudice against Mordecai, even though the Jew's claim on him was strangely insistent. Rationally, such a claim might seem illusory, yet it transformed Mordecai's appeal from a request for sympathy into a grasp on Deronda's struggling conscience. Consciences are not uniform; they vary as widely as our memories. Deronda's conscience included sensibilities enlarged by his early habit of imagining himself into the experiences of others.

What claim did this eager soul make? "You must believe my beliefs—be moved by my reasons—hope my hopes!" To accept this as a direct obligation would be dishonest. Deronda felt thankful that amidst his compassion, he had preserved himself from the bondage of false concessions. Furthermore, the claim rested on a supposition—that Deronda was of Jewish blood—which might be entirely false. Was there ever a more hypothetical appeal?

Yet, since the age of thirteen, Deronda had associated his deepest emotional struggles with the pure supposition that Sir Hugo was his father. That hypothesis had been a source of passionate struggle, a light by which he subdued and cherished feelings. He was accustomed to finding motives in conceptions that might be disproved, and to considering revelations that might alter his view of duty. To live in a state of suspense, charged with emotive activity and scruple, was a familiar attitude for his conscience.

Now, suppose that wish-begotten belief in his Jewish birth and Mordecai's extravagant demand for discipleship were the foreshadowing of an actual discovery and a genuine spiritual result. Suppose Mordecai's ideas made a real conquest over Deronda's conviction? It was conceivable that as Mordecai found replenishment in his belief, Deronda might receive from the Jew's mind the complete ideal shape of the personal duty and citizenship that lay in his own thoughts like sculptured fragments, beautiful but untraceable.

As this possibility arose in his meditation, Deronda defended it. If the influence he imagined submitting to came from an honored professor or a philosopher accepted as a voice of the age, would thorough receptiveness be ridiculed? Only by those who consider being obliged for an idea a sign of weakness. Was there not vulgarity in taking the fact that Mordecai was a poor Jewish workman, perhaps met on a sanded floor in the parlor of the *Hand and Banner*, as proof that no spiritual force within him could affect a white-handed gentleman? There was a legend of Emperor Domitian, who, alarmed by news of a Jewish family destined to rule the world, summoned them only to release them upon seeing their workmen's hands. The Emperor was wrong; the Rabbi waiting at Rome's gate, confident the Messiah would be among the destitute, was also wrong in trusting outward signs. Poverty and poor clothes are no sign of inspiration, yet they have accompanied it in remarkable cases. To dismiss discipleship because of these signs was mere dullness of imagination.

A more plausible reason to reject discipleship was the strain of visionary excitement in Mordecai, which turned wishes into overmastering impressions. Was such a temper likely to accompany the wise estimate of consequences necessary to avoid fatal error? Yet, it remained to be seen if this rare conjunction existed. Perhaps Mordecai's nature fused a wise estimate with the fires of passionate belief, determining the very consequences he believed in. The inspirations of the world have often come this way; even strict science requires that forecasting ardor that feels the agitations of discovery beforehand. In relation to human motives, passionate belief has a fuller efficacy. Here, enthusiasm may possess the validity of proof, giving the type of what will one day be general.

At least, Deronda argued, Mordecai's excitability was hardly a reason to conclude beforehand that he was not worth listening to except for pity. Suppose he had introduced himself as a strict reasoner; did they form a body free from false conclusions? The driest argument has its hallucinations, too hastily concluding its net will hold the universe. Men dream in demonstrations, cutting out illusory worlds of axioms and definitions, excluding fact with a final *Q.E.D.* No formulas save mortals from mistake in their imperfect apprehension. Perhaps an emotional intellect has absorbed into its passionate vision some truth of what will be, the comprehensive massive life feeding theory with new material. Presumptions to the contrary were not to be trusted. One must be patient with the inevitable makeshift of human thinking. Columbus had impressions we call superstitions and arguments we disapprove, yet he possessed sound physical conceptions and the passionate patience of genius to make them tell on mankind. The world's contempt for those deaf to Columbus was a mistake.

"My contempt for them binds me to see that I don't adopt their mistake on a small scale," Deronda said to himself. "I must not make myself deaf with the assumption that there cannot be any momentous relation between this Jew and me, simply because he has clad it in illusory notions. What I can be to him, or he to me, may not at all depend on his persuasion about the way we came together. To me, the way seems made up of plainly discernible links. If I had not found Mirah, I probably should not have become specially interested in the Jews, nor gone on that loitering search after an Ezra Cohen which made me pause at Ram's book-shop and ask the price of *Maimon*. Mordecai, on his side, had his visions of a disciple, and he saw me by their light; I corresponded well enough with the image his longing had created. He took me for one of his race. Suppose that his impression—the elderly Jew at Frankfort seemed to have something like it—suppose, in spite of all presumptions, that his impression should somehow be proved true, and that I should actually share any of the ideas he is devoted to? This is the only question which really concerns the effect of our meeting on my life."

"But if the issue should be quite different?" Deronda continued, wavering. "Well, there will be something painful to go through. I shall almost inevitably have to be an active cause of that poor fellow's crushing disappointment. Perhaps this issue is the one I had need prepare myself for. I fear that no tenderness of mine can make his suffering lighter. Would the alternative—that I should not disappoint him—be less painful to me?"

Here Deronda hesitated. Feelings had lately worked within him, modifying the reluctance he would formerly have shown to consider himself possibly a Jew. He was romantic. That young energy and spirit of adventure which had created legions of youthful heroes seeking hidden tokens of their birth gave him a quivering interest in the bare possibility that he was entering on a like track—all the more because the track was one of thought as well as action.

"The bare possibility." He could not admit it to be more. The belief that his father was an Englishman only grew firmer under the weak assaults of unwarranted doubt. For Deronda to declare that he would be glad if that belief were declared a delusion was impossible. His life-long affection for Sir Hugo, stronger than all his resentment, made him shrink from admitting that wish.

Whichever way the truth might lie, he repeated to himself what he had said to Mordecai: he could not undertake to hasten its discovery without further reasons. Nay, he was tempted now to regard his uncertainty as a condition to be cherished for the present. If further intercourse revealed nothing but illusions, the want of any valid evidence that he was a Jew might save Mordecai the worst shock of refused fraternity. It might even be justifiable to use this uncertainty in keeping up a suspense that would induce Mordecai to accept those offices of friendship that Deronda longed to urge upon him.

These were the meditations that busied Deronda during the interval of four days before he could fulfill his promise to call for Mordecai at Ezra Cohen's, Sir Hugo's demands on him often lasting until an hour so late that the evening expedition to Holborn became impossible.

If there are ranks in suffering, Israel takes precedence of all nations; if the duration of sorrows and the patience with which they are borne ennoble, the Jews are among the aristocracy of every land. Deronda had lately read this passage of Zunz, and it struck him with a sharp contrast as he approached the Cohens' home. Ezra Cohen certainly bore no stamp of distinction in sorrow or any other form of nobility. Dressed not in the sublime pathos of the martyr, he seemed favored by a success in money-getting that had exasperated the greed of Jews throughout the ages of their dispersion. This Jeshurun of a pawnbroker was no symbol of the great Jewish tragedy, yet Deronda felt a strange typicality in the fact that a life like Mordecai's—a frail incorporation of the national consciousness, breathing with difficult breath—was nested within the self-gratulating ignorance of the Cohens' prosperity.

The gladness in their faces glistened when Deronda reappeared. Cohen himself hinted that while a diamond ring, or even a little longer, would have bred more money, he cared not a sixpence for it compared to the pleasure of seeing a young gentleman whose first visit had been so agreeable that the women and children had "done nothing but talk of it ever since." Young Mrs. Cohen expressed regret that the baby was asleep, then great joy that Adelaide was not yet in bed, entreating Deronda to leave the shop and come to the parlor to see "mother and the children." He gladly accepted, having prepared portable presents: a set of paper figures for Adelaide and an ivory cup and ball for Jacob.

The grandmother held a pack of cards, making "plates" with the children. A plate had just been thrown down and kept itself whole.

"Stop!" cried Jacob, running to Deronda as he entered. "Don't tread on my plate. Stop and see me throw it up again."

Deronda complied, exchanging a smile of understanding with the grandmother. The plate bore several tossings before it finally came to pieces, and only then was the visitor allowed to come forward and seat himself. He noted that the door from which Mordecai had issued on the previous visit was now closed, but he wished to show his interest in the Cohens before disclosing his stronger interest in their singular inmate.

It was not until he had Adelaide on his knee, setting up the paper figures in their dance on the table, while Jacob practiced with the cup and ball, that Deronda asked, "Is Mordecai in just now?"

"Where is he, Addy?" asked Cohen, seizing an interval of business to look on.

"In the workroom there," said his wife, nodding toward the closed door.

"The fact is, sir," said Cohen, "we don't know what's come to him these last days. He's always what I may call a little touched, you know"—here he pointed to his own forehead—"not quite so rational in all things, like you and me; but he's mostly wonderful regular and industrious so far as a poor creature can be, and takes as much delight in the boy as anybody could. But these last days he's been moving about like a sleep-walker, or else sitting as still as a wax figure."

"It's the disease, poor dear creature," said the grandmother, tenderly. "I doubt whether he can stand long against it."

"No; I think it's only something he's got in his head," said Mrs. Cohen. "He's been turning over writing continually, and when I speak to him it takes him ever so long to hear and answer."

"You may think us a little weak ourselves," said Cohen, apologetically. "But my wife and mother wouldn't part with him if he was a still worse incumbrance. It isn't that we don't know the long and short of matters, but it's our principle. There are fools who do business at a loss and don't know it. I'm not one of 'em."

"Oh, Mordecai carries a blessing inside him," said the grandmother.

"He's got something the matter inside him," corrected Jacob, coming up. "He said he couldn't talk to me, and he wouldn't have a bit of a bun."

"So far from wondering at your feeling for him," said Deronda, "I already feel something of the same sort myself. I have lately talked to him at Ram's book-shop—in fact, I promised to call for him here, that we might go out together."

"That's it, then!" said Cohen, slapping his knee. "He's been expecting you, and it's taken hold of him. I suppose he talks about his learning to you. It's uncommonly kind of you, sir; for I don't suppose there's much to be got out of it, else it wouldn't have left him where he is. But there's the shop." Cohen hurried out, and Jacob, who had been listening inconveniently near to Deronda's elbow, said with obliging familiarity, "I'll call Mordecai for you, if you like."

"No, Jacob," said his mother. "Open the door for the gentleman, and let him go in himself. Hush! Don't make a noise."

Skillful Jacob seemed to enter into the play, turning the handle of the door as noiselessly as possible, while Deronda went behind him and stood on the threshold. The small room was lit only by a dying fire and one candle with a shade over it. On the board fixed under the window, various objects of jewelry were scattered; some books were heaped in the corner beyond them. Mordecai was seated on a high chair at the board with his back to the door, his hands resting on each other and on the board, a watch propped on a stand before him. He was in a state of expectation as sickening as that of a prisoner listening for delayed deliverance—until he heard Deronda's voice saying, "I am come for you. Are you ready?"

Immediately he turned without speaking, seized his furred cap which lay near, and moved to join Deronda. It was but a moment before they were both in the sitting-room, and Jacob, noticing the change in his friend's air and expression, seized him by the arm and said, "See my cup and ball!" sending the ball up close to Mordecai's face, as something likely to cheer a convalescent. It was a sign of the relieved tension in Mordecai's mind that he could smile and say, "Fine, fine!"

"You have forgotten your greatcoat and comforter," said young Mrs. Cohen, and he went back into the workroom to retrieve them.

"He's come to life again, do you see?" said Cohen, who had re-entered, speaking in an undertone. "I told you so: I'm mostly right." Then in his usual voice, "Well, sir, we mustn't detain you now, I suppose; but I hope this isn't the last time we shall see you."

"Shall you come again?" said Jacob, advancing. "See, I can catch the ball; I'll bet I catch it without stopping, if you come again."

"He has clever hands," said Deronda, looking at the grandmother. "Which side of the family does he get them from?"

But the grandmother only nodded toward her son, who said promptly, "My side. My wife's family are not in that line. But bless your soul! ours is a sort of cleverness as good as gutta percha; you can twist it which way you like. There's nothing some old gentlemen won't do if you set 'em to it." Here Cohen winked down at Jacob's back, but it was doubtful whether this judicious allusiveness answered its purpose, for its subject gave a nasal whinnying laugh and stamped about singing, "Old gentlemen, old gentlemen," in chiming cadence.

Deronda thought, "I shall never know anything decisive about these people until I ask Cohen pointblank whether he lost a sister named Mirah when she was six years old." The decisive moment did not yet seem easy for him to face. Still, his first sense of repulsion at the commonness of these people was beginning to be tempered with kinder feeling. However unrefined their airs and speech might be, he was forced to admit some moral refinement in their treatment of the consumptive workman, whose mental distinction impressed them chiefly as a harmless, silent raving.

"The Cohens seem to have an affection for you," said Deronda, as soon as he and Mordecai were off the doorstep.

"And I for them," was the immediate answer. "They have the heart of the Israelite within them, though they are as the horse and the mule, without understanding beyond the narrow path they tread."

"I have caused you some uneasiness, I fear," said Deronda, "by my slowness in fulfilling my promise. I wished to come yesterday, but I found it impossible."

"Yes—yes, I trusted you. But it is true I have been uneasy, for the spirit of my youth has been stirred within me, and this body is not strong enough to bear the beating of its wings. I am as a man bound and imprisoned through long years: behold him brought to speech of his fellow and his limbs set free: he weeps, he totters, the joy within him threatens to break and overthrow the tabernacle of flesh."

"You must not speak too much in this evening air," said Deronda, feeling Mordecai's words of reliance like so many cords binding him painfully. "Cover your mouth with the woolen scarf. We are going to the *Hand and Banner*, I suppose, and shall be in private there?"

"No, that is my trouble that you did not come yesterday. For this is the evening of the club I spoke of, and we might not have any minutes alone until late, when all the rest are gone. Perhaps we had better seek another place. But I am used to that only. In new places the outer world presses on me and narrows the inward vision. And the people there are familiar with my face."

"I don't mind the club if I am allowed to go in," said Deronda. "It is enough that you like this place best. If we have not enough time I will come again. What sort of club is it?"

"It is called 'The Philosophers.' They are few—like the cedars of Lebanon—poor men given to thought. But none so poor as I am: and sometimes visitors of higher worldly rank have been brought. We are allowed to introduce a friend, who is interested in our topics. Each orders beer or some other kind of drink, in payment for the room. Most of them smoke. I have gone when I could, for there are other men of my race who come, and sometimes I have broken silence. I have pleased myself with a faint likeness between these poor philosophers and the Masters who handed down the thought of our race—the great Transmitters, who labored with their hands for scant bread, but preserved and enlarged for us the heritage of memory, and saved the soul of Israel alive as a seed among the tombs. The heart pleases itself with faint resemblances."

"I shall be very glad to go and sit among them, if that will suit you. It is a sort of meeting I should like to join in," said Deronda, not without relief in the prospect of an interval before he went through the strain of his next private conversation with Mordecai.

In three minutes they had opened the glazed door with the red curtain, and were in the little parlor, hardly much more than fifteen feet square, where the gaslight shone through a slight haze of smoke on what to Deronda was a new and striking scene. Half-a-dozen men of various ages, from between twenty and thirty to fifty, all shabbily dressed, most of them with clay pipes in their mouths, were listening with a look of concentrated intelligence to a man in a pepper-and-salt dress, with blonde hair, short nose, broad forehead and general breadth, who, holding his pipe slightly uplifted in the left hand, and beating his knee with the right, was just finishing a quotation from Shelley (the comparison of the avalanche in his "Prometheus Unbound"):

"As thought by thought is piled, till some great truth
Is loosened, and the nations echo round."

The entrance of the newcomers broke the fixity of attention, and called for a re-arrangement of seats in the too narrow semicircle round the fireplace and the table holding the glasses, spare pipes, and tobacco. This was the soberest of clubs; but sobriety was no reason why smoking and "taking something" should be less imperiously needed as a means of getting a decent status in company and debate. Mordecai was received with welcoming voices which had a slight cadence of compassion in them, but naturally all glances passed immediately to his companion.

"I have brought a friend who is interested in our subjects," said Mordecai. "He has traveled and studied much."

"Is the gentleman anonymous? Is he a Great Unknown?" said the broad-chested quoter of Shelley, with a humorous air.

"My name is Daniel Deronda. I am unknown, but not in any sense great." The smile breaking over the stranger's grave face as he said this was so agreeable that there was a general indistinct murmur, equivalent to a "Hear, hear," and the broad man said, "You recommend the name, sir, and are welcome. Here, Mordecai, come to this corner against me," he added, evidently wishing to give the coziest place to the one who most needed it.

Deronda was well satisfied to get a seat on the opposite side, where his general survey of the party easily included Mordecai, who remained an eminently striking object in this group of sharply-characterized figures, more than one of whom, even to Daniel's little exercised discrimination, seemed probably of Jewish descent.

In fact, pure English blood (if leech or lancet can furnish us with the precise product) did not declare itself predominantly in the party at present assembled. Miller, the broad man, an exceptional second-hand bookseller who knew the insides of books, had at least grandparents who called themselves German, and possibly far-away ancestors who denied themselves to be Jews; Buchan, the saddler, was Scotch; Pash, the watchmaker, was a small, dark, vivacious, triple-baked Jew; Gideon, the

"My spirit is too weak; mortality
Weighs heavily on me like unwilling sleep,
And each imagined pinnacle and steep
Of godlike hardship tells me I must die
Like a sick eagle looking at the sky."

For several minutes, an unusual silence settled over Mordecai's consciousness. He looked up at Deronda, not with confusion, but with a gaze heavy with peaceful satisfaction. Deronda rose and pulled his chair closer, ensuring his voice would carry without effort. To Mordecai, this gesture felt like the gentle easing of a pillow beneath a weary head. He began to speak in a low, intimate murmur, as if articulating thoughts meant only for himself.

"In the doctrine of the Cabbala, souls are reborn again and again in new bodies until they are perfected and purified," Mordecai said. "A soul freed from a worn-out body may unite with a fellow-soul that needs it, so they may be perfected together and complete their earthly work. Then they depart the mortal realm, leaving space for new souls to emerge from the eternal store. It is the lingering imperfection of souls already born that delays the birth of new ones and postpones the Messianic time. Thus, the mind gives shape to what is hidden, casting a shadow of the known and speaking truth through parable. When my wandering soul is finally liberated from this weary vessel, it will join yours, and our work will be finished."

Mordecai's pause hung in the air, an appeal Deronda could not ignore. He searched for words that were true, though for Mordecai's ear, they were inevitably filled with unspoken depths. "Everything I can do in conscience to make your life effective, I will do," Deronda promised.

"I know," Mordecai replied, his tone radiating a quiet certainty that rendered further reassurance unnecessary. "I heard you. You see it all—you stand by my side on the mountain of vision, beholding the paths of fulfillment that others deny."

Silence stretched between them before Mordecai continued, his voice meditative. "You will take up my life where it was broken. I feel myself transported back to that day. The bright morning sun was on the quay at Trieste; the garments of men from every nation shimmered like jewels; boats pushed off into the water. The Greek vessel bound for Beirut was to depart in an hour. I was traveling with a merchant as his clerk and companion. I told myself I would behold the lands and peoples of the East and speak with a fuller vision. I breathed then as you do now, without labor; I possessed the light step and endurance of youth. I could fast, sleep on hard ground, and I had wedded poverty, loving my bride—for poverty was my freedom. My heart exulted as if it belonged to Moses ben Maimon, strong with the vigor of sixty years, knowing the work that awaited him. It was my first time south; the soul within me felt its former sun. Standing on the quay, where the ground seemed to emit light and shadows wore an azure glory as if spirits were made visible, I felt myself swept into a flood of glorious life, where my small, counted existence melted away until I knew it no longer. A great sob rose within me, like the rush of waters too strong to be bliss. So I stood, awaiting my companion, and saw him only when he said, 'Ezra, I have been to the post; here is your letter.'"

"Ezra!" Deronda exclaimed, unable to hold back his shock.

"Ezra," Mordecai repeated, affirmatively, lost in memory. "I was expecting a letter; I wrote constantly to my mother. That sound of my name was like the touch of a wand that recalled me to the body from which I had been released to mingle with the ocean of human existence, free from the pressure of individual bondage. I opened the letter, and the name came again as a cry that would have disturbed me even in the bosom of heaven, making me yearn to reach that sorrow—'Ezra, my son!'"

Mordecai paused, his imagination arrested by the grip of that long-past moment. Deronda's mind hung breathless on what was coming. A strange possibility had suddenly revealed itself. Mordecai's eyes were cast down in abstracted contemplation, and moments later, he spoke again.

"She was a mother of whom it might have been said, 'Her children arise up and call her blessed.' In her, I understood the meaning of that Master who, perceiving his mother's footsteps, rose and declared, 'The Majesty of the Eternal cometh near!' That letter was her cry from the depths of anguish and desolation—the cry of a mother robbed of her little ones. I was her eldest. Death had taken four babes one after another. Then came, late, my little sister, who was, more than all the rest, the desire of my mother's eyes. The letter was a piercing cry to me: 'Ezra, my son, I am robbed of her. He has taken her away and left disgrace behind. They will never come again.'"

Here, Mordecai lifted his eyes suddenly, laid his hand on Deronda's arm, and said, "Mine was the lot of Israel. For the sin of the father, my soul must go into exile. For the sin of the father, the work was broken, and the day of fulfillment delayed. She who bore me was desolate, disgraced, destitute. I turned back. On the instant I turned, her spirit and the spirit of her fathers, who had worthy Jewish hearts, moved within me and drew me. God, in whom the universe dwells, was within me as the strength of obedience. I turned and traveled with hardship—to save the scant money she would need. I left the sunshine and traveled into freezing cold. In the final stage, I spent a night exposed to cold and snow. And that was the beginning of this slow death."

Mordecai let his eyes wander again and removed his hand. Deronda resolutely suppressed the questions urging themselves within him. While Mordecai was in this state of emotion, no other confidence could be sought than what came spontaneously; indeed, Deronda felt a kindred emotion that made him dread his own speech as too momentous.

"But I worked. We were destitute—everything had been seized. And she was ill: the clutch of anguish was too strong for her, working with some lurking disease. At times she could not stand for the beating of her heart, and the images in her brain became chambers of terror where she beheld my sister reared in evil. In the dead of night, I heard her crying for her child. Then I rose, and we stretched forth our arms together and prayed. We poured forth our souls in desire that Mirah might be delivered from evil."

"Mirah?" Deronda repeated, wishing to assure himself his ears had not been deceived by a forecasting imagination. "Did you say Mirah?"

"That was my little sister's name. After we had prayed for her, my mother would rest awhile. It lasted hardly four years, and in the minute before she died, we were praying the same prayer—I aloud, she silently. Her soul went out upon its wings."

"Have you never since heard of your sister?" Deronda asked as quietly as he could.

"Never. Never have I heard whether she was delivered according to our prayer. I know not, I know not. Who shall say where the pathways lie? The poisonous will of the wicked is strong. It poisoned my life—it is slowly stifling this breath. Death delivered my mother, and I felt it a blessedness that I was alone in the winters of suffering. But what are the winters now?—they are far off." Here Mordecai again rested his hand on Deronda's arm and looked at him with that joy of the hectic patient which pierces us to sadness—"there is nothing to wail in the withering of my body. The work will be the better done. Once I said the work of this beginning was mine, I am born to do it. Well, I shall do it. I shall live in you. I shall live in you."

His grasp had become convulsive in its force. Deronda, agitated as he had never been before—the certainty that this was Mirah's brother suffusing his own strange relation to Mordecai with a new solemnity and tenderness—felt his strong young heart beating faster and his lips paling. He shrank from speech. He feared that in Mordecai's present state of exaltation, already an alarming strain on his feeble frame, to utter a word of revelation about Mirah would be disastrous. He feared to make an answer below that high pitch of expectation which resembled a flash from a dying fire, making watchers fear to see it die the faster. His dominant impulse was to do as he had once done before: he laid his firm, gentle hand on the hand that grasped him. Mordecai's hand, as if it had a soul of its own—for he was not distinctly willing to do what he did—relaxed its grasp and turned upward under Deronda's. As the two palms met and pressed each other, Mordecai recovered some sense of his surroundings and said,

"Let us go now. I cannot talk any longer."

In fact, they parted at Cohen's door without having spoken to each other again—merely with another pressure of the hands.

Deronda felt a weight on him which was half joy, half anxiety. The joy of finding in Mirah's brother a nature even more than worthy of that relation to her carried the weight of solemnity and sadness; the reunion of brother and sister was in reality the first stage of a supreme parting—like that farewell kiss which resembles greeting, that last glance of love which becomes the sharpest pang of sorrow. Then there was the weight of anxiety regarding the revelation of the fact on both sides, and the arrangements it would be desirable to make beforehand. One supposes we should all have felt as Deronda did, without sinking into snobbishness or the notion that the primal duties of life demand a morning and an evening suit, that it was an admissible desire to free Mirah's first meeting with her brother from all jarring outward conditions. His own sense of deliverance from the dreaded relationship of the other Cohens, notwithstanding their good nature, made him resolve, if possible, to keep them in the background for Mirah until her acquaintance with them would be an unmarred rendering of gratitude for any kindness they had shown to her brother. On all accounts, he wished to give Mordecai surroundings not only more suited to his frail bodily condition but less of a hindrance to easy intercourse, even apart from the decisive prospect of Mirah taking up her abode with her brother and tending him through the precious remnant of his life. In the heroic drama, great recognitions are not encumbered with these details; and certainly Deronda had as reverential an interest in Mordecai and Mirah as he could have had in the offspring of Agamemnon. But he was caring for destinies still moving in the dim streets of earthly life, not yet lifted among the constellations, and his task presented itself to him as difficult and delicate, especially in persuading Mordecai to change his abode and habits. Concerning Mirah's feeling and resolve, he had no doubt: there would be a complete union of sentiment toward the departed mother, and Mirah would understand her brother's greatness. Yes, greatness: that was the word which Deronda now deliberately chose to signify the impression Mordecai had made on him. He said to himself, perhaps rather defiantly toward the more negative spirit within him, that this man, however erratic some of his interpretations might be—this consumptive Jewish workman in threadbare clothing, lodged by charity, delivering himself to hearers who took his thoughts without attaching more consequences to them than the Flemings to the ethereal chimes ringing above their market-places—had the chief elements of greatness; a mind consciously, energetically moving with the larger march of human destinies, but not the less full of conscience and tender heart for the footsteps that tread near and need a leaning-place; capable of conceiving and choosing a life's task with far-off issues, yet capable of the unapplauded heroism which turns off the road of achievement at the call of the nearer duty whose effect lies within the beatings of the hearts that are close to us, as the hunger of the unfledged bird to the breast of its parent.

Deronda tonight was stirred with the feeling that the brief remnant of this fervid life had become his charge. He had been peculiarly wrought on by what he had seen at the club of the friendly indifference Mordecai must have gone on encountering. His own experience of the small room that ardor can make for itself in ordinary minds had had the effect of increasing his reserve; and while tolerance was the easiest attitude to him, there was another bent in him also capable of becoming a weakness—the dislike to appear exceptional or to risk an ineffective insistence on his own opinion. But such caution appeared contemptible to him just now, when, for the first time, he saw in a complete picture and felt as a reality the lives that burn themselves out in solitary enthusiasm: martyrs of obscure circumstance, exiled in the rarity of their own minds, whose deliverances in other ears are no more than a long passionate soliloquy—unless perhaps at last, when they are nearing the invisible shores, signs of recognition and fulfillment may penetrate the cloud of loneliness; or perhaps it may be with them as with the dying Copernicus made to touch the first printed copy of his book when the sense of touch was gone, seeing it only as a dim object through the deepening dusk.

Deronda had been brought near to one of those spiritual exiles, and it was in his nature to feel the relation as a strong chain, nay, to feel his imagination moving without repugnance in the direction of Mordecai's desires. With all his latent objection to schemes only definite in their generality and nebulous in detail, in the poise of his sentiments he felt at one with this man who had made a visionary selection of him: the lines of what may be called their emotional theory touched. He had not the Jewish consciousness, but he had a yearning, grown the stronger for the denial which had been his grievance, after the obligation of avowed filial and social ties. His feeling was ready for difficult obedience. In this way it came that he set about his new task ungr

Fairy folk a-listening
Hear the seed sprout in the spring.
And for music to their dance
Hear the hedgerows wake from trance,
Sap that trembles into buds
Sending little rhythmic floods
Of fairy sound in fairy ears.
Thus all beauty that appears
Has birth as sound to finer sense
And lighter-clad intelligence.

Gwendolen's thoughts were consumed by Deronda, yet he scarcely thought of her. She spent her days wondering about the substance of his mind, the nature of his "ideas about things," and the rhythm of his existence. It was a futile endeavor, much like a lapdog trying to comprehend the grand motives of a roaming pack; just as impossible for her to conceive of his life being steered by the ancient destiny of the Jewish people was it for him to imagine himself rising on a brazen horse to vanish into the stars.

Burdened by a crushing sense of inferiority, Gwendolen inevitably inflated her own significance in his eyes. She assumed that only the aged and wise fail to recognize their own anxieties reflected in others, yet she, with her youthful isolation, could not resist dwelling on the faintest signs of interest he showed her. She mistook the color and scale of his attention, believing herself to be the sole object of his submission.

What counsel would he offer? "He told me I must cultivate interest in others, acquire knowledge, and care for the highest things—but where do I begin?" She pondered which books he would recommend for her private study, recalling the famous authors she had either avoided or found impenetrable. A mischievous thought crossed her mind: *Could she ask if these were the "medicine for the mind"?* But shame quickly silenced her. Instead, when she was certain of privacy, she selected a miscellany of heavyweights—Descartes, Bacon, Locke, Butler, Burke, Guizot. As a clever young woman of education, she knew these were ornaments of mankind, certain that Deronda had mastered them, and hoping that by rapidly consuming them, she might elevate her perspective to his level.

Yet, time for such intellectual voyages was scarce. She was perpetually required to be the scene of Mrs. Grandcourt, constantly monitored by a husband whose tenacity had found a new motive: to ensure his marriage served every end he chose, and the more completely he crushed any opposing will within her. Whatever rebellion churned inside her, she could not resolve to fail in her performance. No feeling had yet softened her to the thought of any act, word, or glance that might confess her true state to the world. Her greatest fear was a violent impulse that would betray her involuntarily. This very will to remain silent in all other directions had poured a frantic impetuosity into her confidences toward Deronda, to whom her thoughts continually turned as a sanctuary against herself. Her riding, hunting, and socializing were performed with a spirit of grim achievement, devoid of youthful zest, so that in the weeks of the new year at Diplow, Mrs. Grandcourt was regarded as wearing her honors with triumphant ease.

"She disguises it under an air of taking everything as a matter of course," observed Mrs. Arrowpoint. "A stranger might suppose she had condescended rather than risen. I always noticed that doubleness in her."

For her mother, Gwendolen strove to project complete satisfaction. Poor Mrs. Davilow was so deceived that she interpreted the cold distance Gwendolen maintained—as enforced by Grandcourt's handsome but distant provision—as a comparative indifference born of her daughter's new marital interests. Being fetched for lunch and dinner alongside the Gascoignes, driven back shortly after breakfast, and receiving only brief calls where her husband waited outside on horseback or in the carriage, constituted the entirety of her permitted intercourse.

The truth was, when Gwendolen twice proposed inviting her mother with Mr. and Mrs. Gascoigne, Grandcourt had first remained silent, then drawled, "We can't be having *those people* always. Gascoigne talks too much. Country clergy are always bores—with their confounded fuss about everything."
