Many nights were watched through by him in gazing from the open window of his room on the double, faintly pierced darkness of the sea and the heavens; often in struggling under the oppressive skepticism which represented his particular lot, with all the importance he was allowing Mordecai to give it, as of no more lasting effect than a dream—a set of changes which made passion to him, but beyond his consciousness were no more than an imperceptible difference of mass and shadow; sometimes with a reaction of emotive force which gave even to sustained disappointment, even to the fulfilled demand of sacrifice, the nature of a satisfied energy, and spread over his young future, whatever it might be, the attraction of devoted service; sometimes with a sweet, irresistible hopefulness that the very best of human possibilities might befall him—the blending of a complete personal love in one current with a larger duty; and sometimes again in a mood of rebellion (what human creature escapes it?) against things in general because they are thus and not otherwise, a mood in which Gwendolen and her equivocal fate moved as busy images of what was amiss in the world along with the concealments which he had felt as a hardship in his own life, and which were acting in him now under the form of an afflicting doubtfulness about the mother who had announced herself coldly and still kept away.

But at last she was come. One morning in his third week of waiting, there was a new kind of knock at the door. A servant in Chasseurs livery entered and delivered in French the verbal message that the Princess Halm

The spindle spun, a rhythmic counterpoint to the heavy silence of Errina's chamber. With her thick-coiled raven hair and eyes the color of deep agate, she gazed at the surging visions of her destiny, destined to spin byssus in insect-like labor while gods and men wrought deeds worthy of song. But in the quiet of the *Italia*, the spindle was still, and the only movement was the approach of Daniel Deronda.

When Deronda stepped into the antechamber, the two servants exchanged glances, surprised that the doctor his lady had summoned was such a striking young gentleman, whose evening attire seemed adorned rather than burdened by its severity. Yet Deronda paid them no mind until the inner door swung open, revealing a figure waiting at the far end of the vast room.

She was draped in black lace that hung loosely from the summit of her whitening hair to a long train trailing behind her tall form. Her arms, bare to the elbow save for rich bracelets, were folded before her, and the fine poise of her head made her appear handsomer than her weary reality warranted. Deronda felt no pause for observation; he was already close, holding the hand she offered and raising it to his lips. She kept her hand in his, studying him with an examining gaze, while his own consciousness was seized by the piercing quality of her eyes and the mobile nature of her face, which seemed capable of shifting into a different person in the next breath. Even as she studied him, a play of her brow and nostrils formed a tacit language. Deronda dared not move, unable to conceive what manifestation her feeling demanded. He felt his cheeks flushing like a girl's, yet wondered at his own lack of profound emotion; he had lived through so many idealized meetings with his mother in his mind, and they had seemed more real than this stark encounter. He could not conjecture the language she would use. Suddenly, she released his hand, placing both palms on his shoulders. In that moment, her face flashed with admiration, every worn line vanishing to reveal a restored youth.

"You are a beautiful creature!" she said in a low, melodious voice, her syllables possessing a foreign yet agreeable outline. "I knew you would be." She kissed him on each cheek, and he returned the kisses. It was a greeting between royalties.

She paused as the lines of age returned to her face, then spoke in a colder tone. "I am your mother. But you can have no love for me."

"I have thought of you more than of any other being in the world," Deronda replied, his voice trembling.

"I am not like what you thought I was," the Princess said decisively, withdrawing her hands and folding her arms again, inviting him to observe her. He had often pictured her face in his imagination as bearing a likeness to his own; he saw some of it now, but amidst striking differences. She was a remarkable being. What gave her son a painful sense of aloofness? Her worn beauty possessed a strangeness, as if she were not quite a human mother, but a Melusina bound to a world independent of theirs.

"I used to think that you might be suffering," Deronda said, anxious not to wound her. "I wished to be a comfort."

"I _am_ suffering. But with a suffering you cannot comfort," the Princess said, her voice hardening as she moved to a sofa where cushions were carefully arranged. "Sit down." She pointed to a seat near her, then, seeing distress in his face, softened her tone. "I am not suffering at this moment. I am at ease. I am able to talk."

Deronda seated himself, waiting. It felt as though he stood before a mysterious Fate rather than the longed-for mother. He watched her with wonder, separated by a spiritual distance she had erected.

"No," she began. "I did not send for you to comfort me. I could not know beforehand what you would feel. I have no foolish notion that you can love me merely because I am your mother, when you have never seen or heard of me. But I thought I chose something better for you than being with me. I did not think I deprived you of anything worth having."

"You cannot wish me to believe that your affection would not have been worth having," Deronda said, noting her pause.

"I do not mean to speak ill of myself," the Princess said with proud impetuosity. "But I had not much affection to give. I did not want affection. I had been stifled with it. I wanted to live out the life that was in me, unhampered by other lives. You wonder what I was. I was no princess then." She rose suddenly, and Deronda followed, feeling breathless.

"No princess in this tame life I lead now. I was a great singer, and I acted as well as I sang. All the rest were poor beside me. Men followed me from country to country. I was living a myriad lives in one. I did not want a child."

There was a passionate self-defense in her tone. She had cast precedent aside; she sought justification only in the most intense words for her experience. She seemed to fling these words against any possible reproach in her son's mind, who stood clutching his coat-collar as if keeping himself above water. What else could she tell him? She continued with the same intensity, a pale illumination in her face.

"I did not want to marry. I was forced into marrying your father—forced by my father's wishes and commands. Besides, it was my best way of getting some freedom. I could rule my husband, but not my father. I had a right to be free. I had a right to seek freedom from a bondage I hated."

She seated herself again, while a subtle movement in her eyes and closed lips suggested suppressed speech. Deronda remained standing. After a moment, she looked up with less defiance, pleading softly.

"And the bondage I hated for myself, I wanted to keep you from. What better could the most loving mother do? I relieved you from the bondage of being born a Jew."

"Then I _am_ a Jew?" Deronda burst out with deep-voiced energy, causing his mother to shrink back against her cushions. "My father was a Jew, and you are a Jewess?"

"Yes, your father was my cousin," the mother said, watching him with a change in her look, as if fearing what she might have to confront.

"I am glad of it," Deronda said impetuously, in a veiled voice of passion. He could not have imagined beforehand how he would say this, never admitting it before. He could not have dreamed it would be in impulsive opposition to his mother. He was shaken by a mixed anger, against this mother who seemed to have borne him unwillingly, made herself a stranger, and was now making herself known unwillingly.

But the mother was equally shaken by a differently mixed anger, her frame less equal to repression. The shaking was visibly physical, her eyes appearing larger in her pallid excitement.

"Why do you say you are glad? You are an English gentleman. I secured you that."

"You did not know what you secured me. How could you choose my birthright for me?" Deronda said, throwing himself sideways into his chair, leaning his arm over the back while looking away.

He was fired with an intolerance foreign to him, yet he struggled to master himself, fearing he might say something too harsh in this epochal moment. A pause fell before his mother spoke again, her voice becoming more firmly resistant in its finely varied tones.

"I chose for you what I would have chosen for myself. How could I know that you would have the spirit of my father in you? How could I know that you would love what I hated—if you really love to be a Jew?" The last words carried such bitterness that any listener might have supposed hatred had arisen between them.

But Deronda had recovered his fuller self. He recalled his sensibilities to the life that had been and was for her, whose best years were gone and who was now exerting herself to tell him of a past that was not his alone. His habitual shame at accepting events as solely his own helped him here. As he looked at his mother silently after her last words, his face regained a penetrative calm, yet it seemed to have a strangely agitating influence over her. Her eyes were fixed on him with fascination, but not with the repose of maternal delight.

"Forgive me, if I speak hastily," he said with diffident gravity. "Why have you resolved now on disclosing to me what you took care to have me brought up in ignorance of? Why—since you seem angry that I should be glad?"

"Oh—the reasons of our actions!" the Princess said with a ring of sarcastic scorn. "When you are as old as I am, it will not seem so simple a question. People talk of their motives in a cut-and-dried way. Every woman is supposed to have the same set of motives, or else to be a monster. I am not a monster, but I have not felt exactly what other women feel—or say they feel, for fear of being thought unlike others. When you reproach me in your heart for sending you away, you mean that I ought to say I felt about you as other women say they feel about their children. I did _not_ feel that. I was glad to be freed from you. But I did well for you, and I gave you your father's fortune. Do I seem now to be revoking everything? Well, there are reasons. I feel many things I cannot understand. A fatal illness has been growing in me for a year. I shall very likely not live another year. I will not deny anything I have done. I will not pretend to love where I have no love. But shadows are rising round me. Sickness makes them. If I have wronged the dead, I have but little time to do what I left undone."

The varied transitions of tone in her speech were as perfect as any accomplished actress could make. It was a piece of sincere acting; this woman's nature was one in which all feeling, especially the tragic and real, immediately became matter of conscious representation. Experience passed into drama, and she acted her own emotions. In a minor degree, this is common, but in the Princess, the acting possessed a rare perfection of physiognomy, voice, and gesture. It would not be true to say she felt less because of this double consciousness; she felt all the more, but with a difference. Each nucleus of pain or pleasure had a deep atmosphere of spiritual intoxication that both exalted and deadened. But Deronda made no such reflection. All his thoughts hung on the purport of her words; her tones and wonderful face entered his agitation without being noted. What he longed for with an awed desire was to know as much as she would tell him of the strange mental conflict under which he had been brought into the world. His compassionate nature made the controlling idea within him the suffering and confession breathing through her later words, which forbade any further question when she paused, remaining silent with her brow knit, her head turned slightly away, and her large eyes fixed as if on something incorporeal. He must wait.

She spoke again with strange abruptness, turning her eyes upon him suddenly.

"Sir Hugo has written much about you. He tells me you have a wonderful mind—you comprehend everything—you are wiser than he is with all his sixty years. You say you are glad to know that you were born a Jew. I am not going to tell you that I have changed my mind about that. Your feelings are against mine. You don't thank me for what I did. Shall you comprehend your mother, or only blame her?"

"There is not a fibre within me but makes me wish to comprehend her," Deronda said, meeting her sharp gaze solemnly. "It is a bitter reversal of my longing to think of blaming her. What I have been most trying to do for fifteen years is to have some understanding of those who differ from myself."

"Then you have become unlike your grandfather in that," the mother said, "though you are a young copy of him in your face. He never comprehended me, or if he did, he only thought of fettering me into obedience. I was to be what he called 'the Jewish woman' under pain of his curse. I was to feel everything I did not feel, and believe everything I did not believe. I was to feel awe for the bit of parchment in the _mezuza_; to dread lest a bit of butter should touch a bit of meat; to think it beautiful that men should bind the _tephillin_ on them, and women not—to adore the wisdom of such laws, however silly they might seem to me. I was to love the long prayers in the ugly synagogue, and the howling, and the gabbling, and the dreadful fasts, and the tiresome feasts, and my father's endless discoursing about our people, which was a thunder without meaning in my ears. I was to care forever about what Israel had been; and I did not care at all. I cared for the wide world, and all that I could represent in it. I hated living under the shadow of my father's strictness. Teaching, teaching for everlasting—'this you must be,' 'that you must not be'—pressed on me like a frame that got tighter and tighter as I grew. I wanted to live a large life, with freedom to do what everyone else did, and be carried along in a great current, not obliged to care. Ah!" Her tone changed to one of bitter incisiveness. "You are glad to have been born a Jew. You say so. That is because you have not been brought up as a Jew. That separateness seems sweet to you because I saved you from it."

"When you resolved on that, you meant that I should never know my origin?" Deronda said impulsively. "You have at least changed in your feeling on that point."

"Yes, that was what I meant. That is what I persevered in. And it is not true to say that I have changed. Things have changed in spite of me. I am still the same Leonora"—she pointed with her forefinger to her breast—"here within me is the same desire, the same will, the same choice, _but_"—she spread out her hands, palm upward, on each side of her, pausing with a bitter compression of her lip, then letting her voice fall into muffled, rapid utterance—"events come upon us like evil

"La même fermeté qui sert à résister à l'amour sert aussi à le rendre violent et durable; et les personnes faibles qui sont toujours agitées des passions n'en sont presque jamais véritablement remplies."

The next morning, among the letters awaiting Daniel Deronda, one stood out: a missive from Hans Meyrick, spanning four quarto pages in the family's distinctive, elegant script.

"My Dear Deronda," Hans began, "in return for your insightful sketch of Italian movements and your broader view of world affairs, I must report that here at home, the most prudent opinion is simply that 'time will show.' Regarding the causes of past effects, we now see that the recent swindling telegrams explain last year's cattle plague—a refutation of false philosophy and a justification for farmer compensation. My own theory, that a murrain will soon strike the commercial class and reveal itself through the sudden sale of rejected paintings, has been dismissed as unsound analogy. Yet, there are minds willing to rob even the neglected painter of his solace. To my feeling, there is great beauty in the idea that a bad judge might prize my *Berenice* series highly, punishing the city men for my ill-merited luck.

Meanwhile, I console myself for your absence by finding advantage in it—shining like Hesperus when Hyperion has departed. I sit with our Hebrew prophet, studying his head during the hours he used to spend with you, gaining credit as a learned young Gentile who would have been a Jew if he could. I agree with him in the general principle that whatever is best is therefore Jewish. Though I am not a severe reasoner, I see the logic: if whatever is best is A, and B happens to be best, B must be A. On this principle, I could see the force of a pamphlet proving all good art was Protestant. However, our prophet is an uncommonly interesting sitter—a better model than Rembrandt had for his Rabbi. I never leave him without a new discovery. It is a constant wonder that, with all his fiery feeling for his race, he is no straight-laced Jew, spitting after the word 'Christian' and enjoying the prospect of the Gentile mouth watering in vain for roasted Leviathan while Israel sends up plates ad libitum. (You perceive my studies taught me what to expect from the orthodox Jew.) I confess I have always held lightly to your account of Mordecai as merely apologetic, part of your disposition to make an antediluvian point of view lest you do injustice to the megatherium. But now, hearing him in his proper person, I find him a philosophical-allegorical-mystical believer with a sharp dialectic point, capable of pricking any argumentative rattler of peas in a bladder. This mixture may be a Jewish prerogative. His mind is so broad that my own correct opinions lie in it comfortably; how they agree with the vast remainder is his affair, not mine. I leave it to him to settle our basis, never yet seeing one that isn't a world-supporting elephant, expensive to keep. My means won't allow a private elephant, so I go into mystery instead—a cheaper, lasting gas supplied by the decomposition of elephants. If I like the look of an opinion, I treat it civilly. I have a friendly feeling toward Mordecai's notion that a whole Christian is three-fourths a Jew, and that since Alexandrian times, the most comprehensive minds have been Jewish. I intend to point out to Mirah that, aside from Arabic incidents, there is little difference between me and Maimonides.

But lately, I've found that your shallow lover can't help making a declaration. If Mirah's ways were less distracting, and it were less of a heaven to be in her presence, I must long ago have flung myself at her feet, requesting her to tell me, with less indirectness, whether she wished me to blow my brains out. I have a knack of hoping, as good as an estate in reversion, provided one keeps from the temptation of turning it into certainty, which may spoil all. My Hope wanders among orchard blossoms, feels warm snow falling on it through sunshine, and doubts nothing. But catching sight of Certainty in the distance, it sees an ugly Janus-faced deity with a dubious wink and turns quickly away. You, with your supreme reasonableness and self-nullification, know nothing about Hope, that immortal, delicious maiden forever courted, whom fools call deceitful, as if Hope carried the cup of disappointment. It is her deadly enemy, Certainty, whom she only escapes by transformation. (You observe my new vein of allegory?) Seriously, however, truth will prevail, prejudice will melt before it, diversity accompanied by merit will make itself felt as fascination, and no virtuous aspiration will be frustrated—all doctrines of the schools implying that the Jewess I prefer will prefer me. Any blockhead cites generalities, but the mind-master discerns the particular cases they represent.

I am less convinced that my society makes amends to Mordecai for your absence, but another substitute occasionally arrives: Jacob Cohen. It is worth catching our prophet's expression when he has that remarkable young Israel on his knee, pouring forth Semitic inspiration with a sublime look of melancholy patience. Sometimes Jacob thinks Hebrew would be more edifying if he stopped his ears with his palms, imitating the muffled sounds. When Mordecai gently draws down the little fists and holds them fast, Jacob's features take on extraordinary activity, as if walking through a menagerie, succeeding best with the owl and the peccary. He treats me with familiar ease, looking at me as a second-hand Christian commodity likely to come down in price, remarking on my disadvantages with frankness implying thoughts of future purchase. It is pretty to see the change in him if Mirah enters. He turns child suddenly—his age usually strikes one as near forty, yet with an air of recent production. With Mirah, he reminds me of dogs brought up by women, manageable only by them. Still, the dog is fond of Mordecai, bringing sugar-plums to share, filling his own mouth embarrassingly while watching how Mordecai deals with a smaller supply. Judging from this modern Jacob at six, I am astonished that his race hasn't bought us all up long ago, pocketing our feebler generations as slave property. There is one Jewess I should not mind being slave to. But I wish I didn't imagine Mirah gets a little sadder, trying all the while to hide it. It is natural enough while she watches the slow death of this brother, whom she worships with looks of loving devoutness that make me wish myself in his place.

For the rest, we are a little merrier than usual. Rex Gascoigne—remember the head you admired among my sketches, a fellow with a good upper lip reading law—now has rooms in town, with a neat sister (also with a good upper lip) staying with him. I introduced them to my mother and the girls, who found out from Miss Gascoigne that she is cousin to your Vandyke Duchess!!! I put the notes of exclamation to mark the surprise this produced on my feeble understanding. On reflection, I discovered there was no ground for surprise unless I had believed nobody could be anybody's cousin without my knowing it. This sort of surprise depends on a liveliness of the spine with a constant nullity of brain. There was a fellow I used to meet in Rome, in an effervescence of surprise at the simplest information. Tell him you were fond of easy boots; he would always say, "No! are you?" with the same energy of wonder. The very fellow of whom pastoral Browne wrote prophetically: "A wretch so empty that if e'er there be / In nature found the least vacuity / 'Twill be in him." I have accounted for it all—he had a lively spine.

However, this cousinship with the Duchess came out by chance one day Mirah was with them, talking about the Mallingers. Apropos: I am getting so important that I have rival invitations. Gascoigne wants me to go to his father's rectory in August, but I think self-interest will take me to Topping Abbey, for Sir Hugo has invited me and proposes—God bless him for his rashness!—that I paint his three daughters sitting on a bank in the Gainsborough style. He came to my studio recently and recommended I apply myself to portraiture. Of course I know what that means. "My good fellow, your attempts at the historic and poetic are simply pitiable. Your brush is just that of a successful portrait-painter—it has a little truth and a great facility in falsehood—your idealism will never do for gods and goddesses, but it may fetch a high price as flattery. Fate has made you the hinder wheel—run behind, because you can't help it." What great effort it costs our friends to give us these candid opinions! I have even known a man call to tell me I had irretrievably exposed my want of judgment, and if I had asked him, we would have lent him his own judgment. Such was my ingratitude that even while he spoke, I inwardly sketched a Last Judgment with that candid friend's physiognomy on the left. But all this is away from Sir Hugo, whose manner of implying one's gifts aren't of the highest order is so good-natured and comfortable that I begin to feel it an advantage not to be among those poor fellows at the tip-top. His kindness tastes better because it comes out of his love for you, old boy. His chat is amusing. By the way, he told me your Vandyke Duchess is gone yachting to the Mediterranean. I bethink me it is possible to land from a yacht or be taken on board. Shall you have an opportunity of continuing your theological discussion with the fair Supralapsarian? Is Duke Alphonso also theological?—perhaps an Arian who objects to triplicity? (Stage direction: While D. is reading, a profound scorn gathers in his face till at the last word he flings down the letter, grasps his coat-collar in a statuesque attitude and remains with a look generally tremendous throughout the following soliloquy, "O night, O blackness, etc., etc.")

Excuse the brevity. You are not used to more from me than a bare statement of facts. One fact I omitted: the Klesmers on the eve of departure behaved magnificently, shining forth as planets of genius and fortune in conjunction. Mirah is rich with their oriental gifts.

What luck it will be if you come back and present yourself at the Abbey while I am there! I shall behave with consummate discretion and win golden opinions. But I shall run up to town now and then for a peep into Gad Eden. You see how far I have got in Hebrew lore—up with my Lord Bolingbroke, who knew no Hebrew but "understood that sort of learning." If Mirah commanded, I would go to a depth below the tri-literal roots. Already it makes no difference to me whether the points are there or not. But while her brother's life lasts, I suspect she wouldn't listen to a lover, even one whose "hair is like a flock of goats on Mount Gilead"—and I flatter myself few heads would bear that comparison better than mine. So I stay with my hope among the orchard-blossoms.—Your devoted,

Some months prior, this letter from Hans would have divided Deronda's thoughts irritably; its romancing about Mirah would have had an unpleasant edge, scarcely anointed with commiseration for his friend's probable disappointment. But things had altered since March. Mirah was no longer so critically placed with regard to the Meyricks, and Deronda's own position had undergone a change crowned by the revelation of his birth. The new opening toward the future, though he would not trust in definite visions, inevitably shed new lights, influencing his mood toward past and present. Hence, what Hans called his hope now seemed to Deronda not a mischievous unreasonableness, but an unusually persistent bird-dance of an extravagant fancy. He would have felt quite able to pity any consequent suffering of his friend's, if he had believed it probable. But some of the busy thoughts filling that long day, which passed without a new summons from his mother, were given to the argument that Hans Meyrick's nature was not one in which love could strike deep roots that turn disappointment into sorrow. It was too restless, too readily excitable by novelty, too ready to turn itself into imaginative material, wearing its grief as a fantastic costume. "Already he is beginning to play at love: he is taking the whole affair as a comedy," Deronda said to himself. "He knows very well there is no chance for him. Just like him—never opening his eyes on any possible objection I could have to receive his outpourings about Mirah. Poor old Hans! If we were under a fiery hail together he would howl like a Greek, and if I did not howl too, it would never occur to him that I was as badly off as he. And yet he is tender-hearted and affectionate in intention, and I can't say he is not active in imagining what goes on in other people—but then he always imagines it to fit his own inclination."

With this touch of causticity, Deronda got rid of the slight heat currently raised by Hans's naive expansiveness. The nonsense about Gwendolen, conveying the fact that she was gone yachting with her husband, only suggested a disturbing sequel to his own strange parting with her. But there was one sentence in the letter which raised a more immediate, active anxiety. Hans's suspicion of a hidden sadness in Mirah was not in the direction of Deronda's wishes, and hence, instead of distrusting his observation, Deronda began to conceive a cause for the sadness. Was it some event that had occurred during his absence, or only the growing fear of some event? Was it something, perhaps alterable, in the new position made for her? Or—had Mordecai, against his habitual resolve, communicated to her those peculiar cherished hopes about him, Deronda, and had her quickly sensitive nature been hurt by the discovery that her brother's will or tenacity of visionary conviction had acted coercively on their friendship? Had she been hurt by the fear that there was more of pitying self-suppression than of equal regard in Deronda

"My desolation does begin to make / A better life."
—SHAKESPEARE: *Antony and Cleopatra.*

Before Daniel Deronda was summoned for a second audience with his mother, a day slipped by in which she sent only a message stating she was not yet well enough to receive him. But on the third morning, a note arrived: "I leave to-day. Come and see me at once."

He was ushered into the same room as before, yet it was now heavily darkened by blinds and heavy curtains. The Princess was not present initially, but she soon entered, draped in a loose wrap of soft, dusky orange silk. Black lace floated about her head, and her arms were bare beneath her wide sleeves. In the somber light, her face appeared even more imposing; her eyes seemed larger, the lines of her countenance more vigorous. One might have imagined her a sorceress, stretching forth a wonderful hand to mix youth-potions for others, yet scorning to mix them for herself, having drunk her fill of youth.

She placed her arms around her son's shoulders immediately, kissing him on both cheeks, before seating herself among her cushions with an air of assured firmness and dignity that stood in stark contrast to her fitfulness during their first meeting. She gestured for Deronda to sit beside her. He obeyed, asking, "You are quite relieved now, I trust?"

"Yes, I am at ease again. Is there anything more that you would like to ask me?" she replied, speaking with the matter-of-fact tone of a queen rather than a mother.

"Can I find the house in Genoa where you used to live with my grandfather?" Deronda asked.

"No," she answered, making a deprecating movement with her arm. "It is pulled down—not to be found. But regarding our family, and where my father lived at various times—you will find all that among the papers in the chest, better than I can tell you. My father was a physician. My mother was a Morteira. I used to hear all those things without listening. You will find them all. I was born amongst them without my will. I banished them as soon as I could."

Deronda tried to conceal his pained feeling and said, "Anything else that I should desire to know from you could only be what it is some satisfaction to your own feeling to tell me."

"I think I have told you everything that could be demanded of me," the Princess said, looking coldly meditative. It seemed as if she had exhausted her emotion in their former interview. The truth was, she had told herself, "I have done it all. I have confessed all. I will not go through it again. I will save myself from agitation." And she was acting out that scheme.

But to Deronda's nature, the moment was cruel; it turned the filial yearning of his life into a disappointed pilgrimage to a shrine where the symbols of sacredness were gone. It seemed that all the woman lacking in her was present in him. With some tremor in his voice, he asked, "Then are we to part and I never be anything to you?"

"It is better so," the Princess said, her voice softer and mellower. "There could be nothing but hard duty for you, even if it were possible for you to take the place of my son. You would not love me. Don't deny it," she said abruptly, raising her hand. "I know what is the truth. You don't like what I did. You are angry with me. You think I robbed you of something. You are on your grandfather's side, and you will always have a condemnation of me in your heart."

Deronda felt himself under a ban of silence. He rose from his seat beside her, preferring to stand if he had to obey that imperious prohibition against any tenderness. But his mother now looked up at him with a new admiration in her glance.

"You are wrong to be angry with me. You are the better for what I did." After pausing a little, she added abruptly, "And now tell me what you shall do?"

"Do you mean now, immediately," said Deronda, "or as to the course of my future life?"

"I mean in the future. What difference will it make to you that I have told you about your birth?"

"A very great difference," said Deronda emphatically. "I can hardly think of anything that would make a greater difference."

"What shall you do then?" the Princess asked with more sharpness. "Make yourself just like your grandfather—be what he wished you—turn yourself into a Jew like him?"

"That is impossible. The effect of my education can never be done away with. The Christian sympathies in which my mind was reared can never die out of me," Deronda said, his tone growing increasingly tenacious. "But I consider it my duty—it is the impulse of my feeling—to identify myself, as far as possible, with my hereditary people, and if I can see any work to be done for them that I can give my soul and hand to, I shall choose to do it."

His mother fixed her eyes on him with a wondering speculation, examining his face as if she thought that by close attention she could read a difficult language there. He bore her gaze very firmly, sustained by a resolute opposition that was the expression of his fullest self. She bent toward him slightly and said with decisive emphasis, "You are in love with a Jewess."

Deronda blushed and said, "My reasons would be independent of any such fact."

"I know better. I have seen what men are," the Princess said peremptorily. "Tell me the truth. She is a Jewess who will not accept anyone but a Jew. There *are* a few such," she added with a touch of scorn.

Deronda faced that objection which we all know in speaking to those who are too certain of their own fixed interpretations to be enlightened by anything we may say. But besides this, the point immediately in question was one on which he felt a repugnance either to deny or affirm. He remained silent, and she presently said, "You love her as your father loved me, and she draws you after her as I drew him."

Those words touched Deronda's filial imagination, and some tenderness in his glance was taken by his mother as an assent. She went on with rising passion: "But I was leading him the other way. And now your grandfather is getting his revenge."

"Mother," said Deronda remonstrantly, "don't let us think of it in that way. I will admit that there may come some benefit from the education you chose for me. I prefer cherishing the benefit with gratitude to dwelling with resentment on the injury. I think it would have been right that I should have been brought up with the consciousness that I was a Jew, but it must always have been a good to me to have as wide an instruction and sympathy as possible. And now, you have restored me my inheritance—events have brought a fuller restitution than you could have made—you have been saved from robbing my people of my service and me of my duty: can you not bring your whole soul to consent to this?"

Deronda paused in his pleading; his mother looked at him listeningly, as if the cadence of his voice were taking her ear, yet she shook her head slowly. He began again, even more urgently.

"You have told me that you sought what you held the best for me: open your heart to relenting and love toward my grandfather, who sought what he held the best for you."

"Not for me, no," she said, shaking her head with more absolute denial and folding her arms tightly. "I tell you, he never thought of his daughter except as an instrument. Because I had wants outside his purpose, I was to be put in a frame and tortured. If that is the right law for the world, I will not say that I love it. If my acts were wrong—if it is God who is exacting from me that I should deliver up what I withheld—who is punishing me because I deceived my father and did not warn him that I should contradict his trust? Well, I have told everything. I have done what I could. And *your* soul consents. That is enough. I have after all been the instrument my father wanted. 'I desire a grandson who shall have a true Jewish heart. Every Jew should rear his family as if he hoped that a Deliverer might spring from it.'"

In uttering these last sentences, the Princess narrowed her eyes, waved her head up and down, and spoke slowly with a new kind of chest-voice, as if she were quoting unwillingly.

"Were those my grandfather's words?" Deronda asked.

"Yes, yes; and you will find them written. I wanted to thwart him," the Princess said with a sudden outburst of the passion she had shown in the former interview. Then she added more slowly, "You would have me love what I have hated from the time I was so high"—here she held her left hand a yard from the floor—"That can never be. But what does it matter? His yoke has been on me, whether I loved it or not. You are the grandson he wanted. You speak as men do—as if you felt yourself wise. What does it all mean?"

Her tone was abrupt and scornful. Deronda, in his pained feeling and under the solemn urgency of the moment, had to keep a clutching remembrance of their relationship, lest his words should become cruel. He began in a deep entreating tone: "Mother, don't say that I feel myself wise. We are set in the midst of difficulties. I see no other way to get any clearness than by being truthful—not by keeping back facts which may—which should carry obligation within them—which should make the only guidance toward duty. No wonder if such facts come to reveal themselves in spite of concealments. The effects prepared by generations are likely to triumph over a contrivance which would bend them all to the satisfaction of self. Your will was strong, but my grandfather's trust which you accepted and did not fulfill—what you call his yoke—is the expression of something stronger, with deeper, farther-spreading roots, knit into the foundations of sacredness for all men. You renounced me—you still banish me—as a son"—there was an involuntary movement of indignation in Deronda's voice—"But that stronger Something has determined that I shall be all the more the grandson whom also you willed to annihilate."

His mother watched him fixedly, and again her face gathered admiration. After a moment's silence, she said in a low, persuasive tone, "Sit down again," and he obeyed, placing himself beside her. She laid her hand on his shoulder and went on, "You rebuke me. Well—I am the loser. And you are angry because I banish you. What could you do for me but weary your own patience? Your mother is a shattered woman. My sense of life is little more than a sense of what was—except when the pain is present. You reproach me that I parted with you. I had joy enough without you then. Now you are come back to me, and I cannot make you a joy. Have you the cursing spirit of the Jew in you? Are you not able to forgive me? Shall you be glad to think that I am punished because I was not a Jewish mother to you?"

"How can you ask me that?" said Deronda remonstrantly. "Have I not besought you that I might now at least be a son to you? My grief is that you have declared me helpless to comfort you. I would give up much that is dear for the sake of soothing your anguish."

"You shall give up nothing," said his mother with the hurry of agitation. "You shall be happy. You shall let me think of you as happy. I shall have done you no harm. You have no reason to curse me. You shall feel for me as they feel for the dead whom they say prayers for—you shall long that I may be freed from all suffering—from all punishment. And I shall see you instead of always seeing your grandfather. Will any harm come to me because I broke his trust in the daylight after he was gone into darkness? I cannot tell:—if you think *Kaddish* will help me—say it, say it. You will come between me and the dead. When I am in your mind, you will look as you do now—always as if you were a tender son—always—as if I had been a tender mother."

She seemed resolved that her agitation should not conquer her, but he felt her hand trembling on his shoulder. Deep, deep compassion hemmed in all words. With a face of beseeching, he put his arm around her and pressed her head tenderly under his. They sat so for some moments. Then she lifted her head again and rose from her seat with a great sigh, as if in that breath she were dismissing a weight of thoughts. Deronda, standing in front of her, felt that the parting was near. But one of her swift alternations had come upon his mother.

"Is she beautiful?" she said abruptly.

"Who?" said Deronda, changing color.

"The woman you love."

It was not a moment for deliberate explanation. He was obliged to say, "Yes."

"Not ambitious?"

"No, I think not."

"Not one who must have a path of her own?"

"I think her nature is not given to make great claims."

"She is not like that?" said the Princess, taking from her wallet a miniature with jewels around it and holding it before her son. It was her own in all the fire of youth, and as Deronda looked at it with admiring sadness, she said, "Had I not a rightful claim to be something more than a mere daughter and mother? The voice and the genius matched the face. Whatever else was wrong, acknowledge that I had a right to be an artist, though my father's will was against it. My nature gave me a charter."

"I do acknowledge that," said Deronda, looking from the miniature to her face, which even in its worn pallor had an expression of living force beyond anything that the pencil could show.

"Will you take the portrait?" said the Princess more gently. "If she is a kind woman, teach her to think of me kindly."

"I shall be grateful for the portrait," said Deronda, "but—I ought to say, I have no assurance that she whom

"The unwilling brain feigns often what it would not; and we trust Imagination with such phantasies as the tongue dares not fashion into words."

Madonna Pia, abandoned by her husband to the marshes of the Maremma, wanders in Dante's Purgatory among those who repented too late. Her story is one of mutual destruction; the husband, a man of disagreeable manners, sought punishment for his wife by casting her adrift, only to find that the relief he intended for himself was shared. Yet, compared to her, Gwendolen Harleth stands in a far more tragic entanglement. She has not been delivered from her errors, nor cleansed in purgatory; she remains trapped in the fatal meshes of her own making, where the inward torture far outweighs any visible cause.

Grandcourt took Gwendolen to sea not to discard her, but to possess her more completely. He loved the dreamy, absolute freedom of yachting, a state unmolested by society that suited his nature perfectly. He carried her away because he sensed a growing spirit of opposition within her, and because of the sentimental inclination she showed toward Daniel Deronda—a sentiment that, in another man, would have sparked jealousy. In Grandcourt, however, it manifested as a cold resolution to end such "foolery" before it could take root. He believed he was merely fulfilling the terms of their marriage contract. He had provided rank and luxury; she had accepted them, overcoming her initial repugnance. He knew she had not married him out of love, but for security, and he felt entitled to enforce the obligations of that transaction.

Gwendolen, too, understood the bargain. She possessed a root of conscience; she knew she had been wrong. But now, stranded on a tiny plank-island of a yacht, with the blue Mediterranean separating her from the world, she felt the full weight of the price she had paid. She had sold her truthfulness and her sense of justice, throttling them into silence to be dragged behind her husband like a collared beast.

What could she complain of? The yacht was exquisite, smelling of cedar and silk, crewed by elegant figures with ringlets and bronze skin. Mr. Lush had already returned to England, leaving them alone. Gwendolen even enjoyed the sea; watching the rigging and forecasting adjustments offered a semblance of the imaginary rule she craved. The weather was fine, the clay of the coast turning purple and gem-like under the sun. Yet, what stills the hunger of the heart when beauty sickens the eye? What paradise can quiet the fury of moral repulsion that concentrates the mind in poisonous misery?

While Gwendolen sat throned on her cushions, watching the sea soften with a boundless love, she prayed that Grandcourt would not pause near her, not look at her, not speak. Meanwhile, some humble woman in a smoky kitchen was listening for a footstep that promised relief; some loving couple was calculating earnings for a holiday. Gwendolen felt nothing but the crushing weight of her own isolation.

Did Grandcourt understand what churned in his wife's breast? He knew she did not love him, and he did not need to believe she did. He was not accustomed to soothe himself with the idea of universal affection. What he failed to comprehend was that she could feel a specific, deep-seated repulsion toward him personally. How could she? He knew repulsion well; he cataloged the odious familiarities of men and women, their smirks and foolish attempts at agreeability. Before their marriage, he had attracted her through refined negations, and he understood her dislike for Lush. But he could not conceive of her current hatred for Henleigh Grandcourt. Some men believe they are invisible to the world; a few believe they are objects of repulsion without being told. Grandcourt belonged to neither group. He had always viewed himself as the antithesis of revolting men, possessing a flattering view of his own attractiveness. He had no concept of moral repulsion, nor could he believe that resentment could make beauty more detestable than ugliness.

Their behavior scandalized no one—not the foreign maid, not the valet, not the crew who saw them as a model couple. Their companionship was a well-bred silence. Grandcourt made polite gestures, arranging garments or handing objects, and Gwendolen responded with the necessary civility, avoiding the vulgarity of rejection. He pointed out sugar-cane plantations; she agreed, forcing herself to find interest in them. He smoked and walked up and down, pausing to gaze at her with a narrow, immovable stare, while she exerted all her ingenuity to avoid meeting his eyes. At dinner, he commented on stale fruit or offered different wines. A lady must respond suitably. Quarreling with Grandcourt was impossible; it would be like shouting at a coiled serpent. He derived intense satisfaction from this captivity, creating a royal representation of their life where every private protest added to the piquancy of his despotism.

For Gwendolen, the world had shrunk to her husband and her relation to him. Those closest to us interpret reality for us, and Grandcourt was a distorting lens, a piece of yellow glass that made color an affliction. His petty standards and loveless ennui turned her life into a promenade through a pantheon of ugly idols. She dreaded becoming a mother, not as a deliverance, but as the final completion of the injury she had inflicted. In her despair, she clung to the possibility of accident, a refuge from the temptation of action.

Hatred, like love, is a seed that feeds on itself. The most intense form is rooted in fear, driving one to silence and constructive vindictiveness. Hidden rites of vengeance played out in the secrecy of Gwendolen's mind, not with soothing effect, but with terror. Alongside the dread of her husband grew a self-dread, urging her to flee the images her own impulses had wrought. The vision of her past wrongs illuminated every rash effort at freedom with a pale, ghastly light. She saw her actions through the lens of Deronda's judgment. No flattery or dependence could weaken his restraining power; indeed, her effort to restrain herself was a repayment of his own. Amidst the dreary uncertainties of her spoiled life, the possible remedies in Deronda's mind became her only hope. He seemed a terrible-browed angel from whom she could not hide any deed. She felt compelled to be truthful, for his power began in raising a self-discontent that could only be satisfied by genuine change.

Yet, she had no confidence in concealment. Her vision of dread took the form of a fiercely impulsive deed, committed in a dream from which she would wake to find the effects real: death under her hands, followed not by darkness but by daylight, not by satisfied hatred but by the dismay of guilt. Instead of freedom, she faced the palsy of a new terror—a white, dead face she tried to flee but could not escape. Deronda's words echoed in her mind: "Turn your fear into a safeguard. Keep your dread fixed on the idea of increasing your remorse." Temptation and dread met like pale phantoms, each obstructed by its own image, while her fuller self sobbed for deliverance.

Inarticulate prayers swept out into the vast silence, broken only by her husband's breathing or the creaking of masts. If she thought of definite help, it took the form of Deronda's presence and words. These best moments of inward crying came after white-lipped temptations with murdering fingers, when she would lie with wide-open eyes, tears seeming a blessing, thinking, "I will not mind if I can keep from getting wicked."

Days passed, drifting from the Balearic Isles to Sardinia and back toward Corsica. This floating existence, seemingly peaceful, became a nightmare for Gwendolen. One day, after touching at Ajaccio, she ventured to ask, "How long are we to be yachting?"

"What else should we do?" Grandcourt replied. "I'm not tired of it. Where would you go? I'm sick of foreign places. We shall have enough of Ryelands. Would you rather be at Ryelands?"

"Oh, no," Gwendolen said indifferently. "I only wondered how long you would like this."

"I like yachting longer than anything else," Grandcourt said. "I suppose you are beginning to tire of it. Women are so confoundedly whimsical."

"Oh, dear, no!" Gwendolen replied in a flute-like tone of scorn. "I never expect you to give way."

"Why should I?" Grandcourt asked, choosing an orange.

Gwendolen resolved to endure the length of the voyage she could not foresee. The next day, after a squall made her ill, Grandcourt announced they would stay at Genoa for a week to repair the yacht.

"Do you mind that?" Gwendolen asked, lying white amidst her white drapery.

"I should think so. Who wants to be broiling at Genoa?"

"It will be a change," Gwendolen said, made incautious by her languor.

"I don't want any change. Besides, the place is intolerable... I shall go out in a boat, as I used to do, and manage it myself."

A prospect of hope arose. Gwendolen imagined hours alone, since Grandcourt would not take her in the boat. In her exultation, she entertained wild fancies of running away, now seeing it as an escape from her worse self. Visionary relief insinuated itself as a lawful wish.

The fresh current of expectation revived her. She watched the moon sink with less loneliness, harboring a vague impression that rescue might be prepared. She was wakened the next morning by the casting of the anchor in Genoa, from a dream where she escaped over Mont Cenis, only to meet Deronda telling her to go back.

An hour later, she actually met Deronda on the palatial staircase of the *Italia*. Her husband stood by her side. Deronda started in surprise, unsure if Grandcourt would be civil. Grandcourt, however, immediately suspected an arrangement between them. Though he knew it was improbable that Gwendolen had orchestrated such a precise meeting, he refused to admit it was a mere accident. He felt a conspiracy against him, a league between his wife and Deronda. He sat drinking coffee, observing Gwendolen with a certainty that she was counting on an interview whenever his back was turned.

He discerned a fresh ease in her movements, a joyous expectation that made her more bearable. Her troubles had not marred her beauty; if anything, she was handsomer than Gwendolen Harleth had been, informed by a greater variety of inward experience. She seemed more fully a human being, less confident that all things were according to her opinion. Grandcourt interpreted these signs with narrow correctness, seeing only the anticipation of a meeting with Deronda.

"A—just ring, please," Grandcourt said, stretching for his hat. "I'm going to send Angus to find a little sailing-boat for us to go out in... It's uncommonly pleasant these fine evenings."

Gwendolen turned cold. The gleaming content died out of her like a glacier after sunset.

"I would rather not go in the boat," she said. "Take some one else with you."

"Very well; if you don't go, I shall not go," Grandcourt replied. "We shall stay suffocating here, that's all."

"I can't bear to go in a boat," Gwendolen said angrily.

"That is a sudden change," Grandcourt sneered. "Since you decline, we shall stay indoors."

He lit his cigar and walked up and down. Gwendolen's temper urged her to persist, to force him to stay in the hotel. She passed into the adjoining bedroom, throwing herself into a chair, feeling the wave of evil rush back upon her.

Grandcourt entered, threw off his hat, and sat sideways in front of her. "Have you come round yet? or do you find it agreeable to be out of temper. You make things uncommonly pleasant for me."

"Why do you want to make them unpleasant for *me*?" Gwendolen asked, helpless, hot tears rising.

"Now, will you be good enough to say what it is you have to complain of?" Grandcourt asked, using his most inward voice. "Is it that I stay indoors when you stay?"

She could give no answer. In the conflict of despair and humiliation, she began to sob, tears rolling down her cheeks—a form of agitation she had never shown before.

"I hope this is useful," Grandcourt said. "All I can say is, it's most confoundedly unpleasant. What the devil women can see in this kind of thing, I don't know. *You* see something to be got by it, of course. All I can see is, that we shall be shut up here when we might have been having a pleasant sail."

"Let us go, then," Gwendolen said impetuously. "Perhaps we shall be drowned." She began to sob again.

This behavior gave definiteness to Grandcourt's conclusions. He drew his chair close. "Just be quiet and listen, will you?"

There was a magical effect in his proximity. Gwendolen shrank and ceased to sob, keeping her eyelids down and clasping her hands tightly.

"Let us understand each other," Grandcourt said. "I know very well what this nonsense means. But if you suppose I am going to let you make a fool of me, just dismiss that notion from your mind. What are you looking forward to, if you can't behave properly as my wife? There is disgrace for you, if you like to have it, but I don't know anything else; and as to Deronda, it's quite clear that he hangs back from you."

"It's all false!" Gwendolen said bitterly. "You don't in the least imagine what is in my mind. I have seen enough of the disgrace that comes in that way. And you had better leave me at liberty to speak with any one I like. It will be better for you."

"You will allow me to judge of that," Grandcourt said, rising and moving toward the window, playing with his whiskers.

Gwendolen's words had a tremendous meaning for herself, and she dreaded their effect. But his soul was garrison

"Return to your science,
Which means, the more perfect the thing,
The more it feels good, and thus the pain."

Deronda stood upon the staircase, his heart heavy with a preoccupation that had nothing to do with the steps beneath his feet. He had just returned from a second, harrowing interview with his mother, a meeting that had shaken the foundations of his own quiet world. Only two hours had passed since he parted from her, yet the Princess Halm-Eberstein had already departed the hotel. Logically, he should have been on his way to Mainz, delivering the letter from Joseph Kalonymos and securing the family chest. Yet, a strange paralysis held him. It was not a lack of reason, but a mixture of mental states that refused to crystallize into action. A weight of retrospective feeling pinned him to the spot, forcing him to relive the scenes of his mother's life with a fresh, piercing acuteness. In his solitude, he wept with a compassion that seemed deeper than a woman's, mourning a life so intimately connected to his own, yet so tragically distant.

He felt the world shifting beneath him, altered by the certainty of new ties that disrupted his balance of hopes and fears. It was as if, under the cover of night, he had mistakenly joined a band of wanderers, only to find at dawn that his true kindred pitched their tents far away. He sensed a quivering kinship with a grandfather whose strong impulses and beloved thoughts might now be stirring within his own soul. Through this passionate meditation, Mordecai and Mirah were ever present, clasping hands with him in a silent, sympathetic understanding.

Such was the fabric of Deronda's nature: quick and responsive, wrapped in a mantle of self-controlled reserve forged by early hardship.

The persistent ringing of a bell, signaling the hour, prompted him to glance at his guidebook and consider the necessary preparations for the next train to Mainz. He thought of London, of the beings there who anchored his life, but other wishes clung to Genoa with a magnetic force. There was a presentiment of finality, of overshadowing sorrow, that urged him to linger. He did not consciously decide to stay because it was Friday or because he wished to attend the synagogue service; rather, he simply ceased moving. Instead of packing or summoning his bill, he sat still, his mind drifting to the synagogue. He imagined the faces there, perhaps unchanged from his grandfather's time, hearing the Spanish-Hebrew liturgy that had endured through wandering generations like a plant with wandering seeds, binding far-off lands to the exile's home. His thoughts also turned to Gwendolen, with an anxious remembrance of what had passed between them. He felt a half-admitted conviction that it would be cruel to leave immediately without making some effort to show his sympathy, despite Grandcourt's likely displeasure.

In this suspended state, he deferred his departure, eating his dinner without tasting its flavor. Rising quickly, he sought the synagogue. Passing the porter, he inquired if Mr. and Mrs. Grandcourt were still in the hotel and requested their apartment number. The porter supplied the number but added that they had gone out boating. This piece of information struck Deronda with unexpected force, dividing his attention between the sparse *talithim* and the keen, dark faces of the worshippers. Their easy familiarity with the solemn prayers, a blend of Hebrew and Italian, made him reflect that his grandfather must have been an exceptional Jew, akin to Mordecai. Were not men of ardent zeal and far-reaching hope always exceptional? They were the visionaries who molded the passive life of the world, preventing it from shrinking into the narrow tenacity of insects unaware of thoughts beyond their antennae.

A mournful impatience mingled with his solicitude for Gwendolen, urging him to hasten from the synagogue. He chose to take his evening walk toward the quay, a favorite haunt that now held special attraction due to the possibility of catching the Grandcourts returning from their boating. Resolving to greet them deliberately, he prepared to ignore any grounds Grandcourt might have for wishing him elsewhere.

The sun had dipped behind a bank of clouds, casting only a faint yellow farewell upon the waves, which were agitated by an active breeze. Deronda sauntered slowly within sight of the strand, observing the groups concentrating their attention on a sailing-boat advancing swiftly toward the land, rowed by two men. Amidst the clamorous talk in various languages, Deronda decided against asking questions. Instead, he pushed his way to the foreground to become an unobstructed witness. Telescopes were raised, and loud declarations flew: the boat carried someone who had drowned. One claimed it was the *milord*; another insisted the prostrate figure was *miladi*. A Frenchman, lacking a glass, suggested the *milord* had taken his wife out to drown her, a notion an English skipper immediately dismissed as nonsense, correcting that the reclining figure was clearly a woman.

For Deronda, terribly excited by fluctuating fears, the strokes of the oars were punctuated by swift visions of possible and impossible events that might have led to this tragic fragment of an issue, with a worse half undisclosed. Was the woman apparently snatched from the waters truly Mrs. Grandcourt?

Soon, however, there was no longer any doubt. The boat was being pulled to land, and Deronda saw Gwendolen half-raising herself on her hands by her own effort, encased in a heavy covering of tarpaulin and pea-jackets. She was pale as one of the sheeted dead, shivering, with wet hair streaming down her face. Her eyes held a wild, amazed consciousness, as if she had awakened in a world where judgment was impending and the beings around her were coming to seize her. The first rower, drenched through, ran ashore; the sailors surrounding the boat hindered Deronda from advancing. He could only watch as Gwendolen cast scared glances, shrinking with terror as she was carefully, tenderly helped out and led away by the strong arms of the rough, bronzed men. Her wet clothes clung to her limbs, adding to the impediment of her weakness.

Suddenly, her wandering eyes fell upon Deronda, standing before her. Immediately, as if she had been expecting him and looking for him, she tried to stretch out her arms, which were held back by her supporters. In a muffled voice, she cried out, "It is come, it is come! He is dead!"

"Hush, hush!" Deronda said, his tone authoritative; "quiet yourself." Turning to the men assisting her, he declared, "I am a connection of this lady's husband. If you will get her on to the *Italia* as quickly as possible, I will undertake everything else."

He remained behind to hear from the remaining boatman that her husband had gone down irrecoverably and that his boat floated empty. They had heard a cry, arrived in time to see the lady jump in after her husband, and had managed to extract her quickly enough to save her from further harm.

After this, Deronda hastened to the hotel to ensure the best medical help was provided. Satisfied on this point, he telegraphed the event to Sir Hugo, begging him to come forthwith, and also to Mr. Gascoigne, whose address at the rectory offered the nearest known way to inform Gwendolen's mother. Certain words Gwendolen had spoken in the past returned to him with the effectiveness of an inspiration: in moments of agitated confession, she had spoken of her mother's presence as a possible help, if she could have had it.

"The pang, the curse with which they died,
Had never passed away:
I could not draw my eyes from theirs,
Nor lift them up to pray."

That night, Daniel Deronda did not undress. Gwendolen, after insisting on seeing him one last time before yielding to her own exhaustion, had fallen into a silence that was heavy with a repressed, whispering urgency. She had only begged him, with eyes wide and fearful, to promise that he would come to her when she summoned him in the morning. Yet, a shadow lingered in Deronda's mind. The possibility that her state might shift, the danger of a sudden fever, and the suspicion that the trauma of the afternoon was already manifesting in her psyche weighed heavily upon him. He instructed her attendant to call him immediately should any alarming change occur, making it clear to everyone present that he was in constant communication with her family in England and felt a binding duty to watch over her. It was a role he assumed with ease, bolstered by his familiarity with Grandcourt's valet, the sole old servant who had survived the voyage.

When the strange, varied emotions of the day finally claimed him, Deronda slept, only to be woken not by a summons, but by a tangled web of dreams woven from the previous twenty-four hours. These dreams culminated in an image drawn by his pressing anxiety. But morning had come, and there was no call—an omen that brought a fleeting cheer as he dressed. He reflected that it was perhaps too early to inquire further. Later, he learned that she had endured a sleepless night but showed no violent signs of agitation, eventually drifting into a restful slumber. He marveled at the strength dwelling within this creature, so acutely alive to dread; he felt an irresistible conviction that even under the shock of a severe physical blow, she was mastering herself with a determination of concealment. In contrast, he believed his own sensibilities had been dulled by the raw intensity of meeting his mother. To him, his current care for Gwendolen felt less like a fresh passion and more like a revisiting of familiar scenes, his inward response not yet fully revived.

Deronda occupied his time securing a formal statement from the fishermen who had rescued Gwendolen. The details were sparse. The yacht in which Grandcourt had departed was found drifting with its sail loose, towed safely back to port. The fishermen surmised that Grandcourt had been knocked overboard by the flapping sail while maneuvering, lacking the skill to swim. However, their attention had first been seized by a cry of distress, followed by a shriek from the lady and the sight of her plunging into the sea.

Upon returning to the hotel, he was informed that Gwendolen had risen and desired to see him. He was led to a room darkened by blinds and heavy curtains. She sat there, wrapped in a white shawl, gazing toward the open door with the uneasy vigilance of one waiting for judgment. Her long hair was carefully coiled, and the blue stars in her ears remained untouched. As she sprang impulsively to her full height, shrouded in white, her face and neck pale save for a purple line beneath her eyes and lips parted in a peculiar expression of accusation and helplessness, she appeared as the unhappy ghost of the Gwendolen Harleth Deronda had once seen turning from the gaming table with firm lips and proud self-possession. The sight pierced him with pity, stirring the effects of their entire past relationship within him.

"I beseech you to rest—not to stand," Deronda said as he approached. She obeyed, sinking back into her chair.

"Will you sit down near me?" she asked. "I want to speak very low."

She sat in a large armchair, and he drew a small one close to her side. The gesture seemed to touch her deeply. Turning her pale face fully toward his, which hovered near hers, she whispered, "You know I am a guilty woman?"

Deronda himself turned paler. "I know nothing," he replied, daring not to say more.

"He is dead." She spoke the words with the same undertoned decision.

"Yes," Deronda murmured, his voice heavy with a mournful suspense that made him reluctant to speak further.

"His face will not be seen above the water again," Gwendolen said, her tone suppressed yet eager, her hands clenched tightly.

"No."

"Not by any one else—only by me. A dead face... I shall never get away from it."

With an inward voice of desperate self-repression, she spoke these final words, looking away from Deronda toward something distant on the floor. Was she viewing the whole event—her own acts included—through an exaggerating medium of excitement and horror? Was she in a state of delirium, driven by a sense of concealment and the necessity of self-repression? Such thoughts flashed through Deronda as a fragile hope. But he imagined the conflict of feeling that kept him silent. She was bent on confession, and he dreaded hearing it. Against his better will, he shrank from the task laid upon him; he wished, and yet rebuked the wish as cowardly, that she could bury her secrets in her own bosom. He was not a priest. He dreaded the weight of this woman's soul flung upon his own with imploring dependence.

Yet she spoke again, hurriedly, looking at him.

"You will not say that I ought to tell the world? You will not say that I ought to be disgraced? I could not do it. I could not bear it. I cannot have my mother know. Not if I were dead. I could not have her know. I must tell you; but you will not say that any one else should know."

"I can say nothing in my ignorance," Deronda said mournfully, "except that I desire to help you."

"I told you from the beginning—as soon as I could—I told you I was afraid of myself." There was a piteous pleading in the low murmur to which Deronda turned his ear. Her afflicted face tormented him. "I felt a hatred in me that was always working like an evil spirit—contriving things. Everything I could do to free myself came into my mind; and it got worse—all things got worse. That is why I asked you to come to me in town. I thought then I would tell you the worst about myself. I tried. But I could not tell everything. And *he* came in."

She paused, a shudder passing through her, before continuing.

"I will tell you everything now. Do you think a woman who cried, and prayed, and struggled to be saved from herself, could be a murderess?"

"Great God!" Deronda exclaimed, his voice deep and shaken. "Don't torture me needlessly. You have not murdered him. You threw yourself into the water with the impulse to save him. Tell me the rest afterward. This death was an accident that you could not have hindered."

"Don't be impatient with me." The tremor, the childlike beseeching in these words compelled Deronda to turn his head and look at her face. The poor quivering lips continued. "You said—you used to say—you felt more for those who had done something wicked and were miserable; you said they might get better—they might be scourged into something better. If you had not spoken in that way, everything would have been worse. I *did* remember all you said to me. It came to me always. It came to me at the very last—that was the reason why I—But now, if you cannot bear with me when I tell you everything—if you turn away from me and forsake me, what shall I do? Am I worse than I was when you found me and wanted to make me better? All the wrong I have done was in me then—and more—and more—if you had not come and been patient with me. And now—will you forsake me?"

Her hands, which had been so tightly clenched moments before, were now helplessly relaxed and trembling on the arm of her chair. Her quivering lips remained parted as she ceased speaking. Deronda could not answer; he was obliged to look away. He took one of her hands and clasped it as if they were going to walk together like two children; it was the only way he could answer, "I will not forsake you." And all the while, he felt as if he were putting his name to a blank paper which might be filled up terribly. Their attitude, his averted face bearing an expression of suffering he was solemnly resolved to undergo, might have told half the truth of the situation to a beholder who had suddenly entered.

That grasp was an entirely new experience for Gwendolen. She had never before received from any man a sign of tenderness that her own being had needed, and she interpreted its powerful effect on her into a promise of inexhaustible patience and constancy. The stream of renewed strength allowed her to continue, resuming that fitful, wandering confession where the sameness of experience seemed to nullify the sense of time or order in events. She began again in a fragmentary way.

"All sorts of contrivances in my mind—but all so difficult. And I fought against them—I was terrified at them—I saw his dead face"—here her voice sank almost to a whisper close to Deronda's ear—"ever so long ago I saw it and I wished him to be dead. And yet it terrified me. I was like two creatures. I could not speak—I wanted to kill—it was as strong as thirst—and then directly—I felt beforehand I had done something dreadful, unalterable—that would make me like an evil spirit. And it came—it came."

She was silent for a moment or two, as if her memory had lost itself in a web where each mesh drew all the rest.

"It had all been in my mind when I first spoke to you—when we were at the Abbey. I had done something then. I could not tell you that. It was the only thing I did toward carrying out my thoughts. They went about over everything; but they all remained like dreadful dreams—all but one. I did one act—and I never undid it—it is there still—as long ago as when we were at Ryelands. There it was—something my fingers longed for among the beautiful toys in the cabinet in my boudoir—small and sharp like a long willow leaf in a silver sheath. I locked it in the drawer of my dressing-case. I was continually haunted with it and how I should use it. I fancied myself putting it under my pillow. But I never did. I never looked at it again. I dared not unlock the drawer: it had a key all to itself; and not long ago, when we were in the yacht, I dropped the key into the deep water. It was my wish to drop it and deliver myself. After that I began to think how I could open the drawer without the key: and when I found we were to stay at Genoa, it came into my mind that I could get it opened privately at the hotel. But then, when we were going up the stairs, I met you; and I thought I should talk to you alone and tell you this—everything I could not tell you in town; and then I was forced to go out in the boat."

A sob had risen for the first time with the last words, and she sank back in her chair. The memory of that acute disappointment seemed for the moment to efface what had come since. Deronda did not look at her, but he said, insistently,

"And it has all remained in your imagination. It has gone on only in your thought. To the last the evil temptation has been resisted?"

There was silence. The tears had rolled down her cheeks. She pressed her handkerchief against them and sat upright. She was summoning her resolution; and again, leaning a little toward Deronda's ear, she began in a whisper,

"No, no; I will tell you everything as God knows it. I will tell you no falsehood; I will tell you the exact truth. What should I do else? I used to think I could never be wicked. I thought of wicked people as if they were a long way off me. Since then I have been wicked. I have felt wicked. And everything has been a punishment to me—all the things I used to wish for—it is as if they had been made red-hot. The very daylight has often been a punishment to me. Because—you know—I ought not to have married. That was the beginning of it. I wronged some one else. I broke my promise. I meant to get pleasure for myself, and it all turned to misery. I wanted to make my gain out of another's loss—you remember?—it was like roulette—and the money burned into me. And I could not complain. It was as if I had prayed that another should lose and I should win. And I had won, I knew it all—I knew I was guilty. When we were on the sea, and I lay awake at night in the cabin, I sometimes felt that everything I had done lay open without excuse—nothing was hidden—how could anything be known to me only?—it was not my own knowledge, it was God's that had entered into me, and even the stillness—everything held a punishment for me—everything but you. I always thought that you would not want me to be punished—you would have tried and helped me to be better. And only thinking of that helped me. You will not change—you will not want to punish me now?"

Again a sob had risen.

"God forbid!" groaned Deronda. But he sat motionless.

This long wandering with the conscious-stricken one over her past was difficult to bear, but he dared not again urge her with a question. He must let her mind follow its own need. She unconsciously left intervals in her retrospect, not clearly distinguishing between what she said and what she had only an inward vision of. Her next words came after such an interval.

"That all made it so hard when I was forced to go in the boat. Because when I saw you it was an unexpected joy, and I thought I could tell you everything—about the locked-up drawer and what I had not told you before. And if I had told you, and knew it was in your mind, it would have less power over me. I hoped and trusted in that. For after all my struggles and my crying,

"The unripe grape, the ripe, and the dried. All things are changes, not into nothing, but into that which is not at present." Thus spoke Marcus Aurelius, a voice from the past echoing into the quiet room. Deeds are the pulse of Time, his beating life; whether righteous or unrighteous, once done, they must throb in after-throbs until Time itself is laid in darkness, and the universe quivers and breathes upon no mirror more.

In the evening, she summoned him again. It was nearly the hour when she had first been brought in from the sea the day before, though now the light was softer, subdued by drawn blinds and open windows that let in the hush of twilight. She sat gazing fixedly at the darkening water, her cheek resting on her hand. She looked less shattered than when he had last left her, yet a deep melancholy settled in her expression, shifting into an anxious timidity as Deronda approached. She did not offer her hand, merely whispering, "How long ago it is!" Then, with a trembling voice, "Will you sit near me again a little while?"

He placed himself by her side, just as he had before. Seeing her turn to him with that indefinable look—a silent plea for words—he waited patiently. But she only looked toward the window, then back to him, the same expression lingering without becoming speech. Fear held her tongue. To relieve her timidity, Deronda averted his face. Presently, he heard her cry out, imploringly, "You will not say that any one else should know?"

"Most decidedly not," Deronda replied firmly. "There is no action that ought to be taken in consequence. There is no injury that could be righted in that way. There is no retribution that any mortal could apportion justly."

She remained so still during the pause that she seemed to be holding her breath before she spoke again. "But if I had not had that murderous will—that moment—if I had thrown the rope on the instant—perhaps it would have hindered death?"

"No—I think not," Deronda said slowly. "If it were true that he could swim, he must have been seized with cramp. With your quickest, utmost effort, it seems impossible that you could have done anything to save him. That momentary murderous will cannot, I think, have altered the course of events. Its effect is confined to the motives in your own breast.
