Within ourselves our evil will is momentous, and sooner or later it works its way outside us—it may be in the vitiation that breeds evil acts, but also it may be in the self-abhorrence that stings us into better striving."

"I am saved from robbing others—there are others—they will have everything—they will have what they ought to have. I knew that some time before I left town. You do not suspect me of wrong desires about those things?" She spoke hesitantly, her voice barely audible.

"I had not thought of them," Deronda admitted; "I was thinking too much of the other things."

"Perhaps you don't quite know the beginning of it all," Gwendolen said slowly, as if overcoming a heavy reluctance. "There was some one else he ought to have married. And I knew it, and I told her I would not hinder it. And I went away—that was when you first saw me. But then we became poor all at once, and I was very miserable, and I was tempted. I thought, 'I shall do as I like and make everything right.' I persuaded myself. And it was all different. It was all dreadful. Then came hatred and wicked thoughts. That was how it all came. I told you I was afraid of myself. And I did what you told me—I did try to make my fear a safeguard. I thought of what would be if I—I felt what would come—how I should dread the morning—wishing it would be always night—and yet in the darkness always seeing something—seeing death. If you did not know how miserable I was, you might—but now it has all been no use. I can care for nothing but saving the rest from knowing—poor mamma, who has never been happy."

Silence fell again before she spoke with a repressed sob. "You cannot bear to look at me any more. You think I am too wicked. You do not believe that I can become any better—worth anything—worthy enough—I shall always be too wicked to—" Her voice broke off, helpless.

Deronda's heart was pierced. He turned his eyes to her poor, beseeching face and said, "I believe that you may become worthier than you have ever yet been—worthy to lead a life that may be a blessing. No evil dooms us hopelessly except the evil we love, and desire to continue in, and make no effort to escape from. You *have* made efforts—you will go on making them."

"But you were the beginning of them. You must not forsake me," Gwendolen said, leaning with her clasped hands on the arm of her chair and looking at him. Her face bore piteous traces of the life-experience concentrated in those terrible twenty-four hours—the new, awful existence lying on the other side of the deed that fulfilled a criminal desire. "I will bear any penance. I will lead any life you tell me. But you must not forsake me. You must be near. If you had been near me—if I could have said everything to you, I should have been different. You will not forsake me?"

"It could never be my impulse to forsake you," Deronda said promptly, with a voice that, like his eyes, had the unintentional effect of making his ready sympathy seem more personal and special than it truly was. In that moment, however, he was not entirely free from a foreboding of some such self-committing effect. His strong feeling for this stricken creature could not hinder rushing images of future difficulty. He continued to meet her appealing eyes as he spoke, but with the painful consciousness that to her ear his words might carry a promise which one day would seem unfulfilled: he was making an indefinite promise to an indefinite hope. Anxieties, both immediate and distant, crowded on his thought, and it was under their influence that, after a moment's silence, he said,

"I expect Sir Hugo Mallinger to arrive by tomorrow night at least; and I am not without hope that Mrs. Davilow may shortly follow him. Her presence will be the greatest comfort to you—it will give you a motive to save her from unnecessary pain?"

"Yes, yes—I will try. And you will not go away?"

"Not till after Sir Hugo has come."

"But we shall all go to England?"

"As soon as possible," Deronda said, not wishing to enter into particulars.

Gwendolen looked toward the window again with an expression that seemed like a gradual awakening to new thoughts. The twilight was perceptibly deepening, but Deronda could see a movement in her eyes and hands such as accompanies a return of perception in one who has been stunned.

"You will always be with Sir Hugo now!" she said presently, looking at him. "You will always live at the Abbey—or else at Diplow?"

"I am quite uncertain where I shall live," Deronda said, coloring slightly.

She was warned by his changed countenance that she had spoken too rashly, and fell silent. After a little while she began, again looking away,

"It is impossible to think how my life will go on. I think now it would be better for me to be poor and obliged to work."

"New promptings will come as the days pass. When you are among your friends again, you will discern new duties," Deronda said gently. "Make it a task now to get as well and calm—as much like yourself as you can, before—" He hesitated.

"Before my mother comes," Gwendolen finished softly. "Ah! I must be changed. I have not looked at myself. Should you have known me," she added, turning toward him, "if you had met me now?—should you have known me for the one you saw at Leubronn?"

"Yes, I should have known you," Deronda said mournfully. "The outside change is not great. I should have seen at once that it was you, and that you had gone through some great sorrow."

"Don't wish now that you had never seen me; don't wish that," Gwendolen pleaded, while tears gathered in her eyes.

"I should despise myself for wishing it," Deronda replied. "How could I know what I was wishing? We must find our duties in what comes to us, not in what we imagine might have been. If I took to foolish wishing of that sort, I should wish—not that I had never seen you, but that I had been able to save you from this."

"You have saved me from worse," Gwendolen said in a sobbing voice. "I should have been worse if it had not been for you. If you had not been good, I should have been more wicked than I am."

"It will be better for me to go now," Deronda said, worn in spirit by the perpetual strain of this scene. "Remember what we said of your task—to get well and calm before other friends come."

He rose as he spoke, and she gave him her hand submissively. But when he had left her, she sank on her knees, lost in hysterical crying. The distance between them was too great. She was a banished soul—beholding a possible life which she had sinned herself away from.

She was found in this way, crushed on the floor. Such grief seemed natural in a poor lady whose husband had been drowned in her presence.

"Much ado there was, God wot;
He wold love and she wold not."
—Nicholas Breton.

Time, we know, is a deceptive ruler. The length of the sun's journey tells us nothing of life's advancement, nor does the acreage of a field reveal the storms brewing within its soil. A man may march south, stumble over a bone, and spend years reconstructing anatomy from that single fragment; or turn east, unlocking a new language that rewrites the history of races. He might lead an expedition that carves new paths across continents, only to return maimed and weary, finding his neighbors still grumbling about the same parish grievances, the same elderly gentleman shaking his head at the butcher's boy, staring into the same shop window. Even the swiftest thought, moving like a greyhound, cannot outrun the slow, limpet-like crawl of habit. Human experience swings wildly between the revolutionary rush of change and the quiet recurrence of the familiar, marked only by hunger and the turning of the heavens.

This contrast defined the year that had transformed the brilliant, self-assured Gwendolen Harleth of the Archery Meeting into a crushed penitent, desperate to confess her unworthiness where she once craved to be held worthy. Meanwhile, her family at Pennicote Rectory remained largely unchanged, save for the adjustment to a reduced income, fewer visitors, and fainter compliments. The rectory remained a pleasant home; the red and pink peonies bloomed as vibrantly as last year, and the rows of hollyhocks stood tall by the hedges. The rector maintained his cheerful confidence in the goodwill of his patrons, resolved to earn it through diligent duty, regardless of whether they noticed. He wrote two ecclesiastical articles annually, unsigned and attributed to anonymous scholars, a peaceful authorship that required no Dantesque leanness but rather fed on complacency, offering a suffusive sense of achievement far greater than composing a *Divina Commedia*.

There was, too, the father's recovered delight in his favorite son, a happiness that outweighed the loss of eighteen hundred pounds a year. Whatever hidden changes the disappointment of his first love had wrought in Rex, they seemed secondary to the evidence of serious ambition that followed the family's misfortune. Mr. Gascoigne viewed the previous year's anxiety as merely an evaporation of superfluous moisture, a final step in baking the human dough. Rex had recently returned for a summer visit, bringing Anna home. While he displayed the old liveliness with his siblings, he maintained the habits of the eager student, rising early and shutting himself away in the evenings to pursue a fixed course of study.

"You don't repent the choice of the law as a profession, Rex?" his father asked.

"There is no profession I would choose before it," Rex replied. "I should like to end my life as a first-rate judge, helping to draw up a code. I reverse the famous dictum. Give me something to do with making the laws, and let who will make the songs."

"You will have to stow in an immense amount of rubbish, I suppose—that's the worst of it," the rector noted.

"I don't see that law-rubbish is worse than any other sort," Rex countered. "It isn't as bad as the rubbishy literature that chokes the mind. It doesn't make one dull. Our wittiest men have often been lawyers. Any orderly way of looking at things as cases and evidence seems better than a perpetual wash of odds and ends bearing on nothing. From a higher point of view, the foundations and growth of law make the most interesting aspects of philosophy and history. Of course, there will be trouble, drudgery, exasperation. But the great prizes in life can't be won easily—I see that."

"My boy, the best augury of a man's success is that he thinks it the finest in the world," the rector said warmly. "But I fancy it so with most work when a man goes into it with a will. Brewitt, the blacksmith, told me the other day his apprentice had no mind for the trade. 'And yet, sir,' said Brewitt, 'what would a young fellow have if he doesn't like the blacksmithing?' "

The rector cherished a fatherly delight, allowing it to escape only in moderation. He had easily borne the parting with Warham, who had gone to India, but Rex was the romance of later life—a son he recognized as superior to himself, picturing a future eminence based on famous examples. He told his wife with decision, "Rex will be a distinguished man, Nancy, I am sure of it—as sure as Paley's father was about his son."

"Was Paley an old bachelor?" Mrs. Gascoigne asked.

"That is hardly to the point, my dear," the rector replied, forgetting the irrelevant detail. Mrs. Gascoigne felt she had spoken weakly.

This quiet trotting of time at the rectory was shared by the group who had exchanged the faded dignity of Offendene for the low white house known to villagers as "Jodson's." Mrs. Davilow's delicate face showed only a slight deepening of mild melancholy, her hair bearing a few more silver lines from the last year's trials. The four girls had bloomed slightly, freed from the shade, while the good Jocosa preserved her serviceable neutrality toward the world's pleasures and glories, viewing them as made for those not "in a situation."

In the low, narrow drawing-room, enlarged by two quaint projecting windows with lattices wide open to the scent of monthly roses and the murmur of the garden, Rex and Anna joined the usual group of six. Anna, always a favorite with her younger cousins, spoke eagerly of her new experiences in London and the acquaintances she had made. Questions flowed regarding Gwendolen's house in Grosvenor Square, what Gwendolen had said, and what others had whispered. Had Anna seen Gwendolen after the yacht news? No. This silence left speculation free regarding the mysterious vessel, beyond the fact that Gwendolen had written just before departing to say she and Mr. Grandcourt were yachting in the Mediterranean, and again from Marseilles to promise she would love the cabins and send no further letters until she had filled a diary with *dittos*. This movement of Mr. and Mrs. Grandcourt had appeared in the newspapers, making this new phase of Gwendolen's exalted life a striking part of the sisters' romance, with the book-devouring Isabel adding a corsair or two to ensure an adventure that might end well.

But when Rex was present, the girls, following instructions, avoided this fascinating topic. Instead, they animatedly described the Meyricks and their extraordinary Jewish friends, causing astonished questioning from minds to which the idea of live Jews suggested a difference almost zoological, like a strange race in Pliny's *Natural History* sleeping under its own ears. Bertha could not imagine what Jews believed now, having a dim idea they rejected the Old Testament since it proved the New. Miss Merry thought Mirah and her brother could "never have been properly argued with," while the amiable Alice admitted she didn't mind their beliefs but was sure she "couldn't bear them." Mrs. Davilow corrected her, noting that great Jewish families in society were quite proper in London and Paris, though admitting commoner unconverted Jews were objectionable. Isabel asked if Mirah spoke differently, or if one might be with her without realizing she was a Jewess.

Rex, having no partisanship with the Israelites but having made a troublesome acquaintance with the minutiae of their ancient history through cramming, amused himself by playfully exaggerating each speaker's notion. Anna begged them to understand he was only joking, but their laughter was interrupted by the arrival of a letter for Mrs. Davilow. A messenger had run with it in great haste from the rectory. It enclosed a telegram, and as Mrs. Davilow read and re-read it in silence and agitation, all eyes turned to her with anxiety. No one dared to speak. Finally, looking up and seeing the young faces painted with fear, she remembered they might be imagining something worse than the truth, something akin to her own first dread that made understanding impossible. With a sob that was half relief, she said,

"My dears, Mr. Grandcourt—" She paused an instant, then began again, "Mr. Grandcourt is drowned."

Rex started up as if a missile had been thrown into the room. He could not help himself, and Anna's first look went to him. But then, gathering self-command while Mrs. Davilow read what the rector had written on the enclosing paper, he asked,

"Can I do anything, aunt? Can I carry any word to my father from you?"

"Yes, dear. Tell him I will be ready—he is very good. He says he will go with me to Genoa—he will be here at half-past six. Jocosa and Alice, help me to get ready. She is safe—Gwendolen is safe—but she must be ill. I am sure she must be very ill. Rex, dear—Rex and Anna—go and tell your father I will be quite ready. I would not for the world lose another night. And bless him for being ready so soon. I can travel night and day till we get there."

Rex and Anna hurried away through the sunshine, which suddenly felt solemn to them, without uttering a word to each other. She was chiefly possessed by solicitude about reopening his wound; he struggled with a tumultuary crowd of thoughts that were an offense against his better will. The tumult undiminished at the rectory gate, he said,

"Nannie, I will leave you to say everything to my father. If he wants me immediately, let me know. I shall stay in the shrubbery for ten minutes—only ten minutes."

Who has been quite free from egoistic escapes of the imagination, picturing desirable consequences on his own future in the presence of another's misfortune, sorrow, or death? The expected promotion or legacy is the common type of a temptation that makes speech and even prayer a severe avoidance of the most insistent thoughts, sometimes raising an inward shame worse than any other form of unpleasant companionship. In Rex's nature, the shame was immediate, overspreading like an ugly light all the hurrying images of what might come, thrusting themselves in with the idea that Gwendolen was again free. Perhaps these phantasms persisted more because every vision of hope was quickly nullified by a more substantial obstacle. Before the vision of "Gwendolen free" rose the impassable vision of "Gwendolen rich, exalted, courted." If in the former time, when both their lives were fresh, she had turned from his love with repugnance, what ground was there for supposing her heart would be more open to him in the future?

These thoughts, which he wanted to master and suspend, were like a tumultuary ringing of opposing chimes he could not escape by running. During the last year, he had brought himself to a state of calm resolve, and now it seemed three words had undone all that difficult work, casting him back into the wretched fluctuations of a longing he recognized as simply perturbing and hopeless. At this moment, the activity of such longing had an untimeliness that made it repulsive to his better self. Excuse poor Rex; it was not much more than eighteen months since he had been laid low by an archer who sometimes touches his arrow with a subtle, lingering poison. The disappointment of a youthful passion has effects as incalculable as smallpox, making one person plain and a genius, another less plain and more foolish, another plain without detriment, and leaving perhaps the majority without obvious change. Everything depends—not on the mere fact of disappointment, but on the nature affected and the force that stirs it. In Rex's well-endowed nature, brief as the hope had been, the passionate stirring had gone deep. The effect of disappointment was revolutionary, though fraught with a beneficent new order that retained most of the old virtues; in certain respects, he believed it had finally determined the bias and color of his life. Now, however, his inward peace seemed no better than that of republican Florence, and his heart no calmer than an alarm-bell that makes work slack and tumult busy.

Rex's love had been of that sudden, penetrating, clinging sort the ancients knew and sang, creating a fashion of talk for many moderns whose experience lacks a fiery, demonic character. To have the consciousness suddenly steeped with another's personality, to have the strongest inclinations possessed by an image that retains its dominance in spite of change and apart from worthiness—nay, to feel a passion which clings faster for the tragic pangs inflicted by a cruel, reorganized unworthiness—is a phase of love which in the feeble and common-minded has a repulsive likeness to blind animalism insensible to the higher sway of moral affinity or heaven-lit admiration. But when this attaching force is present in a nature not of brutish unmodifiableness, but of a human dignity that can risk itself safely, it may even result in a devotedness not unfit to be called divine in a higher sense than the ancient. Phlegmatic rationality stares and shakes its head at these unaccountable prepossessions, but they exist as undeniably as the winds and waves, determining here a wreck and there a triumphant voyage.

This sort of passion had nested in the sweet-natured, strong Rex, and he had made up his mind to its companionship, as if it had been an object supremely dear, stricken dumb and helpless, turning all the future of tenderness into a shadow of the past. But he had also made up his mind that his life was not to be pauperized because he had renounced one sort of joy. Rather, he had begun life again with a new counting-up of the treasures that remained, feeling a release of power such as may come from ceasing to be afraid of one's own neck.

And now, here he was pacing the shrubbery, angry with himself that the sense of irrevocableness in his lot, which ought in reason to have been as strong as ever, had been shaken by a change of circumstances that could make no change in relation to him. He told himself the truth quite roughly:

"She would never love me; and that is not the question—I could never approach her as a lover in her present position.

"I count myself in nothing else so happy
As in a soul remembering my good friends."

Sir Hugo Mallinger did not depart for Genoa with the haste that had characterized Mr. Gascoigne's movements, and Daniel Deronda resolved to delay his own exit until he had secured a private audience with the baronet. The shadow of Grandcourt's death, compounded by the recent upheaval in his own life, demanded a face-to-face communion that letters could not convey; specifically, Deronda felt unable to detail the fleeting, spectral presence of his mother in written correspondence. It was not until the fifth evening, guided by a telegram, that Deronda awaited Sir Hugo at the station, expecting his arrival between eight and nine. As he gazed toward the platform, anticipating the sight of a familiar face rooted in his earliest memories, a faint smile touched his lips despite the tragedy that had recently marred his spirit. He imagined the baronet's delight in finally mastering his estates, perhaps even the joy of securing a legacy through a grandson, or at least, through the adoption of a makeshift feminine line. One cannot be a miser with another's joy, unless that joy contradicts one's own rigid ideals of righteousness; to do so would only sour the soul, freezing its gaze while one's own desires remained stubbornly misaligned. Yet, there is a necessary comradeship with imperfection. It is possible to feel gratitude even when witnessing a mistake born of affectionate intent, executed over a lifetime of kindness.

Deronda's conscience recoiled from Sir Hugo's role in perpetuating a falsehood—the very word he used to describe the concealment of his origins. Still, the baronet likely lacked clear knowledge of his mother's breach of trust. With his characteristic lightness of spirit, Sir Hugo had deemed it reasonable to raise his son as an English gentleman, especially given his mother's eccentric refusal to sever ties with her child. Daniel's deep affection for Sir Hugo drove him to seek excuses rather than blame. It is as easy to be rigid in principle yet tender in censure as it is to be disturbed by disorder while remaining patient with the one who perceives it wrongly. Had Sir Hugo, in his bachelor days, viewed children merely as accessories to adult comfort, he had shared a common, unspoken assumption of that era. Deronda, recalling his own painful internal struggles, remembered that such experiences had been entirely absent from Sir Hugo's worldview. Ignorant kindness can inflict cruelty, but to condemn it as direct malice is itself a form of ignorance, alien to Deronda's broad capacity for leniency. Perhaps, after the harrowing revelations of the last ten days, which had lifted the veil on lives unlike his own, Daniel was simply less inclined toward the rash indignation that often masks a desire for punishment. When Sir Hugo's familiar figure emerged from the train, the lifelong bond of affection washed over him, drowning out any fresh grounds for blame.

"Well, Dan," Sir Hugo said, seizing Daniel's hand with earnest gravity. No further greeting was needed; the air between them hummed with mutual understanding. They directed the courier and then proposed a slow walk under the mild evening sky, unhurried by the need to reach the hotel.

"I traveled easily and am in fine condition," Sir Hugo remarked as they stepped out beneath the stars, still faint with the day's lingering glow. "I took my time to investigate matters, which allowed me to read your letter to Lady Mallinger before I left. But now, how fares the widow?"

"Growing calmer," Daniel replied. "She seems to have escaped the physical illness one might have feared following her plunge and the terrible excitement. Her uncle and mother arrived two days ago, and she is being cared for well."

"Is there any chance of an heir?"

"Based on what Mr. Gascoigne told me, I doubt it," Daniel said. "He spoke as if the question was merely whether the widow would retain the estates for her lifetime."

"Will the loss of her husband be a great wrench to her affections?" Sir Hugo asked, glancing around.

"The suddenness of his death was a severe blow," Daniel answered, gently sidestepping the deeper implications.

"Do you know the terms of Grandcourt's will?"

"Yes," Sir Hugo replied promptly. "If there is no legitimate heir, he has left everything to a boy he had with a Mrs. Glasher. You know nothing of this, I presume? She was a companion to him for many years, and they have three older daughters. The boy is to take his father's name; he is already known as Henleigh, and he will become Henleigh Mallinger Grandcourt. The Mallinger fortune will be of no use to him, thank heavens, but the young rascal will have more than enough to sustain him during his fourteen-year minority—no need to fill the gaps with my fifty thousand pounds meant for Diplow, which he had no right to touch. Meanwhile, my beauty, the young widow, is to settle for a mere two thousand a year and the house at Gadsmere. A pleasant exile, if she chooses to lock herself away there, though I doubt she will. His mother has lived there for years. I am thoroughly disgusted with Grandcourt. I cannot say I hold him in higher regard because he drowned, though regarding my own affairs, nothing in his life suited him better than ending it."

"In my view, he erred in marrying this woman—not necessarily in leaving his estates to the son," Daniel said, his tone dry.

"I take no issue with him bequeathing the land to the lad," Sir Hugo countered. "But since he married her, he should have provided a substantial allowance, sufficient for her to maintain the rank he had elevated her to. Four or five thousand a year and the London house for her life—that is what I would have done. Given she was penniless, her friends surely could not demand a formal settlement, otherwise, one cannot trust a man's will made after marriage. Even the wisest men often let some folly leak into their wills; my father did, I know. If a man harbors spite or tyranny, he is likely to bottle it up in such documents. It is clear Grandcourt intended his death to extinguish his wife's prospects if she failed to bear him an heir."

"And in the other scenario, I suppose everything would have been reversed—illegitimacy receiving the extinguisher?" Daniel asked, with a note of scorn.

"Precisely—Gadsmere and the two thousand. It is peculiar. One annoyance is that Grandcourt has named me executor; but as he was the son of my only brother, I cannot refuse. I shall mind it less if I can assist the widow. Lush suggests she was not ignorant of the family situation or the will's intent, hinting at a lack of harmony between the couple. But I fancy you are the one who knew best what Mrs. Grandcourt felt or did not feel, eh, Dan?" Sir Hugo dropped his usual jocularity, speaking with a lowered, genuinely interested tone. Daniel felt that evasion would be misconstrued.

"She was certainly not happy. They were ill-suited. But regarding the property, judging by what I have observed of her, I predict she will be quite content with it."

"Then she is unlike most women," Sir Hugo shrugged. "Still, she must be extraordinary, for there must be an entanglement between your horoscope and hers—eh? When that tremendous telegram arrived, the first thing Lady Mallinger said was, 'How very strange that it should be Daniel who sends it!' But I have experienced something similar in my own life. Once, at a foreign hotel, I found a lady abandoned by her husband with no money. When I stepped forward to help, who should it be but an early flame of mine, foolish enough to marry an Austrian baron with a long mustache and short affection? But it was an affair of my own that brought me there—nothing resembling knight-errantry, any more than your journey to Genoa had to do with the Grandcourts."

A silence fell between them. Sir Hugo had shifted to the safer topic of the Grandcourts, yet both men felt a reluctance to achieve total frankness regarding the events binding them. Daniel sensed that his letter, following the first meeting with his mother, had acted more as a thickening of the ice than a breaker. He waited for Sir Hugo to open the door. Just as they were about to lose sight of the port, the baronet paused, turning for a final look, his voice taking on a more serious hue. "And regarding the main purpose of your coming to Genoa, Dan? Have you not been deeply pained by what you have learned? Is there nothing that makes you wish to alter your position? Remember, whatever happens to you will always matter to me."

"I wish to meet your goodness with perfect confidence, sir," Daniel replied. "But I cannot answer those questions with a simple yes or no. Much of what I have heard about the past has pained me. It was painful to meet and part from my mother in her suffering state, as I was compelled to do. But knowing my parentage clears up doubts for which I am grateful. As for my position, my gratitude to you for your fatherly care remains unchanged. However, learning that I was born a Jew may have a momentous influence on my life, one I am hardly able to articulate at present."

Daniel spoke the final sentence with a resolve that overcame his diffidence. He felt that the differences between Sir Hugo's nature and his own would soon reveal themselves more sharply than ever before. The baronet cast him a quick glance and turned to walk on. After a moment of silence, during which he weighed the meaning of Daniel's words, he spoke again.

"I have long expected something remarkable from you, Dan; but, for God's sake, do not descend into eccentricities! I can tolerate any difference of opinion, provided a man states it without dressing himself up as a lunatic. In this age, if a man wishes to be taken seriously, he must avoid melodrama. Do not misunderstand me. I do not suspect you of inventing madness on your own. I only fear you might be led arm-in-arm with a lunatic, especially if he sought a defender. You have a passion for those who are pelted, Dan. I pity them too; but as a basis for companionship, it is poor ground. Nevertheless, I do not ask you to anticipate your inclinations. When you decide on a course requiring funds, I have some sixteen thousand pounds accumulating for you, beyond the interest you have received. And now that I am here, do you wish to return to England as soon as possible?"

"I must first go to Mainz to retrieve a chest belonging to my grandfather, and perhaps visit a friend of his," Daniel said. "Though the chest has lain there for twenty years, I feel an unreasonable nervous eagerness to secure it under my care, as if it were more likely now that something might happen to it. And perhaps I am more uneasy because I lingered after my mother left, instead of departing immediately. Yet, I cannot regret staying—otherwise, Mrs. Grandcourt would have had only servants to act for her."

"Yes, yes," Sir Hugo replied, with a flippancy that masked a hidden vexation; "I hope you are not going to set a dead Jew above a living Christian."

Daniel flushed and suppressed a retort as they turned toward the *Italia*.

"Let us bind love with duty; for duty is the love of law; and law is the nature of the Eternal."

These words echoed in Daniel Deronda's mind as he stood before the white-bearded man in the quiet room of the Schuster Strasse in Mainz. The air smelled of old paper and the faint, dusty scent of travel. Joseph Kalonymos sat at a table scattered with open letters, his hands moving with a practiced rhythm until the knock on the door broke the silence. When he saw Deronda, the older man did not immediately rise or offer a handshake. Instead, he looked up with small, penetrating eyes that glittered like black gems against his pale, yellowish skin.

"It is now you who seek me, young man," Kalonymos said in German, his voice sharp yet warm.

"Yes," Deronda replied, feeling the weight of gratitude settle in his chest. "I seek you as a friend of my grandfather's. I owe you a debt for the trouble you have taken on my behalf." He spoke the language with ease, the foreign accents of his upbringing melting into the fluid German tongue.

Kalonymos smiled, extending a hand this time. "Then you are no longer angry at being something more than an Englishman?"

"On the contrary," Deronda said, accepting the gesture. "Thank you for saving me from ignorance of my parentage, and for guarding the chest my grandfather left in trust."

"Sit," Kalonymos urged, gesturing to a chair nearby. He removed his hat, revealing a head thick with white hair, and stroked his beard as he examined Deronda's face. The moment struck Deronda with the force of an electric shock. To be in the presence of a man who had sworn loyalty to his grandfather—a man who, though dead, seemed to speak through these written memorials—felt like touching the very chain of his ancestry. He bore Kalonymos's scrutinizing gaze with a mixture of delight and awe, a sensation akin to witnessing a sacred ritual.

In Hebrew, Kalonymos quoted a hymn: "As thy goodness has been great to the former generations, even so may it be to the latter." He paused, then continued, his voice trembling slightly with emotion. "Young man, I rejoice that I was not yet gone on my travels when you arrived. I see the image of my friend as he was in his youth—no longer perverted from the fellowship of your people, no longer shrinking in proud wrath from the touch of one who claimed you as a Jew. You come with thankfulness to claim the kindred and heritage that wicked contrivance would have stolen. You declare, 'I am the grandson of Daniel Charisi.' Is it not so?"

"Assuredly it is," Deronda confirmed. "But let me say, I would never have treated a Jew with incivility simply because he was a Jew. I merely shrank from telling a stranger, 'I know nothing of my mother.'"

"A sin, a sin!" Kalonymos exclaimed, closing his eyes in disgust. "A robbery of our people! But it is frustrated. I have frustrated it. When Daniel Charisi was a stripling and I a boy little above his shoulder, we made a solemn vow to be friends. He said, 'Let us bind ourselves with duty, as if we were sons of the same mother.' That was his bent—to fortify his soul with bonds. He believed duty was the love of law, and law the nature of the Eternal. We bound ourselves. Though we drifted apart in later life, the bond never broke. When they sought to rob him, they could not rob him of me. I rescued the remainder of him he prized for his offspring, and I have restored the offspring they stole."

Kalonymos left the room briefly and returned with a clerk carrying a chest. It was not large, but heavy with ornamental gilt iron bracers and handles. The wood was beautifully incised with Arabic lettering.

"So!" Kalonymos said, returning to his seat. "And here is the curious key." He produced it from a small leather bag and handed it to Deronda with a monitory, slightly suspicious look—the way age trusts youth with fragile treasures. "Bestow it carefully. I trust you are methodic and wary."

"I shall be more careful of this than of any other property," Deronda smiled, tucking the key into his breast pocket. "I have never possessed anything that signifies so much cherished hope and effort. And I shall never forget that this effort was partly yours."

"Do you have time to hear more of my grandfather? Or am I trespassing?" Deronda asked.

"Stay yet a while," Kalonymos said, glancing at his watch. "In an hour and eighteen minutes I depart for Trieste. My sons will soon expect my attention. Would you like me to introduce you to them? They dwell here in ease and luxury, though I choose to be a wanderer."

"I would be glad if you commended me to their acquaintance for a future opportunity," Deronda replied. "Pressing claims call me to England—friends in need of my presence. I have been away too long by circumstance. But to know more of you and your family would be motive enough to return to Mainz."

"Good! You will hardly find me, for I am beyond my threescore years and ten, a wanderer carrying my shroud with me. But my sons and their children dwell here in wealth and unity. The days have changed since Karl the Great brought my ancestors from Italy to share knowledge with our rough German brethren. We fought for it. Our youth fell on evil days, but we won. We increase our wealth in safety, and the learning of all Germany is fed by Jewish brains—even if they do not always keep their Jewish hearts. Have you been left ignorant of your people's life, young man?"

"No," Deronda admitted. "Before I suspected my parentage, I was led to study everything belonging to their history with more interest than any other subject. It turns out I have been making myself ready to understand my grandfather." He felt a surge of relief; the conversation was circling back to what mattered most.

"So," Kalonymos mused, studying Deronda's face. "You would perhaps have been such a man as he if your education had not hindered. You are like him in features, yet not altogether. He had an iron will in his face; it braced everyone around him. Even young, he had a deep upright line in his brow. I see none of that in you."

Daniel Charisi used to say, "Better a wrong will than a wavering; better a steadfast enemy than an uncertain friend; better a false belief than no belief at all." He despised indifference. He had longer reasons than I can give you."

"Yet his knowledge was not narrow?" Deronda asked, thinking of the common excuse for indecision—that it stems from knowing too much.

"Narrow? No," Kalonymos shook his head with a compassionate smile. "From childhood, he drank in learning as easily as a plant sucks up water. But he early took to medicine and theories about life and health. He traveled to many countries, spending much of his substance to see and know. What he insisted on was that the strength of mankind depended on the balance of separateness and communication. He was bitterly against our people losing themselves among the Gentiles. 'It's no better,' he said, 'than many sorts of grain going back from their variety into sameness.' He mingled all sorts of learning, like our Arabic writers in the golden time. We studied together, but he went beyond me. Though we were bosom friends, we were as different as the inside and outside of a bowl. I stood by two notions of my own; I took Charisi's sayings as one takes the shape of trees—they were there, not to be disputed. It came to the same thing in both of us: we were both faithful Jews, thankful not to be Gentiles."

Kalonymos leaned forward, his voice softening. "Since I was a ripe man, I have been what I am now, for all but age—loving to wander, loving transactions, loving to behold all things, and caring nothing about hardship. Charisi thought continually of our people's future; he went with all his soul into that part of our religion. I, not so. So we have freedom; I am content. Our people wandered before they were driven. When I am in the East, I lie on the deck and watch the greater stars. The sight satisfies me. I know them as they rise, and hunger not to know more. Charisi was satisfied with no sight; he pieced it out with what had been before and what would come after. Yet we loved each other. As he said, he bound our love with duty; we solemnly pledged to help and defend each other to the last. I have fulfilled my pledge."

Kalonymos rose, and Deronda followed suit. "And in being faithful to him, you have caused justice to be done to me. It would have been a robbery of me too to never know of the inheritance he prepared. I thank you with my whole soul."

"Be worthy of him, young man. What is your vocation?" Kalonymos asked abruptly, a question that made Deronda hesitate. He did not feel entirely honest to claim law-reading as a vocation yet.

"I cannot say that I have any," Deronda answered.

"Get one, get one. The Jew must be diligent. You will call yourself a Jew and profess the faith of your fathers?" Kalonymos placed a hand on Deronda's shoulder, his gaze piercing.

"I shall call myself a Jew," Deronda said deliberately, feeling his cheeks grow pale under the scrutiny. "But I will not say I shall profess to believe exactly as my fathers have believed. Our fathers themselves changed the horizon of their belief and learned of other races. But I think I can maintain my grandfather's notion of separateness with communication. I hold that my first duty is to my own people, and if there is anything to be done toward restoring or perfecting their common life, I shall make that my vocation."

At that moment, the need for speech crystallized into resolve. His respect for the questioner prevented him from declining, and in the necessity of answering, he found the truth for himself.

"Ah, you argue and you look forward—you are Daniel Charisi's grandson," Kalonymos said, adding a benediction in Hebrew.

With that, they parted. Almost as soon as Deronda was back in London, the aged man was once again on shipboard, greeting the friendly stars without any eager curiosity, content in his wandering solitude.

Within the gentle heart, Love shelters him,
As birds within the green shade of the grove.
Before the gentle heart, in Nature's scheme,
Love was not, nor the gentle heart ere Love.

The news of Grandcourt's death sent a tremor through two distinct houses that day. In the white house at Pennicote, the shock was contained by Rex Gascoigne's stoicism. But in the modest home of the Meyricks, the same tidings stirred a violent agitation that Mirah struggled desperately to suppress.

Hans Meyrick had a ritual: he delivered the *Times* to his mother, a woman who devoured news from high politics to the latest marriage lists, treating the latter as a way to finish novels without the labor of reading them. Usually, Hans arrived just as Mirah finished teaching her sister Mab, claiming he came to hear her sing. But on this particular Wednesday, he burst into the parlor with a crackle that shattered the air. Mab was mid-verse on *Lascia ch'io pianga*, her imitation of her teacher faltering as the paper tore open. Mirah, playing the accompaniment, jumped, startled by the thunderous noise.

"O-o-o, Hans!" Mab cried, "Why do you bring a more horrible noise than my singing?"

"What on earth is the wonderful news?" Mrs. Meyrick asked, her eyes darting to her daughter. "Italy? The Austrians giving up Venice?"

"Nothing about Italy, but something *from* Italy," Hans replied, his tone carrying a peculiar weight that made his mother uneasy. He held the paper aloft, his expression suggesting a private triumph. "Nothing bad for anyone we care about. Rather uncommonly lucky, I think. I never knew anybody die conveniently before. Considering what a dear gazelle I am, I'm constantly wondering to find myself alive."

"Oh me, Hans!" Mab snapped, impatient. "If you must talk of yourself, let it be behind your own back. What *is* it?"

"Duke Alfonso is drowned, and the Duchess is alive," Hans announced, pointing a finger at a paragraph. "But the real story is this: Deronda was at Genoa in the same hotel. He saw her brought in by the fishermen who saved her. They say she jumped in after her husband—a less judicious action than I'd expect of the Duchess. However, Deronda was there to take care of her. Lucky fellow."

Mirah sank back onto the music stool, her eyelids lowering, her hands clasping tightly in her lap. Mrs. Meyrick handed the paper to Mab. "Poor thing! She must have been fond of her husband to jump in after him."

"It was an inadvertence—a little absence of mind," Hans said, creasing his face roguishly and slouching into a chair near Mirah. "Who can be fond of a jealous baritone with freezing glances, always singing asides? That was the husband's role, depend upon it. Nothing can be neater than his getting drowned. The Duchess is at liberty now to marry a man with a fine head of hair and glances that will melt instead of freezing her. And I shall be invited to the wedding."

Mirah sprang from her seat, her eyes fixing on Hans with an angry gleam. Her voice, deeply shaken, rang with indignation. "Mr. Hans, you ought not to speak in that way. Mr. Deronda would not like you to speak so. Why will you say he is lucky? Why use words of that sort about life and death when what is life to one is death to another? How do you know it would be lucky if he loved Mrs. Grandcourt? It might be a great evil to him. She would take him away from my brother—I know she would. Mr. Deronda would not call that lucky to pierce my brother's heart."

The room fell silent, struck by the sudden transformation. Mirah's face, usually rich of tint, was pale with a look of anger that might have suited Ithuriel. Hans sat transfixed, blushing as if he were a girl, his composure shattering.

"I am a fool and a brute," he stammered, withdrawing every word. "I'll go and hang myself like Judas—if it's allowable to mention him." Even in sorrow, his improvisations retained a drollery, but the damage was done.

Mirah's anger did not abate. She had spoken as creatures in intense pain bite through their own flesh to make the agony bearable. She said no more, seating herself at the piano and pressing the sheet music as if preparing to play, though her hands trembled.

Mab broke the tension. "Mirah is quite right to scold you, Hans. You are always taking Mr. Deronda's name in vain. And it is horrible, joking in that way about his marrying Mrs. Grandcourt. Men's minds must be very black, I think," she concluded with scorn.

"Quite true, my dear," Hans murmured, rising to walk toward the back window, avoiding his mother's gaze.

"We had better go on, Mab; you have not given your full time to the lesson," Mirah said, her tone higher than usual. "Will you sing this again, or shall I sing it to you?"

"Oh, please sing it to me," Mab rejoiced, eager to ignore the storm that had passed.

Mirah sang *Lascia ch'io pianga*, pouring forth its melodious sobs with new fullness and energy. Hans paused, leaning against the mantelpiece, keeping his eyes carefully averted from his mother. When the final chord faded, Mirah rose. "I must go home now. Ezra expects me."

She offered her hand silently to Mrs. Meyrick, hesitating, unable to look at her, instead of the usual kiss. The little mother drew Mirah's face down, soothingly whispering, "God bless you, my dear." Mirah felt a sharp pang of compunction, sensing she had committed an offense against her mother by rebuking Hans, mixing her suffering with a sense of proud ingratitude. Her friend had divined this guilt.

Hans seized his wide-awake, ready to open the door. "Now, Hans," Mab said, her sisterly tenderness cunningly disguised, "you are not going to walk home with Mirah. I am sure she would rather not. You are so dreadfully disagreeable to-day."

"I shall go to take care of her, if she does not forbid me," Hans said, opening the door.

Mirah said nothing. As he opened the outer door and closed it behind him, he walked by her side unforbidden. She lacked the courage to speak again, conscious that her words had been unbecomingly severe, yet finding only severer words behind them in her heart. A crowd of thoughts thrust forward, interpreting the consciousness that remained unaltered within her.

Hans's mind was equally busy. Mirah's anger had awakened a new perception in him, accompanied by the unpleasant realization that he was a dolt for not seeing it sooner. He wondered if Mirah's heart was entirely preoccupied with Deronda in a character other than that of her brother's benefactor. The supposition brought anxieties that, to his credit, were not wholly selfish. Hans was convinced of a serious attachment between Deronda and Mrs. Grandcourt. He had pieced together fragments of observation, completed by what his sisters heard from Anna Gascoigne, convincing him not only of the Duchess's passion but also of Deronda's concealed love, masked by austere self-repression. Some men, holding such a conviction, would avoid allusions that roused such susceptibility; but Hans's talk naturally fluttered toward mischief, a form of experiment on live animals consisting in irritating his friends playfully. His experiments had satisfied him that what he thought likely was true.

On the other hand, any susceptibility Deronda showed regarding attentions to Mirah, Hans dismissed as accounted for by her dependent position. He credited his friend with unselfish anxiety for those he could rescue. Deronda's insistence that Mirah would never marry one who was not a Jew seemed to exclude himself, since Hans shared the ordinary opinion that Deronda was the son of Sir Hugo Mallinger.

Thus, Hans felt clear about Deronda's affections. But now, the events striking him as conducive to a union between Deronda and Mrs. Grandcourt had called forth a flash of revelation from Mirah—a betrayal of her passionate feeling that made him melancholy for her, as well as for himself. Yet, on the whole, he felt less melancholy than if he had imagined Deronda's hopes fixed solely on her. It was common, he reasoned, for a man to see the beloved object unhappy because his rival loves another, with more fortitude and milder jealousy than if he saw her entirely happy in his rival. At least, it was so with the mercurial Hans, who fluctuated between contradictory states: wounded because Mirah was wounded, and almost obliged to Deronda for loving somebody else. Impossible to give Mirah a direct sign of how he understood her anger, he longed for his speechless companionship to be eloquent in a tender, penitent sympathy, an admissible form of wooing a bruised heart.

Thus, the two walked side by side in a companionship that seemed an agitated communication, like two chords whose quick vibrations lie outside our hearing. When they reached Mirah's home, and Hans said "Good-bye," putting out his hand with an appealing look of penitence, she met the look with melancholy gentleness. "Will you not come in and see my brother?"

Hans could not but interpret this as a sign of pardon. He lacked the understanding of what Mirah's nature had been wrought into by her early experience to divine how the strength of her late excitement made it pass quickly into the resolute acceptance of pain. When he said, "If you will let me," and they went in together, half his grief vanished, replaced by a little romance of how his devotion might make him indispensable to Mirah in proportion as Deronda gave his elsewhere. This was fair, since his friend was provided for according to his own heart; and on the question of Judaism, Hans felt fortified: who ever heard in tale or history that a woman's love went in the track of her race and religion? Moslem and Jewish damsels were always attracted toward Christians, and now, if Mirah's heart had gone forth too precipitately toward Deronda, here was another case in point. Hans was wont to make merry with his own arguments, calling himself a Giaour and antithesis the sole clue to events, but he believed a little in what he laughed at. Thus, his bird-like hope, constructed on the lightest principles, soared again in spite of heavy circumstances.

They found Mordecai looking singularly happy, holding a closed letter, his eyes glowing with a quiet triumph that, in his emaciated face, gave the idea of a conquest over assailing death. After the greeting between him and Hans, Mirah put her arm round her brother's neck and looked down at the letter without the courage to ask about it, though she felt sure it was the cause of his joy.

"A letter from Daniel Deronda," Mordecai said, answering her look. "Brief—only saying that he hopes soon to return. Unexpected claims have detained him. The promise of seeing him again is like the bow in the cloud to me," he continued, looking at Hans. "And to you it must be a gladness. For who has two friends like him?"

While Hans answered, Mirah slipped away to her own room. She did not go to indulge in an outburst of passion. If angels, once supposed to watch the toilet of women, had entered the little chamber with her and let her shut the door behind them, they would only have seen her take off her hat, sit down, and press her hands against her temples as if she had suddenly reflected that her head ached. Then she would rise to dash cold water on her eyes, brow, and hair until her backward curls were full of crystal beads, drying her brow and looking out like a freshly-opened flower from among the dewy tresses of the woodland. Then, giving deep sighs of relief, she would put on her little slippers, sit still for a couple of minutes—an eternity to her, so full of things to come—and rise with an air of recollection to make tea.

Something of the old life had returned. She had been used to remember she must learn her part, go to rehearsal, act and sing in the evening, and hide her feelings from her father. The more painful her life grew, the more she had been used to hide. The force of her nature had long found its chief action in resolute endurance, and today the violence of feeling that caused the first jet of anger had quickly transformed itself into a steady facing of trouble, the well-known companion of her young years. But while she moved about and spoke as usual, a close observer might discern a difference between this apparent calm, the effect of restraining energy, and the sweet genuine calm of the months when she first felt a return of her infantine happiness.

Those who have been indulged by fortune and have always thought of calamity as what happens to others feel a blind, incredulous rage at the reversal of their lot, half believing their wild cries will alter the course of the storm. Mirah felt no such surprise when familiar Sorrow came back from brief absence and sat down with her according to the old use and wont. This habit of expecting trouble rather than joy hindered her from having any persistent belief in opposition to the probabilities which were not merely suggested by Hans, but were supported by her own private knowledge and long-growing presentiment. An attachment between Deronda and Mrs. Grandcourt, ending in their future marriage, had the aspect of a certainty for her feeling. There had been no fault in him; facts had ordered themselves so that there was a tie between him and this woman who belonged to another world than hers and Ezra's—nay, who seemed another sort of being than Deronda, something foreign that would be a disturbance in his life instead of blending with it. Well, well—but if it could have been deferred so as to make no difference while Ezra was there! She did not know all the momentousness of the relation between Deronda and her brother, but she had seen, and instinctively felt enough to forebode

"Fortune is a fickle maid,
Who loves not to abide;
She brushes from your brow the cloud,
And kisses, then is gone.

But Misery, in contrast,
Clings close and holds you fast;
She says she has no haste to go,
And sits beside your bed to knit."

What Mirah had lately watched for as the fulfillment of a threat now manifested itself: the familiar sorrow returned, bringing with it an abundance of heavy baggage.

Having emerged from Knightsbridge after singing at a charitable morning concert in a wealthy household—where she had been recommended by Klesmer, and where groups of onlookers had gathered to see the departing company—she suddenly felt herself dogged by footsteps keeping an even pace with her own. Her concert dress was simple black, covered by a dust-cloak; it could not make her an object of unpleasant attention, nor render walking an imprudence. Yet this reflection did not occur to Mirah. Another, deeper alarm lay uppermost in her mind. She immediately thought of her father, and could no more look around than if she felt herself tracked by a ghost. To turn and face him would be voluntarily to meet the rush of emotions that beforehand seemed intolerable. If it were her father, he must mean to claim recognition, and he would oblige her to face him. She must wait for that compulsion. She walked on, not quickening her pace—for what use was that?—but picturing what was about to happen as if she possessed full certainty that the man behind her was her father. Along with this picturing went a regret that she had given her word to Mrs. Meyrick not to use any concealment regarding him. This regret finally urged her, at least, to try to hinder any sudden betrayal that would cause her brother an unnecessary shock. Under the pressure of this motive, she resolved to turn before she reached her own door, and firmly will the encounter instead of merely submitting to it. She had already reached the entrance of the small square where her home lay, and had made up her mind to turn, when she felt her embodied presentiment growing closer, then slipping to her side, grasping her wrist, and saying, with a persuasive curl of accent, "Mirah!"

She paused at once without any start; it was the voice she expected, and she was meeting the expected eyes. Her face was as grave as if she had been looking at her executioner, while his was adjusted to the intention of soothing and propitiating her. Once a handsome face, with bright color, it was now sallow and deep-lined, bearing that peculiar impress of impudent suavity which comes from courting favor while accepting disrespect. He was lightly made and active, with something of youth about him which made the signs of age seem a disguise; in reality, he was hardly fifty-seven. His dress was shabby, as when she had seen him before. The presence of this irreverent father now affected Mirah with a mingled anguish of shame and grief, repulsion and pity—more than ever, now that her own world had changed into one where there was no comradeship to fence him from scorn and contempt.

Slowly, with a sad, tremulous voice, she said, "It is you, father."

"Why did you run away from me, child?" he began with rapid speech meant to have a tone of tender remonstrance, accompanied by various quick gestures like an abbreviated finger-language. "What were you afraid of? You knew I never made you do anything against your will. It was for your sake I broke up your engagement in the Vorstadt, because I saw it didn't suit you, and you repaid me by leaving me to the bad times that came in consequence. I had made an easier engagement for you at the Vorstadt Theater in Dresden; I didn't tell you because I wanted to take you by surprise. And you left me planted there—obliged to make myself scarce because I had broken contract. That was hard lines for me, after I had given up everything for the sake of getting you an education which was to be a fortune to you. What father devoted himself to his daughter more than I did to you? You know how I bore that disappointment in your voice, and made the best of it: and when I had nobody besides you, and was getting broken, as a man must who has had to fight his way with his brains—you chose that time to leave me. Who else was it you owed everything to, if not to me? and where was your feeling in return? For what my daughter cared, I might have died in a ditch."

Lapidoth stopped short here, not from lack of invention, but because he had reached a pathetic climax, and gave a sudden sob, like a woman's, taking out hastily an old yellow silk handkerchief. He truly felt that his daughter had treated him ill—a sort of sensibility naturally strong in unscrupulous persons, who put down what is owing to them without any *per contra*. Mirah, in spite of that sob, had energy enough not to let him suppose that he deceived her. She answered more firmly, though it was the first time she had ever used accusing words to him.

"You know why I left you, father; and I had reason to distrust you, because I felt sure that you had deceived my mother. If I could have trusted you, I would have stayed with you and worked for you."

"I never meant to deceive your mother, Mirah," said Lapidoth, putting back his handkerchief, but beginning with a voice that seemed to struggle against further sobbing. "I meant to take you back to her, but chances hindered me just at the time, and then there came information of her death. It was better for you that I should stay where I was, and your brother could take care of himself. Nobody had any claim on me but you. I had word of your mother's death from a particular friend, who had undertaken to manage things for me, and I sent him over money to pay expenses. There's one chance to be sure—" Lapidoth had quickly conceived that he must guard against something unlikely, yet possible—"he may have written me lies for the sake of getting the money out of me."

Mirah made no answer; she could not bear to utter the only true one—"I don't believe one word of what you say"—and she simply showed a wish that they should walk on, feeling that their standing still might draw down unpleasant notice. Even as they walked along, their companionship might well have made a passer-by turn back to look at them. The figure of Mirah, with her beauty set off by the quiet, careful dress of an English lady, made a strange pendant to this shabby, foreign-looking, eager, and gesticulating man, who withal had an ineffaceable jauntiness of air, perhaps due to the bushy curls of his grizzled hair, the smallness of his hands and feet, and his light walk.

"You seem to have done well for yourself, Mirah? *You* are in no want, I see," said the father, looking at her with emphatic examination.

"Good friends who found me in distress have helped me to get work," said Mirah, hardly knowing what she actually said, from being occupied with what she would presently have to say. "I give lessons. I have sung in private houses. I have just been singing at a private concert." She paused, and then added, with significance, "I have very good friends, who know all about me."

"And you would be ashamed they should see your father in this plight? No wonder. I came to England with no prospect, but the chance of finding you. It was a mad quest; but a father's heart is superstitious—feels a loadstone drawing it somewhere or other. I might have done very well, staying abroad: when I hadn't you to take care of, I could have rolled or settled as easily as a ball; but it's hard being lonely in the world, when your spirit's beginning to break. And I thought my little Mirah would repent leaving her father when she came to look back. I've had a sharp pinch to work my way; I don't know what I shall come down to next. Talents like mine are no use in this country. When a man's getting out at elbows nobody will believe in him. I couldn't get any decent employ with my appearance. I've been obliged to get pretty low for a shilling already."

Mirah's anxiety was quick enough to imagine her father's sinking into a further degradation, which she was bound to hinder if she could. But before she could answer his string of inventive sentences, delivered with as much glibness as if they had been learned by rote, he added promptly,

"Where do you live, Mirah?"

"Here, in this square. We are not far from the house."

"In lodgings?"

"Yes."

"Any one to take care of you?"

"Yes," said Mirah again, looking full at the keen face which was turned toward hers—"my brother."

The father's eyelids fluttered as if the lightning had come across them, and there was a slight movement of the shoulders. But he said, after a just perceptible pause: "Ezra? How did you know—how did you find him?"

"That would take long to tell. Here we are at the door. My brother would not wish me to close it on you."

Mirah was already on the doorstep, but had her face turned toward her father, who stood below her on the pavement. Her heart had begun to beat faster with the prospect of what was coming in the presence of Ezra; and already in this attitude of giving leave to the father whom she had been used to obey—in this sight of him standing below her, with a perceptible shrinking from the admission which he had been indirectly asking for—she had a pang of the peculiar, sympathetic humiliation and shame—the stabbed heart of reverence—which belongs to a nature intensely filial.

"Stay a minute, *Liebchen*," said Lapidoth, speaking in a lowered tone; "what sort of man has Ezra turned out?"

"A good man—a wonderful man," said Mirah, with slow emphasis, trying to master the agitation which made her voice more tremulous as she went on. She felt urged to prepare her father for the complete penetration of himself which awaited him. "But he was very poor when my friends found him for me—a poor workman. Once—twelve years ago—he was strong and happy, going to the East, which he loved to think of; and my mother called him back because—because she had lost me. And he went to her, and took care of her through great trouble, and worked for her till she died—died in grief. And Ezra, too, had lost his health and strength. The cold had seized him coming back to my mother, because she was forsaken. For years he has been getting weaker—always poor, always working—but full of knowledge, and great-minded. All who come near him honor him. To stand before him is like standing before a prophet of God"—Mirah ended with difficulty, her heart throbbing—"falsehoods are no use."

She had cast down her eyes that she might not see her father while she spoke the last words—unable to bear the ignoble look of frustration that gathered in his face. But he was none the less quick in invention and decision.

"Mirah, *Liebchen*," he said, in the old caressing way, "shouldn't you like me to make myself a little more respectable before my son sees me? If I had a little sum of money, I could fit myself out and come home to you as your father ought, and then I could offer myself for some decent place. With a good shirt and coat on my back, people would be glad enough to have me. I could offer myself for a courier, if I didn't look like a broken-down mountebank. I should like to be with my children, and forget and forgive. But you have never seen your father look like this before. If you had ten pounds at hand—or I could appoint you to bring it me somewhere—I could fit myself out by the day after to-morrow."

Mirah felt herself under a temptation which she must try to overcome. She answered, obliging herself to look at him again,

"I don't like to deny you what you ask, father; but I have given a promise not to do things for you in secret. It *is* hard to see you looking needy; but we will bear that for a little while; and then you can have new clothes, and we can pay for them." Her practical sense made her see now what was Mrs. Meyrick's wisdom in exacting a promise from her.

Lapidoth's good humor gave way a little. He said, with a sneer, "You are a hard and fast young lady—you have been learning useful virtues—keeping promises not to help your father with a pound or two when you are getting money to dress yourself in silk—your father who made an idol of you, and gave up the best part of his life to providing for you."

"It seems cruel—I know it seems cruel," said Mirah, feeling this a worse moment than when she meant to drown herself. Her lips were suddenly pale. "But, father, it is more cruel to break the promises people trust in. That broke my mother's heart—it has broken Ezra's life. You and I must eat now this bitterness from what has been. Bear it. Bear to come in and be cared for as you are."

"To-morrow, then," said Lapidoth, almost turning on his heel away from this pale, trembling daughter, who seemed now to have got the inconvenient world to back her; but he quickly turned on it again, with his hands feeling about restlessly in his pockets, and said, with some return to his appealing tone, "I'm a little cut up with all this, Mirah. I shall get up my spirits by to-morrow. If you've a little money in your pocket, I suppose it isn't against your promise to give me a trifle—to buy a cigar with."

Mirah could not ask herself another question—could not do anything else than put her cold trembling hands in her pocket for her *portemonnaie* and hold it out. Lapidoth grasped it at once, pressed her fingers the while, said, "Good-bye, my little girl

"Moses, despite his opposition to art, was himself a great artist and possessed the true artistic spirit. Yet this spirit in him, as in his Egyptian countrymen, was directed solely toward the colossal and the imperishable. But unlike the Egyptians, who shaped their works from brick and granite, he built pyramids of men, hewed obelisks from human lives; he took a poor shepherd tribe and forged it into a people destined to defy the centuries... he created Israel!"—HEINE: *Confessions*.

Consider the shift in Daniel Deronda's inner landscape between his departure from England and his return. He had sailed for Genoa shrouded in a fog of uncertainty, unsure how far his deepest affections would be nurtured or how the claims laid bare before him might pull him into paths diverging sharply from the ones his mind had recently followed with a hesitant, dangerous consent. He returned, however, bearing something akin to a discovered charter that validated the inherited right his ambition had longed for. He came back not merely with freedom, but with a dutiful bond that his experience had prepared him to embrace gladly, even if it offered no promise of satisfying a secret, passionate longing that had never yet grown into hope. Now, he dared to acknowledge to himself the specific selection of his love. Since the hour he left the Chelsea house in full-hearted silence under the weight of Mirah's farewell look and words—their exquisite appeal stirring in him that deep-seated care for womanhood that had begun when his own lips were still childish—her hold on his feelings had fortified him to remain blameless in word and deed amidst the difficult circumstances they shared. There seemed no likelihood he could ever court this creature who had become dear to him amidst associations that forbade courtship; yet she had taken her place in his soul as a beloved ideal, diminishing the power of other fascinations and creating a void where they once stood. This influence had grown steadily stronger. It was the course of poor Gwendolen's lot that her dependence on Deronda tended to arouse in him an enthusiasm of self-sacrificing pity rather than personal love, while his less constrained tenderness flowed toward an indwelling image in all things unlike Gwendolen. Furthermore, his relationship with Mordecai had brought a new closeness to Mirah, one that was no less agitating because there appeared to be no change in his official position toward her; she was inevitably bound up in all the thoughts that made him shrink from an outcome disappointing to her brother. This process had not occurred unconsciously in Deronda; he was aware of it as one is of a covetousness that would be better nullified by encouraging other thoughts than by giving it the insistency of confession even to oneself: but the jealous fire leaped out at Hans's pretensions, and when his mother accused him of loving a Jewess, every evasion suddenly seemed an act of infidelity. His mother had compelled him to a decisive acknowledgment of his love, just as Joseph Kalonymos had forced him to a definite expression of his resolve. This new state of decision wrought upon Deronda with a force that surprised even himself. There was a release of all the energy long spent in self-checking and suppression due to doubtful conditions; and he was ready to laugh at his own impetuosity when, nearing England on his way from Mainz, he felt the remaining distance become increasingly an obstruction. It was as if he had found an added soul in discovering his ancestry—his judgment no longer wandering in the mazes of impartial sympathy, but choosing, with that partiality which is man's greatest strength, the closer fellowship that makes sympathy practical. He exchanged that bird's-eye reasonableness which soars to avoid preference and loses all sense of quality for the generous reasonableness of standing shoulder to shoulder with men of like inheritance. He wanted now to be with Mordecai again, to pour forth instead of restraining his feeling, to admit agreement and maintain dissent, all while finding Mirah's presence without the embarrassment of obviously seeking it, to see her in the light of a new possibility, to interpret her looks and words from a new starting point. He was not greatly alarmed by the effect of Hans's attentions, but he had a presentiment that her feeling toward him had from the first lain in a channel unlikely to be diverted into love. To astonish a woman by turning into her lover when she has been thinking of you merely as a Lord Chancellor is what a man naturally shrinks from; he is anxious to create an easier transition.

No wonder, then, that Deronda saw no other course than to go straight from the London railway station to the lodgings in that small square in Brompton. Every argument favored his losing no time. He had promised to run down the next day to see Lady Mallinger at the Abbey, and it was already sunset. He wished to deposit the precious chest with Mordecai, who would study its contents, both in his absence and in company with him; and that he should pay this visit without pause would gratify Mordecai's heart. Hence, and for other reasons, it gratified Deronda's heart. The strongest tendencies of his nature were rushing in one current—the fervent affectionateness which made him delight in meeting the wish of beings near to him, and the imaginative need of some far-reaching relation to make the horizon of his immediate, daily acts. It must be admitted that in this classical, romantic, world-historic position of his, bringing as it were from its hiding-place his hereditary armor, he wore—but so, one must suppose, did the most ancient heroes, whether Semitic or Japhetic—the summer costume of his contemporaries. He did not reflect that the drab tints were becoming to him, for he rarely went to the expense of such thinking; but his own depth of coloring, which made the becomingness, got an added radiance in the eyes, a fleeting and returning glow in the skin, as he entered the house wondering what exactly he should find. He made his entrance as noiseless as possible.

It was the evening of that same afternoon on which Mirah had had the interview with her father. Mordecai, penetrated by her grief, and also the sad memories which the incident had awakened, had not resumed his task of sifting papers: some of them had fallen scattered on the floor in the first moments of anxiety, and neither he nor Mirah had thought of laying them in order again. They had sat perfectly still together, not knowing how long; while the clock ticked on the mantel-piece, and the light was fading, Mirah, unable to think of the food she ought to have been taking, had not moved since she had thrown off her dust-cloak and sat down beside Mordecai with her hand in his, while he had laid his head backward, with closed eyes and difficult breathing, looking, Mirah thought, as he would look when the soul within him could no longer live in its straitened home. The thought that his death might be near was continually visiting her when she saw his face in this way, without its vivid animation; and now, to the rest of her grief, was added the regret that she had been unable to control the violent outburst which had shaken him. She sat watching him—her oval cheeks pallid, her eyes with the sorrowful brilliancy left by young tears, her curls in as much disorder as a just-awakened child's—watching that emaciated face, where it might have been imagined that a veil had been drawn never to be lifted, as if it were her dead joy which had left her strong enough to live on in sorrow. And life at that moment stretched before Mirah with more than a repetition of former sadness. The shadow of the father was there, and more than that, a double bereavement—of one living as well as one dead.

But now the door was opened, and while none entered, a well-known voice said: "Daniel Deronda—may he come in?"

"Come! come!" said Mordecai, immediately rising with an irradiated face and opened eyes—apparently as little surprised as if he had seen Deronda in the morning, and expected this evening visit; while Mirah started up blushing with confused, half-alarmed expectation.

Yet when Deronda entered, the sight of him was like the clearness after rain: no clouds to come could hinder the cherishing beam of that moment. As he held out his right hand to Mirah, who was close to her brother's left, he laid his other hand on Mordecai's right shoulder, and stood so a moment, holding them both at once, uttering no word, but reading their faces, till he said anxiously to Mirah, "Has anything happened?—any trouble?"

"Talk not of trouble now," said Mordecai, saving her from the need to answer. "There is joy in your face—let the joy be ours."

Mirah thought, "It is for something he cannot tell us." But they all sat down, Deronda drawing a chair close in front of Mordecai.

"That is true," he said, emphatically. "I have a joy which will remain to us even in the worst trouble. I did not tell you the reason of my journey abroad, Mordecai, because—never mind—I went to learn my parentage. And you were right. I am a Jew."

The two men clasped hands with a movement that seemed part of the flash from Mordecai's eyes, and passed through Mirah like an electric shock. But Deronda went on without pause, speaking from Mordecai's mind as much as from his own,

"We have the same people. Our souls have the same vocation. We shall not be separated by life or by death."

Mordecai's answer was uttered in Hebrew, and in no more than a loud whisper. It was in the liturgical words which express the religious bond: "Our God and the God of our fathers."

The weight of feeling pressed too strongly on that ready-winged speech which usually moved in quick adaptation to every stirring of his fervor.

Mirah fell on her knees by her brother's side, and looked at his now illuminated face, which had just before been so deathly. The action was an inevitable outlet of the violent reversal from despondency to a gladness which came over her as solemnly as if she had been beholding a religious rite. For the moment she thought of the effect on her own life only through the effect on her brother.

"And it is not only that I am a Jew," Deronda went on, enjoying one of those rare moments when our yearnings and our acts can be completely one, and the real we behold is our ideal good; "but I come of a strain that has ardently maintained the fellowship of our race—a line of Spanish Jews that has borne many students and men of practical power. And I possess what will give us a sort of communion with them. My grandfather, Daniel Charisi, preserved manuscripts, family records stretching far back, in the hope that they would pass into the hands of his grandson. And now his hope is fulfilled, in spite of attempts to thwart it by hiding my parentage from me. I possess the chest containing them, with his own papers, and it is down below in this house.
