I mean to leave it with you, Mordecai, that you may help me to study the manuscripts. Some of them I can read easily enough—those in Spanish and Italian. Others are in Hebrew, and, I think, Arabic; but there seem to be Latin translations. I was only able to look at them cursorily while I stayed at Mainz. We will study them together."

Deronda ended with that bright smile which, beaming out from the habitual gravity of his face, seemed a revelation (the reverse of the continual smile that discredits all expression). But when this happy glance passed from Mordecai to rest on Mirah, it acted like a little too much sunshine, and made her change her attitude. She had knelt under an impulse with which any personal embarrassment was incongruous, and especially any thoughts about how Mrs. Grandcourt might stand to this new aspect of things—thoughts which made her color under Deronda's glance, and rise to take her seat again in her usual posture of crossed hands and feet, with the effort to look as quiet as possible. Deronda, equally sensitive, imagined that the feeling of which he was conscious, had entered too much into his eyes, and had been repugnant to her. He was ready enough to believe that any unexpected manifestation might spoil her feeling toward him—and then his precious relation to brother and sister would be marred. If Mirah could have no love for him, any advances of love on his part would make her wretched in that continual contact with him which would remain inevitable.

While such feelings were pulsating quickly in Deronda and Mirah, Mordecai, seeing nothing in his friend's presence and words but a blessed fulfillment, was already speaking with his old sense of enlargement in utterance,

"Daniel, from the first, I have said to you, we know not all the pathways. Has there not been a meeting among them, as of the operations in one soul, where an idea being born and breathing draws the elements toward it, and is fed and glows? For all things are bound together in that Omnipresence which is the place and habitation of the world, and events are of a glass wherethrough our eyes see some of the pathways. And if it seems that the erring and unloving wills of men have helped to prepare you, as Moses was prepared, to serve your people the better, that depends on another order than the law which must guide our footsteps. For the evil will of man makes not a people's good except by stirring the righteous will of man; and beneath all the clouds with which our thought encompasses the Eternal, this is clear—that a people can be blessed only by having counsellors and a multitude whose will moves in obedience to the laws of justice and love. For see, now, it was your loving will that made a chief pathway, and resisted the effect of evil; for, by performing the duties of brotherhood to my sister, and seeking out her brother in the flesh, your soul has been prepared to receive with gladness this message of the Eternal, 'behold the multitude of your brethren.'"

"It is quite true that you and Mirah have been my teachers," said Deronda. "If this revelation had been made to me before I knew you both, I think my mind would have rebelled against it. Perhaps I should have felt then—

"Questa montagna è tale,
Che sempre al cominciar di sotto è grave.
E quanto uom più va su e men fa male."
—DANTE: *Il Purgatorio*

It was not many days after her mother's arrival that Gwendolen consented to remain at Genoa. Her desperate desire to flee that jewel of the sea helped to rally her fading strength and courage. For what place, though it were the flowery vale of Enna, may not the inward sense turn into a circle of punishment where the flowers are no better than a crop of flame-tongues burning the soles of our feet?

"I shall never like to see the Mediterranean again," said Gwendolen to her mother, who thought she quite understood her child's feeling—even in her tacit prohibition of any express reference to her late husband.

Mrs. Davilow, indeed, though formally compelled to regard this time as one of severe calamity, was virtually enjoying her life more than she had ever done since her daughter's marriage. It seemed that her darling was brought back to her not merely with all the old affection, but with a conscious cherishing of her mother's nearness, such as we give to a possession that we have been on the brink of losing.

"Are you there, mamma?" cried Gwendolen in the middle of the night—a bed had been made for her mother in the same room with hers—very much as she would have done in her early girlhood, if she had felt frightened in lying awake.

"Yes, dear; can I do anything for you?"

"No, thank you; only I like so to know you are there. Do you mind my waking you?" (This question would hardly have been Gwendolen's in her early girlhood.)

"I was not asleep, darling."

"It seemed not real that you were with me. I wanted to make it real. I can bear things if you are with me. But you must not lie awake, anxious about me. You must be happy now. You must let me make you happy now at last—else what shall I do?"

"God bless you, dear; I have the best happiness I can have, when you make much of me."

But the next night, hearing that she was sighing and restless, Mrs. Davilow said, "Let me give you your sleeping-draught, Gwendolen."

"No, mamma, thank you; I don't want to sleep."

"It would be so good for you to sleep more, my darling."

"Don't say what would be good for me, mamma," Gwendolen answered impetuously. "You don't know what would be good for me. You and my uncle must not contradict me and tell me anything is good for me when I feel it is not good."

Mrs. Davilow was silent, not wondering that the poor child was irritable. Presently Gwendolen said, "I was always naughty to you, mamma."

"No, dear, no."

"Yes, I was," said Gwendolen insistently. "It is because I was always wicked that I am miserable now."

She burst into sobs and cries. The determination to be silent about all the facts of her married life and its close reacted in these escapes of enigmatic excitement.

But dim lights of interpretation were breaking on the mother's mind through the information that came from Sir Hugo to Mr. Gascoigne, and, with some omissions, from Mr. Gascoigne to herself. The good-natured baronet, while attending to all decent measures in relation to his nephew's death and the possible washing ashore of the body, thought it the kindest thing he could do to use his present friendly intercourse with the rector as an opportunity for communicating with him, in the mildest way, the purport of Grandcourt's will, so as to save him the additional shock that would be in store for him if he carried his illusions all the way home. Perhaps Sir Hugo would have been communicable enough without that kind motive, but he really felt the motive. He broke the unpleasant news to the rector by degrees: at first he only implied his fear that the widow was not so splendidly provided for as Mr. Gascoigne, nay, as the baronet himself had expected; and only at last, after some previous vague reference to large claims on Grandcourt, he disclosed the prior relations which, in the unfortunate absence of a legitimate heir, had determined all the splendor in another direction.

The rector was deeply hurt, and remembered, more vividly than he had ever done before, how offensively proud and repelling the manners of the deceased had been toward him—remembered also that he himself, in that interesting period just before the arrival of the new occupant at Diplow, had received hints of former entangling dissipations, and an undue addiction to pleasure, though he had not foreseen that the pleasure which had probably, so to speak, been swept into private rubbish-heaps, would ever present itself as an array of live caterpillars, disastrous to the green meat of respectable people. But he did not make these retrospective thoughts audible to Sir Hugo, or lower himself by expressing any indignation on merely personal grounds, but behaved like a man of the world who had become a conscientious clergyman. His first remark was, "When a young man makes his will in health, he usually counts on living a long while. Probably Mr. Grandcourt did not believe that this will would ever have its present effect." After a moment, he added, "The effect is painful in more ways than one. Female morality is likely to suffer from this marked advantage and prominence being given to illegitimate offspring."

"Well, in point of fact," said Sir Hugo, in his comfortable way, "since the boy is there, this was really the best alternative for the disposal of the estates. Grandcourt had nobody nearer than his cousin. And it's a chilling thought that you go out of this life only for the benefit of a cousin. A man gets a little pleasure in making his will, if it's for the good of his own curly heads; but it's a nuisance when you're giving the bequeathing to a used-up fellow like yourself, and one you don't care two straws for. It's the next worse thing to having only a life interest in your estates. No; I forgive Grandcourt for that part of his will. But, between ourselves, what I don't forgive him for, is the shabby way he has provided for your niece—_our_ niece, I will say—no better a position than if she had been a doctor's widow. Nothing grates on me more than that posthumous grudgingness toward a wife. A man ought to have some pride and fondness for his widow. _I_ should, I know. I take it as a test of a man, that he feels the easier about his death when he can think of his wife and daughters being comfortable after it. I like that story of the fellows in the Crimean war, who were ready to go to the bottom of the sea if their widows were provided for."

"It has certainly taken me by surprise," said Mr. Gascoigne, "all the more because, as the one who stood in the place of father to my niece, I had shown my reliance on Mr. Grandcourt's apparent liberality in money matters by making no claims for her beforehand. That seemed to me due to him under the circumstances. Probably you think me blamable."

"Not blamable exactly. I respect a man for trusting another. But take my advice. If you marry another niece, though it may be to the Archbishop of Canterbury, bind him down. Your niece can't be married for the first time twice over. And if he's a good fellow, he'll wish to be bound. But as to Mrs. Grandcourt, I can only say that I feel my relation to her all the nearer because I think that she has not been well treated. And I hope you will urge her to rely on me as a friend."

Thus spake the chivalrous Sir Hugo, in his disgust at the young and beautiful widow of a Mallinger Grandcourt being left with only two thousand a year and a house in a coal-mining district. To the rector that income naturally appeared less shabby and less accompanied with mortifying privations; but in this conversation he had devoured a much keener sense than the baronet's of the humiliation cast over his niece, and also over her nearest friends, by the conspicuous publishing of her husband's relation to Mrs. Glasher. And like all men who are good husbands and fathers, he felt the humiliation through the minds of the women who would be chiefly affected by it; so that the annoyance of first hearing the facts was far slighter than what he felt in communicating them to Mrs. Davilow, and in anticipating Gwendolen's feeling whenever her mother saw fit to tell her of them. For the good rector had an innocent conviction that his niece was unaware of Mrs. Glasher's existence, arguing with masculine soundness from what maidens and wives were likely to know, do, and suffer, and having had a most imperfect observation of the particular maiden and wife in question. Not so Gwendolen's mother, who now thought that she saw an explanation of much that had been enigmatic in her child's conduct and words before and after her engagement, concluding that in some inconceivable way Gwendolen had been informed of this left-handed marriage and the existence of the children. She trusted to opportunities that would arise in moments of affectionate confidence before and during their journey to England, when she might gradually learn how far the actual state of things was clear to Gwendolen, and prepare her for anything that might be a disappointment. But she was spared from devices on the subject.

"I hope you don't expect that I am going to be rich and grand, mamma," said Gwendolen, not long after the rector's communication, "perhaps I shall have nothing at all."

She was dressed, and had been sitting long in quiet meditation. Mrs. Davilow was startled, but said, after a moment's reflection, "Oh yes, dear, you will have something. Sir Hugo knows all about the will."

"That will not decide," said Gwendolen, abruptly.

"Surely, dear: Sir Hugo says you are to have two thousand a year and the house at Gadsmere."

"What I have will depend on what I accept," said Gwendolen. "You and my uncle must not attempt to cross me and persuade me about this. I will do everything I can do to make you happy, but in anything about my husband I must not be interfered with. Is eight hundred a year enough for you, mamma?"

"More than enough, dear. You must not think of giving me so much." Mrs. Davilow paused a little, and then said, "Do you know who is to have the estates and the rest of the money?"

"Yes," said Gwendolen, waving her hand in dismissal of the subject. "I know everything. It is all perfectly right, and I wish never to have it mentioned."

The mother was silent, looked away, and rose to fetch a fan-screen, with a slight flush on her delicate cheeks. Wondering, imagining, she did not like to meet her daughter's eyes, and sat down again under a sad constraint. What wretchedness her child had perhaps gone through, which yet must remain as it always had been, locked away from their mutual speech. But Gwendolen was watching her mother with that new divination which experience had given her; and in tender relenting at her own peremptoriness, said, "Come and sit nearer to me, mamma, and don't be unhappy."

Mrs. Davilow did as she was told, but bit her lips in the vain attempt to hinder smarting tears. Gwendolen leaned toward her caressingly and said, "I mean to be very wise; I do, really. And good—oh, so good to you, dear, old, sweet mamma, you won't know me. Only you must not cry."

The resolve that Gwendolen had in her mind was that she would ask Deronda whether she ought to accept any of her husband's money—whether she might accept what would enable her to provide for her mother. The poor thing felt strong enough to do anything that would give her a higher place in Deronda's mind.

An invitation that Sir Hugo pressed on her with kind urgency was that she and Mrs. Davilow should go straight with him to Park Lane, and make his house their abode as long as mourning and other details needed attending to in London. Town, he insisted, was just then the most retired of places; and he proposed to exert himself at once in getting all articles belonging to Gwendolen away from the house in Grosvenor Square. No proposal could have suited her better than this of staying a little while in Park Lane. It would be easy for her there to have an interview with Deronda, if she only knew how to get a letter into his hands, asking him to come to her. During the journey, Sir Hugo, having understood that she was acquainted with the purport of her husband's will, ventured to talk before her and to her about her future arrangements, referring here and there to mildly agreeable prospects as matters of course, and otherwise shedding a decorous cheerfulness over her widowed position. It seemed to him really the more graceful course for a widow to recover her spirits on finding that her husband had not dealt as handsomely by her as he might have done; it was the testator's fault if he compromised all her grief at his departure by giving a testamentary reason for it, so that she might be supposed to look sad, not because he had left her, but because he had left her poor. The baronet, having his kindliness doubly fanned by the favorable wind on his fortunes and by compassion for Gwendolen, had become quite fatherly in his behavior to her, called her "my dear," and in mentioning Gadsmere to Mr. Gascoigne, with its various advantages and disadvantages, spoke of what "we" might do to make the best of that property. Gwendolen sat by in pale silence while Sir Hugo, with his face turned toward Mrs. Davilow or Mr. Gascoigne, conjectured that Mrs. Grandcourt might perhaps prefer letting Gadsmere to residing there during any part of the year, in which case he thought that it might be leased on capital terms to one of the fellows engaged with the coal: Sir

"O, welcome, pure-eyed Faith, white-handed Hope,
Thou hovering angel, girt with golden wings!"

Deronda hesitated before answering Gwendolen's summons. It was not pride that held him back, but a profound, aching sympathy. He feared the weight of another's heart, the terrifying possibility that her needs might exceed what he could ever give. He knew, with a clarity that left no room for debate, that her soul clung to his with a desperate, consuming hunger. One does not argue against the anger or the scorn that vibrates in a voice; one simply feels it, undeniable and absolute. Deronda felt her entire future hanging from a thread, balanced over a cliff of despair. If this tragedy had struck him a year prior, he would have acted without hesitation, driven by an impulsive need to shield her from sorrow, to build a fortress against loneliness, and to complete the rescue he had begun with the necklace. But now, the chains of love and duty bound him differently. That old, saving impulse still lived within him as a painful yearning, a trembling dread at the thought of facing her eyes and words again and again. Yet, it was precisely the strength of his resolve to stay away that made his pity for her fate sharper, more agonizing.

He waited in the back drawing-room, that space of white and crimson where they had once sat together at the musical party. It was here Gwendolen had first declared her lot dependent on his fidelity, her plea melting into the melodic cry, *Per pietà non dirmi addio*. But the melody had belonged to Mirah's voice.

Deronda paced the room, which he had known for years, feeling a strange metamorphosis in his own life. The familiar objects—from Lady Mallinger's gentle portrait to the urbane lions on the chimney-piece—seemed to belong to a past existence, visited only in memory. The house, once a home, now felt alien, transformed by the deep impressions of recent days. He stood there, hat in hand, awaiting the entrance of a young woman whose life was undergoing a tragic shift toward an uncertain result, one in which he felt his actions were still dangerously entangled.

Gwendolen entered, changed. Her mourning dress was obvious, but her expression carried a quiet satisfaction he had never seen before, not even in Genoa. Her contentment stemmed from his presence, yet there was no smile when they met and clasped hands. Both were heavy with remembrance and anxious foreboding.

"It was good of you to come," she said immediately, sitting in the nearest chair. "Let us sit down." He placed himself opposite her.

"I asked you to come because I need to know what I ought to do," she began without preamble. "Do not be afraid to tell me what you think is right, even if it is hard. I have decided to do it. Once, I feared poverty; I could not bear being under anyone else's control, which is why I married. I have endured worse since. I think I could endure poverty if you believe I should. Do you know about my husband's will?"

"Yes, Sir Hugo told me," Deronda replied, anticipating her question.

"Should I take anything he left me? I have been thinking," she said, her voice gaining a nervous urgency. "Perhaps you don't realize how much I truly thought of my mother when I married. I was selfish, yes, but I loved her, and I felt deeply about her poverty. What comforted me most when I was miserable was knowing she was better off because of my marriage. Now, the hardest thing for me would be to see her in poverty again. I have been thinking that if I took enough to provide for her, and nothing for myself, it would not be wrong. I was precious to her—and he took me from her—and he meant to... and if she had known—"

Gwendolen broke off. She had prepared for this meeting by focusing solely on this question of right toward her mother, but the thought carried with it reasons she could not speak aloud. Perilous memories swarmed between her words, making her speech increasingly agitated and tremulous. She looked down helplessly at her hands, now bare of all rings save her wedding band.

"Do not hurt yourself by speaking of that," Deronda said tenderly. "There is no need; the case is simple. I think I can hardly judge wrongly. You consult me because I am the only one to whom you have confided the most painful parts of your experience, and I understand your scruples." He paused, waiting for her to steady herself. The silence felt heavy with the tenderness in his voice, giving her the courage to lift her eyes. "You are conscious of something you feel is a crime against one who is dead. You believe you have forfeited all claim as a wife. You shrink from taking what was his. You want to keep yourself from profiting by his death. Your feelings even urge you to punish yourself—to scourge the self that disobeyed your better will. I have known something of that myself. Do I understand you?"

"Yes—at least, I want to be good, not like what I have been," Gwendolen said. "I will try to bear what you think I ought to bear. I have tried to tell you the worst about myself. What ought I to do?"

"If no one else were involved," Deronda said, "I would hardly dare urge you against your remorseful prompting. But I am guided now by your feeling toward Mrs. Davilow, which seems entirely just. I cannot think your husband's obligations to you are nullified by your actions. He voluntarily entered your life and shaped its course in the most significant way. However, in his position, he was duty-bound to provide for your mother, and he surely understood that if this will took effect, she would share the provision made for you."

"She has had eight hundred a year," Gwendolen said. "What I wanted to do was take that and leave the rest." She had argued inwardly for so long that her mind could not instantly shift to another perspective.

"I think it is not your duty to set such a limit," Deronda replied. "You would create a painful enigma for Mrs. Davilow; an income from which you exclude yourself would be embittered to her. Your own path would become too difficult. We agreed in Genoa that the burden on your conscience is one no one should know about. The future goodness of your life will be best served by saving others from the pain of that knowledge. In my opinion, you should simply abide by your husband's will, letting your remorse influence only how you use your newfound independence."

As he spoke the last sentence, Deronda instinctively picked up his hat from the floor beside him. Gwendolen, sensitive to his slightest movement, felt her heart leap as if it possessed its own consciousness, fearing he would leave. In that same moment, she rose from her chair, unable to consider that his motion signaled his departure. Deronda, of course, also rose, stepping forward slightly.

"I will do what you tell me," Gwendolen said hurriedly. "But what else shall I do?" No other words were possible for her; even these simple ones were too much for her emotional state, where her proud secrecy had crumbled. As the childlike sentences fell from her lips, they reflected her own helplessness back at her, and she could not stop the sob that brought large tears to her eyes. Deronda felt a crushing pain, yet visible consequences urged him to exert every ounce of his conscience. When she pressed her tears away, he asked gently, "You will probably be going with Mrs. Davilow into the country soon."

"Yes, in a week or ten days." Gwendolen waited an instant, turning her eyes vaguely toward the window as if gazing at some imagined prospect. "I want to be kind to them all—they can be happier than I can. Is that the best I can do?"

"I think so. It is a duty that cannot be doubted," Deronda said. He paused between sentences, feeling a heavy anxiety weigh down his words. "Other duties will spring from it. Viewing your life as a debt may seem the dreariest perspective from afar, but it is not really so. What makes life dreary is the lack of motive. Once you begin acting with this penitential, loving purpose you hold in your mind, there will be unexpected satisfactions—newly opening needs that will carry you from day to day. You will find your life growing like a plant."

Gwendolen turned her eyes to him with the look of one thirsting for unseen waters. Deronda felt the gaze as if she were stretching her arms toward him from a forsaken shore. His voice took on an affectionate imploringness when he said, "This sorrow, which has cut down to the root, has come to you while you are so young—try to think of it not as a spoiling of your life, but as a preparation for it. Let it be a preparation..." Anyone overhearing his tones would have thought he was entreating for his own happiness. "See! You have been saved from the worst evils that might have arisen from your marriage, which you feel was wrong. You have had a vision of injurious, selfish action—a vision of possible degradation. Think of a severe angel, seeing you along the road of error, grasping you by the wrist and showing you the horror of the life you must avoid. And it has come to you in your spring-time. Think of it as a preparation. You can, you will, be among the best of women, such as make others glad that they were born."

The words were like the touch of a miraculous hand to Gwendolen. Mingled emotions streamed through her frame with a strength that seemed the beginning of a new existence, stirring something vague yet powerful within her. So pregnant is the divine hope of moral recovery with the energy that fulfills it. So potent is the infused action of another soul, before which we bow in complete love. But this new existence seemed inseparable from Deronda; the hope made his presence permanent. It was not her thought that he loved her and would cling to her—a thought that would have wavered on improbability; it was her spiritual breath. For the first time since that terrible moment on the sea, a flush rose and spread over her cheek, brow, and neck, deepening for an instant before gradually fading. She did not speak.

Deronda stepped forward and extended his hand, saying, "I must not weary you."

Startled by the sense of his departure, she put her hand in his, still silent.

"You look ill yet—unlike yourself," he added while holding her hand.

"I can't sleep much," she answered, returning somewhat to her dispirited manner. "Things repeat themselves in me so. They come back—they will all come back," she ended, shuddering, a chill fear threatening her.

"By degrees they will be less insistent," Deronda said. He could not drop her hand or move away abruptly.

"Sir Hugo says he shall come to stay at Diplow," Gwendolen said, snatching at previously intended words that had slipped away. "You will come too."

"Probably," Deronda said, then feeling the word was cold, he corrected himself, "Yes, I shall come," before releasing her hand with the final, friendly pressure of one who has virtually said goodbye.

"And not again here, before I leave town?" Gwendolen asked, with timid sadness, looking as pale as ever.

What could Deronda say? "If I can be of any use—if you wish me—certainly I will."

"I must wish it," Gwendolen said impetuously. "You know I must wish it. What strength have I? Who else is there?" Another sob rose in her throat.

Deronda felt a pang that showed itself in his face. He looked miserable as he said, "I will certainly come."

Gwendolen perceived the change in his face, but the intense relief of expecting him to return could not yield to any other feeling. There was a recovery of inspired hope and courage in her.

"Don't be unhappy about me," she said in a tone of affectionate assurance. "I shall remember your words—every one of them. I shall remember what you believe about me; I shall try."

She looked at him firmly and put out her hand again, as if forgetting what had passed since those words she promised to remember. Yet there was no approach to a smile on her lips. She had never smiled since her husband's death. When she stood still in silence, she resembled a melancholy statue of the Gwendolen whose laughter had once been so ready when others were grave.

Only by remembering the searching anguish that changed the aspect of the world for her can we understand her behavior toward Deronda—the unreflecting openness, indeed the importunate pleading, with which she expressed her dependence on him. Considerations that would fill the minds of indifferent spectators could not occur to her, any more than if flames had been mounting around her and she had flung herself into his open arms, clinging to his neck to be carried to safety. She identified him with the struggling regenerative process in her which had begun with his action. Was it any wonder she saw her own necessity reflected in his feeling? She was in that state of unconscious reliance and expectation common to us when preoccupied with our own troubles. We diffuse our feelings over others and count on them acting from our motives. Her imagination had not been turned to a future union with Deronda by any imagery other than the spiritual tie that had continually strengthened; nor had it been turned toward a future separation. Love-making and marriage—how could they now be the imagery in which poor Gwendolen's deepest attachment could spontaneously clothe itself? Mighty Love had laid his hand upon her, but what had he demanded? Acceptance of rebuke—the hard task of self-change—confession—endurance. If she cried toward him, what then? She cried as a child whose little feet have fallen backward, crying to be taken by the hand lest she lose herself.

The cry pierced Deronda. What position could be more difficult for a man full of tenderness yet possessing clear foresight? He was the only creature who knew the true nature of Gwendolen's trouble; to withdraw himself from her appeal would be to consign her to dangerous loneliness. He could not reconcile himself to the cruelty of apparently rejecting her dependence, yet in the near or distant future he saw a coming wrench that all present

"One day still fierce 'mid many a day struck calm."
—Browning: *The King and the Book*

While Gwendolen remained oblivious to the shifting currents of Daniel Deronda's life, the waters around Ezra and Mirah grew deeper and clearer. The shadow of their father, Lapidoth, had lengthened over them, casting a stark light on the fragile bonds that held them together.

Lapidoth had abandoned his daughter at the threshold, driven by a desperate gamble that promised relief from the gnawing hunger of poverty. In his mind, the thrill of risking a sum greater than mere survival outweighed any future consequences. Until the very last coin was lost, he refused to consider the alternative: facing his son again or confronting the reality of his emptiness. Initially, he had recoiled from Ezra as one might shrink from a physical blow, but the possession of Mirah's purse silenced those fears. The gambling instinct, a voracious demon, demanded constant diversion, rendering all other emotions dull and irrelevant. It was a state of unjoyous dissipation, a frantic search for excitement on the burning sands of ruin.

Yet, even the most abstract selfishness requires the fuel of daily sustenance. Lapidoth's appetite was modest, but his current existence was shabby and unfriendly, demanding ready cash to survive. After a brief visit to a house marked "Pyramids" on the blind, where he had doubled and trebled Mirah's thirty shillings only to lose them all, he emerged with an empty purse. He stood there, weighing two options: pawn the purse for immediate funds or return it to Mirah with a forced smile, claiming he had settled a debt. Beneath his shame lingered a stubborn belief in his own rights; he convinced himself that his claim on his children's property superseded the justice of his son's anger. To him, returning to his children was the safest harbor, and the thought of meeting Ezra became less daunting as he imagined luck turning. Perhaps his daughter's new friends or her affairs would bring supplies his cleverness could exploit. He clung to the old habit of believing in his own wit, unaware that his charm had faded long ago.

His calculations led him to the little square in Brompton. He hoped that by pacing the streets, he might spot Mirah leaving or returning, making his entry into the house easier. But evening had already fallen—the night following the day he first saw her. Weariness set in, and he considered ringing the bell, asking for her expected return time. Yet, as he approached, he knew she was home; he heard her singing.

Inside, Mirah sat at the piano, pouring out the melody of "Herz, mein Herz." Ezra listened with his eyes closed, lost in the music. Suddenly, Mrs. Adam appeared at the door, her face flushed with embarrassment. "A gentleman below says he is your father, miss," she announced.

"I will go down to him," Mirah said, rising instantly and turning to her brother.

"No, Mirah, not so," Ezra replied, his voice firm. "Let him come up, Mrs. Adam."

Mirah stood frozen, her fingers twisting nervously, her stomach churning with anxiety. She watched Ezra, who had also risen, his face pale and shaken. There was an expression on his brow she had never seen before—a knitted severity, lips hardened by a grim resolve that mirrored the steel in his eyes.

When Mrs. Adam opened the door to admit the stranger, she glanced at the group, noting the striking resemblance between the younger man and the elder. "Father, sure enough," she murmured to herself as she closed the door behind them. The outline was unmistakable, but the spirit within had been forged into sharp contrasts by years of hidden suffering and inconspicuous differences.

Lapidoth wore a premeditated look of melancholy, though a genuine wince tightened his frame as he spoke. "Well, Ezra, my boy, you hardly know me after so many years."

"I know you—too well—father," Ezra answered, his tone slow and biting, making the word "father" sting like a reproach.

"Ah, you are not pleased with me. I don't wonder at it. Appearances have been against me. When a man gets into straits, he cannot act as he pleases. I've suffered enough, I know," Lapidoth said quickly, regaining some of his usual glibness. Turning to Mirah, he held out her purse. "Here's your little purse, my dear. I thought you'd be anxious about it because of that bit of writing. I've emptied it, you'll see, for I had a score to pay for food and lodging. I knew you would like me to clear myself, and here I stand—without a single farthing in my pocket—at the mercy of my children. You can turn me out if you like, without getting a policeman. Say the word, Mirah; say, 'Father, I've had enough of you; you made a pet of me, and spent your all on me, when I couldn't have done without you; but I can do better without you now,'—say that, and I'm gone out like a spark. I shan't spoil your pleasure again." Tears welled in his voice before he finished.

"You know I could never say it, father," Mirah answered, her anguish deepened by the sheer falsity of his words, except for his implied wish to remain.

"Mirah, my sister, leave us!" Ezra commanded, his tone authoritative.

She looked at her brother with faltering, beseeching eyes, awed by his decision yet unable to depart without pleading for this father who felt like a part of her own painful growth. She stepped close, taking his hand. "Remember, Ezra—you said my mother would not have shut him out."

"Trust me, and go," Ezra said.

She left the room, but after climbing a few steps, she collapsed onto a landing, her heart pounding. If her brother's words drove him away...

Lapidoth sensed the storm brewing in his son's mind, yet he began to adjust, seeking a perspective that granted him a cool superiority over any humiliation. This haggard son, speaking from a sepulcher, presented an incongruity that selfishness often mocks in suffering. Until disease clutched its own flesh with unrelenting pincers, Lapidoth dismissed the gravity of the moment. Whatever preaching followed would be taken as a matter of course, like religious howling in an open cathedral during a hailstorm. Lapidoth was not born callous; he had cultivated it.

"This home that we have here," Ezra began, his voice steady despite the turmoil beneath, "is maintained partly by the generosity of a beloved friend who supports me, and partly by the labors of my sister, who supports herself. While we have a home, we will not shut you out from it. We will not cast you out to the mercy of your vices. For you are our father, and though you have broken your bond, we acknowledge ours. But I will never trust you. You absconded with money, leaving your debts unpaid; you forsook my mother; you robbed her of her little child and broke her heart; you have become a gambler, and where shame and conscience were there sits an insatiable desire; you were ready to sell my sister—you had sold her, but the price was denied you. The man who has done these things must never expect to be trusted any more. We will share our food with you—you shall have a bed, and clothing. We will do this duty to you, because you are our father. But you will never be trusted. You are an evil man: you made the misery of our mother. That such a man is our father is a brand on our flesh which will not cease smarting. But the Eternal has laid it upon us; and though human justice were to flog you for crimes, and your body fell helpless before the public scorn, we would still say, 'This is our father; make way, that we may carry him out of your sight.'"

Lapidoth, adjusting himself to the coming blow, had not foreseen the intensity of the lightning or its precise path. He could not anticipate this voice from the soul of his son, which struck the spring of hysterical excitability Mirah had once witnessed in him. As Ezra concluded, Lapidoth threw himself into a chair and wept like a woman, burying his face against the table. Strangely, this hysterical crying was both an inevitable reaction to his son's words and a conscious resource, a tool he had used since youth to deflect resentment.

Ezra sat down again, silent, exhausted by the shock of his own irrepressible utterance, the outburst of feelings he had borne in solitude for years. His thin hands trembled on the chair arms; he would hardly have found the voice to answer a question. He felt as though he had taken a step toward beckoning Death.

Meanwhile, Mirah's quick ear detected a familiar sound. Her heart recognized it, and she could not stay out of the room. Opening the door, her immediate alarm was for Ezra. She rushed to his side, taking his trembling hand, which he pressed gratefully. He did not speak or look at her. The father, face buried, was aware of her presence. He lifted his head, pressed a handkerchief to his eyes, extended a hand toward her, and spoke with plaintive hoarseness. "Good-bye, Mirah; your father will not trouble you again. He deserves to die like a dog by the roadside, and he will. If your mother had lived, she would have forgiven me—thirty-four years ago I put the ring on her finger under the *Chuppa*, and we were made one. She would have forgiven me, and we should have spent our old age together. But I haven't deserved it. Good-bye."

He rose as he spoke the final farewell. Mirah had taken his hand and held him tight. She was not tearful or grieving, but frightened and awe-struck. "No, father, no!" she cried. Turning to her brother, she pleaded, "Ezra, you have not forbidden him?—Stay, father, and leave off wrong things. Ezra, I cannot bear it. How can I say to my father, 'Go and die!'"

"I have not said it," Ezra answered, with great effort. "I have said, stay and be sheltered."

"Then you will stay, father—and be taken care of—and come with me," Mirah said, drawing him toward the door.

This was exactly what Lapidoth wanted. For the moment, he felt a strange comfort in recovering his daughter's dutiful attendance, making a change of habits seem possible. She led him down to the parlor below. "This is my sitting-room when I am not with Ezra, and there is a bedroom behind which shall be yours. You will stay and be good, father. Think that you are come back to my mother, and that she has forgiven you—she speaks to you through me." Mirah's tones were imploring, yet she could not offer one of her former caresses.

Lapidoth quickly recovered his composure, beginning to speak to Mirah of the improvement in her voice and other easy subjects. When Mrs. Adam came to lay out his supper, he engaged her in conversation to prove he was not a common person, despite his ragged clothes.

But in his usual wakefulness at night, his mind drifted to wonder what money Mirah possessed. He replayed old Continental hours at *Roulette*, reproducing the methods of his play and the chances that had frustrated him. He had had reasons for coming to England, but for most things, it was a cursed country.

These were the stronger visions of the night for Lapidoth, overshadowing the worn frame of his ireful son uttering a terrible judgment. Ezra did pass across the gaming-table in his memory, and his words were audible; but he passed like an insubstantial ghost, his words having the heart eaten out of them by numbers and movements that seemed to weave the very tissue of Lapidoth's consciousness.

The divine spark within us twists our noblest acts from the reluctant clay of our own souls.

For Daniel Deronda, the return from the Abbey brought a bitter shock: the unwanted father had already taken root in the Brompton lodgings. Mirah, driven by a mixture of necessity and fear, had felt compelled to introduce her father to Deronda. She had carefully curated her narrative, weaving a tale of friendship with Ezra that began innocently, emphasizing the sympathy that bound them while meticulously omitting the rescue from drowning and the shelter provided by the Meyricks. She could not bear to let her father's gaze fully inspect the depth of her connection with Deronda. Consequently, Lapidoth, for reasons of his own, did not press for details regarding her flight or arrival in England. Yet, he was deeply intrigued by the existence of a benevolent friend who appeared to hold high station in the world.

It was Ezra's brother who first informed Deronda of this new, heavy addition to their lives. "I have grown calm in watching him now," Ezra concluded, his voice weary. "I try to believe that my sister's tenderness and the daily taste of peace may win him to stay away from temptation. I have forbidden her to give him money, convincing her that he would spend it on his own ruin."

Deronda arrived at the lodgings on the third day since Lapidoth's arrival. His new clothes were not yet ready, and wishing to make a favorable impression, he refused to appear in his old attire. Watching him from the window, Lapidoth was struck by Deronda's youthfulness—a detail Mirah had omitted, which Lapidoth had instinctively dismissed as irrelevant in a figure associated with the grave studies and somber demeanor of Ezra. Lapidoth quickly surmised that Deronda's true motive must be love for Mirah. To him, this was a blessing; a bond with Mirah promised more indulgence for her father than a tie to Ezra. Lapidoth hoped to recommend himself to Deronda, softening any hard prepossessions he might hold. He behaved with studied amiability, eager to domesticate himself within the household. He immersed himself in Mirah's music, feigned docility regarding smoking (which Mrs. Adam forbade in the parlor), and strolled the square with his German pipe, fueled by tobacco Mirah supplied. He was too sharp to remonstrate against Mirah's refusal to give him money, accepting her insistence on this solemn duty as a promise to her brother. He was content to wait.

On the next visit, Lapidoth, freshly dressed and pleased with his reflection, sat in the room with Ezra, who was teaching himself, as a severe penance, to tolerate his father's presence whenever imposed. Deronda felt cold and distant. The sight of this man, who had blighted the lives of his wife and children, created in him a repulsion that bordered on physical discomfort. Yet, Lapidoth did not falter. He asked permission to stay and listen to the reading of papers from the old chest, eventually proving useful by helping to decipher a difficult German manuscript. This led him to suggest transcribing the manuscript, offering his services to copy papers in Roman characters as well. Though Ezra's eyes were failing, Lapidoth's remained strong. Deronda accepted the offer, interpreting it as a sign of grace in Lapidoth's willingness to be employed usefully. He noted a gratified expression on Ezra's face, though Ezra soon interjected, "Let all the writing be done here; I cannot trust the papers out of my sight, lest there be an accident by fire or otherwise."

Poor Ezra felt as though he held a convict on leave under his guard. Unless he saw his father working, he could not believe he would labor in good faith. By this arrangement, Ezra fastened upon himself the crushing burden of his father's presence. It was made painful not only by his deepest, longest associations but also by Lapidoth's restless temperament, which grew more apparent as he became familiar with his situation and lost any awe he might have felt for his son. In truth, Ezra was placing a severe constraint on himself, confining his attention solely to win Deronda's favor. Like a man trapped in an uncomfortable garment, he sought relief at every opportunity: going out to smoke, pacing and talking, or slumping back in his chair in silence, yet incessantly communicating a dumb language of facial movements and gestures. If Mirah was present, he would revert to his old habit of gossiping with her about their former doings, companions, and the plots of plays he once adapted, believing he could command the vivacity of his earlier self. This was a mortal infliction for Ezra. When Mirah was home, she tried to alleviate his suffering by keeping her father in the parlor and watching over him. What duty is forged from a single difficult resolve? The difficulty lies in the daily, unflinching support of consequences that mar the blessed return of morning with the prospect of irritation to be suppressed or shame to be endured. Such burdens were borne by these, as by many other heroic children of an unworthy father, with the hope, at least to Mirah, that they would stretch onward through the solid part of life.

Meanwhile, Lapidoth's presence had erected a new, impalpable partition between Deronda and Mirah. Each dreaded the soiling inferences of the other's mind, each misinterpreting the increased reserve and diffidence of the partner they loved. But it was not long before light broke for Deronda.

As soon as he could, after returning from his brief visit to the Abbey, Deronda called at Hans Meyrick's rooms. He felt it a due of friendship that Hans should be acquainted with the reasons for his recent journey and the changes of intention it had wrought. Hans was not there; he was said to be in the country for a few days. Deronda left a note and waited a week, expecting a reply. Receiving none, and fearing some capricious mood in the incalculably susceptible Hans—whose planned sojourn at the Abbey had already been deferred—he made a second call. He was admitted to the painting room, where he found his friend in a light coat without a waistcoat, his long hair still wet from a bath, yet with a face looking worn and wizened—anything but country-like. Hans had taken up his palette and brushes and stood before his easel when Deronda entered, but the equipment and attitude seemed arranged on short notice.

As they shook hands, Deronda remarked, "You don't look much as if you had been in the country, old fellow. Is it Cambridge you have been to?"

"No," Hans replied curtly, throwing down his palette with the air of someone who had begun to feign by mistake. Pushing forward a chair for Deronda, he threw himself into another, leaning backward with his hands behind his head. "I've been to I-don't-know-where—No Man's Land—and a mortally unpleasant country it is."

"You don't mean to say you have been drinking, Hans," Deronda said, seating himself opposite in anxious survey.

"Nothing so good. I've been smoking opium. I always meant to do it sometime to try how much bliss could be got by it; and having found myself just now rather out of other bliss, I thought it judicious to seize the opportunity. But I pledge you my word I shall never tap a cask of that bliss again. It disagrees with my constitution."

"What has been the matter? You were in good spirits enough when you wrote to me."

"Oh, nothing in particular. The world began to look seedy—a sort of cabbage-garden with all the cabbages cut. A malady of genius, you may be sure," Hans said, creasing his face into a smile. "And, in fact, I was tired of being virtuous without reward, especially in this hot London weather."

"Nothing else? No real vexation?" Deronda pressed.

Hans shook his head.

"I came to tell you of my own affairs, but I can't do it with a good grace if you are to hide yours."

"Haven't an affair in the world," Hans said in a flighty way, "except a quarrel with a bric-à-brac man. Besides, as it is the first time in our lives that you ever spoke to me about your own affairs, you are only beginning to pay a pretty long debt."

Deronda felt convinced that Hans was behaving artificially, yet he trusted that the old frankness would return if he gave his own confidence.

"You laughed at the mystery of my journey to Italy, Hans," Deronda began. "It was for an object that touched my happiness at the very roots. I had never known anything about my parents, and I really went to Genoa to meet my mother. My father has been long dead—died when I was an infant. My mother was the daughter of an eminent Jew; my father was her cousin. Many things had caused me to think of this origin as almost a probability before I set out. I was so far prepared for the result that I was glad of it—glad to find myself a Jew."

"You must not expect me to look surprised, Deronda," Hans said, changing his attitude, laying one leg across the other and examining the heel of his slipper. "You knew it?"

"My mother told me. She went to the house the morning after you had been there—brother and sister both told her. You may imagine we can't rejoice as they do. But whatever you are glad of, I shall come to be glad of in the end—_when_ exactly the end may be I can't predict," Hans said, speaking in a low tone, which was as usual with him when out of humor with his lot, yet bent on making no fuss about it.

"I quite understand that you can't share my feeling," Deronda said. "But I could not let silence lie between us on what casts quite a new light over my future. I have taken up some of Mordecai's ideas, and I mean to try and carry them out, so far as one man's efforts can go. I dare say I shall by and by travel to the East and be away for some years."

Hans said nothing, but rose, seized his palette, and began working his brush on it, standing before his picture with his back to Deronda. Deronda also felt himself at a break in his path, embarrassed by Hans's embarrassment.

Presently Hans said, again speaking low and without turning, "Excuse the question, but does Mrs. Grandcourt know of all this?"

"No; and I must beg of you, Hans," Deronda said, rather angrily, "to cease joking on that subject. Any notions you have are wide of the truth—they are the very reverse of the truth."

"I am no more inclined to joke than I shall be at my own funeral," Hans replied. "But I am not at all sure that you are aware what are my notions on that subject."

"Perhaps not," Deronda said. "But let me say, once for all, that in relation to Mrs. Grandcourt, I never have had, and never shall have the position of a lover. If you have ever seriously put that interpretation on anything you have observed, you are supremely mistaken."

There was silence for a little while, and to each the silence was like an irritating air, exaggerating their discomfort.

"Perhaps I have been mistaken in another interpretation, also," Hans said presently.

"What is that?"

"That you had no wish to hold the position of a lover toward another woman, who is neither wife nor widow."

"I can't pretend not to understand you, Meyrick. It is painful that our wishes should clash. I hope you will tell me if you have any ground for supposing that you would succeed."

"That seems rather a superfluous inquiry on your part, Deronda," Hans said with some irritation.

"Why superfluous?"

"Because you are perfectly convinced on the subject—and probably have had the very best evidence to convince you."

"I will be more frank with you than you are with me," Deronda said, still heated by Hans's show of temper, yet sorry for him. "I have never had the slightest evidence that I should succeed myself. In fact, I have very little hope."

Hans looked round hastily at his friend, but immediately turned to his picture again.

"And in our present situation," Deronda said, hurt by the idea that Hans suspected him of insincerity, and giving an offended emphasis to his words, "I don't see how I can deliberately make known my feeling to her. If she could not return it, I should have embittered her best comfort; for neither she nor I can be parted from her brother, and we should have to meet continually. If I were to cause her that sort of pain by an unwilling betrayal of my feeling, I should be no better than a mischievous animal."

"I don't know that I have ever betrayed _my_ feeling to her," Hans said, as if vindicating himself.

"You mean that we are on a level, then; you have no reason to envy me."

"Oh, not the slightest," Hans said with bitter irony. "You have measured my conceit and know that it out-tops all your advantages."

"I am a nuisance to you, Meyrick. I am sorry, but I can't help it," Deronda said, rising. "After what passed between us before, I wished to have this explanation; and I don't see that any pretensions of mine have made a real difference to you. They are not likely to make any pleasant difference to myself under present circumstances. Now the father is there—did you know that the father is there?"

"Yes. If he were not a Jew I would permit myself to damn him—with faint praise, I mean," Hans said, but with no smile.

"She and I meet under greater constraint than ever. Things might go on in this way for two years without my getting any insight into her feeling toward me. That is the whole state of affairs, Hans. Neither you nor I have injured the other, that I can see. We must put up with this sort of rivalry in a hope that is likely enough to come to nothing. Our friendship can bear that strain, surely."

"No, it can't," Hans said impetuously, throwing down his tools, thrusting his hands into his coat-pockets, and turning round to face Deronda, who drew back a little and

"All thoughts, all passions, all delights,
Whatever stirs this mortal frame,
All are but ministers of Love,
And feed his sacred flame."

Deronda's heart burned with a fervor that Hans's words had kindled. The assurance that Mirah needed relief from the torment of jealousy became the catalyst for his resolve. On his next visit to Ezra, he arrived determined to seize an opportunity for private confession. If Mirah accepted his love, he felt invincible regarding the consequences; as her betrothed husband, he believed he would gain a protective authority, a shield against future storms with her father. Though he saw no signs of restlessness in Lapidoth, nor any waning desire for his daughter's favor, Deronda harbored deep forebodings. He feared a coming struggle, a mortification, or an unbearable increase in domestic discord that might leave Ezra and Mirah helpless victims.

These fears were rooted in a truth Lapidoth did not know. The restlessness that plagued Ezra was, for the father, merely an irksome submission to restraint, endured only because he viewed it as a temporary price for eventual, well-conditioned freedom. Lapidoth had begun by hoping for a chance to extract a substantial sum from Deronda, but his mind wandered curiously, searching for where Mirah hid her money and keys. The imperious gambling desire that drove him through every other aspect of life, weaving a continuous net of imagination, would have been satisfied instantly if he could have captured a fortune. Yet, Mirah, with her practical clear-sightedness, guarded against any breach of her promise to Ezra. She entrusted all her savings, save what she needed immediately, to Mrs. Meyrick's care. Lapidoth felt trapped in a lunatic asylum where every resource was secured against his reach. To open a desk and pocket banknotes would have felt to him a harmless domestic appropriation, a liberty men often claim with others' property even beyond the law's grasp—much like how spoons are safer investments than mining shares. He felt grievously treated by his daughter, believing he deserved access to her earnings as readily as she did her apple-tarts. Yet, he remained submissive. The temptation that most nearly broke him was not an insistence on Mirah, but an appeal to Deronda. Those clever persons who have nothing else to sell can often command a high price for their absence. Lapidoth's desperate search for an exit forced upon him the notion that his family would be happier without him, and that Deronda, eager to be rid of him, would advance a considerable sum. But despite his practiced hardihood, he still held a secret awe of Ezra's imposing friend, deferring his purpose indefinitely.

On this particular day, Deronda entered the room glowing with a gladdened consciousness that inevitably brightened his air and speech. Lapidoth, however, was at a crisis of discontent, his mind busy with schemes of freedom, and Deronda's newfound amiability only fueled his restless plotting. This preoccupation was so strong it interfered with his usual pretense of interest in the proceedings, causing him to sit listlessly even when reading was underway. After a while, he excused himself to smoke and walk in the square, leaving the two friends at ease. Mirah was not present, but Deronda knew she would return before he left, and his eyes glowed with secret anticipation. He imagined that when he saw her again, he would find a sweetness of recognition in her gaze that had been sealed from him before. There was an added playful affectionateness in his manner toward Ezra.

"This little room is too close for you, Ezra," Deronda said, breaking off his reading. "The week's heat we sometimes get here is worse than the heat in Genoa, where one sits in the shaded coolness of large rooms. You must have a better home now. I shall do as I like with you, being the stronger half." He smiled toward Ezra, who replied, "I am straitened for nothing except breath. But you, who might be in a spacious palace, with the wide green country around you, find this a narrow prison. Nevertheless, I cannot say, 'Go.'"

"Oh, the country would be a banishment while you are here," Deronda said, rising and pacing the double room, which offered no long promenade, fanning himself with his handkerchief. "This is the happiest room in the world to me. Besides, I will imagine myself in the East, since I am getting ready to go there some day. Only I will not wear a cravat and a heavy ring there," he concluded emphatically, pausing to remove those superfluities and place them on a small table behind Ezra, who sat before a desk covered with books and papers.

"I have been wearing my memorable ring ever since I came home," Deronda continued as he reseated himself. "But I am such a Sybarite that I constantly put it off as a burden when I am doing anything. I understand why the Romans had summer rings—if they had them. Now then, I shall get on better."

Soon, they were absorbed in their work. Deronda read a piece of rabbinical Hebrew under Ezra's correction and comment, paying little heed when Lapidoth re-entered and took a seat somewhat in the background.

Lapidoth's rambling eyes quickly alighted on the ring that sparkled on the dark mahogany. During his walk, his mind had been occupied with the fiction of an advantageous opening abroad, requiring a sum of ready money. If communicated to Deronda in private, it might immediately draw a question regarding the amount needed. This part of his forecast was the most debatable; there was a danger in asking too much and a prospective regret in asking too little. His own desire gave him no limit, and he lacked guidance on the limit of Deronda's willingness. But now, amidst these airy conditions preparatory to a receipt that remained indefinite, the ring, which on Deronda's finger had become familiar to Lapidoth's envy, suddenly shone detached and within easy grasp. Its value was certainly below the smallest of the imaginary sums his purpose fluctuated between; yet, as a solid fact before him, his desire leaped into the thought—not yet an intention—that if he were quietly to pocket that ring and walk away, he would have the means of comfortable escape from present restraint, without trouble and without danger. Any property of Deronda's, available without his formal consent, was all one with his children's property, since their father would never be prosecuted for taking it. The details of this thinking followed each other so quickly they seemed to rise before him as one picture. Lapidoth had never committed larceny; yet larceny is a form of appropriation for which people are punished by law. Taking this ring from a virtual relation, who would have been willing to make a much heavier gift, would not come under the head of larceny. Still, the heavier gift was preferable if Lapidoth could only make haste enough in asking for it. The imaginary action of taking the ring, which kept repeating itself like an inward tune, sank into a rejected idea. He satisfied his urgent longing by resolving to go below and watch for the moment of Deronda's departure, when he would ask leave to join him in his walk and boldly carry out his meditated plan. He rose and stood looking out of the window, yet all the while he saw what lay beyond him—the brief passage he would have to make to the door close by the table where the ring was. However, he was resolved to go down; but, by no distinct change of resolution, rather by a dominance of desire like the thirst of the drunkard, it so happened that in passing the table his fingers fell noiselessly on the ring, and he found himself in the passage with the ring in his hand. It followed that he put on his hat and quitted the house. The possibility of again throwing himself on his children receded into the indefinite distance, and before he was out on the square, his sense of haste concentrated itself on selling the ring and getting on shipboard.

Deronda and Ezra were just aware of his exit; that was all. But by-and-by, Mirah came in and made a real interruption. She had not taken off her hat, and when Deronda rose to shake hands with her, she said, in a confusion at once unaccountable and troublesome to herself, "I only came in to see that Ezra had his new draught. I must go directly to Mrs. Meyrick's to fetch something."

"Pray allow me to walk with you," Deronda urged. "I must not tire Ezra any further; besides, my brains are melting. I want to go to Mrs. Meyrick's: may I go with you?"

"Oh, yes," Mirah said, blushing still more, with the vague sense of something new in Deronda, and turning away to pour out Ezra's draught. Ezra meanwhile threw back his head with his eyes shut, unable to get his mind away from the ideas that had been filling it while the reading was going on. Deronda stood for a moment thinking of nothing but the walk, until Mirah turned round again with the draught, when he suddenly remembered that he had laid aside his cravat. Saying, "Pray excuse my dishabille—I did not mean you to see it," he went to the little table, took up his cravat, and exclaimed with a violent impulse of surprise, "Good heavens, where is my ring gone?" beginning to search about on the floor.

Ezra looked round the corner of his chair. Mirah, quick as thought, went to the spot where Deronda was seeking and asked, "Did you lay it down?"

"Yes," Deronda replied, still unvisited by any other explanation than that the ring had fallen and was lurking in shadow, indiscernible on the variegated carpet. He moved the bits of furniture near, searching in all possible and impossible places with hand and eyes.

But another explanation had visited Mirah and taken the color from her cheeks. She went to Ezra's ear and whispered, "Was my father here?" He bent his head in reply, meeting her eyes with terrible understanding. She darted back to the spot where Deronda was still casting down his eyes in that hopeless exploration which we are apt to carry on over a space we have examined in vain. "You have not found it?" she said hurriedly.

He, meeting her frightened gaze, immediately caught alarm from it and answered, "I perhaps put it in my pocket," professing to feel for it there.

She watched him and said, "It is not there?—you put it on the table," with a penetrating voice that would not let him feign to have found it in his pocket; and immediately she rushed out of the room. Deronda followed her—she was gone into the sitting-room below to look for her father—she opened the door of the bedroom to see if he were there—she looked where his hat usually hung—she turned with her hands clasped tight and her lips pale, gazing despairingly out of the window. Then she looked up at Deronda, who had not dared to speak to her in her white agitation. She looked up at him, unable to utter a word—the look seemed a tacit acceptance of the humiliation she felt in his presence.

But he, taking her clasped hands between both his, said, in a tone of reverent adoration, "Mirah, let me think that he is my father as well as yours—that we can have no sorrow, no disgrace, no joy apart. I will rather take your grief to be mine than I would take the brightest joy of another woman. Say you will not reject me—say you will take me to share all things with you. Say you will promise to be my wife—say it now. I have been in doubt so long—I have had to hide my love so long. Say that now and always I may prove to you that I love you with complete love."

The change in Mirah had been gradual. She had not passed at once from anguish to the full, blessed consciousness that, in this moment of grief and shame, Deronda was giving her the highest tribute man can give to woman. With the first tones and the first words, she had only a sense of solemn comfort, referring this goodness of Deronda's to his feeling for Ezra. But by degrees the rapturous assurance of unhoped-for good took possession of her frame: her face glowed under Deronda's as he bent over her; yet she looked up still with intense gravity, as when she had first acknowledged with religious gratitude that he had thought her "worthy of the best;" and when he had finished, she could say nothing—she could only lift up her lips to his and just kiss them, as if that were the simplest "yes." They stood then, only looking at each other, he holding her hands between his—too happy to move, meeting so fully in their new consciousness that all signs would have seemed to throw them farther apart, till Mirah said in a whisper: "Let us go and comfort Ezra."

"The human nature unto which I felt
That I belonged, and reverenced with love,
Was not a punctual presence, but a spirit
Diffused through time and space, with aid derived
Of evidence from monuments, erect,
Prostrate, or leaning toward their common rest
In earth, the widely scattered wreck sublime
Of vanished nations."
—WORDSWORTH: _The Prelude_.

Sir Hugo fulfilled his plan to spend the autumn at Diplow, and by early October his presence had injected a fresh cheer into the neighborhood, reaching from the stately halls of Brackenshaw and Quetcham down to the respectable shop-parlors of Wanchester. Sir Hugo was a man of affable display, a Liberal of noble lineage who trusted in reform without fearing it would disrupt the cherished English habit of adorning society with hereditary rank. Consequently, he transformed Diplow into a delightful gathering place, carefully curating his guest list to avoid mixing disparate social strata. He invited old solicitors and young curates alike, yet ensured they did not dine together as equals, lest the younger ones feel slighted. Easy-going Lord Brackenshaw might have welcomed the company of Robinson the attorney, but Robinson would have been naturally offended to be asked to mingle with those he considered his peers. Sir Hugo, keenly aware of these nuances, secured popularity for himself and pleasure for others—a dual result that perfectly suited his nature. The rector of Pennicote now found a reception at Diplow vastly different from the haughty tolerance he endured under Grandcourt. It was not that the baronet liked Mr. Gascoigne; rather, he sought a marked cordiality with him on behalf of Mrs. Grandcourt, for whom Sir Hugo's chivalry had grown increasingly devoted. Why? The chief reason remained a secret, one he would not share even with Lady Mallinger, demonstrating that his chivalry included a rare reticence regarding a woman's secrets.

After becoming engaged to Mirah, Deronda felt compelled to declare his position and purposes to Sir Hugo, choosing the letter as his medium. He sensed that his fatherly friend would feel some dissatisfaction, perhaps pain, at this turn of fate. Reading unwelcome news offered the advantage of avoiding a hasty, regrettable outburst; Deronda dreaded the verbal collision that could make pardonable feelings lastingly offensive.

Sir Hugo, though not entirely surprised, was thoroughly vexed. His immediate recourse was to show the letter to Lady Mallinger, expecting her astonishment to serve as a counterweight to his discontent. Indeed, when she expressed shock and distress that Daniel's wonderful talents and the comfort of his presence should end in such madness over the Jews, the baronet could dismiss her concerns. "Oh, nonsense, my dear! Depend upon it, Dan will not make a fool of himself. He has large notions about Judaism—political views which you can't understand. No fear but Dan will keep himself head uppermost." Yet regarding the prospective marriage, she offered no solace. The gentle lady admitted, without rancor, that she had little dreamed of the coming storm when Mirah sang at her musical parties. After hesitation, she confessed that it had crossed her mind that Daniel might eventually marry Mrs. Grandcourt, given his presence in Genoa at that precise time. Though she was not fond of widows, she conceded such a union might have been preferable to his total departure with the Jews. Sir Hugo, sharing this opinion so strongly, could not correct it as a feminine error; his ill-humor at the disproof of his conclusions regarding Gwendolen remained unventilated. He instructed Lady Mallinger to say nothing until further notice, reasoning that if it were an unkind blow to Gwendolen, the delay would be kinder in her nervous state, and she would learn the truth best from Daniel himself. Sir Hugo's conjectures, fueled by his knowledge, led him to believe he understood the entire situation.

Meanwhile, his residence at Diplow allowed him to continue his fatherly attentions to Gwendolen, a role Lady Mallinger was willing to support despite her reservations about widows. The move to Offendene had been executed, and Gwendolen settled there with a calm surpassing her mother's hopes. She experienced a peaceful melancholy born of renouncing demands for self and accepting the ordinary good of existence—even kindness from a dog—as a gift beyond expectation. Does one who has nearly perished in a pit of darkness complain of the sweet air and daylight? One learns to view daily life as an escape, taking the quiet return of dawn and evening, or the star-like glow of pure fellowship, as a salvation that reconciles us to hardship. Those possessing a self-knowledge prompting self-accusation, like Hamlet's, understand this habitual feeling of rescue. Gwendolen dwelt on every word of Deronda's that pointed to her past deliverance from the worst evil within herself and the infliction of it upon others, clinging to words that offered resistance to self-despair.

Yet she was also buoyed by the prospect of seeing him again. She did not imagine him otherwise than always within her reach; her supreme need blinded her to the separateness of his life, filling the entire scene of his existence with her own relation to him. This was not unique to Gwendolen, for we are all prone to this passionate egoism of imagination, extending even toward God. The future she faced with a willing step was one where she would continually assimilate herself to a type he would hold before her. Had he not first risen in her vision as a corrective presence, initially resented but finally loved and trusted? She could not spontaneously conceive of an end to that reliance, which had become to her imagination the firmness of the earth, the only condition of her walking.

Deronda was not long in arriving at Diplow, a more convenient distance from town than the Abbey. He had planned to take Ezra and Mirah to a mild coastal spot while preparing a new home for Mirah as his bride, where they might jointly watch over her brother. But Ezra begged not to be removed unless it were to accompany them to the East. Outward solicitations became a burden to him, yet his mind dwelt on the possibility of this voyage with visionary joy. In his preparations for a marriage he hoped to conclude within two months, Deronda wished to consult Sir Hugo regarding his resources and affairs, providing a reason for his visit. But he valued another reason more: his promise to Gwendolen. The sense of blessedness in his own lot still held an aching anxiety at his heart—a paradox, for the beloved lover is always called happy, and happiness is often viewed as indifference to sorrow outside it. Yet human experience is frequently paradoxical. It was no treason to Mirah, but part of his full nature, that his joy in her could hold beside it the care for another. For what is love itself, for the one we love best? An enfolding of immeasurable cares which yet are better than any joys outside our love.

Deronda visited Diplow twice and saw Gwendolen twice, yet he returned to town without telling her of the change in his lot and prospects. He blamed himself, but in all momentous communications likely to cause pain, one feels dependent on some preparatory turn of words or associations, some agreement of the other's mood with the probable effect of what is to be imparted. In the first interview, Gwendolen was so absorbed in what she had to say—questions about arranging her life, how to reduce her ignorance, how to be kindest to everyone and amend her selfishness—that Deronda utterly shrank from waiving her immediate needs to speak of himself, or inflicting a wound when she leaned on him for help. In the second interview, when he went with new resolve to steer the conversation, he found her in deep depression, overpowered by distasteful, miserable memories that seemed more real than any new material for her future. She cried hysterically, claiming he would always despise her. He could only offer soothing words, and when she gradually revived with that pathetic look of renewed childlike interest, it was impossible to lay another burden on her.

Time passed, and he felt a pressing duty to make the difficult disclosure. Gwendolen never recognized his having other affairs, nor had she ever asked why he happened to be in Genoa. This unconsciousness would make a sudden revelation of affairs determining his life course a heavier blow; if he left the revelation to others, she would feel he had treated her with cruel inconsiderateness. He could not communicate in writing; his tenderness could not bear the thought of her reading his virtual farewell in solitude, perhaps feeling his words full of hard gladness for himself and indifference for her. He descended to Diplow again, feeling that every other peril was preferable to returning and leaving her in ignorance.

On this third visit, Deronda found Hans Meyrick installed with his easel at Diplow, beginning a picture of three daughters on a bank in the "Gainsborough style," and varying his work by sketching village children at Pennicote to improve his acquaintance with the Gascoignes. Hans appeared to have recovered his vivacity, yet Deronda detected feigning in it, much like the artificiality of a lady's bloom that is too high-toned and persistently steady. Despite his grateful friendship and admiration for Deronda, Hans could not help a certain irritation, such as incautious, open natures feel when a friend's reserve breaks to reveal a state not merely unsuspected but the reverse of what had been hoped. Poor Hans had always cared chiefly to confide in Deronda, remaining incurious about any confidence given in return, yet what outpourer of his own affairs is not tempted to think any hint of his friend's affairs is an egotistic irrelevance? It was a sore reflection to Hans that while he had naively opened his heart about Mirah, Deronda had kept secret a feeling of rivalry that now revealed itself as the important determining fact. Friends turning out to be something more than we are aware of is always at our peril. Hans must be excused for these promptings of bruised sensibility, since he had not allowed them to govern his substantial conduct; he possessed the consciousness of having done right by his fortunate friend, or as he told himself, "his metal had given a better ring than he would have sworn to beforehand." Hans had always declared himself a _dilettante_ in virtue: very fond of it in others, but cutting a poor figure if he meddled with it himself. Perhaps in reward for his good behavior, he gave his tongue more freedom, too fully possessed by the notion of Deronda's happiness to conceive what Deronda was feeling about Gwendolen, so he spoke of her without hesitation.

"When did you come down, Hans?" Deronda asked, joining him in the grounds where he studied the requisite bank and trees.

"Oh, ten days ago; before the time Sir Hugo fixed. I ran down with Rex Gascoigne and stayed at the rectory a day or two. I'm up in all the gossip of these parts; I know the state of the wheelwright's interior, and assisted at an infant school examination. Sister Anna, with the good upper lip, escorted me, else I should have been mobbed by three urchins and an idiot, because of my long hair and a general appearance which departs from the Pennicote type of the beautiful. Altogether, the village is idyllic. Its only fault is a dark curate with broad shoulders and broad trousers who ought to have gone into the heavy drapery line. The Gascoignes are perfect—besides being related to the Vandyke duchess. I caught a glimpse of her in her black robes at a distance, though she doesn't show to visitors."
